# Value Momentum Investing — Near-Verbatim Master Record

### Adam Khoo's Whale Investor / VMI Course, section by section in lecture order

---

## About This Document

This is a lecture-order written record of 55 course videos. Each video is
transcribed at full fidelity and condensed only by removing filler, restarts, greetings and
verbatim repetition. It runs at about 61% of the spoken word count
(128,637 words from 209,902).

**Fidelity rules applied to every section**
- His qualifiers are kept as spoken ("around", "roughly", "about", "I think", "usually").
  An approximate figure was not turned into an exact one, or the reverse.
- Every number, example, story, analogy, warning and aside is kept in the order he gave it.
- Where he points at something on screen without saying the value aloud, the text says the
  value was shown on screen and not stated.
- Obvious speech-recognition errors in terms or tickers are fixed; uncertain ones are marked [unclear].
- Gap-fill pass: where the new transcript was garbled or cut off, or a point was missing, it was
  checked against an earlier, separately transcribed record of the same lectures. Anything added
  from that earlier record carries the tag [from earlier pass] or [filled from earlier pass].
  Where the two records disagree on a figure, the text keeps the new reading and notes the other.
- Scope: the core VMI lessons. The ETF, broker/IBKR, options, leverage and bonus-webinar videos
  are not included.

# Part 1 — Lessons 06–10

### 06 - VMI Course Introduction (Must Watch) 2025

#### [00:00] Welcome and what the course promises
Adam Khoo welcomes students to the Whale Investor Course, also known as the Value Momentum Investor Course, and says it is a life-changing decision to join. In this course you learn exactly what it takes to achieve consistent profits from your investments in a stress-free, low-risk way, and more importantly in a way that is sustainable over the long run. You will learn the most effective strategies and be given the most effective tools to build your net worth and wealth consistently, so you can achieve what you ultimately want: financial freedom from the rat race. The course focuses on what truly works in the market and cuts out the noise and the "bullshit" taught by the financial media, as well as many impractical theories some people may have learned in business school or financial academia.

#### [00:53] The four keys to successful investing (W-W-H-W)
- First W: knowing exactly what to buy. Only buy the top 1% of the strongest stocks in the markets.
- Second W: knowing when to buy. Only buy the strongest stocks when they are attractively priced, when they are undervalued.
- H: knowing how many shares to buy. You learn how to size your position and build a diversified, balanced portfolio of great companies.
- Fourth, again a W: knowing when to sell. You learn all the rules of when to exit an investment.

If you follow the methodology strictly and the investment rules he teaches, you will be able to beat the market, get double-digit returns over the long run, and do a lot better than most retail investors and in fact the majority of professional money managers.

#### [01:49] Can you fail?
Some people ask whether they can fail in their investment journey. The answer is yes, but the only way is if you fail to take action, fail to apply what you learn, or don't follow the rules. If you break the rules, you can fail, because the rules are there to keep you safe and to ensure you do well in the market. Follow the rules and you will do very, very well.

#### [02:12] Complete every lesson
To derive the maximum benefit, he highly encourages you to complete all the lessons. Every single lesson is important; do not skip any. There are 12 lessons, each between two hours, with some of them three hours.

#### [02:39] The 12 lessons
- Lesson 1, right after this: stock market basics. He highly encourages you to do it even if you have years of experience, even if you are a professional money manager, because you have to go through the basics one more time with him.
- Lesson 2: how to invest in exchange traded funds (ETFs), the simplest way to get started in the markets.
- Lesson 3: the keys to profitable investing, an overview of the investing methodology.
- Lesson 4: fundamental analysis, learning how to analyze a business inside out and identify great companies behind stocks.
- Lesson 5: stock valuation. Really important: you have to learn how to value businesses so you know whether a stock is expensive or cheap.
- Lesson 6: technical analysis. How to optimize entries and exits using chart patterns.
- Lesson 7: everything is put together into a checklist and methodology called the seven-step VMI formula (value momentum investment formula), a concrete plan to analyze and identify great companies. Very important, though in fact they are all important.
- Lesson 8: once you can identify great companies, how to build a portfolio of them, a bulletproof portfolio that will do well under all kinds of economic conditions and grow over time.
- Lesson 9: how to find great stocks and investment opportunities, as well as exit rules: how do you know it is time to say goodbye to a stock and replace it with a new one?
- Lesson 10: to him the most important lesson, though it is number 10, and to him all are key lessons. It is psychology. 70% of an investor's success is their mindset, the ability to manage temperament and emotions. He highly recommends watching this one more than once, as many times as you can.
- Lesson 11: how to choose the right stock broker that meets your objectives.
- Lesson 12: how to turbocharge your portfolio using options. Although this is an investing course, you learn the basics of how he uses options to generate extra income and turbocharge the portfolio.

So there are 12 core lessons, each roughly about two to three hours.

#### [05:04] How to learn
Go through the course as many times as you need, because repetition is the mother of skill. The best way to learn is not to listen passively, where it goes in one ear and out the other, but to learn actively by taking notes. Even though you are given slides with all the notes, make your own notes, pause the video, repeat it, watch it.

At the end of every lesson there is usually a quiz. Do your best to attempt the questions, then run through the answers; it is important to test yourself and make sure you understood everything important in that lesson. After every lesson you can download the course notes, and there are also resources such as Excel templates to manage your investments and the intrinsic value calculator, all under course notes and resources.

#### [06:46] Additional sections
There are additional sections, for example one on how to use the charting platform he uses, mainly TradingView, and one on how to use the Interactive Brokers platform. You can use any broker you want, because once you learn how to use one broker the rest are pretty similar; just ensure it is a reputable broker that meets all your needs. There is also a bonus section with past additional webinars he has done for students.

#### [07:23] How long will the course take?
It is up to you. Some people go full intensive, 12 or 13 hours a day, and finish in two days. At about two hours a day, roughly half a lesson or a full lesson depending on length, it takes about 13 days, about a week and a half. At a really slow, comfortable pace of an hour a day, maybe a third or half a lesson a day, it takes about 26 days, or just a month. Whatever it takes, finish the course.

#### [08:14] The community
His students have been so successful not just because of the content and lessons but because of the community, which is on a Discord channel. You are invited to join. They chat seven days a week, even on weekends and public holidays, 15, 16 hours a day. Ask questions and the community will help; to direct a question to him specifically, tag @Adam Khoo. Ask questions relevant to what you learned in the course. Many times people jump in and ask questions already answered in the lessons, which is why people are let into the chat group only seven days after enrolling: you get a chance to watch the lessons first, and most questions will already be answered. If you still don't understand something he taught, or have additional questions, that is when you ask in the chat group.

Members are also encouraged to share ideas. If you have a great idea for a stock and have analyzed it using the methods you learned, share it; when everyone shares, everyone benefits from the collective wisdom. But please don't ask "Adam, what do you think about this stock?" He is not there to spoon-feed or say buy this or sell that; he is there to guide you to make your own investment decisions and your own analysis. After you analyze a stock, share your analysis and ask whether you have analyzed it correctly or for feedback, and he is happy to do that.

#### [10:19] Beware of scams
In the chat group, turn off your direct messages (DMs), because on any social media platform there are people impersonating him or someone else to scam you. Do not respond to any direct messaging. He will personally never send you a direct message; he only communicates through the main chat group, which is the main chat stock group under the investing course. There is another section called Friendly Chit Chat, which is more social, where you can talk about anything under the sun not directly related to the course; whatever is related to the course goes in the main chat group. You can also check out the other subsections.

Welcome to the course; see you in lesson number one, stock market basics.


### 07 - Stock Basics 2025 Part 1 (Parts of a Business)

#### [00:00] Lesson 1 overview
Whether you are a complete beginner or an experienced investor, it is important to run through the basics once again, especially the way he runs through them. Questions this first lesson answers: what is a share of stock (the focus is investing in shares of stock), what drives stock prices up and down every single day, what are stock exchanges, what is a stock index, and how do you profit from owning stocks.

#### [00:24] What is a share of stock?
When you buy a share of stock you are not buying a lottery ticket where you predict it will go up the next day, next week or next month and hope it doesn't go down. You are buying a piece of a business. "Please write this down": when you buy a share of stock, you become the part owner, a shareholder, of an underlying business.

A business, whether McDonald's, Microsoft or Apple, sells products and services in order to make money, so it is basically a money-making machine. The more money the business makes, the more it is worth; the more the business is worth, the more valuable the shares; and over time the price of the shares will increase. As owner of a piece of the business you have a claim over its profits. Always remember: when you buy a stock, you are buying a business (he likes to use "business" and "stock" interchangeably; it is the same thing).

If the business does well and makes more and more money, the shares will do very well; it is as simple as that, and "everything else is noise and a distraction". If the business makes less and less money over time, the value of the shares will go down. That is all that is important. The financial media likes sensational things like geopolitics, elections, war, the yield curve, inflation, what the Fed is doing; honestly none of that is important. The question to ask is whether the business you want to buy, whether McDonald's, Microsoft or Apple, is going to make more money over time. If so, the business will go up in value, the shares will be worth more, and the share price will keep going up.

Example: one of his reasons for being a Microsoft shareholder is that regardless of who is president of the United States, regardless of the yield curve, inflation, or what wars there are in the world, every year people have to use Microsoft, and more and more people use it. They raise their prices all the time and make more money every year. As a shareholder he has a bigger and bigger claim on those profits, and the shares will keep going up over time.

#### [03:30] Shares outstanding (the pizza analogy)
[The transcript is patchy here.] Shares outstanding are the shares that can be bought and sold. His example company, "Success Company", has a hundred million shares outstanding, like a pizza cut into a hundred million slices; you can buy as many slices as you want and so own as much of the pizza as you want. A company can issue more shares, so the number of shares can increase; it can also reduce the number by buying shares back from the market and cancelling them. So shares outstanding can increase and decrease over time, but right now this company has a hundred million shares. If you buy a million shares out of a hundred million in total, you own one percent of the company. That entitles you to one percent of the profits and one percent of the voting rights, but voting rights are not a focus of this course; "we're just interested in the money". [from earlier pass]

#### [05:21] Is there a minimum number of shares to buy?
It depends on the market. The great thing about the US market, the biggest market in the world, is that there is no minimum number of shares; you could buy as little as one share. Some brokers, like Interactive Brokers (the one he uses), even allow fractional shares, so if you can't afford one share you can buy a fraction of one. For example, right now one share of Meta Platforms (which owns Facebook and Instagram) is priced at close to $600. So the US is very flexible, with no minimum.

On the Singapore stock exchange you have to buy in lots of 100 shares. For example, if you want DBS Bank at $40 a share, you must buy a minimum of 100 shares, so the minimum investment is $40 x 100 shares = $4,000. Asian markets are less flexible than US markets.

#### [07:11] Basic information about a business: net income
The first thing to know is how much money the business is making. Go to finance.yahoo.com and search for Meta Platforms (just type the name). The ticker symbol is META; every stock has a ticker symbol (Apple is AAPL, Microsoft is MSFT). You don't have to memorize tickers; they show up when you search the company.

If you want to own a piece of this business, the first question is how much money it makes: the net income. Net income, net profits and earnings are used interchangeably; they are all the same thing. Net income means net income after tax: after deducting everything, what is left as the net profit or earnings.

On Yahoo Finance click "Statistics", scroll down and look for "net income available to common shareholders" (when you buy shares you are buying common shares). Net income is always over a 12-month period; "TTM" means trailing twelve months, the last 12 months from the day you look. He recorded this in December 2024, so the last 12 months is December 2023 to December 2024. Meta's net income is $55.54 billion. One billion is 1,000 million, so that is 55,540 million: the total profit the business earned over the last 12 months.

#### [10:55] Earnings per share
As a shareholder you want to know your portion. First find the total number of shares outstanding, on Yahoo Finance under Statistics. On the right it shows shares outstanding of 2.18 billion, and another figure, implied shares outstanding, of 2.52 billion. Which one? Usually take the implied shares outstanding. Shares outstanding is how many shares (slices of pizza) there currently are in the market. But companies give employees free shares using options, which employees can convert into shares in the future; if all of them convert, there will be more shares and you would share with more people. Implied shares outstanding includes that. To be conservative, always take the implied shares outstanding, here 2.52 billion, i.e. 2,520 million shares.

Earnings per share = total profit divided by total number of shares: $55.54 billion divided by 2,520 million. You don't have to calculate it yourself, as it is on the website; he is just explaining how it is derived. On Yahoo Finance's Summary page (the first page), EPS (TTM) is $21.17. Every share you own entitles you to $21.17 in profits (in the transcript the cents are garbled; he says "17 cents" after the $21).

#### [15:05] How do you actually get your share of profits? Four ways
You are entitled to the $21 per share, but the company doesn't simply send you a check for it. There are essentially four things the company can do with the profits.

1. Pay you cash, through dividends. Some companies don't pay any dividends and keep the profit for you, like a savings account; others pay a lot. Does Meta pay shareholders? On the Summary page, look for the dividend. "Forward" means the next year. If you buy the shares now you are expected to receive $2 in dividend per share. So out of the $21 the company earns per share in a year, it pays $2 and keeps the rest for you.

2. Buy back shares from the market. As long as they buy back shares when the shares are undervalued and the business is doing well, making more and more profits, that is a good sign. Why? When the company buys back shares, there are fewer shares outstanding, which means you share with fewer people, so each share is worth more. Extreme example: the company uses its profits to buy back half of all the shares outstanding. With 2,520 million shares, half is left, which he calculates on his calculator as 1.26 billion [the transcript garbles this as "260 million"]. The profit earned ($55.4 billion) is now divided over fewer shares, so the earnings per share will double (roughly from $21 to about $42 per share). [from earlier pass] Another way to see it: if each share entitled you to one quarter of the pizza, and after the buyback you own one slice out of only two slices, you go from owning 25% of the pizza to 50%. By buying back shares, the company indirectly gives you a greater percentage of the business, without you coming up with more money; the company buys the shares on your behalf. That is why he loves companies that buy back shares. Eventually you can sell your shares at a much higher price in the future.

3. Reinvest the profits in the business: research and development, entering new markets, building new factories, creating new products. That leads the business to make more revenue and profit in the future, the shares being worth more, and the share price increasing, and you benefit.

4. Keep the cash as retained earnings (covered at the end of this video).

#### [21:55] Return on invested capital (ROIC)
If the company takes the profits owed to you and reinvests them, can it get a higher return than if it returned the cash and you invested it elsewhere? To find out, he looks at return on invested capital, ROIC. You can't find it on Yahoo Finance, so he goes to another website, Stock Oracle (app.stockoracle.com; he shows the subscriber version but ROIC is on the free version too). Sign in, enter the ticker (META) in the search or top right corner. He notes this part was recorded a couple of months later, so numbers may not exactly match what he showed earlier, since the videos get updated with the latest information. Go to "Company Metrics".

There are several places to find ROIC. On the chart, ROIC for the last 12 months is 22.81%. It is also under "return on invested capital" at 22.8%, and in his favorite-metrics view, again 22.81%.

Is that good? Yes. Generally, when a company has an ROIC of 12% and above, it's a good business; more than 15%, an excellent business. But ROIC does not apply to financial services companies such as banks and insurance companies; for those just look at return on equity. Same for REITs (real estate investment trusts): you cannot use ROIC. It can be used for all other kinds of companies.

What does 22.81% mean? Whatever profits a business like Meta makes, by reinvesting them in the business they are growing 22.8% a year on average. As an investor, is it better to get the cash as dividends, or let the company reinvest your money at 22.81%? It depends on your objectives. If your main objective is cash dividends to pay your expenses, go for companies that pay dividends. But if your goal is compounding your wealth, you would rather leave the cash in the business at 22.81%. Example: Warren Buffett's company Berkshire Hathaway has not paid any dividends for 60 years; as a shareholder he'd rather reinvest the money within the business at a high ROIC.

#### [26:00] Retained earnings, and dividend taxes
Companies can also simply keep the cash as retained earnings and not return it to you. If a company keeps cash and does nothing productive with it, shareholders are not happy: "why are you keeping my cash?", you're not returning it as a dividend, buying back shares, or reinvesting at a good return. [The transcript is garbled in this passage.] His preference is companies that return capital through the first three methods (dividends, buybacks, productive reinvestment). [from earlier pass]

On tax: when a company pays a dividend you may pay tax, depending on where you live. If you are a US resident, you collect dividends from US companies and pay tax; for non-US residents it's a 30% withholding tax. Whereas if he buys Singapore or Hong Kong listed stocks, there is a 10% withholding tax [as stated; garbled in transcript], and as a Singaporean he would rather invest in Singapore-listed stocks where there's no dividend tax. So it is basically a bit different depending on the country. [Earlier pass reads this as: Singapore/Hong Kong listed stocks are generally free of dividend tax, with certain Hong Kong stocks carrying a 10% withholding tax; and that 30% on US dividends means only about 70% of each dollar is received. It also says he does not like buying US stocks for dividends because of the 30% tax and prefers them for capital gains, which carry no tax for a Singaporean.] [filled from earlier pass]

### 08 - Stock Basics 2025 Part 2 (P/E & PEG)

#### [00:00] Recap: earnings per share and how you get paid
In the earlier part he showed how to find the net income of the whole business (also called net profits, also called earnings) and the shares outstanding, or more specifically the implied shares outstanding. Total profit divided by shares outstanding gives earnings per share, which, if you own one share, tells you how much profit you are entitled to in a year. For Meta Platforms, earnings per share for the last 12 months was $21.17. As a shareholder you are entitled to this $21, but how do you actually get the money?

First, you may collect it as cash dividends, on which you have to pay taxes. Second, the company could do share buybacks, reducing the number of shares outstanding, which increases your percentage stake in the business and so the value of the share and the share price in the future. Third, the company can reinvest the profits at a high return on invested capital (ROIC), which also leads to a higher share price. Fourth, the company merely retains the cash in the business, like a piggy bank or savings, which also increases the value of the shares, although more slowly.

#### [01:46] Is $21 per share good or bad? Share price and market cap
That depends on the price you have to pay for the share. At the time of recording, Meta's share price closed at roughly $600 (he says about $599). The share price goes up and down every day; when the market is open it changes every second. By the time you watch it may be higher than 600 or may have dropped below 599; he doesn't know. But over time, if it is a company that makes more and more money, the share price will go up, so Meta would go to 650, 700, 800 over the long run.

If you buy one share right now you pay $599, and you are entitled to $21 of profit in a year. What if you wanted to buy all the shares, to own 100% of the company, theoretically? Take the price of one share, $599, and multiply by the total number of shares outstanding. That gives the market capitalization (market cap). You don't have to calculate it; any website has it. On the Yahoo Finance summary page Meta has a market cap of $1.51 trillion. One trillion is $1,000 billion, so this is $1,514 billion.

Is that a big company? Yes. Generally any company with a market cap of 10 billion or more is a large cap company; between 2 billion and 10 billion is a midcap; less than 2 billion is a small cap. It doesn't matter whether you buy small, mid or large cap, as long as it is a great business that fulfills the investment criteria taught in this course. But personally the majority of companies he buys are large companies, because generally large companies are the strongest and safest in the markets.

#### [04:23] The price to earnings (PE) ratio
For Meta, EPS is $21.17 and buying today would cost $599. Is that a good deal? Many investors calculate the price to earnings ratio (PE ratio): price per share divided by earnings per share. $599 divided by $21.17 gives a PE ratio of 28.31 times [earlier pass has $599 divided by about $21.70]. You can get this from most financial websites; on Yahoo Finance's summary page it is 28.33 (a slight rounding difference). This is based on TTM, the trailing 12 months of earnings. The PE ratio changes every day, and when the market is open, as the price fluctuates, it changes every second.

What does it tell you? The PE ratio is 28 times earnings, so for every $1 of earnings per share you are paying $28 to acquire that share. How long to break even? It depends. Assuming earnings per share does not grow, the company earns $21 every year, and to get back your $599 takes 28 years: $21 a year, and in 28 years you get back $599.

You may think that is a bloody long time and a bad deal. "Relax." In reality a business's earnings per share changes; a good business's earnings per share grows over time. Extreme example: earnings grow at 100% a year. This year the company earns $21 per share; next year it doubles to $42; then $84; then $168; then $336. Would it take 28 years to get back your investment? No, much faster because earnings are accelerating. Add up the profits and you get more than $650 [his figure], so in one, two, three, four, in less than five years you get back your money.

So whether the PE ratio is considered high or low depends on the growth of the earnings. The higher the EPS growth, the higher the PE ratio that is justified. PE ratio by itself is quite useless and meaningless unless you know the growth of the earnings. One of the most common mistakes investors make is to just look at the PE ratio and think a low PE means cheap and a high PE means expensive; that is "absolute rubbish". A stock with a high PE can be cheap if earnings growth is high, and a stock with a low PE can be expensive if earnings growth is low. Always look at the PE ratio in the context of earnings growth.

#### [09:30] Finding earnings growth
In the past you could find projected earnings growth on Yahoo Finance and GuruFocus, but they have taken it out recently (he doesn't know why). Two places to find it for free:

- finviz.com: enter the ticker (META), scroll down to see the last five years growth. Over the past five years Meta grew earnings at 14%, and for the next five years it is projected to grow at 17%. This is based on one website.
- Zacks.com: a different opinion (you need more than one opinion). Enter the quote; the earnings growth projection for Meta is 20.05%.

So Meta's earnings growth is somewhere between 17% and 20%; that is the range.

#### [10:50] Fair value: PE should equal the growth rate
Is Meta expensive at a PE ratio of 28 times with earnings growth of 17 to 20%? Theoretically, for a fairly priced company, the PE ratio should be equal to the earnings growth rate. If earnings grow at 15% a year, the PE ratio should be 15 times; at 5% growth, the PE should be 5; at 20% growth, the PE should be 20.

#### [11:45] The PEG ratio
A metric many investors use is the PEG ratio. It is slightly better than the PE ratio because it incorporates the earnings growth rate. Formula: PEG ratio = PE ratio divided by the earnings growth projection. For Meta the PE ratio was 28.31 and the earnings growth is between 17 and 20% for the next five years. If you take 20%, PEG = 28.31 / 20 = 1.41.

Is that expensive or cheap? If you studied finance, a finance degree or CFA, in theory a PEG ratio more than one is considered expensive (PE higher than the earnings growth rate), and below one the stock is undervalued. That is in theory. In practical real life, if you only look for stocks with PEG below one you get very cheap stocks, but you would miss out on a lot of very good companies. In his own investing career he found it okay to have a PEG above one, but not more than 1.5; above 1.5 it is getting a bit expensive on PEG. So Meta's PEG of 1.41, based on 20% earnings growth, is considered reasonably priced.

#### [14:06] Test: Nvidia vs Ford
Compare Nvidia (semiconductors) and Ford Motor Company (automobiles). Nvidia is at $137 per share with a PE ratio of 53.97 times earnings (on Finviz). Ford is at a share price of only $10 with a PE of 11.44 times. Which is cheaper?

The amateur mistake: Nvidia looks more expensive at $137 and PE 53 times, while Ford is only $10 a share and PE 11. Wrong. Ford is not cheaper, because PE ratio by itself is meaningless until compared with the earnings growth rate.

- Ford: PE 11.44; earnings projected to grow at only 3% (3.06) over the next five years, very slowly. PEG = 11.44 / 3.06 = 3.74, very expensive. Ideally PEG should not be more than 1.5: below 1.5 is okay/cheap, above 1.5 is getting a bit expensive.
- Nvidia: PE looks expensive at 53.97, but earnings are projected to grow at 35% for the next five years (in the last five years it grew at 48%), still pretty good growth. 53.97 / 35 = PEG 1.54, slightly above 1.5. So Nvidia is not cheap, but not super expensive; slightly expensive. But Nvidia is cheaper than Ford based on PEG.

#### [17:38] PE vs forward PE
Why are there two PE ratios, 53.97 and a "forward PE" of 30.95? PE = price (P) divided by earnings (E). Price is the price now, today. Earnings could be last year's or next year's. Using the last 12 months of earnings gives the PE ratio; using projected earnings over the next 12 months, a projection of the future, gives the forward PE. For Nvidia, forward PE is lower than the trailing PE because next year's earnings are projected to be much bigger than the last 12 months'; bigger earnings in the future means a lower forward PE.

#### [18:54] Why PE is a poor way to value a stock
Many investors make the mistake of avoiding high PE stocks and buying low PE stocks. Very often, not always, low PE stocks tend to go a lot lower and high PE stocks tend to go a lot higher.

Nvidia has been one of his best investments for the last three years (2022, 2023, 2024). When he first bought it in October 2022, many people avoided it because the PE was so high. On TradingView (indicators > "price to earnings ratio") the PE ratio when he bought in October 2022 was about 39 times (39.9), about 40 times earnings. Some said it was too expensive. From the time he bought to today, after three years, the share price has gone up 1,133%, like a 12x. A very, very good investment.

On the other hand many people bought Ford because the PE looked very low; he didn't, because he knew it is not a very good company. In October 2022 Ford's PE was only 3.8. You would think it is so cheap. If you bought Ford then and held for three years, you would not have made any money, because the price basically went nowhere in three years. That is why he keeps saying PE ratio itself is totally useless and totally meaningless; "Don't look at it". The only thing to consider is the PEG ratio: compare the PE with the earnings growth rate.

Having said that, the PEG ratio is also not the best way to value a stock; it is only a rough guide. In this course you learn different valuation methods, and the most comprehensive, taught in a later lesson, is the discounted cash flow method, the one he primarily uses because it gives the most accurate intrinsic value of the stock.

#### [22:50] Demo on Stock Oracle: Microsoft
With a Stock Oracle account you can get all this information fast. Put in the ticker, MSFT. As of recording (he says 2026), Microsoft is selling at $397 per share. Scrolling down: market capitalization $2.9 trillion, or $2,958 billion. Under financial snapshot: net profit a year is $119 billion, and shares outstanding 7.46 billion. Net profit divided by shares outstanding gives EPS; share price divided by EPS gives the PE ratio over the last 12 months of earnings: 24.93 times. Share price divided by next year's projected earnings gives the forward PE: 20.95 times. The PEG: PE ratio divided by the projected growth rate of 19.62% gives a PEG of 1.27, everything on one page under the stock's overview.

#### [25:00] His 10 favorites on Stock Oracle
He also finds these under "favorites", which you can customize (three dots > edit favorites; there are 479 metrics you can add; he says 497 in a second mention). These are the 10 things he likes to look at for every stock, for Microsoft:
- PE ratio based on last 12 months of earnings (24.93).
- Forward PE (share price divided by next year's projected earnings): 20.95.
- Projected earnings growth for the next five years: 19.62%.
- Long-term growth rate beyond the next five years (Stock Oracle is the only website that shows this): projected 8.9% for Microsoft.
- Shares outstanding: 7.46 billion.
- PEG ratio: 1.27.
- ROIC (covered earlier): return on invested capital 26.71%.
- Return on equity (ROE), another profitability ratio he teaches later: 34.39%.

These are the 10 favorite things (he names eight values in the transcript). There are many other things, but these first 10 give him a quick overview of a stock.

### 10 - Stock Basics 2025 Part 4 (What Drives Stock Prices)

#### [00:00] Why prices are so volatile
If you are a beginner, you'd be surprised how volatile prices are when the market is open: they go up and down like crazy every single day. One day a stock could go up 5%, the next day down 5%, the next up 8%, then down 3%, up and down, up and down. What drives that? A lot of investors make the mistake of thinking the price of a stock always reflects what the business is worth; that is totally false. Take Microsoft: the price could drop 5% a day and the next day go up 5%. Does that mean that in two days Microsoft made 5% more or 5% less profit, or that 5% more or fewer people use Microsoft? No. It is the exact same business making the same profits, in fact probably making more profits every year; people are still using Microsoft. A business doesn't change that much in a few days, a few weeks or even a few months.

#### [01:02] The market is an auction: demand and supply
The stock market is an auction-driven market. If you have watched an auction, people get very emotional: "do I hear 25, go to 26, do I hear 28, sold to the man for $29", and people don't want to be outbid. Same thing in the stock market. What drives prices every day is demand and supply for a particular stock. On a day with more demand, when more people buy, the price goes up, though it is the same company. On another day, with more selling, more supply than demand, the price goes down, same company.

What drives demand and supply, what makes people buy one day and sell the next? Logic or emotions? As human beings, most of the time, not just in the stock market but in life, we make decisions driven by emotions and then justify them with logic. Two main emotions drive demand and supply: fear and greed. When people are fearful and scared ("oh my God"), they sell. When people are greedy and excited, they buy. Fear also causes people to buy: the fear of missing out as it keeps going up. So in the short term the stock market is driven by emotions of fear and greed.

Emotions can change in a heartbeat. What changes emotions? News. When people read news of a global lockdown, a recession, a depression, a war, they panic, they sell, and the market goes down. Then they see good news, the company announces great earnings, and they buy and the price goes up. That is what drives the price up and down every single day.

#### [03:20] Ignore the news and go opposite of everyone else
As investors, to make money we have to learn to ignore all this news and disassociate emotionally from the market. If you get emotional in the market, keep watching the news and get emotional like everyone else, you will lose money; "I guarantee you that". The way to make money is to go opposite of everyone else.

#### [03:44] Example: Microsoft in 2019-2020
He made a lot of money by staying rational and calm when everyone panicked, with one of his favorite stocks that he has owned for a long time, Microsoft (MSFT). In 2019 Microsoft was going up very nicely and the share price was $190.19. He had calculated the intrinsic value of Microsoft to be $230. How you calculate intrinsic value is covered in the later lesson on stock valuation; by the end of the course you will be a master at calculating intrinsic value, which is what the shares are actually worth. So at 190 the price was already undervalued; it was a great business and kept going up.

Then in 2020 came the unexpected COVID-19 pandemic and a global lockdown. Once it was announced, people panicked: "we're going to have a recession and depression". Everyone started selling and all the stocks came down, whether McDonald's or Microsoft, because of panic selling. Microsoft dropped from $190 to $133 in four weeks. Think logically: did the business change? It is the same business, same employees, selling the same products and services, making the same money. In fact, during the pandemic Microsoft made more money; revenues and profits went up. Whether you work from home or in the office, you still have to use Microsoft and pay the subscription. Nothing changed in the business; in fact it was worth more. Yet the price sold at 35% less. In the short term the market price doesn't always reflect the actual value of the business; prices are driven by emotions.

Not just that, but by manipulation: market makers and institutions manipulate the market to cause fear in retail investors, to make them panic and sell at the bottom so that the big institutions buy up the cheap shares. That is how they make a lot of money by scaring retail investors. "This game goes on all the time."

#### [06:23] Why would anyone sell at $133 if it's worth $190?
Reason one: because they are stupid (he says that is one reason). 90% of investors in the world have no idea about the business behind the stock. They don't know how to calculate intrinsic value, they don't understand revenue and profits, and they treat the market like a casino going up and down. When it drops they panic: "what if it never comes back?" They sell at 133, not knowing they are selling a piece of a great business worth 190.

Reason two: they are forced to sell, by the stockbroker. When the market is going up and people are making a lot of money, they get greedy and borrow money from the broker to buy more shares. That is buying on margin. As the price goes up they think they are really smart. To borrow from the broker they use their existing shares as collateral. When the market suddenly drops, the collateral reduces in value, and the broker says "you've got to pay up, put in more cash, or we won't lend you the money to keep your shares". Many of these investors don't have the cash, so the broker liquidates them: forces them to sell all their shares at a very low price. With all these brokers selling and selling, there is more supply and the price goes down even more. That is why in a market panic the price keeps dropping in free fall.

Is that good for us as rational investors? Yes, because it lets us take advantage of this huge mispricing. When something worth 190 is temporarily selling 30, 40% less because of panic selling and forced liquidations, we use that to our advantage by buying shares of these great companies. That is what he did and what his students did: bought when it was undervalued.

#### [09:17] You can't know the bottom
How do you know where the bottom is? No one knows; there is no way to predict the bottom. As long as we buy when the stock is undervalued, that is good enough. Whether you bought here or here or you were so lucky to buy right at the bottom, it doesn't matter, because wherever you bought, as long as it's undervalued and you hold on, eventually emotions change, people become optimistic again, the market price reflects what the stock is really worth, and you make a lot of money over time. Don't worry about having to buy the bottom. As long as it is undervalued we can add shares. We never buy all at once at one price; we always nibble, adding in small tranches (covered in later lessons). This is an overview of how we approach the market differently from the majority of gamblers.

#### [10:24] Benjamin Graham: the pendulum
He quotes Benjamin Graham, mentor to Warren Buffett and known as the father of value investing, author of the book The Intelligent Investor, which he highly recommends you read. Graham said: the market is a pendulum that forever swings between unsustainable optimism that makes stocks too expensive at times and unjustified pessimism that makes them too cheap at times. The intelligent investor is a realist who sells to optimists when stocks are too expensive and buys from pessimists when stocks are too cheap. "That's how the game is played."

Drawing it out: a great business makes more and more money over time. Same for the S&P 500 (to be learned later), the 500 biggest companies, which over time make more and more profits. So the intrinsic value of the S&P 500 or of a great company rises over time. But the share price doesn't go up in a straight line, because it is driven by emotion; it goes down and up and down and up like a pendulum, too expensive at times because of optimism and too cheap at times because of pessimism. Our job is to be a buyer when there is pessimism and things get cheap, and if you want to sell, to sell up when people are really optimistic and it's getting expensive. But you will learn later in the course that we don't always want to sell a great business just because it is temporarily expensive.

#### [12:29] Peter Lynch
Peter Lynch is one of the very few fund managers who did very well, beating the market by a wide margin during his career, and he has learned from him extensively. Lynch said that often there is no correlation between the success of a company's operations and the success of its stock price over a few months or even a year or so. A company could be doing well, with growing revenue and profits, but the share price could be dropping because of short-term panic. Conversely, you may have a company losing money, a lousy business, whose price goes up, because in the short term price is driven by emotions and manipulation.

But in the long term, and by long term he means at least two years or more (two, three, four, five years or more), there is a hundred percent correlation between the success of a company and the success of its stock price. This disparity is the key to making money. It pays to be patient and to own successful companies.

#### [13:47] What drives prices in the long run: five things
Looking at the S&P 500 chart, the 500 biggest companies in the US: in the short term it goes up and down, but over 3, 5, 10, 20, 30 years the overall US market always goes up. It has been going up for over 120 years. Over time, stock prices are driven by five things:

1. Earnings. Companies make more and more profits; the 500 biggest US companies as a whole make more profits over time. "Write this down": corporate earnings over the long run grow an average of 8% a year. As companies generate more earnings per share, each share is worth more and the share price adjusts higher. Besides earnings per share, more important is free cash flow per share (more in the lesson on fundamental analysis), because ultimately it's not just the reported accounting profits but how much cash the company can generate from its business. To him free cash flow is a better measure than earnings because cash flow is more tangible.
2. Share buybacks. As great companies buy back their shares there are fewer and fewer shares, so you share with fewer people and each share is ultimately worth more.
3. Dividends. The more dividends a company (or the S&P 500) pays, the more it is worth, because people want dividends and are willing to pay more for them.
4. Future earnings growth and potential. Many times the share price goes up even more than what the company has earned, because people are willing to pay more in anticipation of future earnings, not just current earnings. Some companies are not yet making money, even making losses initially, but the share price still goes up because people anticipate they will make money in the future, so the price already reflects future growth of earnings or cash flow.
5. Money supply. In the US the money supply is in a way controlled by the Federal Reserve. When the Fed reduces interest rates and buys bonds, that creates more money supply; more money in the system inflates asset prices and drives stock prices higher. Conversely, when the Fed raises interest rates, money supply falls. For example, in 2022 and 2023 the Fed reduced the money supply by 40% because it raised interest rates and stopped buying bonds. But the funny thing is that in 2023 the stock market went up even though the money supply dropped. Why? Because even if money supply goes down, if earnings are growing the market will still go up. Of course if all five are happening (money supply up, earnings up, dividends up) the market goes up even stronger.

#### [18:24] Price vs earnings per share chart
On the chart, the red line is the price of the S&P 500 (the 500 biggest companies in the US market); it goes up and down, but over time up. What drives it higher is earnings per share; the blue line is forward earnings, the projected earnings of the companies, and the two always move together over the long run.

#### [18:47] Summary: fundamentals vs technicals
Share prices are volatile, driven by demand and supply. Over the longer term, meaning at least two years or more, what drives demand for a share is the intrinsic value of the share. In lesson five you learn how to calculate the intrinsic value of McDonald's, Meta, Microsoft, Apple and so forth. Intrinsic value, what the business is worth, is determined by how much revenue it generates, how much profit, its cash flow, its assets and liabilities. The study of all these things to analyze the business is fundamental analysis, covered in this course: how to read a business inside out and how to value it.

In the short term (the next one day, one week, one month, three months), what drives the price up and down is not so much the value of the business but the emotions of fear and greed. What drives fear and greed? News, which drives demand and supply in the short term, and market sentiment. Can we study the emotions driving price in the short term? Yes: the study of the emotional patterns that drive prices in the short term is technical analysis. This course covers both.

Which is more important? It depends. For short-term speculative trading, technical analysis or short-term price action is more important than fundamentals. But for investing, which is the focus of this course, 90% (write this down) of the decisions we make, whether to buy or sell, should be based on the fundamentals of the underlying business, and only 10%, our decisions about timing optimal entries and exits, is based on technical analysis.

To maximize investing profits we want to know two things: which are the great companies we can invest in, and what the stock is really worth, the intrinsic value. We spend a lot of time learning to value shares to arrive at the intrinsic value (fundamental analysis). At the same time we learn to identify the short-term trend of price movement (technical analysis), which has certain patterns, taught in the technical analysis lesson.

#### [22:26] Trend patterns
Price goes into patterns: an uptrend (waves up with pullbacks down), a downtrend (the reverse), and a consolidation, where the price goes sideways, up and down. [The transcript's description of the drawn waves is repetitive and unclear.] By understanding which trend we're in, whether uptrend, downtrend or consolidation, we can use some techniques to better time our entries, whether to get in or get out. But ultimately this is the cherry on the cake; the main cake is the fundamentals of the business.

#### [23:22] Example: McDonald's
McDonald's is a great company, around for many years, and almost everyone he knows, including himself, goes to McDonald's once in a while: not the healthiest thing, but the most convenient. Over time it keeps making more and more money: revenue, profits and cash increase all around the world, so the intrinsic value of the shares increases over time. The black dotted line on the chart represents the intrinsic value of the business. Does the share price move exactly with the value of the business every day, week, month? No. In the short term the share price goes up and down like a yo-yo because of volatility, emotions and manipulation. In the long term the share price will always go up and roughly reflect the intrinsic value of the business, but in the short term it moves in trends.

He draws the chart: first the price goes sideways (consolidation), then an uptrend, then a downtrend, then another big uptrend, a short downtrend, uptrends, a wedge pattern and an uptrend [the transcript is garbled and repetitive]. These are price action patterns that technical analysis identifies. Zooming into the short term, there is a downtrend; what are the emotions during a downtrend? Fear: "oh my God, we're going to die, no one's going to eat burgers anymore, I hate Ronald McDonald". Then people get excited again, "we love McDonald's", and it goes into an uptrend; then fear again, downtrend, then up again. The market is like a bipolar, manic-depressive person: happy and sad, happy and sad, and these emotions create these patterns.

Finally, the majority of the time the price movements, whether down or up, are caused by reasons that have nothing to do with the company's long-term business potential, its long-term fundamentals and intrinsic value.

# Part 2 — Lessons 11–14

### 11 - Stock Basics 2025 Part 5 (How to Make Money in Stocks)

#### [00:00] Prices move in waves, driven by emotion
Looking at the chart of the S&P 500, which represents the US stock market, price goes up and down but always in wave patterns: wave up, wave down, wave down, wave up, and so on, always in these wave patterns. What causes the waves? Emotions: excitement, fear, excitement, fear. Human emotions keep fluctuating.

#### [00:26] Don't ask "why did the market go up or down?"
Many new investors keep asking why the market went down or why it went up. People ask "Adam, why did the market go down?" and when it goes up, "why did the market go up?" He calls this the most stupid question to ask and says please don't ask it. Whenever the market goes up or down, analysts will always find a reason to explain and justify it. If the market goes up they say it went up because of GDP; if it goes down they say it went down because of the war in Gaza. There are all kinds of reasons in the world that get attached to the price movement. [A portion of the transcript here is garbled and the exact words are unclear.] His point: don't waste your time asking why the market went up or down or trying to justify market movement. Prices just move up and down; that's all you have to know. As investors, we just take advantage when they wave down. Whenever the market waves down and the companies we like get undervalued, we just buy. That's it; keep it very simple. When the waves are up and prices are expensive, we don't buy, and if for some reason we want to sell certain companies, those are the times to sell. Asking why the market moved a certain way is totally irrelevant; people find reasons to justify recent price movements, which is totally a waste of time and not useful for making investment decisions.

#### [02:20] Procter & Gamble: intrinsic value vs. price
Take Procter & Gamble (P&G). If you go to your bathroom, a lot of the shampoos and soaps probably come from P&G. It is a great company that has been around for many, many years. Over time its sales keep going up, its profits keep going up, and the company is worth more and more, so the intrinsic value (the green line) always goes up over time. The share price does not go up every week, every month, every year; it is driven by emotions, manipulations, wave up, wave down, all kinds of reasons, and we take advantage of it. Price goes down, goes up, like a pendulum: above the green line, below the green line, above, below, and it keeps repeating. When the share price is above the green line (intrinsic value) the stock is overpriced; when it is below the intrinsic value, it is underpriced. Our job is very simple: add shares of great companies like P&G when they are below the green line, when they are undervalued. As simple as that. Why the price went down or up is totally irrelevant.

#### [04:05] You don't need to buy at the bottom; nibble and average in
Do we have to buy right at the bottom to make money? No. He would love to, but no one can predict where the bottom of a wave down is. As long as the price goes below intrinsic value and we add shares, that's good enough. Personally, once the share price goes below intrinsic value by a certain amount, say 10% undervalued, he will nibble a little bit. If it goes even lower by another 5 to 10%, he nibbles a little more; if it drops another 5% to 10%, a bit more. He slowly buys in to average his position, so his average cost ends up somewhere in the middle, not necessarily at the bottom. Over time the intrinsic value goes up, and when the share price eventually goes back above intrinsic value he will be very much in the money, in the profit zone. Price is not value. Price is what you pay; value is what you get. The value is the green line; the price is the fluctuating line over it.

#### [05:24] Loving bad news on great companies: the Facebook/Meta example
Once he identifies a great company, he loves when it gets hit by bad news, because people panic, sell, the price goes down, and then he buys. A stock he has held for many, many years is Facebook, renamed Meta Platforms. Today Meta is close to $600, but a couple of years back it was selling between $120 per share and $360 per share. During that time every year they were making more profits and the intrinsic value (green line) kept going up. (Students will learn to calculate intrinsic value so they can see for themselves that the shares are worth more over time.) During that period there was always some news that scared the market and created emotions:

- April 2018: the Facebook data scandal, where Facebook was accused of selling data to other providers; panic, and the share price went below intrinsic value. A great time to buy.
- Facebook fined for the Cambridge Analytica scandal: people panic, "they're fined, we're going to die." Also a great time to buy.
- Facebook shares extend slide as Starbucks [and other] global brands [filled from earlier pass: headline reads "Starbucks joins major global brands"] join a social media ads boycott. Lawsuits and boycotts are opportunities to buy from the panic. The price just went down; again a great time to buy. This specific bad news could be some macro event that we take advantage of.
- A headline that tech stocks closed sharply lower as bond yields jumped (long-term interest rates rose, causing broad panic and a price drop), again a buying opportunity. [from earlier pass]
- A whistleblower's FTC complaint that Facebook's platform was used to promote human trafficking and domestic servitude [as transcribed: "domestic service"]. He says he is not condoning that or saying it's good; the point is that no matter what company you're invested in, whether McDonald's or Visa or MasterCard, bad news comes and we can take advantage of it.
- Facebook's outage frustrates advertisers heading into the holiday season.

All of this causes people to panic and sell when it drops; it drops, they sell, it drops, they sell. That's why most people buy high, sell low, buy high, sell low, and end up losing all their money. If instead you think rationally, look at the fundamentals of the business (are they making more money, generating more profits, increasing the value), add shares when it's below valuation, and hold patiently, you make a lot of money. Facebook, now Meta Platforms, is $600 per share, and if you had added at any time in that period you would have made a lot of money on this one stock. This is as of the time of recording; by the time you watch, maybe Meta is at 650, 700, 800, $1,000 per share, "I don't know." That is why we want to hold on to great companies.

#### [09:26] Two ways to make money from stocks
There are basically two ways: number one, capital appreciation; number two, collecting cash dividends.

#### [09:26] Way 1: Capital appreciation
Capital appreciation is when the share price increases over time. Don't expect that after you buy, the price will go up the next day, next week or even next month, because in the short term price movement is random: it can go up or down, we can't control it, and we can't predict people's emotions in the short term (nor the news). But over time, as the company makes more and more money, the share price will go up. He keeps repeating this because it is so important. You have to be patient. The profit you get when you eventually sell at a higher price than you bought is how you make money.

Example with Apple: Apple was initially at $320, somewhere around there. Say you're bullish on Apple (you think it will increase in value and price) because it's a great company, people use its products and services, and it's undervalued. You buy at $320 per share. After you buy it goes up and down, up and down, but over time as Apple sells more MacBooks, iPhones, iPads and so on, it makes more money. In the future, for some reason, you may say you need the money and want to sell. You sell on a wave up at 520 (he says "502" when computing) to close your position. You bought at 320 and sold at 520 [as stated; he says 502]; he states you make $182 profit per share. That is capital appreciation.

With investing you don't want to sell too early; you want to give your business time to compound into huge amounts of money. His best investments are not ones he sold when they went up 20%, 30% or 50%. His best investments were those he held on to, which went up 5 times what he paid (5x), or 10x, or 20x, or even 50x. That's how you get really rich.

#### [12:07] Taxes on capital gains
It depends on which country you're from. He is from Singapore and invests in US markets, and the great thing about not being a US citizen is that he pays zero capital gains tax on US investing. Same if he invests in the Singapore markets: zero capital gains tax. He knows it sounds a bit unfair, because Americans who buy US shares have to pay capital gains tax based on their income tax bracket, but as a non-American he pays zero. That is for him personally; you have to check with your tax lawyer.

#### [12:34] Way 2: Cash dividends
You can also make money by holding shares of great businesses and collecting cash dividends. Some companies pay out a percentage of their profits every year as dividends to shareholders, usually once every three months (every quarter), or some companies twice a year, depending on the company. Do all companies pay dividends? No. Usually the companies that pay dividends are very mature, large companies that have been around many, many years and have excess cash: they generate so much profit that even after reinvesting what they need, they have extra cash and don't know what to do with it, so they return it to you as shareholders. US companies that pay a lot of dividends include Pepsi (PepsiCo) and Johnson & Johnson.

Personally, if he buys stocks for dividends, he prefers Singapore-listed banks: on the Singapore Stock Exchange, DBS Bank, OCBC and UOB pay a lot of dividends, which he loves. Some companies don't pay dividends, such as Nvidia and Palantir (which he owns), because they are high growth companies. Growth companies reinvest all their profits in the business or buy back shares instead of paying dividends. For these you don't get any cash, but the share price goes up significantly and you make your money from capital appreciation.

When you buy stocks, ask yourself what you want: more capital appreciation or more dividends. They differ, because companies that pay a lot of dividends (Pepsi, Johnson & Johnson, OCBC Bank) see share prices go up very slowly. If you want cash regularly, you buy those dividend-type stocks. He personally has several portfolios: some focused more on capital gain, holding companies whose share price goes up a lot but who pay less dividends; and a different portfolio where he buys stocks like UOB, OCBC and DBS, where the share price goes up slower. (More on this in the lesson on how to build winning portfolios.)

#### [15:59] Dividend yield
If you're buying a stock for dividends, look at the dividend yield: the dividend paid to you divided by the current share price. Example: a stock selling at $20 per share that pays $2 of dividends a year (based on the last 12 months). You buy at $20 and get $2 a year, a dividend yield of 10%. Is that attractive? Pretty good. You would compare it to the interest you get from the bank or from buying a bond. Generally, in the US markets he'd say a dividend yield of 3% is considered attractive; in the Singapore markets, a dividend yield of 5% to 6% is considered attractive. It depends on which market you look at.

Personally, for dividends he prefers the Singapore market because dividend yields are much higher, 5% to 6%, and they are tax-free (zero tax on dividends in Singapore). The US market is more for capital gains, because US dividend yields are lower, usually 2 to 3%, and there is a tax on the dividends.

#### [17:46] Two examples on Yahoo! Finance
- PepsiCo (symbol PEP, US market): as of the last closing price, $152.89 per share. Each share pays a dividend of $5.42 (the forward dividend, i.e. what is expected to be paid in the next 12 months). $5.42 divided by the current share price gives a dividend yield of 3.55%. For US stocks a yield of about 3% is considered quite attractive, but remember you have to pay tax on the dividends.
- UOB Bank (a big three bank in Singapore): share price $36.2 [earlier pass has $36.02]. For Singapore stocks, like most Asian stocks, you have to buy a minimum of a hundred shares and multiples of a hundred. It pays a dividend of $1.76; $1.76 divided by $36 gives a dividend yield of 4.89%. Singapore stocks generally have higher dividend yields than US, and Singapore-listed dividends are tax-free.

#### [19:30] Dividend information in Stock Oracle
He shows DBS Bank in Stock Oracle (the DBS shares listed on the US OTC markets). Very soon, by the end of 2026, Stock Oracle will also show Singapore-listed as well as China and Hong Kong-listed shares; for now you can find Singapore or China shares listed on the US markets, like DBS here. Click on dividends for all the dividend information:

- Current dividend yield of DBS: 3.86%.
- Dividend payout ratio (to be learned more about later): 63.86%. This means that out of 100% of the bank's profits, about 63.86% is paid out in dividends [he says "3% in dividends" in the transcript; the figure shown is 63.86%], and the remainder is kept as retained earnings.
- Dividend growth rate: the great thing about companies like DBS is that they increase dividends consistently. In the last five years, dividends grew 26% per year on average. Scrolling down: in the last 10 years, dividends have grown at 16% a year; last five years, 26% a year; last three years, 23% a year, which is amazing. Because it is listed in Singapore, the dividends are tax-free.
- Dividend yield history: over 10 years the yield has gone up, down, up and down, and obviously you want to buy when the yield is high. Usually when the share price goes down the yield goes up. Recently, as he records, DBS shares have gone up a lot and the yield has dropped from about 5.8% to less than 4%. In terms of attractiveness, it is not the most attractive time to buy DBS because it's a bit overvalued right now. He prefers the share price to drop; he doesn't know when. When the share price drops and the yield gets back above 4%, it becomes attractive again; if the yield goes near 5% or above 5%, it would be very attractive.
- Dividend per share chart (in purple): over time the dividend per share has increased, except for COVID when they were forced to cut dividends because of MAS, and then increased them again. What we want to see is a company increasing its dividends consistently over time.
- In the last financial year (2025): earnings per share of $2.95 (profit per share); out of this they paid $1.88 in dividends [transcribed as "$188"], and they also generated $3.37 in free cash flow [earlier pass has roughly $3.7x; raw transcript says $3.37].
- Scrolling down shows all the dates on which they paid dividends. If you own the shares before a particular date called the ex-date ("X date"), you are paid the dividend on the pay date. They pay quarterly, four times a year. Everything can be found in this section of Stock Oracle.

### 12 - Stock Basics 2025 Part 6 (Stock Index Performance)

#### [00:00] Stock exchanges
He focuses primarily on the US market because it is the world's biggest market, with the best companies in the world. The US market comprises about 24 stock exchanges, of which the three biggest are the New York Stock Exchange, the American Stock Exchange and the NASDAQ Stock Exchange. Coca-Cola is listed on the New York Stock Exchange, whereas Apple is listed on NASDAQ. Do you need to know this? No, it's not important: whatever broker you use, if you key in that you want to buy Apple shares, they route your order to the relevant exchange. It's just general knowledge.

Other exchanges around the world:
- China: three main exchanges: the Shanghai Stock Exchange and Shenzhen Stock Exchange on the mainland, and the Hong Kong Stock Exchange in Hong Kong.
- UK: the London Stock Exchange.
- Europe: several exchanges, one of the biggest being Euronext (based in Amsterdam) and the Frankfurt Stock Exchange.
- Asia: the Singapore Exchange, the Tokyo Stock Exchange, the Bombay Stock Exchange, the Jakarta Stock Exchange, and so on.

#### [01:23] Stock indexes
Really important. Every stock market has at least one index that measures its performance and tells you how well the market is doing. We always want to look at the index to see how well the market does over time, and our job as investors is to beat the index, i.e. get a better return than the overall market. The index is a basket of the biggest companies used to represent all the companies in the market.

Example, the US market: there are over 10,000 stocks in total. Of these, they select the 500 biggest companies to represent all 10,000, like members of parliament or Congress chosen to represent all the people. If you take these 500 biggest companies, add the price together and average it, you get the S&P 500 index, which most investors look at to represent the whole US market.

Other indexes:
- Dow Jones Industrial Average: instead of the 500 biggest, they select the 30 biggest companies out of the 10,000; add their prices together and you get the Dow Jones Industrial Average. Are these 30 within the 500? Yes.
- NASDAQ 100: out of the 10,000 stocks, the 100 biggest companies listed on the NASDAQ exchange, many of them technology companies.
- These three are the main indexes representing the US market. They consist only of large companies, where the market cap is at least $10 billion.
- Russell 2000: for the mid-size or small companies. They take 2,000 small or mid-sized companies from the 10,000, put them in a basket and add together to get the Russell 2000 index.

When you see "the US market goes up 20% this year", it doesn't mean all 10,000 stocks went up 20%; it usually means the S&P 500, the 500 biggest companies that represent all the stocks.

Singapore example: a very small market, only 600 stocks in the whole market. Out of the 600 they select the 30 biggest companies to represent all, add the price together, and get the Straits Times Index (STI). These 30 represent the entire Singapore stock market.

Which indexes contain which stock: Apple is in the S&P 500, the Dow Jones and the NASDAQ 100; not in the Russell 2000, because Apple is a big company. Coca-Cola is in the S&P 500 (one of the 500) and the Dow Jones, but not in the NASDAQ 100. Some stocks appear in a few indexes; some appear in only one.

#### [06:08] Major indexes around the world
- US: the Dow Jones (30 biggest companies representing the whole US market), the S&P 500 (500 biggest), the NASDAQ 100, the Russell 2000.
- Singapore: Straits Times Index, the weighted average of the 30 biggest companies.
- London: the FTSE 100 ("Footsie 100"), the 100 biggest companies listed on the London Stock Exchange, representing the whole UK market.
- Europe: several indexes. The main one is the Euro Stoxx 50 (spoken "Euro stocks 50"), the 50 biggest companies from 11 European countries. The DAX, the weighted average of the 40 biggest companies on the Frankfurt Stock Exchange. The CAC, the 40 stocks on the Paris Stock Exchange. Those are the three main European indices.
- China: the Shanghai Composite Index, the Shenzhen Composite Index, the Hang Seng Index and the CSI 300. Usually the CSI 300 represents mainland China stocks (the 300 biggest Chinese companies on the mainland). The Hang Seng Index represents the Hong Kong stock market.
- Japan: the Nikkei 225, the 225 biggest Japanese companies on the Tokyo Stock Exchange.
- Australia: the ASX 200, representing the Australian stock market.
- Malaysia: the KLCI, Kuala Lumpur Composite Index, made up of the 30 largest companies on Bursa Malaysia.

But we are going to focus primarily on the US market.

#### [08:42] The Dow Jones: constituents change, and why it is the "safest" investment
The Dow Jones is made up of the 30 biggest companies representing the US market (the list of the current 30 was shown on screen). Are they always the same? No, they change all the time. When a company is not doing well, is losing its status, not making as much money, not representing the industry as much, it gets kicked out of the index and is replaced by a better company, so the index is always changing. In the past, companies like Citi (as transcribed, "Citig"), AIG and General Electric were in the Dow but were kicked out and replaced over time by better companies such as Nike, Salesforce and Amazon. He wouldn't say "the best", but it's always the most representative companies in the market that get included.

Currently in the Dow: Amazon, American Express, Apple, Boeing, Cisco, Nvidia, Visa, Disney, Walmart, 3M, Nike and so forth. Intel had been in the Dow for many years; it just got kicked out and was replaced by Nvidia a few months ago. Notice that these 30 companies don't just sell to the US consumer; they sell to the entire world. Whether you're from Singapore, UK, Japan, Europe, wherever, he bets that once in a while you drink a Coca-Cola, use a product from Johnson & Johnson, Nike or Procter & Gamble. These are global companies, so the Dow Jones in a way represents the global economy.

When people ask him what is the safest thing to invest in, that he knows over time is bound to go up, his response is the Dow Jones, because it represents the global economy. Everyone in the world eats at McDonald's once in a while; everyone uses Apple products. The S&P 500 index is more followed by retail investors because it includes more companies than 30, up to 500 of the largest companies in the US market.

#### [11:51] The S&P 500 on Finviz: sectors and weighting
On Finviz (visited earlier), click Maps to see the components that make up the S&P 500. These are the 500 companies, and you can view performance over one day, one week, one month, one year, and year to date. He records this on December 24; green means price increased year to date, red means decreased. The 500 companies are grouped into sectors:
- Technology sector: companies like Microsoft, Apple, Oracle, Nvidia, Broadcom, Salesforce and ServiceNow.
- Consumer cyclical sector (also known as consumer discretionary): Amazon, Tesla and Home Depot. (More on sectors in the next lesson.)
- Financials: Visa, JP Morgan, American Express, Mastercard.
- Healthcare: Eli Lilly, Johnson & Johnson, Merck, UnitedHealth.
- Consumer defensive: Pepsi, Walmart and so on.

The 500 companies do not all have the same weight in the index. Nvidia has a much bigger weightage than, say, Texas Instruments, because Nvidia has a bigger market cap whereas Texas Instruments has a smaller one. The bigger the market cap, the more weightage in the index. If Nvidia goes up 1%, it has a bigger effect on the index than Texas Instruments going up 1%. Recall: market capitalization is the share price multiplied by the total shares outstanding, giving the market cap of the whole company.

#### [14:00] The index as benchmark; S&P 500 returns over 20, 10 and 5 years
The index shows how the stock market has performed over a period and serves as a benchmark for investors to beat, to get higher returns than the index. One reason he and his students are successful is not just that they make money but that over the long term they get much higher returns than the S&P 500, their benchmark.

- Last 20 years (January 2005 to end of 2024): the S&P 500 started at about 80 points and after 20 years is at 595 points: a total gain of 613%. This includes dividends reinvested into the index. With a financial calculator, that's equivalent to a compounded annual return of 10.3%. If you invested in the S&P 500 20 years ago and rode through the ups and downs, your money would have grown at about 10% compounded, accumulating to 613% in total. The market didn't go in a straight line: many ups and downs, many corrections and bear markets (people call them crashes). One of the biggest was 2008 to 2009, the great financial crisis; another big crash in 2020, the COVID pandemic; a trade war crash in 2018; and the 2022 bear market caused by high inflation and high interest rates. In the short term the market will have crashes and go up and down, which causes panic. But with a long-term perspective there is no need to panic, because over time companies make more money and great companies are put into the index while lousy ones are kicked out, so your wealth grows if you are invested in the market, especially the US market.
- Last 10 years (January 2015 to end of 2024): started at about 170 points and ended at 595 points: a total gain of 246% over 10 years, equivalent to 13% compounded growth a year, through all the crashes along the way.
- Last five years: the index started at 300 points, promptly crashed because of COVID, went up, crashed again, up, down, up and up, down again. The US market gained 100% over the last five years, a compounded annual growth of 14.9%.

#### [17:54] Comparing world markets on TradingView
Use the charting software from TradingView (tradingview.com); you can download it and use the free version, no need for the paid one. For tutorials on using the TradingView charts, setting up charts and putting in lines and studies, see the charting and brokerage platform section of the course (the investment course videos).

He starts with the S&P 500 (symbol SPX), first over five years, then drags back to 2015 for a 10-year period, then compares it to other indexes using the plus sign:
- Hang Seng Index (Hong Kong/China): in the last 10 years, down 16% versus the S&P 500.
- India, Nifty (the 50 biggest stocks on the Indian market; India also has the Sensex): up 179%, "very close to the US market".
- Singapore Straits Times Index: up 14% over 10 years, a lot lower than the US and India.
- UK, FTSE 100: 24% return over 10 years.
- Europe, Euro Stoxx 50: up 60% in the last 10 years, better than the UK.
- Australia, ASX: 53%, slightly below Europe.

So over the last 10 years the best-performing market is the US at 191% growth; second, India at 179%; third, Europe at 60%; followed by Australia, then the UK, then Singapore, and in last place China ("very sad").

#### [22:03] Currency adjustment
These indexes are in different currencies (the US in US dollars, India in rupees). The 179% in India is in Indian rupees, but the rupee has been depreciating a lot against other currencies, so we need to factor in the currency loss or gain. When you see the word "mixed", it means they are in different currencies; you can adjust everything to one currency, such as the US dollar or Singapore dollar. Adjusted to US dollars, the S&P 500 far outgains India because of the big currency loss of holding the Indian rupee, and the US market still outperforms all other markets. He forgot Japan: adding the Nikkei 225, it performed better than the Nifty in India over the last 10 years, so the second place is actually Japan. For the last five years (currency adjusted), the best performer is the US, followed by Japan, then India, then Europe, Singapore, Australia, the UK and China. If your objective is the highest capital gains, it makes sense to focus on the US market because it gives the best returns compared to all other markets.

#### [24:14] Quiz for Lesson One (nine questions)
That concludes lesson one on stock market basics; it's just the beginning, with powerful strategies in later lessons. Attempt the quiz of nine questions (in the downloadable PDF notes, or pause the video as he flashes them: three, three, and three), then he runs through the answers.

1. Which statements are false? (A) In the US markets I can buy less than one share (fractional shares): true, with certain brokers like Interactive Brokers, the one he uses. (B) In the Hong Kong market I have to buy a minimum of 100 shares: tricky; the answer is false, because in Hong Kong it depends on the stock: some stocks have a 100-share minimum, some 500; it depends on the counter. (C) In the Singapore and Malaysian markets I have to buy a minimum of 100 shares: true. (D) In the US markets there's no maximum number of shares I need to buy: true. So the false one is B (transcribed as "V").
2. A company earned 350 million net income in the last 12 months and has 100 million shares outstanding. EPS = total net income divided by shares outstanding = 350 million / 100 million = $3.50. Answer B.
