3. Share price currently $50; what is the P/E ratio? Share price divided by the last-12-months EPS: $50 / $3.50 = 14.3 times. Answer B.
4. Share price $50 and 100 million shares outstanding; market capitalization (how much you'd pay to buy all the shares)? $50 x 100 million = 5,000 million = 5 billion. Answer A.
5. What is the PEG ratio of the stock: P/E multiplied by earnings growth rate, or P/E divided by earnings growth rate? Answer B (divided). A PEG ratio of 1.5 or less is considered reasonable price; below one would be cheap.
6. Share price $50, paid $5 in dividends in the last 12 months; dividend yield = $5 / $50 = 10%. Answer A (transcribed "8").
7. If a stock you own generates earnings per share of $5, how does the company return this capital to you (you're entitled to the $5)? First, by paying dividends, which are taxable in the US markets but not in other markets like Singapore. Second, buy back shares: indirectly returning capital to you, because when they buy back shares you share the business with fewer people, so you own a bigger percentage of the business. Third, retain the earnings, like a savings account. Fourth, reinvest the capital back in the business.
8. Company A has a P/E of 10 and projected earnings growth of 5%; company B has a P/E of 40 and projected earnings growth of 100%. Which is cheaper? PEG = P/E divided by growth rate. Company A: 10 / 5 = 2. Company B: 40 / 100 = 0.4. Company B has the lower PEG, so it is the cheaper stock. Answer B (transcribed "V").
9. True or false: (A) Great investors can predict where the share price goes in the short term: false; no one can predict it, we can only guess with a probability; no one knows for certain because there are too many moving parts. (B) In the short term, share prices are driven by emotions, manipulation and forced selling: true. (C) In the long run, share prices are driven by the fundamental value of the business: true. (D) Short-term trading or speculation has much higher win rates than long-term investing: absolutely false. In his trading experience, the best win rate in trading would be 50 to 60%, whereas in long-term investing, when you buy great companies, 90% or more of the time the companies will make money over time. Investing has a much higher win rate than trading.

He congratulates students on completing lesson one and sends them to lesson two.

### 14 - Shocking Market Facts

#### [00:00] Welcome to Lesson Two: investing in ETFs
Lesson two is on investing in exchange traded funds (ETFs). It teaches the simplest way to get started in the stock market: even before picking individual stocks, you can simply buy ETFs that track the market index. Recall the indexes from lesson one (in the US: the Dow Jones, the S&P 500, the NASDAQ 100); you can simply buy an ETF that tracks the performance of an index. The lesson covers: why indexes go up in the long run and how often they go down; what the stock market cycle is; what kinds of ETFs you can buy to track those indexes; how to invest in an ETF; when is the best time to buy and when is the best time to sell.

#### [00:56] Why invest in the index (specifically the US index)? Fact 1: the US stock index always goes up over the long run
The chart shows the Dow Jones Industrial Average over the last 121 years, from January 1904 to December 2024. He uses the Dow instead of the S&P 500 because the S&P 500 doesn't go back 100 years, only, "if I'm not wrong, like 60 years"; the Dow is the oldest index with the longest history.

Over 121 years there were: World War I, World War II, the Great Depression, the Vietnam War, the assassination of US President JFK, the Cuban Missile Crisis, the Black Monday stock crash, the Gulf War, the 21-day US government shutdown, the Asian financial crisis, the dot-com crash, the great financial crisis in 2008-09, the European sovereign debt crisis, the US credit rating being downgraded because of excess debt (transcribed "access and debt"), the US-China trade war of 2018, the COVID-19 pandemic in 2020, and the Fed fighting high inflation in 2022. There is always something to worry about; there's always a crisis in the world. But despite all these crises and worries, the market keeps going up. In 121 years the US market gained 11,188,000% (as transcribed), and the compounded annual growth rate over this period is 10.04%. You invest in the Dow; it goes up and down, up and down, but your money is compounding at 10% a year.

Why does the index always go up in the long run? Two reasons.
- Number one: what ultimately drives stock prices is earnings, the profits of companies. Take a company like Microsoft or McDonald's or Coca-Cola that has been in the market for 100 years. Is McDonald's making more money today than 10, 20, 30 years ago? Of course. Each burger is priced higher today; a Big Mac costs more than 20 years ago. They keep raising prices, and they sell more burgers today to more people in the world than 20 or 30 years ago. As prices increase and volume increases, the companies make more and more money, so the shares are worth more and the share price keeps going up.
- Number two: the index consists of a group of companies (the Dow, the 30 biggest; the S&P 500, the 500 biggest), and they are not always the same; they change all the time. As in lesson one, whenever a company is no longer profitable or doing well, it gets kicked out of the index and replaced by a better company. For example, AIG, Citi ("Citig") and General Electric were in the Dow but were kicked out and replaced by better companies like Apple, Salesforce and Goldman Sachs. Because of that, the index is engineered to always go up. "Isn't that a great thing as an investor to know that it is designed to always go up?"

#### [04:44] Fact 2: the US market doesn't go up every year, but it goes up most years
Looking at the last 74 years, from 1951 to the end of 2024: some years the market goes up (1951 up 23%, 1952 up 18%) and some down (1953 dropped 1%), and so on. Counting the down years: 16 out of 74 years the market went down, which is 21.6% of the time; 58 years out of 74 it went up, which is 78.4% of the time. If you buy US stocks in January and go to December for a year, your probability of making money in any given year is 78%. Is that good? Not bad; it's better than the casino. In a casino, no matter what game you play, the odds are less than 50-50. When you invest in the US market your odds of making money are over 70%, in this case 78% over the last 74 years.

Holding longer than a year raises the probability further:
- Five-year periods: in the last 74 years there were altogether 70 five-year periods (1951 to 1955, 1952 to 1956, 1953 to 1957, every single possible combination). Out of the 70, 6 ended with a loss after holding five years and the rest ended with a gain. So holding the US index five years, the probability of ending with a gain is 64 out of 70 periods, 91.4%; the chance of a loss after five years is 6 out of 70, which is 8.6%.
- Ten-year periods: altogether 65 ten-year periods (1951 to 1960, 1952 to 1961, 1953 to 1962, every single combination). The only time you ended with a loss after 10 years was during two 10-year periods around the dot-com crash. The probability of ending with a gain after holding 10 years is 96.9%; the odds of losing money after 10 years are just 3.1%.

How many years must you hold so that it's a 100% guarantee you end up with a gain? Looking at history, the answer is about 15 years. Even if you bought at the top of the dot-com bubble or the great financial crisis, if you hold long enough you always end up with a gain. But in this lesson he will teach you not to buy blindly at the top when things are so expensive; you'll learn how to buy when the market is undervalued or near fair value. That way, "I wouldn't say 100%, but almost" every year you're going to see gains in your portfolio if you follow these simple rules.

#### [08:37] Distribution of annual returns (last 74 years)
Over the long term the US market goes up on average about 10% a year, but does it go up exactly 10%? No. The market tends to go up between 10 to 20% most of the time. Over the last 74 years:
- 23% of the time the market goes up 10 to 20% in a calendar year.
- 20% to 30%: 21.6% of the time.
- 30 to 40%: less frequently, but 13.5% of the time.
- 40% to 50% in one year: only once, in 1958, so only 1.4% of the time.
- A 50% gain in a year: only in 1954.

How often does the market go down?
- Zero to minus 10%: 12.2% of the time.
- 10 to 20% down: 5.4% of the time (as transcribed).
- 20 to 30% down: 2.7% of the time.
- 30 to 40% down by the end of the year: once, in 2008 (great financial crisis), only 1.4% of the time.

Financial media like to scare you: "this year the market's going to crash 20%, 30%." Could it happen? Of course it's possible. But the chance of the market going down 20% to 30%, looking at history, has only happened twice in 74 years: a 2.7% probability. The chance of the market going up 20% to 30% is 21.6%. So the market is like 10 times more likely to go up 20 to 30% than to go down 20 to 30%. Don't be caught up in the pessimism the financial media portrays to sensationalize and scare people. Over the long run, of the 74 years (transcribed "24 years"), 58 were bullish and 16 were bearish. It pays to stay invested and to stay bullish: if you stay invested you are happy 78% of the time and 21% of the time you won't be very happy, but it doesn't matter, because over the long run the gains from the up years more than offset the losses from the down years, so you always end up with a gain over time.

#### [12:15] Other markets: Singapore
Are these odds as great for other stock markets? No. Most stock markets always go up, but the probability of a gain in a particular year is not as high as the US. He focuses on one market he is familiar with and invests in, mainly for dividends: Singapore. The only drawback is that he doesn't have the 70-year history; he has only 22 years of data. The Straits Times Index goes back a lot more than 22 years, but the majority of the gains of the Singapore market comes from dividends, so he cannot use the index itself, since it doesn't show total return including dividends. He uses the Straits Times Index ETF, ticker ES3, which only started in 2003. That gives 22 years of data, "but it's better than nothing." Using the STI ETF (including dividends): in 2003 the market went up 36% (inclusive of dividends), and in 2004 it went up 18.96%, and so on.

- One-year: in the last 22 years, 5 years ended with a loss. 5 out of 22 is a 22.73% probability of loss. This includes dividends because for Singapore most of the gains come from dividends and not price increase, very unlike the US market, where the majority comes from price increase. 17 out of 22 years ended with a gain: a 77.27% chance of a gain. So if you buy Singapore stocks in January and hold to December, your chance of making money is about 77% including dividends collected. Without dividends it is a lot lower, only about 56%.
- Five-year periods (2003 to 2007, 2004 to 2008, every single combination): there were 18 of them and only one, 2011 to 2015, ended with a loss even after collecting dividends. Chance of gain after holding five years: 94.44%. Chance of loss: 5.56%. Pretty good.
- Ten years: if you hold the Singapore index through the ETF for 10 years and collect dividends, it's 100%: 13 out of 13 periods made money, zero out of 13 (transcribed "30") lost money. Pretty good odds.

#### [16:09] Fact 3: stock prices don't go up in a straight line; they move in cycles (trends)
Recap: fact 1, the market always goes up in the long run; fact 2, the market goes up 78% of all years for the US market. Fact 3: prices move in cycles, or trends.
- Bull market (uptrend): there are periods when the market goes through a series of higher highs and higher lows, where prices trend higher. This is usually related to economic growth and there's a lot of optimism in the markets. Most of the time the stock market is in a bull market, on an uptrend.
- Bear market (downtrend): there are periods when the market goes into a bear market phase, a downtrend, where prices make a series of lower highs and lower lows. Often these bear markets are associated with recessions (not all the time, but most of the time), or a lot of pessimism in the markets.

On the actual S&P 500 chart over a period, from the beginning it always ends up higher but goes through these cycles. If you take a pen you can roughly pencil in the bull market cycles (he points at three) and, between them, the bear market cycles (also three).

#### [18:34] Fact 4: bull markets are opportunities for investors
Stock markets are in a bull market close to 80% of the time. In a bull market do prices still go down? Yes, but every time they go down, they go even higher: down, higher; down, higher. It makes a pattern of higher highs and higher lows. Bull markets are made up of what he calls wave up and wave down patterns (he'll say this a lot): prices move in a series of waves. It's like a breathing pattern: breathe out, breathe in. No matter how strong you are, you can't breathe out forever; you run out of air, so eventually you have to breathe in to breathe out again. Same with price: it can't go up every single day; it has to come down before going even higher.

- Pullback: whenever the wave down is a drop of between 1 to 9 percent, we call that a pullback. On average, the stock market goes through about three pullbacks a year, i.e. the market drops at least between 1 to 9 percent three times a year. When does it happen? No one knows; it will happen when it happens.
- Correction: of those three times it drops, one of those times the price would drop between 10 to 90% [as transcribed; stated as a bigger drop] [earlier pass has 10% to 19%], which is a bigger drop he calls a correction. Corrections happen on average once a year. Pullbacks are small wave downs; corrections are bigger wave downs.
- Duration: pullbacks and corrections last between one to four months. These are great buy opportunities. Whenever we want to buy stocks, we want to buy during a wave down, either a small wave down (a pullback) or a big wave down (a correction).

#### [21:13] How to guess the bottom of a wave down: moving averages
A common question: "Adam, how do I know it's the end of the wave down so I can get in before the next wave up?" We can never predict the absolute bottom of a wave down; no one can. But we can guess where possible bottoms are. One way is by using moving averages (a later lesson under technical analysis covers them in greater detail). Moving averages are the colorful lines on the chart: the blue line is the 50-day moving average, the green line is the 150-day moving average, and the red line is the 200-day moving average. They are calculated and plotted automatically by the charting software; you will learn how to put them in.

Moving averages help identify support levels. A support level is where, when the price drops, it tends to bottom and then bounce back up. Is it a guarantee? Does it happen all the time? No, but it's a good probability. For example, over one period the S&P 500 used the blue line (50-day moving average) as support: every time the price waves down, it hits the blue line and bounces up, goes down, bounces, repeatedly. So moving averages act as support. Later you'll learn that once the price drops to a moving average support, that's a possible bottom and that's where we can start adding shares. Could it drop even lower to the next moving average? Yes, that's also possible. That's why when we buy, we never buy in one go; we add in tranches at these support levels. More on that later.

---

# Part 3 — Lessons 20–22

### 20 - Trend Following Part 1

#### [00:00] Recap: buy and hold, and the idea of trend following
In the last video the first method of investing was simply buy and hold through the ups and downs, through bull markets and bear markets: bull market, bear market, bull market, bull market. Essentially you buy consistently every couple of months regardless of the trend and hold through the ups and downs. Done consistently, on average in the long run you get about 10% return per year.

The second way is trend following. Here we attempt to time the markets by following the trend: identify the uptrends (bull markets) and identify the bear markets. Trend following is more active. We buy only during the uptrend (the bull market). Once we identify that it is going into a bear market, we sell all our shares. Only when the bear market has ended and a new bull market starts do we get back in. So we get in during the bull market, get out during the bear market, and keep repeating. This is trend following.

#### [01:26] Using moving averages to spot trend changes
How to do this effectively: moving averages. On the chart shown there is a bull market (uptrend), then a bear market, then a new bull market. The question is how to know that the bull market has reversed into a bear market, so we can sell when the bear market begins and avoid the entire bear market, which on this chart took about a year. Then we want to buy back once the new bull market has started. To identify a change in trend, two moving averages are used: the 50-day moving average (blue line; he uses blue for the 50) and the 150 moving average (green line).

A moving average is a mathematical calculation where you take the average of the stock price over a certain period. For the 50 moving average the computer takes the last 50 days of the stock's closing price, adds them together and divides by 50, repeats this every day, and plots the blue line. Same for the 150: the software adds the last 150 days of closing prices, divides by 150, and repeats every day until it plots the green line. Later he shows how to install this into the software.

#### [03:38] Downtrend rules: how to recognise it and how it reverses
Always remember: on a downtrend, the 150-day moving average (green) is always above the 50-day moving average (blue). When the 150 green line is above the 50 blue line, it is a confirmed downtrend, a bear market.

Memory trick: imagine green is the Incredible Hulk and blue is a Smurf. When the Hulk is on the Smurf, it goes down. Or imagine the 150 is a 150-kg man and the 50 is a 50-kg man: when the big man sits on the small man, it goes down. Also notice that on a downtrend both moving averages slope down.

How do you know when the downtrend has reversed into an uptrend? Two conditions must be met (write this down, really important).
- Condition 1: both moving averages must change their slope. From sloping down, both lines must flatten or slope upwards. The blue must flatten or slope up and the green must also flatten or slope up. If either line is still sloping down, it is still a downtrend and not a change in trend. Both must be flat or sloping up.
- Condition 2: the 50 moving average (blue) must cross above the 150 moving average. There must be a crossover.

The moment the blue is above the green and both are flat or sloping up, that is the confirmed change in trend: an uptrend / bull market confirmation, and that is when we buy to get back into the market.

#### [06:52] Uptrend rules and the reversal into a downtrend
Note the difference: on a downtrend the 150 (green) is above the 50 (blue), a bear market. On an uptrend the 50 moving average is always above the 150 moving average. By the metaphor: when the small 50-kg man is above the big man, the big man can carry the small man, so it goes up (the Smurf is on the Hulk, the Hulk carries the Smurf and it keeps going up). On an uptrend both lines slope upwards.

How do you know when the uptrend has reversed into a downtrend? Again two criteria.
- Criterion 1: both moving averages must change slope, from sloping up to flattening or sloping down. The blue must flatten or slope down and the green must also flatten or slope down. If one moving average is still sloping up, it is not a change in trend. A very important criterion.
- Criterion 2: the 50 moving average must cross below the 150, so the blue crosses below the green and the big man is back above the small man.

When you have these two criteria, it confirms a change in trend, it is now a bear market, and you want to sell. As long as the blue is below the green, the downtrend continues.

#### [09:50] Example on the Dow Jones / S&P chart, 2006 to about 2011
Looking at it on charts: during roughly 2006 to about 2011 there was a big crash, the financial crisis. If you had bought the Dow Jones or the S&P and held it for five years, you would end up with only a very small return. If you had followed the trend you would have sold before the bear market and bought back to catch the bull market. To do this the moving averages are plotted by the software: the 50 moving average (blue) and the 150 (green).

Zooming in: the 50 crossing above the 150 means an uptrend, a buy. That would have been the buy signal. As long as it is an uptrend, we can keep buying when the price retraces or corrects to the moving average. Recall wave up, wave down: we buy on the wave down. When it waves down and hits one of the moving averages, we can add more shares. It goes up, waves down, hits the moving average, and we buy more; as long as blue is above green it is still an uptrend and a bull market, so we buy more again on the next wave down.

Then this time it drops and goes up, but now the 50 crosses below the 150, the downtrend signal. Check the two criteria. Criterion 1: both moving averages have changed slope; the blue is sloping down and the green has flattened, so they are not sloping up anymore. Criterion 2: the 50 has crossed below the 150. That is an exit signal; we sell all the shares in the ETF and get out before the bear market, and are out of the market for a year. Although the price went up and up afterwards, we would not get back in, because as long as the blue is below the green we do not get back in.

When do we get back in? Later the 50 crosses back above the 150 and both criteria are met: the 50 is sloping up now (from sloping down) and the green has flattened. A change in slope plus a crossover: we buy back in. We ride the trend up, and during the wave ups and wave downs we can buy more on each wave down. Further on there is another sell signal: the blue crosses below the green, the blue slopes down, the green has flattened, so we sell. After selling, we buy back again where the blue crosses back above the green, the blue is sloping up and the green is sloping up.

Result of that sequence: the first trade (get in, get out) was a 22% return, avoiding the entire bear market. Get back in, sell: a 31% return. Then buy and hold to the end: a 19% return. Adding up the three returns gives a 72% return over this five year period, which would have been better than simply buying at the start and holding to the end.

#### [15:09] Applying it on real charts (Think or Swim)
You can use any charting software; all allow you to put in moving averages, whether TradingView or your broker's charting software. He is using the Think or Swim (TD Ameritrade) charting software and puts in SPY, the S&P ETF.

By the way, when you use the 50 and 150 moving average crossover method you must ensure it is on daily candles. It only works on daily candles. Do not use it on weekly or monthly candles for the change-in-trend method. It has to be daily candles.

He sets daily candles and will back test to show how the method enters and exits the market over, say, the last 20 years. Clicking the 20-year range automatically switches to monthly candles, so he changes the timeframe back to daily candles: 20 years, daily candles. This shows the last 20 years of the market. There was the dot-com crash, a bear market, and the question is how we would know when to get back in to the bull market and avoid this. Another bear market came during the financial crisis: bull market, bull market, bear market, then the bull market started. How do we get out, sell, and buy back so we avoid these bear markets? We put in the moving averages.

#### [17:53] Installing the moving averages
Go to the studies section, type "moving averages", and choose simple moving average. There are two kinds, simple and exponential; for this 50/150 method we use the simple moving average. Double-click and by default you get the 9-day moving average; click the edit button and change 9 to 50. The 50 is always blue ("because Adam says so, follow Adam"). Make the width a bit thicker, about 2 or 3, it doesn't matter. OK. Add a second one for the 150 moving average, and 150 is always green (again, because Adam says so). Later we will put in the 200 moving average, which is red, but that is for later. For now just 50 and 150. You can save it, for example as "50-150 SMA" (he already has it saved in his own settings, so he doesn't save it again).

Once done, the software plots the 50 line and the 150 line. The chart now shows the year 2001. He is going to back test, moving forward in time, and asks you to pretend you don't know the future, can't predict it, and make decisions only on what you see right now and right here.

### 21 - Trend Following Part 2

#### [00:00] Back-test begins: 2001, downtrend, false cross
Right now the chart shows the year 2001. He will back test the strategy by moving through time; pretend you don't know the future and decide based only on what you see. This is today, the current day, and we go day by day. First question: uptrend or downtrend? A downtrend, a bear market. Why? Because the blue 50 is below the 150: as long as the 150 is above the 50, the big man is on the small man, the Hulk on the Smurf, and we are going down. Do we buy? No.

Moving day by day, the 50 just crossed the 150. Do we buy right there? No. Remember there are two criteria for a downtrend to change into an uptrend. The first: both moving averages have to flatten or slope upwards. Has that happened? No. Although the blue has sloped up, the green is still sloping down, so it is not a change in trend. You have to watch the green line and wait for it to flatten before confirming the uptrend. A few days forward, the green is still sloping down, so not a buy, and the downtrend continues.

The mistake a lot of people make is they see the 50 above the 150 and buy, and it was a fake-out. You cannot just look at the 50/150 crossover; you have to look at the slope of the moving averages. The slope is the most important thing. The 50 then goes back below the 150, the downtrend continues, a false reversal.

#### [02:46] First confirmed buy: 9489
It crossed again. Is this a confirmed reversal? Yes: criterion 1, the blue line is now sloping up and the green line sloping up, both sloping up and not down; criterion 2, the 50 is above the 150, the blue has just crossed above the green. That is an uptrend, a bull market, we buy. To keep it simple, we buy the next day right after the cross, at the open of that day. On the left, O is the opening price of the day, H the high, L the low, C the closing price. Putting the mouse on the candle after the cross shows the O of 9489 (the number he reads out) [earlier pass has 94.89]. That is the price at which we buy the SPY ETF: 9489. He keys it into his Excel spreadsheet, and later we sell when we see a new bear market and calculate profit or loss. Again: buy the day after the cross.

#### [04:27] Staying in, a crossover that is not yet a sell, then sell at 109.62
Now we are in the market; tell me when to sell, once you see a bear market or downtrend. Moving along, we have a crossover. Is that a sell? Check the two criteria. Criterion 1: for an uptrend to reverse into a downtrend both moving averages must flatten or slope down. Has that happened? No, because the green is still sloping up, so it is not a change in trend, not yet. Even though there is a crossover, the slope has not changed, so we don't take action. The moment the green slopes down, we sell. Moving on, zooming in, the green has flattened, so it is a changing trend. We sell right there at the open price of 109.62. We are out of the market based on the moving averages.

#### [06:28] Short-lived downtrend, buy back at 113.12
The market crosses back. This downtrend was very short-lived. Now the market trends back up, the 50 crosses back above the 150, blue above green. Have they changed their slope back? Yes: the blue is sloping up and the green has flattened. That is a downtrend-to-uptrend reversal confirmation, so we buy back there, at the O price of 113.12.

#### [07:24] Another crossover that is not a sell; then sell at 125.2
Keep moving ("just keep swimming"). Another crossover: the 50 has crossed below the 150. Is that a sell, a downtrend? No. Because the green is still sloping up; they must both flatten and slope down to confirm the downtrend. Wait a few more days; the green has to slope down for us to get out. The green is still sloping up, and the blue is going up now, catching up. The uptrend continues.

He notes: he is not predicting the future. No one can predict where the market is going to go; he is not a fortune teller. He is following the trend, following the river: if the river is flowing north he swims north; if it flows south he may get off the boat. We follow the trend and the market; we don't predict it, because the market can't be predicted. On an uptrend, going north, we stay in the boat.

Then another crossover, blue crossing below green. Do we meet the two criteria? Is each moving average flattened or sloping down? The blue is sloping down definitely; the green is pretty flat. So that is a sell, at this candle at 125.2 (he says "125.2... let's get out at 125.5" then "point two", so 125.2 is the figure). We are out. After selling it reverses back up: this was a downtrend no doubt but it didn't last very long. We can identify when a trend will change, but we can't identify how long the new trend will last. You can't predict the future; you just follow the trend.

#### [10:00] Buy back at 132.12, a non-sell, sell at 147.93
It looks like an uptrend again, the river reversed up. Crossover again: the 50 is back above the 150, blue sloping up, green sloping up, an uptrend. We buy back at 132.12. We are back in the saddle and riding the trend. We are not predicting the future, not reading the news or newspapers; we ignore everything, everything is noise. We just look at price action and follow the trend and the market. Don't outsmart the market; just follow it.

Another cross: the blue is back below the green. Is that a sell? No, because the green is sloping up; they must both flatten or slope down to confirm a downtrend. It is still an uptrend. Then it crosses back down again: the 50 is now back below the 150. Is it still sloping up? No: blue sloping down, green sloping down (zoomed in). That is a sell. We sell right after the cross at 147.93. If you look at the price action you also see a change: an uptrend makes higher highs, but now it makes lower highs, so there is definitely a change in trend. We sell there.

#### [12:37] Avoiding the 50% plunge; buy at 9367
After we sell it goes down. As long as the blue is below the green we stay out of the market. By getting out we avoided this entire bear market, a 50% plunge. Then you tell me when to get back in. A cross happens: the 50 is above the 150; do we buy in? Not yet. Why? The blue is sloping up but the green is still sloping down. You have to wait for the green to flatten or slope up. Give it a few more days. The green has flattened, so we can buy now: at this candle at 9367 [earlier pass has 93.67]. We are back in the market, have caught the new bull market, and are on the way back up.

#### [14:26] Sell at 106.02, buy at 118.1, sell at 124.42, buy at 128.73
Another cross: the 50 crossing below the 150. Both sloping down or flat? Yes: blue sloping down, green flattened, a sell. We sell the day after the cross, on this candle, at 106.02. Out again. You can't predict how long the downtrend will last; it goes back up, we cross back up. Again this was an uptrend and then a downtrend that didn't last very long. We get back in: blue above green, blue sloping up, green sloping up, confirmed uptrend, we buy at the crossover at 118.1.

Looks like it will cross back down: the 50 crossing below the 150. Sell? Not yet, the green is still sloping up; both have to flatten or slope down. Wait to see if the green slopes down. The green has flattened: a downtrend. We sell at this candle, where there is a gap down, at 124.42. Another crossover: do we buy it back? No, not yet, because the green is still sloping down; you have to wait for both to flatten or slope up. If you are not sure, wait a few more days. The green has flattened, the blue is sloping up, we can take it: back in the boat at 128.73.

Another crossover: sell? No, the green is sloping up; wait to see only if the green changes to sloping down. No, now the blue is sloping up and they are both sloping up, so it is still an uptrend and we stay in the boat.

#### [18:17] Adding shares: buy the dip on the uptrend
Notice that in trend following, the moment we see a new uptrend (the 50 above the 150, sloping up) we get in and add shares. We can't tell how long this uptrend will last; this one is obviously lasting pretty long, it could last for many, many months or many, many years. As long as it is on an uptrend, can we keep adding more shares every few months? Yes. When? Remember we buy on the wave down: wave up, wave down, impulse, correction. Every time it drops near the moving average, there is nothing wrong with adding shares during a correction. Each time it pulls back, "breathes in", we can add shares: once in three months, once in six months, once in a year, it's up to you. He calls this buying the dip on the uptrend. "Write this down guys. Buy the dip on the uptrend." A dip, also known as a correction; dip, correction and retracement are all the same thing.

#### [19:56] The parabolic drop; sell at 187.49 (for the exercise only)
Suddenly the market just dropped 90 degrees. This is called a parabolic drop: the market drops 90 degrees straight down. Usually an uptrend goes up at about 45 degrees and comes down about 45 degrees; that is a normal changing trend. A 90-degree drop is a parabolic drop. It does happen in the market once in a while. When it does, by the time the moving averages cross and you sell, you tend to sell really, really low. He will teach later that when this happens, you don't sell (he repeats: you don't sell), because it is too late; the market went down so fast you can't get out. But for the sake of this exercise, to follow the rules of the game, we sell; in reality he won't. He doesn't remember exactly why it happened (it doesn't matter why; manipulation or whatever); he could go back and look. Following the rules, we sell at the crossover on this candle's opening price, 187.49. In reality he doesn't sell on a 90-degree drop and explains why in the next part.

#### [22:32] Buy at 210.12; moving averages act as support
We are out and will get back in if we see a new uptrend confirmation. The 50 has gotten back above the 150. Is that a buy? No, the green is still sloping down; we wait for the green to slope up or at least flatten. Green still sloping down... (a double dip). Then a crossover again, blue sloping up, now the green sloping up, meeting all criteria. We buy back at 210.12.

Notice again, on wave up, wave down ("breathe out, breathe in"), that during these dips price tends to find support at one of the moving averages. The moving averages act like a support level, which is where he knows he can add more shares.

#### [24:06] Sell at 266.52, buy at 284.7 (pandemic crash)
Carrying on, the uptrend could last for many, many years; just stay in the boat as long as it lasts. A cross, but the green is sloping up, so no sell. Another crossover: is it a sell? Blue sloping down; green flattened? Not clear. Give it a few more days. It looks like the green may still be sloping up slightly. If I'm not sure, I give it a few more days. Still slightly sloping up; then it looks like the green has flattened. All right, I'm getting out: sell at 266.52.

We cross back up but the green is still sloping down, so I'm not getting back in; wait for the green to flatten. It has flattened: I buy back in at 284.7, catching the new trend up.

#### [26:32] The pandemic crash, second parabolic drop; sell at 249.52, buy at 320.13
This was the pandemic crash, and the market dropped 90 degrees: another parabolic drop. Like he said, in reality when he sees a 90-degree drop he doesn't sell. It's like a rubber ball: throw it down really hard and it bounces back up really fast. There is no point selling because it's too late. For this experiment he sells; but those who have followed him know that in reality he did not sell in February. The 50 crosses the 150 and we sell here. The limitation of this method: if the market drops 90 degrees, by the time the moving averages cross it's too late; you're selling after it has crashed. That is why he won't sell. Never mind, sell anyway: the day after the cross, the opening price of this candle, 249.52.

Then it crosses back up. Do we buy it back? Yes: the blue is sloping up, the green has flattened. We buy it back at 320.13.

#### [28:37] Result of the 20-year back test
As of today (2020), back to the present: 390.25, the last closing price yesterday. Over the 20 years, by riding the big trends, there were 9 buys and 9 sales (he counts "1, 2, 3 ... 9 transactions, 9 buys and 9 sales in 20 years"). Do we make money? Like any method, there is no 100% right; you win some, you lose some, but you win more than you lose. There were many profitable trades; one loss, because of the plunge in February 2020, but very quickly after the plunge it made it all back. Overall you make money.

This is called trend following. The advantage: it helps identify the start of a bear market. Looking at an earlier bear market, we were in a bull market, and with the crossover we got out and avoided that bear market completely for one year, and then it got us back into the market. That is what's great about trend following.

#### [30:40] The limitation, and what he does in reality
Remember, no method is foolproof; there are always pros and cons. This method fails when we get a sudden 90-degree drop. If the market is going up nicely and reverses down, we can get out early based on the moving averages, and if it reverses back up that is fine. But when the market suddenly drops 90 degrees because of some pandemic or whatever, the moving average is no longer useful: by the time the moving averages cross, you sell too late, only after the crash, when there is no point selling anyway.

In reality he does look at moving averages, but when he sees a 90-degree drop like February 2020, he ignores the moving averages completely; he didn't bother to sell. "Too late, just hold it." That is fine because if you hold great companies they bounce back really fast anyway. The question is how to know when to buy more shares to take advantage of the big discount. Instead of waiting for the 50 to cross back above the 150, he started buying right at the bottom. If he had waited for the 50 to cross back above the 150, he would have bought only after the big recovery, a bit too high; he wants to get in as low as possible. His theory: when a market drops 90 degrees, it is like a rubber ball; the harder you throw it down, the faster it bounces back up.

#### [32:31] How to add at the bottom: staged buying by drawdown
How do you know when to add shares at the bottom? You can't predict the exact bottom, but you can guess. He has studied the markets for the last 28 years and has seen six bear markets. By studying the history of bear markets, he found that the average bear market (average over 100 years) drops 36%. Once the market drops 20% from the high, that is considered a bear market; anything more than 20% is a bear market. The average drop is 36%, but it has gone as low as 55% during the financial crisis.

So the moment the market drops by about 25%, he starts to add shares. If he wants to add, for example, 100 shares of the ETF, he won't add a full 100 at the 25% mark; he adds maybe 25 shares, a quarter position. If the market drops to 35% or 36% (the average), he adds another quarter position, so he has half of the planned position. If the market drops another 10%, he adds the next quarter position. Only if the market drops to 55%, the biggest drop the market has ever seen in history, does he know it is definitely the bottom, and adds the last quarter (the last 25). Because you never know where the bottom is: sometimes at minus 25 you reverse, you could reverse here or there. You can never know the exact reversal, so you buy in stages. If it reverses at 35, he doesn't get to buy all the shares he wants, but he gets at least half of the position. Once the moving averages cross back and he confirms the uptrend, he adds more shares later on.

That was his strategy for the recent crash of February 2020, the COVID pandemic crash. If you want to see exactly what he did and how he navigated the bear market, go to YouTube and search for "Adam Khoo Bull Market". The first video he created on whether the stock market had bottomed was dated the third of April 2020 (he first says "third of August" then corrects to April): that is when he called the bottom of the market, or said it was near the bottom at least. Then, in another video, on April the 10th he called the new bull market and said it was time to get back into the markets. Watch these videos for more insight into the additional parameters and indicators he uses as well.

### 22 - Strategy Execution

#### [00:00] Using the 200 moving average
Besides the 50/150 crossover, you can also use the 200 simple moving average, which you can install into the chart as well. It is the red line (he likes red for the 200; it's his preference). The 200 works alone. When the price of the stock or ETF is above the 200 moving average and the 200 is sloping up, it is an uptrend. Two criteria for an uptrend: the price must be above the 200, and the 200 must slope up. It cannot be flat and cannot be sloping down; it must slope up. This is a difference: for the 50/150, flat or sloping up counts for an uptrend (and flat or sloping down for a downtrend), but the 200, being only one moving average, must be more strict. We only allow sloping up to be an uptrend.

How do we know an uptrend reverses into a downtrend? Two criteria again. The price must go below the 200 moving average (cross below) and the 200 moving average has to slope downwards. On the chart, that is an uptrend; the price dropped below the 200 in two places but that is not a downtrend because the 200 is still sloping up. Later, when it starts to slope down, that is the downtrend confirmation. Then the price again goes above the 200 but the 200 is still sloping down, so it is not an uptrend yet. Only when the 200 slopes up and the price remains above is it an uptrend signal.

#### [02:20] Which signal to follow: whichever comes first
Which one do we use, the 50/150 or the 200? The answer: he combines all three to determine the trend direction. Again, this trend-reversal method only works on daily candles. Which signal does he listen to? The one that comes first; whichever comes first is the signal of a changing trend.

Example: in an uptrend, the 50/150 signal for a downtrend is the 50 crossing below the 150 with both flat or sloping down. Based on the 50/150, the cross with a change in slope was there, so the exit would be there. Based on the 200, he would get out only later, at the point where the red line started sloping down and the price remained below the red line. Which came first? The 50/150 signal, so he takes that. Sometimes the 200 signal comes first and sometimes the 50/150, so whichever comes first.

Similarly in a downtrend, to identify the change to an uptrend: based on the 50/150, a crossover with the 50 crossing above the 150 and the 150 flat, so the entry is there. Based on the 200, entry would be only somewhere later, when the 200 slopes up. Again the 50/150 came first. We follow whichever signal comes first; the 50/150 doesn't always come first, sometimes the 200 will. Once again, whichever is the earlier signal.

#### [05:21] Summary of the trend-following strategy for ETFs
We buy when the index or ETF is making a dip on the uptrend. The uptrend is defined as: the 50 moving average above the 150, and both moving averages flat or sloping up (not sloping down); or the price of the ETF above the 200 moving average (the red line) and the 200 sloping up. Either condition meets an uptrend. As long as it is an uptrend, we buy when the price retraces or dips to the moving average.

When do we sell/get out of the ETF? When the index reverses into a downtrend, which could be a potential bear market. The signal: the 50 moving average crosses below the 150 and the two moving averages have flattened or are sloping down; or the ETF price crosses below the 200 moving average and the 200 slopes down. Either one, whichever comes first.

The exception to the rule is the 90-degree (parabolic) drop, like the pandemic of 2020. When that happens and you see the crossover, there is no point selling because it's too late; it has already dropped, and selling would be near the bottom, which doesn't make sense. In those cases he won't sell, he'll just hold it, because if you hold a good ETF, good companies, then like a rubber ball, the faster it drops 90 degrees the faster it will bounce back. He adds more shares only when the 50 crosses back above the 150 [filled from earlier pass], but by the time that happens it would have gone up quite a bit. So, as mentioned, usually when the market drops by 25% from the top he starts to add more shares; at 35% he adds more, 45%, and at 55% that's the bottom and he's all in, maybe.

#### [07:25] Summary of the three methods
Method 1, the simplest: no need to time the market, no need to look at the trend, simply do dollar cost averaging, investing a fixed dollar amount at regular intervals regardless of whether it's an uptrend or downtrend. Done consistently, on average you buy more at a low price and less at a high price, so you average your cost to a reasonable level, and you always make money over time. You can do this on a portfolio. The only requirement is consistency. It can be done on a portfolio of equity ETFs or a portfolio of multi-asset ETFs, and it is a passive approach, buy and hold through the ups and downs; if you are colourblind, just do this [from earlier pass].

Method 2, trend following (this one requires that you can see the colours of the moving averages [from earlier pass]): is it an uptrend or a downtrend, based on the 50, 150, 200 moving averages? As long as it's an uptrend we can buy. We buy when the index or ETF starts an uptrend and can subsequently add more shares as long as the price is making a dip on the uptrend. Remember that during an uptrend you get wave up, wave down repeatedly, and every time there is a wave down or dip near the moving averages (that's near the bottom), you buy. In this method we do get out of the market when we see the uptrend has reversed into a downtrend or bear market. There is no telling how long bear markets will last: one month, six months, one year. You get out and get back in when the new trend forms. [A short section between roughly 08:00 and 08:48 on method 2's wave-down buying is missing/garbled in the transcript.]

Method 3, the one he actually uses, is a hybrid of the first two: dollar cost averaging, i.e. every six months buy shares with a fixed amount of money, but not blindly: he looks at the trend of the market. He only buys when the market is making a dip on the uptrend, or when the market is at the support level of a consolidation. If the market is on a downtrend, he avoids dollar cost averaging.

#### [10:47] Buy and hold vs trend following: back test result
A common question: which gives the best returns in the long run, buy and hold or trend following? He asks you to guess. He did a back test over the last 26 years comparing buying and holding (buying consistently through dollar cost averaging regardless of uptrend or downtrend, holding through ups and downs) versus trend following (buying only on the uptrend, getting out at the start of a downtrend, buying back in, as in the back test just done). "The answer is you have gotten a total return..." [the result figures are missing from the transcript; the audio jumps from here to about 13:04, so the stated outcome was not captured]. Buy and hold had a total return of 644%, and trend following a total return of 696%. In the long run the two are about the same; trend following gives slightly better results, but passive buying and holding would have done pretty well too. Whatever strategy you use, the most important thing is to get started as early as possible, and the worst thing a person can do is procrastinate and never start [filled from earlier pass].

#### [13:04] Start early; cash vs the market
Only invest with cash you don't need in the short term: if you need the cash for emergencies or expenses in the next one to two years, don't invest it, keep it in cash or time deposits [from earlier pass]. To compound your wealth, start as early as possible. Some people never get started because they think the market is very volatile and risky, and that is true in the short term. On the chart, in the short term, holding cash seems safer than the stock market. Example of a year the market dropped a lot: 2022, during the bear market (bear markets happen once in a while). During the 2022 bear market, if you put money in the stock market, it dropped up to 20% in that one year. If you just held cash, you may feel your cash didn't drop; but of course cash loses value from inflation. That is the short-term picture. In the long run the relationship inverts: the longer you hold cash, the more risky it becomes, and the longer you hold stocks, the safer it becomes [from earlier pass].

Stretch it to the longer run, 5 to 10 years: when you hold cash, your cash hardly grows; in the long run it doesn't build your wealth and you won't have enough for retirement because of inflation. If you put it in the markets and allow time to grow, even with the ups and downs, bear market, bear market, over time you'll be much better off than someone who stayed in cash for five to ten years.

People have an irrational fear: what if I put my money in and it crashes next week or next month; I read in the news that a recession is coming, a bear market is coming. First, you can never predict when the next crash will come. Even if you are so unlucky as to buy before the crash, you'd still be better off in the long run than holding cash. On the chart, imagine you were so unlucky: you bought right at the top, it crashed, you bought again and it crashed, again and it crashed; even buying at the top, if you held on over the long run, you are still better off than the guy who stayed in cash all the way. Very, very important to understand.

#### [15:59] Five scenarios, 2001 to 2020: bad timing beats procrastination
The worst thing you can do in investing is to procrastinate and never get started. Even bad timing, putting your money in at the worst time, is still better than not starting at all. The chart is from 2001 to 2020; imagine you wanted to invest $2,000 annually into the S&P 500 ETF. Five scenarios:
1. Perfect timing: you invest $2,000 at the lowest point of the market every year (impossible, but theoretically). After the 20 years, your money would have grown to $151,000.
2. Invest immediately at the start of every year (e.g. 2nd or 3rd of January, the first day, $2,000). After 20 years, you end with $135,000: slightly lower than perfect timing but still pretty good.
3. Dollar cost averaging: divide the $2,000 into 12 months, $167 per month, every month. At the end of 20 years, you end up with $134,856, very close to the guy who put it in at the beginning of every year.
4. The "Charlie Brown" loser with bad timing: he buys at the worst point every year, investing $2,000 at the top of the market. After 20 years he has built his savings to $121,000. Not as great as perfect timing or dollar cost averaging, but still pretty good.
5. The worst: the guy who stayed in cash for 20 years, saying "I'll invest when it goes lower" (some people always say they will start when it gets lower, or "what if I buy and it crashes tomorrow", and keep waiting; if you keep saying that you never start). After 20 years, his $2,000 a year in time deposits grows to $44,000.

Remember the lesson: even bad timing beats procrastination.

#### [18:56] Macroeconomic data: ignore it
A common question: in making investment decisions, do you look at macroeconomic factors like interest rates, GDP, employment data? No, not at all. He reads them for entertainment purposes but never uses them in making investment decisions. Why? If you look at macroeconomic data (GDP, inflation, interest rates), not only will it not help you in making investment decisions, it will actually make you make worse decisions. It will affect your returns adversely: you will buy at the worst time and sell at the worst time.

The stock market is not the economy; they are two totally separate animals. The stock market is a leading indicator of the economy; the stock market leads the economy by about six to nine months. When the stock market goes from an uptrend to a downtrend, six to nine months later the economy will show a recession. Similarly, when the stock market reverses from a downtrend into an uptrend, six to nine months later the economy will show a recovery based on GDP. The stock market moves ahead; that is why we focus only on price action, the trend of the market. If you focus on economic data, you are six to nine months late and will always make late decisions.

A lot of people made this mistake in 2020: they sold at the worst time, at the bottom of the crash, failed to get back in, and missed the huge rally. In early April 2020 the news was showing all the bad economic data, a recession; when people read all this bad economic news they sell stocks. [The transcript skips from about 21:07 to 22:00, so part of this passage is missing.] The stock market doesn't care what is happening to the economy now; it cares what will happen to the economy in six to nine months to one year. So the stock market is the leading indicator of the economy, and it is not the economy.

#### [22:00] The stock market vs GDP chart
This chart shows the relationship: the stock market is the red line (stock price) and the economy is the green line (GDP). At the market top, the market reverses into a downtrend. Would you know it's a downtrend? Yes: the 50 moving average crossing below the 150, sloping down. As trend followers, that is when you could start to exit the market and sell the stocks. But notice that while the stock market is falling, the green line (the economy) is in full recovery [as read by him; the exact spot on the chart was shown on screen]. Many investors who don't understand this read the news, see the economy doing well, and buy stocks; they are reading economic news, which is a different thing from market trend. By the time they sell... [garbled in transcript, 23:15-24:12]. Investors reading the news see a strong economy and buy right at the top, then wonder why it goes down; when the recession finally appears they sell, and by then they are selling near the bottom of the stock market cycle, which is why most retail investors buy high and sell low [filled from earlier pass].

At the market bottom the market starts a new uptrend. Would you know it's a new uptrend? Yes: price above the 200 moving average picking up. Smart investors say it's an uptrend and start to buy there. But most retail investors who can't read the trend won't buy there; they say "it's a recession, how can you buy in a recession", because they're looking at the green line, which is still going down (GDP still dropping), so they don't buy. Later, when the economy is recovering, they start to buy, and by the time they buy they are buying near the top; the market goes up a bit and then collapses. So: ignore the economy, ignore GDP, ignore inflation, ignore interest rates, ignore employment.

#### [25:44] The 2020 example: bad news, and what he did
A recent example of how many retail investors got hurt by reading the economic news. Late March / early April 2020: the mainstream news all said "we're going to die".
- 30th of March: jobless claims passed 3 million to a record high; coronavirus job losses could total 47 million; the unemployment rate may hit 32%.
- 6th of April: Janet Yellen (as he describes her, the Federal Reserve Chairperson) says second quarter GDP could decline by 30%, and unemployment is already at 12 to 30% [as stated].
- 9th of April: death toll reaches a third straight record, 799; the IMF chief says the pandemic will unleash the worst recession since the Great Depression.

Most investors, ignorant, panicked, read this and sold. He was buying. He started buying in early April, on the 4th of April (as stated), 6th of April, 9th of April; he was buying shares every single day, and so was the entire community in the investment group, because they know the stock market is not the economy. After all this bad economic news, on the 9th of April the bull market started: the Dow jumped 500 points, up 13% on the week.

Looking at the 2020 crash: the market bottomed on the 23rd of March. At that point the media was most pessimistic, saying the depression was coming, the worst in history, and most people were selling like crazy. Many banks were recommending clients sell everything (he knows because he has a lot of friends who are bankers). He was one of the few people buying; he started buying on the 25th of March, two days after the bottom.

For subscribers of the Ultimate Investors Playbook (UIP), they get his buy and sell notifications within a couple of minutes of his trades. Then there were 1,200 members; now a few thousand more. On the 24th of March, the day right at the bottom, he said: just a heads up, I'm looking to add MasterCard into my portfolio as it's at the 150 MA support. His decision to buy is not because he predicts it's the bottom or that it will go up; no one can predict the bottom. As long as it's a great company, undervalued, at the support level, he will buy, and he knows he'll do very well.

On the 25th of March he bought MasterCard and sent a notification: added MasterCard at $236 (adding at 236.4), with the intention of adding more if it goes down to 210. On the 30th of March he added Facebook (now called Meta Platforms) at $159.43 [filled from earlier pass]. He was one of the few people, together with all his students, buying shares when everyone else was selling in panic. As a result, by the end of 2020, when most people didn't make money, his first portfolio grew 44% and his second portfolio grew 41.37%. In actual dollar gains he made $791,000 [filled from earlier pass] in profits during that recession and bear market year. To make this return he didn't just invest in the index ETF; he picked individual companies that were super high quality and very undervalued. That is what comes next: so far you have learned how to invest in index ETFs, very simple; the next stage is how to analyze individual companies that will beat the market, in the next lesson.

# Part 4 — Lessons 25–29

### 25 - Part 1: The Three Steps to Successful Investing (Lesson 3, Part 1)

#### [00:00] Lesson 3 intro: from index investing to beating the market
In the previous lesson you learned the simplest way to invest is to buy an ETF that tracks an index, so you hold the 30 or 500 biggest companies. But then you only get market returns. The purpose of this course is to learn how to beat the market and get much higher returns than the index provides. From here on the focus is how to pick individual stocks that outperform. This is a relatively shorter lesson because he wants you to understand the broad concept first; later lessons go into detail.

#### [00:45] Step 1: Identify great businesses
The first step to all successful investing is to identify the great businesses. When you buy a share you are not buying a lottery ticket; you are buying a piece of a business, owning a business. He only wants to buy great businesses, the best of breed, not mediocre or lousy ones. In the US market there are over 10,000 businesses and you can buy any you want, so why waste time on low-quality ones? He only focuses on the top 1% of the great businesses and ignores the 99%.

#### [01:40] Step 2: Buy only when the price is right (below intrinsic value)
Once you identify a great business, only buy it when it is selling below its intrinsic value, when it is undervalued. A great business can be a lousy investment if you pay too high a price, i.e. buy it when it is overvalued. A great business becomes a great investment only when you can buy it for less than it is worth. This holds for any asset.

Property analogy: you find a great apartment with a great view and location. The bank values it at a million dollars. You get excited, the seller asks a very high price, and you pay, say, 1.5 million. That is not a good investment because you paid 50% above the valuation; it will be hard to profit, and even if you eventually do, you won't get a high return because you overpaid. Now suppose the seller is desperate (getting a divorce, or lost his business and needs the cash) and you take it for $700,000. You bought a $1 million property for $700 grand: a great investment because you paid 30% less than the intrinsic value.

In real estate it is really hard to find a great property with a desperate motivated seller; you can, but it takes a lot of effort. In the stock market it happens all the time. He wouldn't say every day, but at least once a year or once every few years a great business goes on sale at a 20, 30, 50% discount because desperate sellers are panicking over bad news. He does this all the time; that is how he got really rich and how people like Warren Buffett got really rich.

So step two: invest when the share price is below intrinsic value, is undervalued, and ideally when the price has waved down to a support level. A support level is a technical analysis term (covered in lesson six): a price level where the price drops to that level and there is a high potential for it to reverse and bounce back up. How to identify support levels and calculate intrinsic value comes in subsequent lessons.

#### [04:34] Step 3: Hold (you shouldn't ever have to sell)
You may think step three is "when do I sell". Here's the thing: if you have done steps one and two very well, you shouldn't ever have to sell. "I'm not kidding." If you identified a great business and bought it undervalued, you should never have to sell until you need the cash to pay for something. If you don't need the cash, stay in the business because if it is great it keeps growing and compounding your wealth. If you sell it, you must reinvest in another great business, which is not easy because there are not that many around. Once you latch onto a great business, like having the perfect wife, you want to hold on for the rest of your life, unless you have to sell some of it to pay for something.

Examples of holding:
- Apple: one year gives anywhere from, say, 16% to 35%, depending on where you sell. Held for the last five years, wealth would have grown 191%. Ten years, 792%. Twenty years, 22,830%. The big money is in staying invested in a great business and letting it compound your wealth massively; that is how you make millions and build generational wealth. You don't build it by taking profit at 20%, 30%, 50%; you want a 10x, 15x, 20x on your money. That's why you hold great companies.
- MasterCard (another stock he has held for many, many years): one year, about 20%. Five years, 68%. Ten years, 563%, despite the COVID crash, the 2020 bear market and the trade war. Twenty years, up 6,000% despite recessions, crashes and the great financial crisis of 08/09.
- Nvidia (one of his biggest holdings, leader in the AI revolution): one year, up 144%. Five years, up 2,200%. Ten years (from 2015), up 29,615%, despite all the crashes along the way.

It must be a great business; he keeps emphasizing it. A low-quality or average business you don't want to hold for long. American Airlines, a lousy business: one year, 33% return; but five years you would have lost 33%; ten years, down 60%. The longer you hold it, the worse off you are. "All airlines, write this down, all airlines are lousy businesses." It's not the fault of management; it's the nature of the industry. Certain industries (airlines, property development) are just not very profitable by nature. Only great businesses compound your wealth over time; mediocre and lousy businesses stagnate or reduce it. "How do I know it's a great business?" He says we're coming to that.

#### [09:31] When you do sell
In a perfect world you buy a great business cheap and never sell until you need the money, but we don't live in a perfect world. You sell when the fundamentals of the business have deteriorated. That does not mean a short-term drop in profits or sales, which is cyclical and very normal. It means the long-term business model is disrupted or the company loses its competitive advantage. Then you get out. Exit rules are covered in detail in a later lesson. Examples of formerly great businesses: Intel and General Electric were great many years ago, not anymore, because over time they lost their competitive advantage and got disrupted. The moment you see that, get out and reinvest in a great business.

You can also exit a great business if there is an even better business; sell and reinvest in the even greater one. Or, even if it's a fantastic business, you may sell if the share price has gone too crazy, way overvalued, way too expensive. There is nothing wrong with selling part or even all of it, then holding cash and waiting for it to get cheap again or reinvesting in a great business that is more attractively priced. This is all covered in detail later.

#### [11:29] What makes a great business
A great business is one where you are confident that over the next 3, 5, 10, 20 years the business will consistently increase in value. On the chart, the green line represents the value of the business, also called the intrinsic value of the business or of its shares. As the business is worth more and more, people are willing to pay more, so the share price goes up over time.

What makes a business more valuable? A business is a money-making machine (said before), and the more money it makes, the more it is worth. When it generates increasing sales revenue, net income (also known as earnings) and increasing cash flow from operations, the intrinsic value keeps rising. How do you know it will keep increasing revenue, profits and cash flow over the next 5, 10, 15, 20 years? There are criteria that give a very high probability; the five criteria are taught in detail in lesson seven. Ideas of them:
1. Track record: a great business has a track record of at least the last five to ten years of consistently increasing revenue, net income and cash flow from operations. But history does not predict the future, so that's not good enough.
2. A sustainable competitive advantage that protects it from competition, called an economic moat.
3. High return on equity / capital, so management is extremely efficient. (As spoken: "high return on equity, high return on equity capital".)
4. A strong balance sheet with a low, conservative debt structure and high levels of cash.
5. Strong growth catalysts and drivers.

Meeting all five is very rare; less than 1% of businesses in the market meet these five criteria. When they do, it is highly likely a great business that will keep growing in value. American Airlines fails these criteria. Microsoft, Nvidia, Apple and MasterCard meet them. On the Microsoft chart, the intrinsic value keeps going up.

#### [14:53] Why price deviates from value (pendulum and the dog on a leash)
Although the business goes up in value, the share price does not go up in a straight line and does not always reflect the value, because the share price is determined by demand and supply in an auction-driven market. In the short term the market price is not driven by logic all the time; it's driven by news and emotions. So at times the price goes below intrinsic value (undervalued); at times of good news, euphoria and excitement it goes way above (a bit overvalued); then bad news and pessimism and it is undervalued again. It's like a pendulum from overvalued to undervalued, and the pattern keeps repeating. When the price gets very far from intrinsic value we have mispricing, and taking advantage of mispricing is how we get really rich.

Visa as another example: a great business, because over time it keeps increasing sales, profits and cash flow, so its intrinsic value keeps going up. But the short-term share price, driven by news, emotions and manipulation, gets mispriced every so often: undervalued, overvalued, over and over.

Analogy he read: a man walks in a straight line from his house to his office. He has a dog on a pretty long leash, and as he walks the dog runs all over the park, excited, sometimes all the way right, sometimes all the way left; you have no idea where it is darting (a cat, a tree). That's the market: in the short term it is very random, you have no idea if it goes up or down tomorrow. But eventually the dog meets back with the man when he reaches his office, because the dog is on that leash.

#### [17:48] Step 2 in practice: wait, then buy in tranches
Step two is to patiently wait for the stock price to go below the intrinsic value (the green line). Lesson five covers how to value stocks; different businesses are valued a bit differently. Our job is to wait for the price to go below the green line, and that is when we start to add, ideally at support levels, which lesson six (technical analysis) teaches you to draw.

As mentioned in lesson one, do we have to buy right at the bottom of every correction or wave down? "I would love to, but no one can predict the exact bottom." As long as we buy below the intrinsic value, that's good enough. He never buys all at one go; he always buys in three to four or even five tranches. Once the price goes below intrinsic value by a certain margin of safety, say by about 10, 15%, he starts to add some shares at the support level. If it goes lower, say 20% below intrinsic value, he adds even more, and if it goes even lower, even more. He slowly averages into the position, like dollar cost averaging except that we add at support levels: a bit here, a bit here, a bit here. The average cost may be somewhere in the middle; it is not at the bottom and doesn't have to be, because as long as you buy below intrinsic value, over time the price will get back above it, plus the intrinsic value itself is increasing. That is how you see growth in the value of your shares. As Warren Buffett said many years ago, the time to be greedy is when others are fearful, and the time to be fearful is when others are greedy.

#### [19:51] Why would a great business sell at a discount?
Same reason you sometimes find a great apartment at a discount: the owner is desperate to sell because he's in trouble. In the stock market many people at times are desperate to sell: panicking because of emotions, or forced to sell by their broker because they were on margin. "We don't care" why they are selling at a discount; we just take advantage of it. Common reasons a stock sells below intrinsic value:

1. Overall stock market correction. On average once a year the whole market drops by at least 10%. There is always a reason to trigger the drop, which we can never predict: a war, a disaster, geopolitical tensions, tariffs, weak economic numbers. Whatever it is, the market drops on average 10% once a year, and most companies, good and bad, go down together. Those are the times to buy good companies that are going down, because the drop has nothing to do with the company; it's overall market sentiment.

2. Bear market. A correction happens more frequently and is when the market drops more than 10% but less than 20%. A bear market is a drop of more than 20% from the high, usually because of a recession, economic crisis or financial crisis. Can never predict when; looking at the last hundred years it happens on average once every six years, though not a perfect occurrence: from 2009 to 2019, for 10 years, there was no bear market, but 2020 had one and 2022 had another. Sometimes they come together, sometimes far apart. In a bear market everyone panics and good and bad companies fall 20, 30, 40%, and you buy a great company at a discount. "I love bear markets. I love it. After every bear market my portfolio grows exponentially because I get to add shares at huge discounts."

3. The stock is in a sector or industry that is unloved, or "out of rotation". (Recall from lesson two there are 11 sectors: technology, financial, healthcare, etc.; within a sector like technology there are industries like semiconductor, hardware, software.) Sectors take turns to be loved and unloved by investors. When a sector is loved, institutional investors put money into it, say financials, and we say the financial sector is in rotation; most or all stocks in it go up, the good, the bad, the ugly, like a rising tide lifting all boats. Money going into financials has to come from somewhere, maybe healthcare, so healthcare is out of rotation, unloved, and all healthcare stocks drop. Rotation keeps happening and is almost impossible to predict, so his job is not to predict it but to take advantage of it.

   Example: in 2024, especially, and in fact 2023, the whole healthcare sector was out of rotation, with money flowing out for various reasons. Number one was politics: usually in an election year money flows out of healthcare because of uncertainty over new regulations. Another was COVID, which caused many people to delay non-essential surgeries; after COVID they all went for surgeries, which increased insurers' claims costs, so costs went up and profit went down. In 2024 healthcare stocks got very, very cheap and he bought them when unloved and cheap, and later when people get excited about healthcare again and money flows back, you get 20, 30, 40% returns. In the long run will healthcare do well? Yes: everyone needs healthcare, it is a recession-proof sector, and with an aging population it is a long-term growth driver.

   Second example: in 2024 consumer staples were unloved. These are companies selling low-growth essential goods, like Coca-Cola, Pepsi, Procter & Gamble, Colgate: very low growth essential items, very defensive, recession-proof. When the economy is doing very well people don't want these boring companies and sell them, so they tend to drop during good economic growth, and when they are cheap he buys. In 2024 he bought a lot of consumer staple stocks (Pepsi, for example) and a lot of healthcare stocks. Whenever there is a fear of recession (timing unknown), everyone gets scared, buys healthcare and consumer staples again, and they fly. He takes advantage of this rotation.

### 26 - Part 2: The Three Steps to Successful Investing (Lesson 3, Part 2)

#### [00:00] Reasons 4 and 5: company-specific short-term bad news
The first three reasons a stock price goes down have nothing to do with the company itself; it's the economy or the sector, and the stock is just collateral damage in the short term. The fourth reason is that the company itself has been hit by some kind of short-term bad news. When you buy a great business that meets the five criteria he will teach, 99.9% of the time any bad news that hits the company is short-term; eventually they sort it out and the company does very well again. Examples of short-term problems:

- Lawsuits. Great companies get sued all the time: by consumers, by investors (class action when the price drops), and by the government (e.g. the Department of Justice wanting to sue for being a monopoly). Every time a great company gets sued and the share price drops on the bad news, he is buying, because it's always temporary and you rebound very fast. 2024 example: Google, Apple and Visa were sued by the US Department of Justice in 2024. In fact Google lost, causing a temporary drop that made it undervalued and a great time to buy; by the end of the year all three were back at all-time highs.
  - Apple: in March of 2024 the Justice Department sued, allegedly for illegally monopolizing the smartphone market. The shares dropped about 10%, and by the end of the year were back at an all-time high.
  - Google: on 5 August, Google lost its landmark US antitrust case over search dominance (sued for being a monopoly). The share price plunged, and by the end of the year (in fact the following year) it was back at all-time highs.
  - Visa: on 24 September the DOJ sued Visa over a debit card market monopoly; the share price dropped, was undervalued very briefly, then all-time highs again.
  When a company is hit by bad news it is a great time to buy, but it must be a great business.

- Warren Buffett and American Express (1963). In 1963 he was looking at American Express, then not yet listed on the stock market, sold over the counter as a pink sheet. He wanted to buy; it was a great business but too expensive at about $60 per share, so he patiently waited for bad news. In 1963 came the salad oil scandal (he won't go into detail; Google it): American Express was sued for negligence and lost, I think, like $30, $50 million because of the lawsuit, causing a short-term drop in profits. Buffett realized they lost money in the short term but the brand was still very strong: every time you think of a credit card or of traveling you think American Express, and the famous advertisement said "don't leave home without it". The scandal caused the share price to drop by about 50%, from $60 to $30, and Buffett pounced. Once the scandal was resolved the price went back to $60 and a lot higher. He is still holding the shares, not sold since 1964 when he bought, and so far has made like over $20 billion from just one great business.

- Earnings misses. A great company has good years and bad years, good and bad quarters; sometimes it misses expectations (earnings or revenue lower than expected). It happens to all great companies. People panic and sell, the price drops 10, 20, 30%, a great time to buy because eventually it rebounds strongly.

- Cyclical slowdowns. Even a business in a long-term growth industry has them. Example: digital advertising is a long-term growth industry; you can bet that five, ten years from now more companies will spend more on online advertising, and the biggest players are Google, Amazon and Meta (Meta owns Facebook, Instagram and WhatsApp). In the long run ad revenue grows, but not every year or month.
