In 2022 there was a short-term cyclical slowdown in advertising spend; Google and Meta revenue slowed and the share price dropped, I think, 50 or 20% [earlier pass has roughly 15-20%; raw transcript supports NEW]. A great time to buy and he bought a lot more in 2022; since then it has rebounded significantly as advertising spend re-accelerated. Same with cybersecurity: in the long run more companies will spend on cybersecurity systems. His favorites are Palo Alto and Fortinet. In 2022 and early 2023 there was a short-term slowdown in cybersecurity spending, and Palo Alto and Fortinet's share prices dropped again 10, 20, 30%, way below intrinsic value, and he added a lot of shares; today it has made him very nice gains.

- Other bad news: product recalls (usually auto companies; he doesn't buy auto companies, reasons explained later), and diseases. Many years ago there was mad cow disease and no one dared eat beef; McDonald's, which sells primarily beef, suffered, profits dropped and the share price dropped 30%. He said let's buy McDonald's, undervalued, because he knew it was a short-term problem: the cow won't be mad forever, one day the cow will be happy again, people eat beef again. Sure enough McDonald's went back to all-time highs.

#### [07:50] Reason 5: even great companies make stupid mistakes
Sometimes even great companies make stupid mistakes that hit short-term profits, but their competitive advantage, brand and economies of scale (their economic moat) remain intact. American Express in 1963: they made the mistake of certifying salad oil held by a commodities trader who falsified the records, so they were sued for negligence and the share price dropped 50%. But Buffett realized the brand was still very strong: people use American Express credit cards and travelers checks every day. He bought at half price and made billions. Mistakes happen all the time and we take advantage.

A more recent example is Nike, a great brand with the biggest marketing budget among athletic footwear companies. In 2020, was it 2020? sorry, 2019, they hired a new CEO who made very bad management decisions. To cut a long story short, he cut out all the wholesalers, wanting to sell direct to consumers, a bad mistake. He killed a lot of the innovation: instead of a football department and a running department building the most innovative shoes, he reorganized Nike into only men's, women's and children's departments. Innovation fell, they lost shelf space, and for the first time revenue growth decelerated and profit decelerated, and the share price dropped 40%. That was when he accumulated some shares, thinking the brand is still very strong with a long runway. They fired that CEO, got a new one to repair everything, and it is on the path of recovery. At the time of recording, Nike shares have not yet rebounded to prior glory; it is in the process of rebounding.

#### [10:00] Chart walk-through: Alphabet (GOOGL)
Examples of great companies hit by bad news, dropping below intrinsic value, when you could add shares. Alphabet, symbol GOOGL, better known as Google (two share classes, GOOGL and GOOG; no difference, you can buy either; the only difference is that the L has voting rights). Over a two-year period, it's a great business with revenue and profits growing year after year, so intrinsic value keeps going up, but the share price goes overvalued, undervalued, up and down like a crazy yo-yo because of news:
- February 2023: shares dropped and became very undervalued because their new AI chatbot made a mistake. People panicked. Once the bad news was over people realized Google is still a great company, "I can't live without Google", and it went up again.
- 24 October 2023: Google reported earnings not as good as expected (cloud business revenue missed expectations, profit missed), a sharp drop, then back to new highs.
- March 2024: not because of Google; the entire market dropped on fears of inflation coming back. The Dow Jones index dropped 475 points ("400 and 75"), the S&P 500 had its worst day since January. When the whole market goes down all stocks go down, good, bad and ugly. Google became really undervalued again and then went back to all-time highs.
- September 2024: reported that Google lost its fight against a 2.7 billion EU antitrust fine (fined $2.7 billion). The share price was really undervalued and he added more shares; it rebounded.
- October 2024: Google faces US government attempt to break it up. People panicked, but then it went up again. As he speaks, Google is near the intrinsic value, slightly below it. Be patient and you will get a chance to get a bite.

#### [13:31] Chart walk-through: Johnson & Johnson
Years ago he owned J&J, before they spun off their consumer health division, when it was a very diversified healthcare company. He doesn't own it anymore since the spin-off left a pure pharma stock, which he doesn't invest in. Year after year revenue and profit were growing, so intrinsic value kept growing, but the share price went up and down on bad news:
- It dropped quite a bit, got really undervalued, and he was adding more shares because they failed to overturn a $2.1 billion (as spoken "$2.1.2 billion") verdict in a Supreme Court appeal. J&J were sued over baby powder having some carcinogen / cancer-causing materials, lost, and had to pay $2 billion in fines. That caused a short-term profit drop, but it's still a great business making more money every year, strong brand, great drugs, and sure enough the price went up again.
- 23 February: faced another 25,000 baby powder lawsuits, set aside $4 billion in litigation expenses (lawyers fees); the share price dropped ("we're going to die!") and went up again.
- Supreme Court rejects J&J's appeal against the $2 billion penalty in the baby powder case; drops again, goes up again.

Warning: as shown in all these examples, we only do this on great businesses, high-quality companies. Do not do this on low-quality companies: companies that don't make profits, or whose profits are inconsistent, or whose revenue, profits and cash flow are not growing consistently. For those, when bad news drops the share price, it may never come back. "This is only for great businesses. Please remember that."

#### [16:01] Final step: when to sell and take profits
If you did a good job buying a great business that keeps growing, there's no reason to sell unless you need the money to buy something (a new car, a new house, a new wife or husband); if not, stay invested. Having said that, there are times he sells. Exit rules are covered in great detail in lesson nine; here is the broad concept.

1. Long-term fundamentals / competitive advantage have deteriorated. Examples of stocks he sold: Estee Lauder and Disney, which he used to own. They were great businesses but he doesn't think they are great anymore because they've lost competitive advantage; the long-term moat is not as strong as it once was. He sold and reinvested in a company with a very strong moat.
2. He found another business that is even better/greater; he swaps (sells this, reinvests in that).
3. The share price is overvalued, way above intrinsic value. How much is "way above" (20%? 30%?) depends on the kind of stock.
   - Mid to lower growth stocks: very predictable but not high growth, e.g. Pepsi, Procter & Gamble, McDonald's, Yum Brands, Lockheed Martin. Great companies that grow at maybe 10% or 12%. For these, the moment the price gets about 20% or more above intrinsic value he may start to sell some, or even all.
   - McDonald's example: intrinsic value at the time of recording is $273. He bought shares somewhere on the chart and also added shares at about 252, a pretty good discount. After he bought, the price went up like crazy, a parabolic move (it goes up 90 degrees; more in the technical analysis lesson six). Once it went up like 90 degrees he felt it was overextended: like a rubber band, once it goes up too much it tends to snap back. At 300 bucks it was about 15, 20% overvalued, and he needed cash to buy something else he wanted, so he sold and bought something cheaper. He didn't sell right at the top, he sold near the top. After he sold, it came back down. Has he bought it back? No, because although it dropped it's still above his valuation. If it drops back to maybe 265 or 259, he'll add it back. So for lower growth stocks: buy when undervalued, sell when quite overvalued, then buy back or buy something else.
   - High growth companies: Nvidia, Palantir, Amazon, Meta, Microsoft, Salesforce, which make up the bulk of his portfolio. In the past, many years ago, he bought them undervalued and sold when overvalued, and that was one of the biggest mistakes he made, because after selling they went down a bit but before he could buy back they zoomed even higher and he missed a lot of returns. With Apple and Microsoft, bought over 15, 16, 18 years ago, he sold when overvalued and bought back, jumping in and out many times, and made okay money; but looking back, if he had just bought and stayed invested through the ups and downs, today he would have compounded his wealth probably five to ten times more. A lesson learned as he became more experienced. Today, when he buys a great company like Nvidia, Palantir or Microsoft, even if it gets overvalued he won't sell, because though short-term overvalued and maybe coming down a bit, the long-run intrinsic value will continue to compound; it makes more sense to stay invested. But if it gets crazy overvalued, with the price more than 100% above intrinsic value, he may sell part of it, especially if it takes too big an allocation in his portfolio. If it's kind of 30, 40, 50% overvalued for a high growth company, he stays invested and doesn't sell. More in lesson nine under portfolio management and exit rules.

#### [22:46] Wrap-up
That concludes lesson three, giving a broad concept of what investing is about. Next lesson dives deeper into analyzing businesses: how to read a business inside out to know if it is a great, mediocre or lousy business. Lesson four: fundamental analysis.

### 28 - Introduction (Lesson 4: Fundamental Analysis)

#### [00:00] Why fundamental analysis matters
Lesson 4 is going to be one of the most important lessons in the course. Fundamental analysis is the art of learning how to analyze a business: how to read a business inside out to know whether it is a good, mediocre or lousy business. 70% of your investment decisions is really based on the fundamentals of the business. When you buy a stock you are not buying a ticker that just goes up and down; you are buying a piece of an underlying business, and if the business does well, the stock will do well over time.

Using fundamental analysis you learn to identify great businesses. When you buy the stock of a great business, you have no worries even if the price goes down temporarily because of emotions and manipulation, 20, 30, 40%. You don't sweat it, because eventually it is going to bounce back a lot higher; in fact you use the temporary drop to add more shares. If you buy a lousy or average business and the price goes down, you have reasons to panic because you don't know if it will come back. Fundamental analysis is not just about identifying and buying great companies, but more importantly about identifying lousy companies and avoiding them. The main reason investors lose money is that they buy stocks of lousy businesses: in the short term the price goes up on speculation or hype, they get seduced and drawn in, and when the bubble bursts the price crashes and never comes back because the underlying business is not great. That's why fundamentals is the most important skill in investing.

He keeps reinforcing to students that an investor's job is not to predict where the market will go next week, next month or next year, because it is impossible to predict short-term price movements with certainty. The job is to invest in a portfolio of great businesses that will thrive and grow under all economic conditions. We can't predict future interest rates, inflation or GDP, but we don't have to: with a portfolio of great businesses, rain or shine, recession or no recession, inflation high or low, interest rates high or low, the businesses will grow consistently and grow our portfolio and wealth over time.

#### [03:11] Horror stories: Noble Group, Hyflux, China Evergrande
You've read horror stories of investors losing all their money because a stock went to zero or collapsed and never came back. In Singapore a very well-known collapse was Noble Group, a blue chip company listed on the Singapore Stock Exchange and part of the Straits Times Index, one of the biggest companies in Singapore and in Asia. It collapsed, people said they were cheated because all their money was gone, and many analysts had in fact recommended the stock. Another is Hyflux, another blue chip company, a water treatment company in Singapore that collapsed; share investors and bondholders lost most of their money. And China Evergrande, one of the biggest real estate developers in China, again a blue chip, on the verge of collapsing, defaulting on its debt. Blue chip companies are not necessarily safe: "blue chip can get blue black if you invest in the wrong ones", so don't be deceived by the word blue chip.

How to avoid such companies? Very simple. He has never invested in any of these, because he studies the financial statements and does a fundamental deep-dive analysis. Once you learn what he teaches and apply the principles, you would never ever invest in such companies; all of them failed his fundamental criteria. It's not just for finding great companies but more importantly for avoiding lousy businesses whose stocks can crash and never come back, whether you are a shareholder or a bondholder.

#### [05:18] The three financial statements
Companies report results four times a year: a quarterly report each quarter, and an annual report at the end of every financial year. They report three things:
1. The income statement: how much money they made, how much they spent, and what the profit is at the end of the day.
2. The balance sheet: how financially strong the business is, assets minus liabilities, and what the shareholders' equity is.
3. The cash flow statement, the most important: how much cash they generated from the business, how much cash they paid out, and how much they borrowed or paid back.

You don't have to read the quarterly and annual reports directly, although you can download them from the company's website, because there are great financial websites that already compile all this data and calculate many of the ratios we need. He will show his favorite websites and how to know when a company will next announce results.

#### [06:51] Earnings dates and why they don't matter much
Most charting software indicates when a stock next announces earnings. Example in TradingView: put in Apple, and you see an "E" icon; click it and it says earnings will be announced on Thursday 30 January 2025. As he records it is 28 January, so in two days. They announce either before the market opens ("BMO") or after the market closes; here after the close at 4 p.m. Eastern time. Looking at previous announcements: in one quarter earnings were worse than expected (analysts expected $1.60 earnings per share, they reported 97 cents, 39% below expectations) but revenue was above expectations by 0.44%. The stock dropped a bit, but it doesn't matter because eventually it goes up again. In another, earnings and revenue were better than expected and interestingly the stock price initially dropped, then went up again.

Should you be concerned about buying before or after earnings are released? "It is totally unimportant." A great business will have good quarters and bad quarters, maybe good years and bad years; no great company has a good quarter every quarter. Don't be concerned that this quarter the company beat earnings so the stock jumped or missed so it gapped down. In the long run, as long as the company grows revenue, profits and cash flow from operations with an upward trend over the years (we're looking at years), it's a great business. Sometimes the market is illogical: a company beats earnings and revenue yet the price drops; or announces bad results and the price goes up. In the short term the market is often manipulated. When a company announces earnings, anything can happen, up or gap down; it's a coin flip. Don't be concerned about the short-term quarterly reports.

Once you've invested in a company, it is useful to look at the quarterly financial statements when they announce, and read the CEO conference call transcript, to make sure the company is following its plan, that whatever revenue and profits they projected they are more or less delivering. But don't be too concerned about short-term hits and misses; we're looking at the long term.

A great business doesn't change overnight into a lousy one. Does a great business become a lousy business? Yes, it has happened: Intel was a great business with a strong economic moat, but over time it lost its competitive advantage to Nvidia and AMD. Boeing was a great business, but because of a lot of management missteps it lost a lot of its reputation. This does not happen in one quarter or one year; it takes years for a great company to mess up and deteriorate. Look at the long-term trends, the year-on-year growth of revenue, profits and cash flow.

#### [11:50] Where to find data: Yahoo Finance earnings calendar
Another site for earnings dates is Yahoo Finance, finance.yahoo.com/calendar/earnings, which shows which companies are announcing earnings for a certain period. For your own company, e.g. Microsoft, type MSFT and search; as shown, the next earnings will be announced on the 25th of April 2022.

#### [12:19] Stock Oracle walk-through
Two main websites for financial statements. The first is Stock Oracle, which presents the information in a very easy to understand way. Put the ticker symbol in the top right, e.g. Apple, ticker AAPL. The first thing you notice is the next earnings date: next reported on 30 April 2026 (he says he is recording in February; the transcript says 2016, apparently a mis-transcription of 2026). Go to Financials. It shows the information visually, as a line chart or bar chart. In the bar chart you see sales revenue going up nicely, operating income (orange) going up nicely, net income going up nicely. On the right is cash flow data (covered in detail later): operating cash flow in orange going up nicely, free cash flow in dark green going up nicely. A free basic account gives five-year data; a paid account gives 10-year data. Scroll down for the income statement, balance sheet and cash flow statement (statement of cash flows); the next part goes through what he looks at in each. Free version shows the last five years (2021 to 2025, shown on screen); paid goes to 10 years, and you can look at annual or quarterly results (every three months). Stock Oracle covers all US-listed stocks. Non-US listings (India, South Korea, Malaysia, Singapore, China) are not yet available, but he says by the end of 2016 [as spoken; likely 2026] Singapore and China, Hong Kong listed stocks will be on Stock Oracle; for now not.

#### [15:40] Yahoo Finance walk-through
An alternative for non-US stocks is Yahoo Finance. Example: DBS Bank, symbol D05. Click Financials: same thing, an income statement, balance sheet and statement of cash flows, exactly the same. The free version, similar to Stock Oracle, gives about four years of data plus the last 12 months, annual or quarterly.

Pay attention to differences. On Yahoo Finance the data is arranged from right to left (years in sequence 22, 23, 24, 25 as displayed) and numbers are reported in thousands. On Stock Oracle, and in fact most other websites, the data is presented in millions, so be aware of the denomination. Also on Stock Oracle the data is arranged left to right (2020, 22, 23, 24, 25 and the last 12 months, as spoken). In the next part of the video: what to read in the income statement, balance sheet and statement of cash flows.

### 29 - Income Statement (Lesson 4)

#### [00:00] What the income statement tells you
There are three parts to the financial statements; first is the income statement, which tells you how good the company is at making money: how much sales it generates, what all its costs and expenses are, and what profit it earns for shareholders at the end of the day. This is not an accounting course, so he won't go through every line item, only what is most important to focus on. First a very simple example on the drawing board.

#### [00:53] Simple example income statement
- Sales revenue (also known as revenue): the first thing to look at in any business. Imagine a company selling shoes at $500 each, selling 1,000 shoes a year: total sales $500,000.
- Less cost of making the sales, COGS (cost of goods sold): say it costs $250 to make each shoe, times 1,000 shoes = $250,000.
- Sales minus cost of making the shoes = gross profit: $250,000.
- Is that the profit the company makes? No, because you must deduct operating expenses (OPEX), also known as overheads, the cost of running the business: staff salary, rental, utilities, depreciation, R&D (research and development), marketing costs. Depreciation is a non-cash expense: cash doesn't really go out. For example you bought equipment five years ago and the cash went out five years ago, but you depreciate the equipment over 10 years, so every year you deduct a certain amount, non-cash. Say all these expenses are $100,000.
- Gross profit minus operating expenses = operating income: $250,000 minus $100,000 = $150,000. This is the profit the company makes from its core business, an important thing to look at.
- Non-operating expenses: not really related to the business but still a cost, e.g. the company got sued and has to pay the lawyer (lawyer fees), taxes (also non-operating expenses), restructuring costs, and interest payments on borrowed money. Say these cost another 50 grand.
- Other income (not operating): extra income not from the core business, e.g. dividends from a company it invested in, or selling a property or asset for a capital gain. Say other income of 60 grand.
- Net income / net profit after tax: $150,000 minus $50,000 plus $60,000 = $160,000.

#### [06:16] What to look at: revenue, net profit, operating income
Is this company good at making money, is it very profitable? First, sales revenue: it all depends on whether revenue is growing. Look at at least the last five years and make sure sales revenue is increasing over time. Next the net profit, also increasing over the last five years. It's all about "is it increasing?"

Sometimes sales increase but profit doesn't; profits could suddenly drop, and sometimes not because the business is doing poorly but because of non-operating expenses: a big tax deal, the company got sued, restructuring. Many times non-operating expenses skew or distort the profits. Or the company has a special profit/income from a good investment. These are one-off gains or losses that are not recurring and not based on the core business. If net profit is not consistent because of this, that's okay, as long as operating income is increasing consistently. So we want to see operating income increasing consistently; if net profit is not increasing consistently, it's okay as long as operating income is.

#### [08:23] Profit margins
Two profit margins: gross profit margin and net profit margin.
- Gross profit margin = gross profit divided by sales revenue, as a percentage: $250,000 / $500,000 = 50%. For every $1 in sales the company keeps 50 cents to pay its other expenses. Is 50% good or bad? It's comparative: compare to the closest competitors. If competitors have higher gross margins, not very good; if this company has higher gross margins than the rest, good. Also look at the gross margin over the last five years: increasing or consistent is good; falling over the last five years is a bad sign, meaning increasing cost of making the products.
- Net profit margin = net profit after tax divided by sales revenue: $160,000 / $500,000 = 32%. For every $1 in sales the company keeps $0.32 in net profit, which it can use to pay off debt, pay dividends, buy back shares, or reinvest. Compare to competitors: want the same or higher net profit margins than the closest competition. Over the last five years, want it increasing or consistent; falling net profit margins is a bad sign.

#### [11:44] Reading Apple's income statement on Stock Oracle
Put the stock (Apple, AAPL) in the top right, go to Financials and scroll all the way down to the income statement, balance sheet and statement of cash flow. For the income statement and cash flow statement, always look at the annual figure (how much money the company made in one year), and compare annual figures of revenue, profits and costs. Later for the balance sheet you want the latest asset, liability and equity, using the quarterly statements for the latest snapshot in time.

Figures on Stock Oracle (and most websites except Yahoo Finance) are in millions of US dollars. E.g. Apple sales revenue in the last 12 months shows 435,617. That is 435,617 million; since 1,000 million is 1 billion, that is $435.617 billion.

Last financial year: not all companies end their year in December; Apple's ends in September, when they have the annual report. The last column, TTM, means trailing/last 12 months: last 12 months of revenue, operating income, etc. The last 12 months may or may not equal the last financial year; it's basically the last four quarters. You can check: click quarterly, add up the four most recent quarters and you get the trailing 12 months.

Reading it as in the illustration:
- Sales revenue for the last financial year: $416.1 billion.
- Less cost of goods sold (cost of making the computers, phones): 220,000 [million, as spoken "220,000"]; revenue minus this = gross profit of $195,201 [million].
- Less operating expenses, total $62.151 billion. Operating expenses are made up of selling and general administrative expenses of $27.6 billion and R&D of $34.55 billion. Gross profit minus operating expenses gives operating income (value shown on screen for that year; for the year he discusses later it is $114 billion).
- Interest: if the company borrows, it pays interest on debt, but it may also have a lot of cash on which it earns interest. For Apple the figure is not shown in the last two financial years (sometimes they don't show it and take it as zero). Looking at the year 2023: Apple takes on debt as well for taxation reasons; interest paid on debt is 3.9 billion; interest income from the bank is 3.75 billion. Net net, they still pay out a bit of interest: 183 million in net interest expense, considered very low for a company.
- Operating income $114 billion, less $183 million net interest expense, and less $382 million from other non-operating income/expenses. Non-operating income could be income from investments; non-operating expenses could be restructuring or legal fees. These are not from the core business. The result is earnings before taxes: $130.736 billion for this particular year (as spoken; he does not reconcile it with the $114 billion figure) [earlier pass has $113.736 billion; raw transcript supports NEW].
- Less income tax expense gives net income: $96.95 billion [earlier pass has $96.995 billion].

#### [19:40] The only three lines you need
You don't have to go through every line item. The three to pay attention to:
1. Revenue (sales revenue).
2. Operating income.
3. Net income after tax.
Other line items are not so important unless you're digging for information. Ensure revenue is growing consistently and net income is growing consistently; if net income isn't growing consistently because non-operating income and expenses distort it, just look at operating income and ensure it is increasing consistently.

#### [20:31] Apple's trend
Look at the last five years, ten years; the further back the better. Starting with 2020 (revenue, annual): 274 billion, 365 billion, 394 billion, a slight drop to 383 billion, then increase to 391 billion, then 416 billion, and last 12 months 435.617 billion. Is it increasing consistently? Yes, pretty consistent. Increasing consistently doesn't mean it must go up every single year; one or two years of dropping is fine as long as the general trend is upwards.

Operating income: 65 billion, 108 billion, 119 billion, 114 billion (slight drop), 123 billion, then 113, 113, and last 12 months about 141 billion (as spoken "one for one") [filled from earlier pass]. Besides the small drop, generally an upward trend; we want to make sure it grows over time.

Net income, which can be distorted by non-operating items: 57 billion, 94 billion, 99 billion, 96 billion (slight dip), 93 billion (so two years of dipping), then 112, 117. Generally an uptrend. Easiest way is the chart on Stock Oracle: line chart or bar chart, set to annual, look visually: revenue on an uptrend? Net income in green generally on an uptrend? If not, look at operating income in orange.

#### [23:29] Summary of what to look at, and speculative growth companies
Summary: (1) ensure sales revenue is growing consistently for at least the last five years, even better 10 years; (2) ensure net profit (net income after tax) is also growing consistently for the last five years.

Some will say, Adam, there are new companies like Roku or Palantir, or Airbnb (the earlier pass also lists Sea, spoken "C") [from earlier pass], whose revenues are growing but are not profitable yet; would you invest? Sometimes. These are speculative growth companies, which are more risky because they're not profitable yet; you believe they could be profitable in the future and may want to buy before they are. They usually have negative income or profits during initial growth years. He does buy them once in a while, but usually a very small allocation, and when he does he wants to make sure that although profit isn't positive yet, the company already has positive free cash flow (more later). Example: Palantir, a new growth company, revenues growing, not profitable yet (as he describes it), but free cash flow has already turned positive. That is one of his requirements.

#### [25:00] One-off distortions in net income
If revenue is growing and net income is increasing but recently had a big drop, ask why: is the business not doing well, or non-business-related costs like taxation, a merger or a lawsuit? If non-business-related, it's okay because it's not recurring, a one-off expense. Sometimes net profit suddenly jumps, not because the business is doing very well but because the company happened to make a gain on an investment: invested in another company and sold it for a big profit, or bought a building and sold it for a big profit. These are non-recurring income (happens once, will not recur) or non-recurring costs, not part of the core business, exceptional items we can disregard when analyzing the business. If net income is inconsistent because of that, it's okay as long as operating income is increasing consistently.

Operating income reports the profit from the business's ongoing operations, and is very useful because it doesn't include one-off non-operating items that may skew the profit in a given year. Examples: Pepsi's profits go up but in one or two years had a big drop; nothing to do with the business doing well, but taxation changes or forex losses/gains from cross-border transactions; ignore because it won't happen every year. Salesforce recently spent a lot of money acquiring other companies; because of the acquisition there was a big cost and profit had a big drop last year. These are non-operating expenses; disregard them in analyzing business performance. Operating income does not include non-operating expenses like taxation, interest costs, lawsuit costs, restructuring costs, inventory write-off; nor non-operating income like dividends, capital gain on investments, interest income and forex gains. None relate to the core business.

#### [29:00] Profit margin trends on Stock Oracle
He also checks gross profit margin and net profit margin, wanting them consistent or increasing over time, not decreasing. Best place is Stock Oracle: go to Company Metrics and scroll down (the free basic account has it too, he believes with the last five years of data). You'll see gross profit margin and net profit margin, and also operating profit margin. Net profit margin may be inconsistent because it's skewed by non-operating items; if so, no worry, look at operating profit margin instead. As long as gross profit margin and operating margin are both consistent or growing, that's fine.

Apple, last five years (ignoring the current-year column): gross profit margins 43%, 44, 46, 46.9, 47: increasing, they are getting more and more profitable, which is what we want. The current gross margins are higher than the 10-year average, a good sign. Operating margins: 30.29%, 29.82 (slight drop, no need to panic), 31.51, 31.97, 32: generally rising over the last five years and higher than the 10-year average. Net profit margin: 25.31, 25.31, 23 (dropped a bit), then 26, 27. "Everything looks fantastic." The visual charts (he believes need the paid account, scroll up): gross profit margins in blue rising, operating margins rising, net profit margins rising too. That's all you need to know for the income statement; next part is the balance sheet.

---

# Part 5 — Lessons 30–32

### 30 - Balance Sheet

#### [00:00] Income statement vs balance sheet, and the three parts
The income statement tells us how profitable the business is, how good it is at making money. The balance sheet tells us how financially strong the business is. It has three main parts: the company's assets (what the company owns, split into current and long-term assets), the liabilities (what it owes, again current and long-term), and the shareholders' equity. Assets minus liabilities gives you the shareholders' equity: what the company owns, minus what it owes, is what is left behind for the shareholders, which is you and me. He will not go into every detail, only the major parts.

#### [01:21] Period of time vs point in time
The income statement covers a period of time (how much money the company made over a year), and we compare this year to last year to the previous year, periods to periods. The balance sheet is very different: it is a snapshot at a point in time, e.g. how much cash the company has right now and how much debt it has. Because it is a point in time, we always want the latest point in time, so for the balance sheet we always look at the company's latest quarterly report, not the annual report.

#### [02:16] Assets
Assets are what the company owns: current assets (short-term assets) and non-current assets (long-term assets). Short-term assets are things the company owns that it can easily convert to cash within three months; long-term assets can't be sold or converted to cash that easily.

Current assets include: cash and equivalents (cash in the bank, plus cash equivalents such as short-term bonds, commercial paper and fixed deposits that can be converted to cash within three months); short-term investments the company has made that it can again sell and convert to cash easily; inventory; and accounts receivables. Later he will focus more on cash and equivalents and short-term investments in the valuation of the business and in calculating how safe the business is.

Non-current assets are assets the company can't convert to cash easily. Physical assets (things you can touch): real estate, machinery, plant, equipment. Intangible assets: goodwill, patents, the brand.

#### [04:32] Liabilities
Liabilities are what the company owes: current (short-term) and non-current (long-term).

Current liabilities are things the company has to pay within three months. Accounts payable: the company owes suppliers money, or owes employees salary it has not paid yet; it has to pay within three months or may get sued, for example. Taxes payable: taxes not yet paid. But the most important item he will focus on is short-term debt: debt the company has borrowed and has to pay back within three months. He will use this a lot in calculations later.

Long-term liabilities have different items, but the most important is long-term debt. The two most common forms: (1) term loans the company has taken, and (2) bonds the company has issued (corporate bonds). They have to pay back this debt over the long run, more than three months, and in the meanwhile pay interest on it. This is something to really focus on in calculating financial stability and valuation. There are many other line items, but these are the main ones.

#### [07:18] Shareholders' equity
Total assets minus total liabilities gives the shareholders' equity. It has three main parts.

1. Common stock and paid-up capital: the money the company raised when it first sold its shares, usually in the IPO (initial public offering). Whatever money it gets from those shares goes into common stock and paid-up capital. Is that important? Not really.
2. Retained earnings: this is more important. Over the years, as the company makes profits, if it doesn't pay them out as dividends it retains the cash, recorded as retained earnings. A good business is one where you see a lot of retained earnings accumulated over the years: accumulated savings from the profits. All of this belongs to the shareholders.
3. Treasury stock: when a company makes profits it can pay dividends, reinvest, keep it as retained earnings, or buy back shares. When it does a share buyback there are fewer shares in the market, so each share becomes worth more. Generally that is good for shareholders. It is only good if the company buys back shares when the shares are cheap and when the company is already profitable. Bought-back shares show as treasury stock, which is always a negative and reduces shareholders' equity. Is that bad? Actually it is good: with less equity the company can make profits using less equity, and you share with fewer shareholders, so each share is worth more.

#### [10:19] How to tell if the company is safe: ratios
The key question is whether the company is safe to invest in, whether it will go bankrupt, whether it is financially strong. To answer that we divide certain things by certain things. One example: compare current assets to current liabilities; you want current assets to be more than current liabilities. That is the current ratio, covered in a while.

Another: total debt = short-term debt plus long-term debt. How much debt is too much depends on how much money the company earns. If the company earns a billion dollars a year and has a billion in debt, that is okay; it can pay back all the debt in one year. If it makes a billion in profits but has 20 billion in debt, it will take 20 years to pay back, which is too much debt. So how much is too high depends on how much the company makes; the comparisons come later.

#### [11:27] Real balance sheet: Pepsi (PEP)
On Stock Oracle (or Yahoo Finance financial statements), put in ticker PEP, go to financials, then balance sheet. Because it's a point in time, choose the quarterly report, and check the units (here millions of US dollars).

Total assets in the latest quarter are 107,399 (millions of dollars). Total liabilities are 86,852. Total assets minus total liabilities gives total shareholders' equity, which he states as 2406 [as heard in the transcript; the subtraction as stated does not match, value as spoken].

Under assets, divided into current assets and non-current (long-term) assets. Current assets: cash and equivalents plus short-term investments, together called cash and short-term investments. For Pepsi that is 9,530 million, or $9.53 billion. Of all the things to look at, one he really looks at is how much cash and short-term investments the company has (9,530 million here). This is important because when you later learn to calculate the intrinsic value of a business, cash and short-term investments is one of the inputs.

Also under current assets: accounts receivables, and after deducting bad debt, net accounts receivables, which is what people owe the company and it has yet to collect: 9035; then inventories. Added together, total current assets are 27,901.

Is 27,901 good or bad, high or low? The figure by itself is meaningless; all these figures are meaningless unless compared with other figures. It's all relative. The most important thing is calculating the debt ratios, and you don't have to do it yourself; they're pre-calculated on all the websites. For example total current assets divided by current liabilities gives the current ratio: 27,901 divided by total current liabilities of 32,764. Again it's all done for you; he's just showing what makes up the balance sheet.

Non-current (long-term) assets: property plant and equipment, long-term investments, goodwill and so on. Total non-current assets are 79,498 [as heard]. Current plus non-current gives total assets; "you can add it all up for fun."

Liabilities: assets are what the company owns, liabilities what it owes. Current liabilities (things owed that have to be paid back within a year, as he says here) are made up of accounts payable, short-term debt and other current liabilities. What is high or low is all relative, depending on the debt ratios learned later.

Out of all these, only a few things he looks at. Earlier: cash and short-term investments, for the intrinsic value. The other: the total debt of the company. Total debt is not total liabilities, because liabilities include a lot of other stuff besides debt. Total debt = short-term debt plus long-term debt. Short-term debt here is 6861. Long-term debt, money owed but which they can take their time to pay back over many, many years, is 42,321 (spoken as "42... 3 to 1", split across a transcript line break) [filled from earlier pass]. Add short-term and long-term debt to get total debt. This matters because it is used later to calculate the intrinsic value of the stock; he is just showing where things are found.

Other non-current liabilities: long-term leases, provisions for risks and charges, etc. "Don't worry, you don't have to learn all these technical details; not that important."

#### [18:05] Shareholders' equity on the Pepsi sheet
Made up first of common stock and additional paid-up capital. Not really important for investors, but for general knowledge: this is the money raised when the company first sold shares to the public in its IPO. That happened many, many years ago when the company first listed. Nowadays when you and I buy and sell stock, e.g. Apple, the money does not go into the company; it goes to the shareholder who sold the stock to me. Money only goes into the company if the company issues new shares to the public, which is not very common for most US companies. It is common for certain stocks like REITs, which issue new shares often, but very, very rare for companies like Apple and Microsoft, because they have so much cash they don't need to raise more from the public.

Then add retained earnings: profits the company has made over time and kept in the company rather than paying out as dividends. So 23 plus 4,451 plus 72,788, then deduct treasury stock (when a company buys back its shares from the market and holds them). Treasury stock is always deducted, always a negative, although the negative sign is not shown here. Then there is another item, accumulated other comprehensive income, minus 15 [as heard]; deducting these gives total shareholders' equity. This is basically any unrealized loss or profit from the company's investments. E.g. Pepsi buys other companies, and if those fall in value it's an unrealized loss, because they don't sell, they hold. Or Pepsi holds a lot of cash overseas; if the US dollar gets stronger against foreign currencies there's a foreign exchange unrealized loss. All of these unrealized losses and profits from foreign exchange and investments are recorded in this line item. Is this important for an investor? No, it's really not; it's general knowledge, "I will tell you what is important when we come to that." Don't worry that you need to know all this; seriously, you don't. The purpose is just to show these items are on the balance sheet.

#### [22:33] Negative shareholders' equity: an important exception
Total assets minus total liabilities gives shareholders' equity. Most of the time you want assets to be more than liabilities, e.g. 100 million in assets and 50 million in liabilities gives equity of 50. Generally we want shareholders' equity to be positive. If it is negative, normally that's a bad thing, because liabilities are bigger than assets.

But there is an exception, and this is the important part. Certain great companies like Domino's Pizza, McDonald's, Yum Brands have negative shareholders' equity. Not because the business is doing poorly or losing money; on the contrary, they have been making a lot of money and doing very well. It is negative because they have been buying back a lot of shares, and when you buy back a lot of shares the treasury stock is negative and it reduces shareholders' equity.

Example: McDonald's, balance sheet, latest quarterly statement. Shareholders' equity is minus 790 (as spoken). A lot of new investors freak out, thinking the equity is negative so the company is worthless. No, don't worry; it is not worthless. McDonald's is doing very, very well: retained earnings of 70 billion, so a lot of money made, but 79 billion of treasury stock. Those are shares bought back from the market; they no longer receive dividends, have no voting rights, and are kept in "cold storage". It is a negative number: take this, minus this, you get negative shareholders' equity.

To conclude: a company with negative shareholders' equity is not always bad. It is fine as long as the company is making a lot of money, profits are increasing consistently, free cash flow is increasing consistently, and the reason for the negative equity is simply the large treasury stock. Scrolling up for McDonald's: very profitable, revenue increasing, net income increasing, free cash flow also increasing. Very good business.

#### [26:06] The three ratios for financial strength
There are many debt ratios, but only three he looks at; these three are sufficient.

1. Current ratio = current assets divided by current liabilities. You want more assets, so the top bigger than the bottom: greater than one is safe. It's pre-calculated on the financial websites.

2. Debt-to-EBITDA ratio = total debt divided by EBITDA. EBITDA is another kind of earnings: earnings before deducting interest expenses, taxes, depreciation and amortization. Total debt = short-term debt plus long-term debt. Example: total debt $3 billion, EBITDA $1.5 billion, so 3 billion divided by 1.5 billion = a debt-to-EBITDA ratio of 1.5 times. Anything less than 3 is safe. It means the company can pay back all its debt within one and a half years of its profits. If the ratio is three, it will take three years to pay back the debt based on annual profits, before taxes, interest and depreciation. (We don't include taxation because if the company is making losses there are no taxes.) Pre-calculated on the website.

3. Debt servicing ratio = net interest expense divided by cash flow from operations, times 100%. Net interest expense: the company pays interest on money borrowed but may also earn interest from its investments; take the net of the income and the expense. Cash flow from operations is how much cash the company generates from operations (covered in the next section on cash flow). We want it to be less than 30%: less than 30% is safe, more than 30% very dangerous. Example: a company pays a million dollars of interest a year on its debt and generates $10 million in cash flow from operations: that's 10%, safe. Personal analogy: you earn 5,000 a month take-home salary (your cash flow from operations), and pay 1,000 in interest on bank loans: 20%, safe. If you paid $4,000 in interest while earning $5,000, it's too high. Same for an individual and a company.

#### [31:43] Where these three ratios do not apply
These three ratios apply to all companies except banks (a different ratio is used), financial companies like insurance companies, property developers, REITs and commodity companies. These are by the nature of their business highly leveraged, taking on a lot of debt compared to equity, so they all fail these ratios. Having said that, he personally avoids property developers and commodity companies because of their high leverage, but does invest in banks, insurance companies and REITs, for which a different measure of debt is used. On Stock Oracle all the debt ratios are already calculated; you just have to know where to find them.

#### [32:33] Example: Meta Platforms ratios
Go to company metrics (they're found in a few places) and scroll to debt ratios. Current ratio: last quarter 2.6, more than one; five-year average 2.72, way above one. Debt servicing ratio (want 30% or less): zero for Meta, very conservative. Debt-to-EBITDA (want 3 or less): only 0.59; five-year average only 0.3 [he says "five-day" in transcript].

#### [33:42] Other ratios: interest coverage and cash ratio
Interest coverage ratio: popular with many people; he doesn't use it himself because it overlaps, but you can look at it. = EBITDA (earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization, i.e. profits before deducting taxes and interest) divided by net interest expenses. Example: company earns 100 million in profit before taxes and interest and pays $10 million in interest expenses: ratio is 10 times, i.e. the company can cover its interest expenses 10 times over from one year of profits, very safe. Safe = three or more; five or more is very, very safe; below three it's getting a bit risky, especially below two. For Meta it's 71, very, very safe.

Cash ratio: he thinks it's not necessary. Current ratio is current assets over current liabilities; the cash ratio instead takes only cash and cash equivalents in the numerator (excluding accounts receivables and inventory), divided by current liabilities. A cash ratio of 0.5 and above is very, very safe; below 0.3 it's getting risky. Meta's is about 1.97, very safe.

Personally he only looks at three: current ratio, debt servicing ratio, debt-to-EBITDA. Must all three pass? Ideally yes, but if the company passes two out of three he's also okay investing.

#### [36:53] Companies that intentionally have a current ratio below one
Some very profitable companies intentionally keep the current ratio below one. Example: Pepsi, a great, very profitable company: revenue growing very strongly, free cash flow very consistent and positive, net profits very positive. But its current ratio is less than 1, about 0.85. Some say that's not good, but Pepsi does it intentionally. Current ratio = current assets over current liabilities, and part of current liabilities is accounts payable: Pepsi has many suppliers it owes money to, and because it's so big it has a lot of leverage over its suppliers. So it purposely drags the payments to suppliers ("I know it's not a very good thing, but that's what they do"): instead of paying in 30 days, they may pay in 60 to 90 days. This reduces the working capital Pepsi needs, a good thing, but inflates accounts payable and hence current liabilities, pushing the current ratio below one.

So you take it in the context of other ratios. Pepsi's debt servicing ratio is 9.79 (as spoken), way below 30%; interest coverage ratio 12.52, way above 3, very safe; debt-to-EBITDA 2.59, below 3. You can't look at one ratio in isolation. Later he'll cover how well a company manages working capital, e.g. the cash conversion cycle: Pepsi's is very, very low (good), and its accounts receivables grow slower than its revenue growth. Looking at everything, Pepsi is very safe and very profitable, so a current ratio below one is nothing to be concerned about. By the end of the lesson you'll learn all these ratios and look at everything in totality.

Another example: ServiceNow, a software company like Adobe and Salesforce, selling subscriptions. People pay the subscription up front, so the company collects one year of service in advance; since it hasn't provided the service yet, this is deferred revenue, recorded as a current liability. A lot of deferred revenue expands current liabilities and pushes the current ratio below one. ServiceNow is very profitable and high quality, yet its current ratio is 0.95. But the other ratios: debt servicing ratio zero (very safe), interest coverage ratio 90 (very high), debt-to-EBITDA 0.55. The current ratio is important, but if it's below one it doesn't matter if the other ratios are very strong.

#### [41:24] Banks: CET1 ratio and NPL ratio
For banks you don't use those three ratios. First, the common equity tier one (CET1) ratio. It is part of the Basel III requirements, the international standard for banks: a bank's CET1 ratio should be at least 4.5%. He is more conservative and wants it more than 10%. It's complicated to calculate, so don't; search online. Basically it is the bank's common equity tier one (what the bank holds in common stock and retained earnings, i.e. cash, common stock, retained earnings) divided by its risk-weighted assets.

How to find it: search Google. When he invested in DBS Bank in Singapore he typed "DBS Bank common equity tier 1 ratio": DBS (the biggest and most profitable bank in Singapore) has 14.1%, above his 10% minimum and way above the international 4.5%, which is why Singapore banks are the safest in the world on this ratio. JP Morgan: 12.4%, very safe, above 10%. Bank of China: 11.28%.

Second ratio for banks: the NPL (non-performing loan) ratio. Banks lend money to companies and people, and some don't pay back. If they don't repay within a certain period it's a non-performing loan, which could eventually become bad debt. Non-performing loans divided by all loans gives the NPL ratio; no need to calculate, just look it up. In general we want it less than 5%. DBS Bank NPL ratio is only 1.5%, which is great: of all loans given by DBS, only 1.5% are non-performing. Less than 5% is safe.

#### [45:50] REITs: gearing ratio
He invests a lot in REITs (real estate investment trusts): buying a REIT means buying a portfolio of real estate properties. REITs take loans from banks to finance the properties; too many loans is risky, they pay a lot of interest, and if interest rates rise they could be in trouble. So always ensure a REIT doesn't have too much debt, using the gearing ratio = total debt of the REIT divided by total assets (also called aggregate leverage). He wants the gearing ratio / aggregate leverage less than 45%: out of all assets (the property), less than 45% financed by debt, the rest by equity.

To find it, go to the website of the REIT, read the financial statements, and they will tell you. Example: he's invested in Ascendas REIT ("Ascenders" in the transcript): download the annual report/presentations; aggregate leverage (the gearing ratio, same thing) is 37.6%, less than 45%, safe. That's how you determine the financial strength of a company, a bank or a REIT.

### 31 - Statement of Cash Flows

#### [00:00] Why the cash flow statement is the most important
Having covered the income statement and balance sheet, now the statement of cash flows, the final part of the financial statements. Which of the three is the most important, if you could only look at one? The statement of cash flows: "This is the most important of all." It tells us how much cash is actually coming into and going out of the business.

Why? A company may report high profits. Example: reported profits of five million dollars, but cash flow from operations (the actual cash coming in) was not five million but only two million. Is that possible? Yes. Reasons:

- When the company sells products to customers it records the profit, but the customer didn't pay; it becomes a bad debt, so the cash didn't come in. Which is more important, recording profit or collecting cash? Cash. That is why cash from operations is more important than net profit.
- Sometimes a company records a profit when a product was not actually sold. Example: years ago he looked at a palm oil company that owned palm trees worth about $50 million. The next year the trees grew bigger and they revalued the assets: now worth $100 million, so they said they had made a gain on the assets of $50 million and added it to other income. This inflated net profit by 50 million, but no cash came in because they didn't sell the trees. Which is more real, revaluing assets and recording the profit, or generating cash? Generating cash.

Many times companies could creatively inflate their profits so the company looks profitable but isn't really generating cash, and this is what causes a lot of companies that look profitable to go bankrupt. The most important thing is not the recorded net income but the actual cash generated by the business.

#### [03:32] The three parts
**1. Cash flow from operations**: how much cash comes in or goes out as a result of the company's operations. For McDonald's, operations are selling burgers and fries. This is the most important part, because we use it later to calculate the intrinsic value of the business and the shares. We want it positive and increasing consistently: for the last five years, increasing consistently and positive. However, certain companies by the nature of their business have cash flows from operations that are inconsistent (could be negative, could be positive): banks, commodity companies, property developers. This rule doesn't apply to those three types; for all other companies cash from operations should be positive and increasing consistently.

**2. Cash flow from investing**: cash in or out from investment activities. If the company buys equipment, buys land, builds a plant, cash goes out: an outflow, a negative figure. Not necessarily bad; it just tells you cash is going out because the company is investing in machinery, plant and equipment, commonly known as CAPEX (capital expenditures). If the company sells a machine, equipment or an asset, money comes in: inflow. So in this section inflow means the company is selling assets, equipment and investments; outflow means it is buying assets, equipment (CAPEX). Positive doesn't mean good and negative doesn't mean bad; it just tells you the company's investment activities.

**3. Cash flow from financing**: cash in and out from financing activities, i.e. the company raising money. To grow, a company sometimes needs to raise money to make investments and expand. Two ways: borrowing (from the bank, or selling bonds), which brings cash in; or selling new shares through a rights issue, which also brings cash in. If the company pays back bank loans or buys back shares, cash goes out. So positive means the company is raising money; negative doesn't mean bad because it could be buying back shares and paying back loans.

So for the second and third sections positive and negative is not good or bad; it just tells you how money is moving. The one that's important is the first: it has to be positive and increasing over the last five years.

#### [08:28] Real example: Alphabet (Google)
On Stock Oracle (or Yahoo Finance), financials, cash flow statement. Quarterly or annual? Like the income statement, for the cash flow statement we look at annual figures, since we want to know how much cash came in or went out over 12 months. You can look at the last financial year (for Alphabet, ended 31 December 2025) and the last 12 months (the last four quarters, TTM, trailing 12 months). It's divided into the three parts.

Cash flow from operations: for Google, operations are selling advertising through search and YouTube. Net operating cash flow for the last 12 months is 164,713 million (as spoken). This is the amount of cash that came in as a result of operations, and it's the most important thing he looks at to identify the quality of the business, and later for intrinsic value. The last 12 months figure is exactly the same as the last financial year because it so happens that the last 12 months coincide with the last financial year.

A high-quality business is one where operating cash flow increases consistently over time. Google's cash from operations going back: 54 billion, 65 billion, 91 billion, 91 billion (a slight drop there), 101 billion, 125 billion, 164 billion. Increasing over the last 5, 10 years? Yes; high-quality business. If net operating cash flow is not consistently positive (positive, negative, negative, positive, negative, positive) that is usually a low-quality business. Or if cash from operations goes up, down, up, down, up, down, those are businesses he doesn't like to invest in. He wants it growing consistently, which tells him the company generates more and more cash from its business over time, and when that happens the value of the business increases over time.

Second part, net cash flow from investing: when the company buys plant, machinery, equipment, cash goes out (negative); when it sells machines, plants, or shares it owns in other companies, cash comes in. For Google it's been negative, negative, negative, negative, and for the last 12 months negative 120,000 million, i.e. 120 billion cash outflow from investing. Negative isn't bad; it's not good or bad; it means the company is making investments. If positive, the company is not making investments but selling off investments and cash is coming back in. But for net operating cash flow we want positive; if negative, something's wrong: the company in doing business is losing more cash than it earns.

Within investing, a key item is capital expenditure or CAPEX, because of free cash flow: free cash flow = net operating cash flow (OCF) minus CAPEX. You don't have to calculate it; there's a line at the bottom that says free cash flow.

Third, net cash flow from financing: for Google negative, negative, negative, etc. Not bad, not good. When a company borrows from the bank or sells bonds, cash comes in (positive); if it sells new shares, cash comes in. So positive generally means the company is raising money through debt or equity, which normally happens for new companies. Very mature, big, profitable companies don't need to raise money because they make a lot through the business. Companies like Microsoft, Google and Nvidia have so much money they use cash to buy back shares and pay dividends; when they do, cash goes out and is recorded as a negative. So the negatives mean Google is buying back shares and paying dividends. Is that good for us as shareholders? Yes.

#### [16:47] Free cash flow
Free cash flow is already calculated on the statement. FCF = net operating cash flow minus capital expenditures (CAPEX). We want free cash flow consistently positive: whatever cash the company makes from its business, after deducting CAPEX or investments, it has money left behind. With positive FCF it can pay dividends (which we like), buy back shares (which we like), keep the money as retained earnings, or pay off debt. That's why it's very important for FCF to be consistently positive.

Negative FCF for one or two years, once in a while, is okay. But consistently negative for two or three years in a row is very dangerous: more cash going out than coming in, and eventually the company runs out of cash. Then it's forced to raise cash: sell more shares (diluting shareholders), take more bank loans, sell more bonds (increasing debt). We don't like that. We want FCF consistently positive, and ideally also consistently increasing. Google's FCF: positive, positive, positive.

#### [19:30] AI CAPEX and falling free cash flow
Recording in 2026: in the last few years companies like Microsoft, Google and Meta Platforms have been investing very heavily into AI and data centers, spending billions and billions. FCF is operating cash flow minus CAPEX, and these companies (also Nvidia, Microsoft, Google) have recently increased CAPEX significantly because of AI, which has caused FCF to go down. Their FCF is still positive but has dropped because of the CAPEX increase.

Is he concerned? No, for two reasons. First, the increase in CAPEX will lead to higher growth in the future and let them further strengthen their economic moat. Second, the increase in CAPEX is temporary, not forever: they're making massive investments now for AI, and once done, which will take maybe two, three, four years, it will drop and FCF will go up again.

So if a business's FCF has dropped recently, it's not always bad. If FCF dropped because operating cash flow dropped, that is a cause for concern. But if operating cash flow is going up and the FCF drop is because CAPEX is going up temporarily as the business invests for future growth, that is fine.

#### [21:26] Visual charts and comparisons
With a Stock Oracle Pro account you can see all this visually by scrolling up: line chart or bar chart. Over the last 10 years Google's operating cash flow (orange) goes up consistently, a high-quality business, and free cash flow (dark green) is also increasing consistently and positive.

**Intel (INTC)**, semiconductor company (not all semiconductor companies are high quality). Bar chart: operating cash flow increased from 2017 to 2020, but after 2020 it's been dropping and going sideways. Not what he likes; not a high-quality business for now, because he doesn't see consistent increase in net operating cash flow. Free cash flow (dark green) increased then decreased then turned negative: negative, negative, negative; five years of negative FCF. "I won't touch this kind of business."

**Singapore Airlines**: the shares listed on the US market, in US dollars (by end of 2026 the Singapore-listed shares will be on Stock Oracle). SIA is, to him, one of the best airlines in the world, but he won't buy the shares, because all airline businesses are very cyclical: dependent on tourism and the economy, with very high maintenance CAPEX (refurbishing and repairing planes, buying new planes), high fuel costs; a very tough and competitive business. Not just SIA but all airlines have very inconsistent cash flow from operations and free cash flow, which is why he avoids airlines even though SIA is a very good airline. It's not the fault of management; it's the nature of the industry. Certain businesses are just very tough to manage.

Bar chart: net operating cash flow flat, going down, negative during COVID, positive, up, then down again: inconsistent cash from operations, not a high-quality business he'd invest in. FCF (dark green because negative): negative, negative, negative... five years of negative FCF. When a company keeps losing cash, it has to keep issuing bonds, raising debt, and selling more shares, diluting shareholders and creating a lot of debt. After 2022, "boom", very high FCF, but it's been going down ever since. Not what he likes; he likes consistency.

**Mastercard**, a business he loves and has invested in: an airline business versus a payment technology business. Operating cash flow very, very consistent; free cash flow very, very consistent. There are many ways to make money, and he'd rather make money the easy way, in businesses where cash flow generation is very consistent and predictable.

#### [26:46] Summary
For the statement of cash flows: look for cash from operations increasing consistently, and free cash flow consistently positive. Free cash flow = net cash from operations minus capital expenditure. Why positive? When FCF is positive the company can pay dividends, pay back debt, buy back shares and retain earnings. If FCF is negative for a few years it becomes dangerous: cash reserves reduce and the company is forced to take on more debt or sell more shares to avoid going bankrupt. "So that's a lousy business."

### 32 - Financial Metrics

*(Note: the source transcript for this video has gaps in the timestamps/text, e.g. between 03:39 and 04:40 and between 08:11 and 10:21 and 16:38 to 17:09, and some passages are garbled; only what is present is recorded.)*

#### [00:00] ROE (return on equity)
After the income statement, balance sheet and cash flow statement, a couple of last ratios and a final summary of fundamental analysis. First, ROE, "one of Warren Buffett's favorite criteria in investing in the company". ROE tells us how good the company is at making money based on the shareholders' stake in the business. ROE = net profit after tax (net income) divided by shareholders' equity, as a percentage. Example: net income $126 million, shareholders' equity $420 million: 126 divided by 420 = ROE of 30%.

What's a good ROE? Generally a company with an ROE of 12% is a good business, and about 15% or above is an excellent investment. Among companies he invests in, the ROE is often above 15%. Look at ROE over the last five years to ensure it is consistently above 12 to 15%.

Negative equity: companies like McDonald's and Yum Brands have negative shareholders' equity because they keep buying back shares, creating lots of treasury stock. Then ROE is undefined. Some websites show McDonald's ROE as negative, which is not accurate: profit generated from negative equity is actually infinity, so ROE is infinite, which is very good. If equity is negative because of high treasury stock causing ROE to show negative, it's actually a good thing, because it basically means the company makes profit with little or no shareholder equity.

#### [03:39] ROIC (return on invested capital) *(transcript partly missing)*
The profit measured on the invested capital, both equity and debt. The formula: take the EBIT (earnings before interest and taxes), multiply by one minus the tax rate, divided by shareholders' equity plus total debt minus cash and equivalents. "You say, my God, that's so complicated." ROIC is a measure of how efficient a company is. The threshold lines are fragmentary in the transcript: "over 12% good, over 5%..." [the second threshold is cut off in the transcript; the earlier pass reads it as: over 12% good, over 15% excellent, same as ROE, filled from earlier pass]. Look at both ROE and ROIC.

Why ROIC exists: ROE alone only tells one part of the story. Some companies artificially inflate their ROE by taking on a lot of debt and using the debt to buy back shares, and ROIC penalizes companies that do that. [from earlier pass]

ROIC does not apply to banks: banks have very high debt by nature of their business, are highly leveraged, so ROIC is artificially very low. For banks just look at ROE and ignore ROIC; for all other companies look at both.

#### [05:08] Finding ROE and ROIC
On Stock Oracle, e.g. Mastercard, go to company metrics, under profitability ratios: return on equity for the last 12 months is 210%, really good; ROIC for the last 12 months 58.4%, really high. You can see the latest number, the five-year average and the ten-year average; with a pro account you can see charts (line or bar) under the trending chart. ROE increasing is not required; 12 and above is a pretty good business, but increasing is even more powerful. ROIC has been pretty consistent too; hovering shows latest numbers 210 ROE and 58.41 ROIC.

If you can't find your stock on Stock Oracle (e.g. a market not yet covered), go to Morningstar.com. It is normally a paid website but has a free section with ROE and ROIC. Example: Ping An Insurance (China/Hong Kong), H shares listed in Hong Kong: go to key metrics, then profit and efficiency ratios; ROE is there, ROIC is not provided. ROIC is also not applicable to insurance companies, and Ping An is both an insurance company and a bank, so just look at return on equity.

#### [08:11] Revenue vs accounts receivables *(transcript partly missing)*
Another thing he looks at to ensure the company is safe: revenue growth, ensuring that receivables have not grown faster than revenue; when companies record fake revenue, no money is coming in. A higher, increasing accounts receivables is a major red flag. [Transcript gap follows.]

Accounts receivable means the company sold a product (usually to another company) and has not been paid yet, i.e. revenue that has not been converted to cash; too much of it means the company is not collecting its debts quickly. If receivables grow faster than revenue, it is very dangerous, and it can mean one of three things: the customers are in trouble and can't pay; the company is bad at collecting payments; or the revenue is fictitious. He has seen companies generate fictitious, fake revenue, and since no money comes in, to fool the public they show it under accounts receivables. [from earlier pass]

On Stock Oracle you can see the chart simply: e.g. Mastercard, go to financials, one of the pre-set default charts; sales revenue is growing a lot faster than receivables.

In TradingView: put in the ticker of the stock (e.g. Alphabet), click indicators, look for revenue and plot it against accounts receivable. But Piranha Profits has created a custom indicator that combines both. To add it: go to the Piranha Profits lesson (this lesson), and at the end of the lesson find "Course notes and resources"; below it is the "total revenue versus accounts receivables" custom indicator for TradingView. Click the link; you should have a TradingView account (free is fine; register and log in). Scroll down and click "Add to Favorites". Then on TradingView (website charts or the desktop app) click indicators, go to Favorites, and "Revenue versus accounts receivables" appears. It plots revenue in green versus accounts receivables in red. For Alphabet, revenue is growing faster than accounts receivables. Great.

#### [13:36] Cash conversion cycle (CCC)
For retail companies that sell physical goods, e.g. Nike or Apple, also look at the cash conversion cycle to ensure the company is managed effectively. It does not apply to companies that don't sell a physical product, e.g. Google, YouTube, Microsoft; but for Walmart and Costco it's really important.

CCC tells you the length of time in days it takes for a company to convert its investments in inventory and other resources into cash, i.e. when customers pay and the company gets the money back. [Transcript gap.] You invest in marketing, materials, and finally they pay: that's the cash conversion cycle. Examples: a restaurant takes out cash, buys ingredients, cooks, sells, and the customer pays, so the cycle is roughly one to three days; a real estate company takes out cash, buys land, spends on marketing and materials, and buyers pay progressively as construction proceeds, so the cycle is roughly three to five years, or two or three years. [from earlier pass] It doesn't matter how long or short it is; what matters is that it is consistent. A consistent or decreasing CCC is good: they're getting their money back faster, the company is very efficient. If the CCC is taking longer and longer, that's a red flag: the company could be in trouble because it's not able to sell the inventory fast enough to get back the cash.

Formula: cash conversion cycle = days inventory outstanding plus days sales outstanding minus days payable outstanding. Days inventory outstanding = average inventory divided by cost of goods sold times 365. Days sales outstanding = accounts receivable divided by net credit sales (times 365, implied; the rest garbled). "Breathe a sign of relief": no need to calculate it yourself.

Example in TradingView: Nike, five-year time frame, financials, type "cash conversion cycle", choose quarterly for more data points. We want consistent or falling, not increasing. The trend: not going up; went up a bit then coming down again; looks fine. You can also find the CCC chart and numbers on Stock Oracle: e.g. Pepsi (sells physical goods), financials, scroll down to the cash conversion cycle chart. Pepsi's CCC is negative all the way. Very, very good: a negative CCC means the business collects cash up front before it pays its suppliers, the best. Not all companies are negative. You can see numbers by mousing over; another place is company metrics, under efficiency ratios: cash conversion cycle for the last 10 or 5 years depending on your historical package, with the current, 5-year average and 10-year average.

#### [19:33] Summary: the 12 things
"Probably the heaviest, most technical lesson in the entire course... The rest is going to be a lot easier, I promise." The financial statements give 12 things, summarized on one page (transcript partly missing):

- Profitability: sales revenue and net income increasing consistently over the last five years; profit margins and net profit margins consistent; ROE 12 to 15% or higher; ROIC [threshold cut off in the transcript; the earlier pass has 12 to 15% or higher, filled from earlier pass]. The earlier pass also lists, in this group: cash flow from operations increasing consistently over the last five years; free cash flow consistently positive (FCF = operating cash flow minus capex); and, if net income is not consistent, operating income should be growing consistently, with margins also compared against the closest competitors. [from earlier pass]
- Financial strength (so it's not going to go bankrupt easily; something you can buy and sleep soundly at night): current ratio one or more; debt-to-EBITDA ratio three or less; debt servicing ratio less than 30%. For banks, common equity tier one ratio more than 10%; for REITs, gearing ratio less than 45%.
- Management effectiveness (ensure management is running the business smoothly): cash conversion cycle consistent or declining, but not increasing (applies only to retail companies that sell physical goods); and ensure accounts receivables are not growing faster than sales revenue.

If the company you invest in ticks all 12 points, it is a great company: very profitable, very safe, and well managed.

---

# Part 6 — Lessons 33–37

### 33 - Hyflux Case Study

#### [00:00] Why put every stock through the 12 financial metrics
Behind a stock is an underlying business. If the business does well, the stock will do well. If the business goes bust, the stock will drop and never come back. So before investing, put the business through the 12 financial metrics and make sure it passes the 12 steps. This ensures you invest only in great companies, and, more importantly, helps you avoid mediocre companies, lousy companies and dangerous companies that could go bust.

#### [00:28] The Singapore example: Hyflux
In Singapore one such company went bust and a lot of people lost a lot of money. The stock was once the darling of the Singapore Stock Exchange and many people bought it because it was widely promoted by analysts and stockbrokers. It was Hyflux, a water treatment company that was a blue chip in Singapore. People invested because on the surface it looked like a good company: profits were going up, revenue was going up, and the Singapore government's Temasek fund was a big backer of the stock, which gave people a lot of confidence. He never bought it because he saw that the fundamentals failed many of the metrics; he stayed away and told his students to stay away.
