Unfortunately a lot of people don't know how to analyze a business, so they bought, and it collapsed from $2 a share (in fact about $2.50 a share) all the way down to $0.4, and eventually to zero. A lot of people screamed bloody murder; not only the stock investors lost everything, but even bond investors lost hope of recovering any money at all.

How do you avoid companies that can go bust? Very simple: put them through the 12 metrics. Hyflux fails many of those metrics, which is why his students never invested and stayed safe.

#### [02:21] The red flags in the financial statements
On the surface revenues and profit were growing, but digging into the statement of cash flows is where you find the problems. That is why he keeps saying the statement of cash flows is the most important statement of all. The company did not go bust overnight; over the years there were many warning signs.

**Red flag 1: negative, inconsistent operating cash flow.** As far back as 2006, operating cash flow was negative and inconsistent. Across 2006, 2007, 2008, 2009, 2010, 2011 the figures were minus 26.8 million, 74 million, 29 million, 60 million, 49.5 million negative (as read aloud; exact year-by-year pairing and signs were shown on screen). You only want companies where operating cash flow increases consistently over the years. Here it is extremely inconsistent: negative, negative, negative.

**Free cash flow.** Free cash flow has to be positive. Take operating cash flow and deduct the purchase of PPE (property, plant and equipment), which is the capital expenditures; operating cash flow minus capex gives free cash flow, and it must be consistently positive. Minus 26 million deducting 19 million is again negative free cash flow. Another year, 29 million minus 18 is still positive, but again it turns negative in other years.

**Red flag 2: paying dividends with negative free cash flow.** The company had been paying dividends to shareholders. If your company has negative free cash flow, should you pay dividends? No, you should conserve cash. You should only pay dividends if free cash flow is positive and you have excess cash. So the first warning sign is negative cash from operations and negative free cash flow; the second is paying dividends while free cash flow is negative.

**Red flag 3: receivables jumping far ahead of revenue.** Revenue grew: 142 million, 192 million, 554 million, 524 million, 569 million (as read aloud). Accounts receivables (trade receivables) went from 30 million to 46, 41, 27, and suddenly 182 million (spoken as "1.8"; consistent with the 575% jump from 27 million) [filled from earlier pass]. While revenue grew at 8% in 2010, accounts receivables grew at 575%. When receivables suddenly jump far above revenue growth, something is wrong with the business.

**Red flag 4: unsustainable debt.** One of the ratios is debt to EBITDA, which should be three or less, ideally less than three. Loans and borrowings under current liabilities are short-term debt; the long-term debt is added to give total debt, which he stated as $59 million (as spoken; loans and borrowings of 95 million in 2010 were also mentioned) [earlier pass has $599 million, which fits the stated ratio of 5.64 against EBITDA of $106 million]. EBITDA (earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization) was $106 million. The debt to EBITDA ratio was 5.64, a big fail.

#### [06:51] Why so many investors still bought
With such bad fundamentals, why did so many invest? Most investors are lazy or incompetent. They don't do deep-dive research into fundamentals; they buy because of what they read in the media or are told by bankers, analysts or brokers, and they don't do their own research.

- Many retail investors said they invested because Temasek Holdings (Singapore government's sovereign wealth fund) was a major investor, so it must be safe. That is a very, very wrong belief system. Just because Temasek invested doesn't make it a safe company. What people don't realize is that Temasek invested in Hyflux very early and had already sold most of its shares for a big profit; they were already out of the stock when people bought it.
- The celebrity status of the CEO, Olivia Lam, who was featured on Forbes magazine, was entrepreneur of the year, and a nominated member of parliament. He is not saying she isn't a good businesswoman; he thinks she is a very good one. But no matter how great you are, you make mistakes as well, and so does he. Don't be seduced by how good someone is; you can always do the fundamental research on the companies they represent.
- Blindly following recommendations by analysts and banks. Example shown: Daiwa Capital Markets said it believed Hyflux's share price reached an inflection point on 7 March ("blah blah blah"), expected the share price to continue to rise as sentiment improves, with a buy rating and target price of $2.40. The chart then collapsed all the way to $0.4 (spoken as "$4" in the transcript) and went to zero right after [earlier pass has 4 cents].
- People thought that since it is a water treatment business it must be a strategic investment, because everyone needs water.

Lesson: do not be fooled by surface reasons for investing. You can always dig deep into the financial metrics of any investment.

### 34 - Great Business Models

#### [00:00] The six pillars of a great business
As investors we only want the best businesses in the world, with great business models that survive and thrive through all economic conditions. There are six pillars he always looks at before investing in any company:
1. Able to reduce its input costs over time.
2. Able to increase its output of products and services.
3. Able to self-finance its growth.
4. Weak suppliers.
5. Growing customers / a huge customer base.
6. Weak competition (protection against competition).

#### [00:55] Pillar 1: reduce input costs over time
In any business there is input (the cost that goes into the business) and output. There are three main input costs.

**Infrastructure and capital equipment.** For the majority of businesses you need factories, office space, equipment. You want a business that can grow without constantly spending more on new infrastructure and equipment; spend one time, then scale with the same infrastructure.

Businesses he doesn't like:
- Airlines: to grow they must constantly buy new planes (and replace old ones), causing a lot of cash outflow through constant new capital expenditures; once bought they must keep spending to maintain and repair. Constant spending on infrastructure and capital equipment reduces profitability over time.
- Shipping companies: must buy new ships to replace old ships, and repair them. He avoids these.
- Auto manufacturers: must constantly invest in new factories and equipment to build cars more efficiently. This sucks up cash flow and creates very low free cash flow.

Businesses he likes invest one time in capital equipment/infrastructure and scale without adding more costs:
- Credit card payment technology companies like Visa and MasterCard: they invested heavily in payment infrastructure, and once they have it they dominate the industry and can expand to all countries and many platforms without additional costs.
- Software companies: once the software is made they can scale production without investing a lot more in capital equipment.

In short, he likes companies with very little physical assets and more intellectual assets. Nike doesn't make its own shoes; it designs them, owns the intellectual property, and outsources manufacturing to China or Vietnam, where the manufacturers invest in the capital equipment. Apple doesn't make its own iPhones or laptops; it designs, creates the software, owns the IP and the brand, and outsources manufacturing to other countries and companies. Coca-Cola doesn't bottle its own drinks; it owns the syrup and the brand and outsources bottling and production to suppliers. McDonald's and Yum! Brands (owner of KFC and Pizza Hut) use a franchise model: they don't really own their restaurants or invest in capital equipment, they own the brand and recipes and sell the franchise. He loves these because they don't have to spend a lot more on future infrastructure or equipment to scale. As a result, when there is inflation they are not affected, because they don't have to keep spending more on input costs.

**Raw materials.** Most businesses use raw materials to create the finished product. Example: Starbucks, whose raw materials are coffee and sugar. If sugar or cocoa bean prices go up, their input costs go up and profits could drop. Ask: could raw material costs go up? If they do and the company can raise the price of its drinks and pass the cost to customers, that's okay, because they are hedged against inflation. If the company can't pass the cost on and absorbs it, that really screws up profitability and profit margins.

**Labor costs.** Ask whether there are key man risks. He tends to avoid companies whose success depends on the brilliance of one man, for example Elon Musk: one reason he is very nervous about Tesla or SpaceX is that a lot is driven by his personality and vision. What if he dies or gets hit by a truck? He likes companies not affected by one man or a few people. There's an old saying, he thinks by Warren Buffett "if I'm not wrong", that it's best to invest in a company where any idiot can run the business, because one day an idiot may run it. You want the business to be great because of the business model itself, so anyone can run it. Next question: is unique talent required to maintain the competitive advantage? If special talent is required, that's dangerous, because once you lose the talent the whole business is gone. He also avoids companies with very strong unions. Another reason he avoids airlines, especially in the US and European countries: very strong unions, and if pilots or air stewards go on strike it brings the entire company to its knees.

#### [08:25] Pillar 2: increase output of products and services
Output leads to more revenue and profits. What he looks for:
- A business unique enough to command pricing power: companies able to raise prices every year. Raising price each year raises revenue even if quantity sold is the same, and hedges against inflation: if labor and raw material costs rise but they raise prices, that's fine.
- High switching costs for customers: once the customer buys, it is very expensive or difficult to change brand, so they are stuck for the rest of their life. Example: Microsoft. Once people are familiar with Microsoft Office, PowerPoint and Word, it is very difficult to change to another word processor or spreadsheet app. It locks in customers. Many companies he invests in lock in customers for life, guaranteeing the business can continuously increase sales and profits.
- Recurring income: customers buy once and keep paying year after year. He doesn't like businesses that sell a product once. He invests in Adobe, Microsoft and Salesforce because once a company uses the software service it pays the subscription fee every year.
- Multiple product revenue streams. One reason he doesn't invest in Zoom, although it is very popular: it is a one-trick pony with one product, Zoom video software. If people get sick of it or jump to Microsoft Teams or a competitor, they're done. He invests in Alphabet because it has multiple product lines: YouTube, Google Search, Google Maps, multiple streams, diversified. Another example: Disney, with Disney parks and recreation experiences, Disney Plus, ESPN, Hulu, network TV; very diversified revenue streams, which is a lot safer.

How to know a company achieves the first two pillars: look at the profit margins. As long as gross profit margins and net profit margins are consistent or increasing over the last five years, that reflects the first two pillars.

#### [12:19] Pillar 3: growing customers with rising purchasing power
You want customers growing and able to buy more products and services over time, so purchasing power is rising. Questions:
- Is the customer base sizable and growing?
- Are customers' wages rising? Higher wages let them purchase more or pay more as you raise prices.
- Is there customer concentration risk? He avoids companies with very few customers. Example: Upstart (ticker UPST), which a lot of people have been talking about, a fintech company providing credit rating software services to banks and similar lenders (he also mentioned property developers as customers) [from earlier pass] to rate customers' credit and decide whether to give loans. He finds the business model pretty interesting, but the concern is that 80% of their revenue comes from three customers. That's customer concentration risk: if they lose one or all three customers they're done. You want thousands or hundreds of customers, so losing one or two or three or four doesn't matter.
- Are customers able to switch to substitutes or competitors easily? Are they loyal to the brand? If customers can switch very easily with no loyalty, that's a lousy business. Strong brand loyalty example: Apple. Even though Samsung or Huawei have a cheaper phone with more features, people still buy iPhone because they're in the ecosystem where everything works seamlessly. They're locked into the Apple ecosystem: high switching costs and brand loyalty.
- Another example: Costco. People pay membership fees every year in the US to be members, keep coming back, and there is a lot of brand loyalty. Costco and Apple are great businesses because they lock in customers; people who buy Apple products tend to have higher disposable income, are willing to pay more for a premium product, and the customer base is growing all around the world.

#### [15:37] Pillar 4: weak suppliers
Opposite of customers: you want customers growing and strong, but suppliers weak in comparison to the business. Your business should have stronger bargaining power so it can squeeze suppliers for lower input cost. If suppliers are stronger, they can raise prices and increase your costs. Ensure the business has many alternative suppliers so if one raises price you can go to another; you want suppliers to have weak bargaining power.

#### [16:37] Pillar 5: weak competition (the most important one)
If you want sales and profits to constantly grow, the business must fend off competition. The one thing that can kill a business or reduce revenue and profits is competition wanting to take away your customers. A great business protects itself so competitors cannot take away customers and market share. That only happens if it has a strong economic moat. (Covered a bit in the previous lesson under the three steps of investing, and in more detail in lesson seven, the seven-step Value Momentum Investing formula.) A strong moat/competitive advantage means:
- A brand monopoly, e.g. Google; Facebook/Instagram in social media.
- High switching costs that lock in the customer for life.
- Huge economies of scale that competitors cannot compete with.
- High barriers to entry, where it's difficult for new players to enter because of regulations, for example, or high cost of entry.

You also want competition to be weak. Many competitors is not good; you want very few competitors with very small market share and a weaker competitive position. So before investing he always looks at all the closest competitors to ensure they are weaker than the business he wants. If a competitor is stronger, he would rather invest in the competitor. Always invest in the top dog, the strongest business in the industry. If there's no one strong business, forget it; invest in an industry where there is clearly one leader and be with that leader.

How do you know? It comes out in the financial metrics. With strong competitive advantage and weak competition, the company increases revenue and income consistently, has consistently growing cash flow from operations, and positive free cash flow. All of this is reflected in the metrics learned earlier; now you understand what goes behind creating those metrics.

#### [19:54] Pillar 6: self-financing
As a business grows it needs money to grow. You want businesses that grow organically, generating enough cash to grow themselves without borrowing more or selling more shares. Questions: is the company able to grow without issuing new shares or debt? If so, that's a great business. Does the company have a conservative debt structure? Is the company buying back shares? In general you want a conservative debt structure as learned: current ratio more than one, debt to EBITDA ratio three or less, debt servicing ratio less than 30%, ROE and ROIC above 12% or above 15%. These are the hallmarks of a great business. In general he likes companies buying back their own shares, provided they are profitable and provided the shares are undervalued in the first place.

#### [21:10] Wrap-up
Before he invests in a stock he looks at the underlying business and asks whether it has a great business model. If it meets these pillars (he said "seven pillars" in closing, after listing six) and this is reflected in all the financial metrics, he has the confidence to invest, and over time it will grow under all economic conditions and thrive.

### 36 - Introduction & PEG Ratio

#### [00:00] Lesson five: stock valuation, and why it matters
In this lesson you learn how to value the shares of a business so you know whether they are expensive or cheap. Earlier lessons covered identifying a great business, and we only want to buy great companies. But a great business can be a lousy investment if you pay too high a price.

Property analogy: you identify a great apartment with a great view and location; the bank values it at a million dollars but the seller is asking $2 million, you get excited and pay $2 million for an apartment valued at $1 million. If you overpay it will be hard to make money if you resell, unless you wait for many, many years. A great business becomes an excellent investment if you can buy the shares for less than they are worth, when they are undervalued. Back to the apartment: if the bank values it at a million dollars but the seller is desperate or panicking and has to sell, and you buy it at $800,000 (spoken as "$8,000" in the transcript; he said 20% below the valuation), that's a big deal.

In the stock market no bank does a valuation for you, so you learn to do your own, to know the fair intrinsic value of the business and its shares.

#### [01:22] Buffett quote: great business first, valuation second
Warren Buffett said it's far better to buy a wonderful company at a fair price than a fair company at a wonderful price. Valuation is really important, but what's more important is that it is an excellent, great business. Don't buy a stock just because it's very, very cheap: "cheap crap is still crap." Only buy an excellent business when it is cheap, and it's better to buy an excellent business that's slightly cheap than a mediocre, lousy business that is very, very cheap. The priority is always great business first, valuation second, but valuation is important.

#### [02:16] Absolute price and recent price moves mean nothing
You can't simply look at the absolute price; it can be very misleading. A $1,000 stock could be cheap if intrinsic value is more than $1,000, and a $5 stock could be very expensive if intrinsic value is only a dollar. Likewise, whether a stock is expensive or cheap has nothing to do with how much the share price has moved recently. In the last one year Nvidia has gone up 120%, but calculating intrinsic value shows it is still not expensive; it is fairly priced. AMD, Nvidia's competitor, has fallen 50% over the last one year, and even after falling 50% it is still expensive, still above intrinsic value. What matters is the intrinsic value today and whether the share price is above or below it.

#### [03:40] How to value a business: the five methods
When you buy shares you are buying a piece of a business, and a business is a money-making machine: the more money it makes, the more it is worth. The value of a business is determined by how much money it makes, which we measure by cash flow from operations, free cash flow, and net income. Many methods exist; he focuses on the five main ones he uses:
1. **PEG ratio method** (learned in the first lesson, reviewed here): comparing the PE ratio to the earnings growth rate. Very simple but not that comprehensive.
2. **Discounted cash flow (DCF) method**: most of the time he uses this one, because to him it is the most comprehensive and most accurate. A business is a money-making machine and the best way to calculate how much it makes is the cash it generates for us as shareholders. The value of a business is the present value of all expected future cash flows, so you project all the cash the business will generate in the future. He takes 20 years and discounts that to present value. The assumption is that the business must have consistently increasing cash flow from operations and positive free cash flow. If cash flow is negative you can't use this method.
3. **Discounted net income method**: for companies whose cash flow is inconsistent or not always positive, where the second method can't be used. You project the net profit the company will earn in the future and discount it to present value. Not as great as the cash flow method but better than nothing, because net income is an accounting entry that can sometimes be manipulated or distorted.
4. **Price-to-book ratio method**: used for certain kinds of businesses such as banks and real estate investment trusts (REITs), and also for businesses that aren't making money or are distressed and on the verge of bankruptcy.
5. **Price-to-sales ratio method and price-to-sales growth ratio method**: for companies where revenue is growing but not yet profitable, or very cyclical companies whose profits are inconsistent (some years they make money, some years not).

#### [07:08] Stock Oracle Pro example (Nvidia)
Subscribers to Stock Oracle Pro get intrinsic value calculated automatically. Example: Nvidia, current share price $184, people wonder if it is cheap or expensive. Clicking intrinsic value shows several methods: discounted cash flow gives $258 based on the latest free cash flow; discounted net income gives $370; price-to-sales ratio method gives $226. Different methods give slightly different valuations; taking all into account the algorithm gives the most appropriate valuation, $208, so at the current share price it is slightly undervalued.

Not everyone has a subscription and some want to calculate manually, so the lesson goes step by step through the nuts and bolts. Download the intrinsic value calculator Excel template under Lesson 5, Course Notes and Resources (scroll all the way down); it contains the different intrinsic value calculators on different tabs.

#### [09:07] Method 1: PEG ratio
Already learned in lesson one. It is a very quick check of a stock's value; very simple and easy, but limited and not as comprehensive as the other methods. He doesn't really use it to value a stock; he uses it for screening, to shortlist stocks for further research (covered more in lesson nine), because there are over 10,000 stocks and we can't look at all of them. It is a criterion for screening stocks. It can only be used when the company's earnings are positive: the company must be making money and earnings increasing consistently, otherwise the PEG ratio won't be accurate.

PEG ratio = PE ratio divided by the projected earnings growth rate. The higher the PEG, the more expensive the stock; the lower, the cheaper. If you study finance or take the CFA, they say a PEG above one (PE higher than earnings growth rate) means overvalued, in theory, and PEG below one means undervalued, so you should only buy when PEG is less than one. The trouble is that in reality you'd buy a lot of cheap stocks that may not be great companies, while many great companies have a PEG above one, so you'd miss out on a lot of great companies. It's okay to buy above one from his experience; as a rule of thumb, look for stocks with a PEG ratio of 1.5 or less. If it's way above 1.5 it may be a bit expensive; at 1.5 or less take a further look.

Limitation: the PEG is based on the net income reported by the company, which can be distorted by non-operating expenses and non-operating income. That is why you do a more comprehensive valuation with the discounted cash flow method, the next method.

#### [12:30] Getting the PEG: Finviz example (Nvidia vs AMD)
You can calculate PEG yourself or find it at Finviz or Simply Wall Street. On Finviz, Nvidia has gone up over 100% over the last one year, whereas AMD has fallen more than 50%. Some think Nvidia is more expensive because it went up so much and AMD looks cheaper because it fell; you can't look at it that way, so use the PEG as a very rough measure to begin.

- Nvidia: PE ratio is 54 times (54.69 on screen); projected earnings growth rate for the next five years is 61% (61.17%). 54.69 divided by 61.17 gives a PEG of 0.89. That is less than 1.5 and less than 1, so Nvidia looks cheap.
- AMD: despite the share price falling 50%, the PE ratio is still 112, and the earnings growth rate for the next five years is 33%. 112 divided by 33 gives a PEG of 3.4, way above 1.5. Based on the PEG, the cheaper stock is Nvidia.

#### [14:26] How much to trust the PEG, and the screener preview
The PEG is a very quick way to value a stock but not the most comprehensive. Does he trust it completely? No; he uses the other, more comprehensive methods. It's just a quick check, and he uses it to quickly shortlist stocks with his screener (more detail in lesson 9, quick preview now). On the Finviz Screener there are a total of 9,900 stocks in the database, and we can't go through all of them, so we put in criteria to narrow the search to great companies that are not too expensive. One screen is the PEG ratio. Setting PEG less than one gives very cheap stocks but you miss great ones. There's no 1.5 option, unfortunately, so he can only put PEG under 2, which screens from 10,000 stocks down to 700, a shorter list. Of course he doesn't look only at PEG; other criteria narrow the search further, more in lesson 9.

### 37 - Discounted Cash Flow Method Part 1

#### [00:00] The DCF method: concept
The second method is the DCF (discounted cash flow valuation) method. He uses it most of the time, 90% of the time, because it's the most comprehensive. You need the intrinsic value calculator (downloadable, shown earlier). First understand the concept. We are attempting to value a business, which is a money-making machine that prints cash over the next 10, 20, 30 years. To find out what the business is worth we project the future cash it will generate; by default he takes the next 20 years, discounts it to present value, and that is the value of the business. Divide by the number of shares to get the intrinsic value per share.

Formal definition: the intrinsic value of a stock equals the present value of its future expected cash flow or earnings. He prefers cash flow because cash flow is more tangible than accounting profits.

#### [01:33] The magic money-printing machine analogy
- Scenario 1: a machine that prints $100 today (time zero). It is worth $100, since you pay $100 and get your money back.
- Scenario 2: a machine that prints $100 only one year later. It is worth less than $100 today, because you wait a year to get your $100. How much less depends on the opportunity cost of your money. If you could invest elsewhere and get a 5% return, that is the opportunity cost, called the discount rate. Here the discount rate is 5%: if you invest your money elsewhere with the same risk you could get a 5% return. So the machine is worth $100 divided by (1 + discount rate) = $100 / 1.05 = $95. This is the time value of money: money received in the future is worth less than money received today. Future value $100 discounted to present value is only $95.
- Scenario 3: you wait two years to get $100. Take the $100 and divide by 1.05, but discount twice (one year, two years), so square it: $100 / 1.05 squared = $91. Another way to think: $95 invested at 5% becomes $100 in one year; $91 invested at 5% for two years becomes $100.
- Scenario 4: the machine pays $100 three times: $100 right away, $100 one year later, $100 two years later (as he describes it, one year later and another year later). The machine is worth the sum of all three: $100 + $95 (discounted one year) + $91 (discounted two years) = $286.

This is the exact same way we value the shares of a business. The difference is that this money-making machine will not print cash for only three years; we assume at least 20 years. In the calculator we project the cash it will generate for the next 20 years and use an appropriate discount rate to discount all that future cash to present value. In addition we add the company's total debt as well as the current cash and equivalents (as spoken) to come up with an appropriate value for the business today. For the method to work, the company must generate positive cash flow, cash flow from operations must be increasing consistently, and we must be able to confidently expect consistent increases in cash flow for the next 20 years.

#### [07:17] Which cash flow: free cash flow
Which do we use: cash flow from operations, investing, financing, or free cash flow? Most of the time the default is free cash flow, because it tells us how much cash the business generates from operations after deducting capital expenditure, what's left for shareholders, which they can use to pay a dividend, buy back shares, or keep as retained earnings. It is the actual earning power of the business. The assumption is that free cash flow must be consistently increasing. Example chart: Mastercard, where free cash flow (dark green) increases consistently. Increasing consistently doesn't mean it goes up every single year; there could be one or two years it drops, which is fine as long as generally you see an uptrend.

#### [08:46] Using the intrinsic value calculator for Mastercard
Key in only the white boxes. Do not touch the coloured boxes, because the formulas are there and touching them will mess up the formula.

1. Name of the stock: Mastercard; symbol MA. You don't have to memorize symbols; any financial website gives it (Stock Oracle, or Yahoo Finance). Valuation method: choose discounted free cash flow.
2. Financial data: click financials; the income statement, balance sheet and cash flow statement are there. Focus on the cash flow statement (to see how much cash the business generates), click annual. Check the denomination: on Stock Oracle numbers are in millions of US dollars, whereas on other websites like Yahoo Finance they are in thousands. Get clear about the denomination.
3. Latest free cash flow: how much the business is making in the last 12 months. Available from any financial website; a free Stock Oracle account shows it. Scroll down in the annual cash flow statement to free cash flow; take the latest 12 months (the first column is the last 12 months; the next is the last financial year; sometimes they overlap, here more or less overlapping). It is 16,186 million. Key in 16186, in millions, not 16,186,000,000, because it's already in millions.
4. Total debt: not total liabilities; total debt is short-term debt plus long-term debt. Found on the balance sheet, always the latest point in time, so use the quarterly figure. Under liabilities: short-term debt 749 plus long-term debt 18,251 gives total debt of 19,000 million (19 billion). Key in 19,000.
5. Cash and short-term investments: under assets on the balance sheet, latest quarter. The transcript reads 11,059 and then keys in 11459 (figure as spoken; the on-screen value was not clearly stated) [earlier pass has 11,459 million as the keyed-in figure].

#### [13:57] Why 20 years, not perpetuity
Why 20 years? It's his default. If you study finance (as when he took his finance degree) or take a CFA, a DCF calculator by default assumes the company lasts forever, to perpetuity, using a terminal value. He is more conservative and doesn't assume the company lasts forever; he values it on making money for 20 years, and anything beyond 20 years is a bonus. That's his preference. Many great companies he's invested in have lasted more than 20 years: Coca-Cola has been around for 106 years, Walmart 55 years, Apple and Microsoft 45 years and 39 years respectively. So a 20-year horizon is quite conservative to him. Valuing to perpetuity is the DCF terminal method; he won't teach it because he doesn't use it, but it is shown and calculated automatically on Stock Oracle as "DCF terminal". He uses the 20-year method.

#### [15:51] Choosing a reasonable growth rate: consensus analyst estimates
One of the most important things in valuation is a reasonable growth rate. How do we know future growth? Look at consensus analyst estimates: hundreds of analysts study the company and have projected growth rates based on business plans and strategy, and companies themselves (mainly US companies) give guidance on future growth. Find this on many financial websites.

- Stock Oracle (data collated from FactSet): go to company metrics and scroll down. The first block is historical growth (last one, three, five, ten years); scroll down to projected growth rate. He usually uses the projected three to five years EPS growth rate as a proxy for the cash flow growth rate, because over the long run earnings should grow in line with cash flow or vice versa. The projected long-term growth rate can also be looked at, but primarily this one: 14.94% for Mastercard (next three to five years projection).
- Finviz: earnings growth for the next five years, 15.68%.
- Zacks (another well-known investment research website): projected growth rate 15.98%.

It's like going to different doctors and getting different opinions. Which to use? You can use just Stock Oracle, or average the websites: (14.94 + 15.68 + 15.98) / 3 = 15.53%. So for the first five years the growth rate based on the average of three websites is 15.53%.

For years 6 to 10, options: the first option is the same rate as the first five years (15.53% for both), the base case scenario, a constant rate. But mousing over the cell says maximum 15%: his own rule is that anything more than 15% he caps at 15% for years 6 to 10. Since it's 15.53, he caps it at 15. If it were 8% it would stay 8%; if 20% it would be capped at the maximum 15%.

For years 11 to 20 (the next decade), to be conservative he uses 4% by default, which assumes the business then grows roughly at nominal GDP plus another 2%. You don't have to follow him exactly; if you're very conservative you might put 2%. It's his style; adjust to yours.

#### [22:00] Shares outstanding
The calculator takes the whole business value and divides by the number of shares to get intrinsic value per share. On Stock Oracle under overview (also in his favorites), shares outstanding; they use the diluted average, 898 million. Key in 898 million.

#### [23:15] Discount rate
The discount rate is the opportunity cost of your money. Different analysts use different rates. If you study CFA or finance (as he did at university) they use the weighted average cost of capital (WACC), which you can find on Stock Oracle. He doesn't use WACC, because he's not doing a DCF terminal valuation and not assuming the company lasts forever; he assumes 20 years, so he uses a modified discount rate. Warren Buffett has often said he just uses the 10-year treasury bond rate (10-year treasury yield).

His own discount rate: risk-free rate plus beta multiplied by the market risk premium, taking the average of the last five-year risk-free rate and the average of the last five-year market risk premium. You don't have to calculate this yourself; it's already in the calculator and updated every year (the update is sent to you to download). If you want to do it yourself there's a website link to calculate the risk-free rate and average market risk premium, but you needn't. Based on this formula there is a simple table: use the appropriate discount rate according to the stock's beta.

Beta is a measure of volatility. Example Apple (just to give an example) with a beta of 1: when the S&P 500 goes up 1%, Apple goes up 1%; it moves in line with the market; if the S&P drops 1%, Apple drops 1%. A stock with a beta of 3, like Tesla: when the S&P goes up 1%, Tesla tends to go up 3%; if the market drops 1%, Tesla drops 3%. A beta of 0.5 means not very volatile, like Coca-Cola, which has a very low beta: market up 1%, Coke up 0.5%; market down 1%, Coke down 0.5%. The higher the beta, the more volatile the stock, and we use a higher discount rate to penalize the stock for being too volatile, or at least that's the financial theory. Warren Buffett doesn't use this; he is very simple, just the risk-free rate, he uses 3.6% (as stated). He is adding an element to account for volatility.

For Mastercard, the beta (on Stock Oracle under overview) is 0.83, meaning less volatile than the overall market. The closest in the table is 6.1, so the discount rate is 6.1%.

#### [27:48] The result: base case valuation
With everything keyed in, the intrinsic value of Mastercard is $655.97; the current share price is $514.72. On this base case valuation Mastercard is 21.5% undervalued.

Intrinsic value is never one number; it's always a range, and different people may get different numbers based on growth assumptions. Example: Buffett's partner Charlie Munger. Buffett tends to be more optimistic and Munger more pessimistic. For American Express, Buffett said based on his calculation it was worth $60 intrinsic value; Munger said no, you're too optimistic, using a lower growth rate his intrinsic value was $50. But the share price was selling at 35. So it doesn't really matter. That's why we want to buy when the price is way below intrinsic value, for a margin of safety: even if we misestimate or are too optimistic, we buy cheap enough that we are still safe.

#### [29:43] Pessimistic and doomsday scenarios
What he calculated is the base case (base assumption): $655.97 with the growth rates used. Can you be more pessimistic? Yes.
- More pessimistic: instead of the average of the three, take the lowest first-five-year growth rate, 14.94. For years 6 to 10, instead of the same number, go to Stock Oracle company metrics, projected growth, and use the long-term growth rate, 13.02%, which is lower. Intrinsic value is now $600 (the more conservative one). Buying at $514, he's still getting a 14% discount, so still pretty safe.
- Doomsday scenario: after the first five years the growth rate drops by half. 14.94 divided by two is 9.97. Putting 9.97, intrinsic value becomes $545.50 (very conservative). Even so, he's still buying 5% below intrinsic value. These are the kind of stocks he likes to buy because it's pretty safe even if things don't work out as planned.

#### [32:55] Stock Oracle Pro cross-check
With a Stock Oracle paid Pro account, click intrinsic value and under discounted free cash flow it automatically calculates the intrinsic value, pretty close to the manual calculation, with possible slight differences due to rounding errors. In the Pro account you can also scroll down to an in-built intrinsic value calculator, change it to the discounted free cash flow method, and all numbers are preset (pulled from the balance sheet and cash flow statement). You can change them, for example change the rate to 9% or anything else, and it recalculates automatically based on your own assumptions if you want to be even more conservative.

# Part 7 — Lessons 38–41

### 38 - Discounted Cash Flow Method Part 2

#### [00:00] What if free cash flow is not consistently increasing?
In the previous lesson he said that to use this method you have to ensure the free cash flow is increasing consistently, at least for the last 5, 10 years. What if it is not? Remember that free cash flow = cash flow from operations minus capital expenditures (CAPEX). If free cash flow is inconsistent because cash from operations is inconsistent, you can't use this method at all. You have to use a different method or forget the stock and look for another one with consistent cash flow growth. However, if cash flow from operations is consistent but CAPEX is not, that is fine.

#### [00:58] Inconsistent CAPEX in great companies
A great company's CAPEX may not always be consistent. CAPEX is the amount of money a company invests in new plants, new machinery and new equipment. Some companies have more or less the same CAPEX every year, so it is very consistent. Others in certain years invest heavily (build data centers, lots of R&D), so CAPEX goes up and free cash flow goes down a lot; in other years they don't have much R&D or factories being built, CAPEX is very low and free cash flow goes up. For such companies free cash flow is inconsistent because CAPEX is inconsistent.

Example: many hyperscalers like Microsoft, Meta Platforms, Google, or even Amazon recently spent a lot of money building AI data centers to strengthen their economic moat for the future and have higher growth. Because CAPEX went up a lot recently, their free cash flow dropped significantly, so it may not be consistent recently. In such cases he is not concerned at all, because the underlying business is doing very well and making a lot of money from cash from operations; it is the recent CAPEX increase that caused free cash flow to drop.

#### [02:29] The fix: normalize CAPEX with the five-year average
If CAPEX is not consistent or suddenly went up a lot because of new investments, he takes the five-year average of the CAPEX: add the CAPEX for the last five years and divide by five to normalize it. The free cash flow used to value the company is then the current cash from operations minus the five-year average CAPEX.

#### [03:21] Amazon example: why it needs normalizing
Amazon's cash from operations (orange) is increasing fairly consistently, but its free cash flow (dark green) goes up, plunges and goes negative, goes up positive, then plunges again, so it is not consistent. The reason is that CAPEX has been increasing a lot recently: in recent years from spending $50,000 (million) to $150,000 (million), tripling their CAPEX because they are building data centers to strengthen their moat and investing a lot in robots and automation.

#### [04:13] Contrast: Singapore Airlines
If cash from operations is not increasing consistently, as in Singapore Airlines, you can't even use this method. Its operating cash flow goes sideways, drops, goes up and drops again. He would never buy the stock in the first place because it fails one of his key criteria: it does not have consistently increasing revenue, net income and cash flow from operations. Its net income (light green) goes down, up, down; very inconsistent. If forced to value Singapore Airlines, the only method he could use is the price to sales ratio method, which is taught later and is used for companies that are very cyclical and have inconsistent profits.

#### [05:30] Valuing Amazon: inputs
Step by step in the calculator: name Amazon, symbol AMZN, method discounted free cash flow. Usually he keys in the most recent free cash flow (Stock Oracle or any financial website such as Yahoo Finance: financials, cash flow statement, annual figures, free cash flow). Here free cash flow went negative 14 billion, negative 16 billion, then 32 billion, dropped to 7 billion. Very inconsistent, so the most recent free cash flow can't be used: it would make the intrinsic value artificially very low because of the high CAPEX. So normalize CAPEX.

Free cash flow = cash flow from operations minus CAPEX. Take the five-year average CAPEX: 61,053 + 63,645 + 52,729 + 82,999 [filled from earlier pass; as heard "8299", and 82,999 reconciles with the stated average of 78,449] + 131,819, divided by 5, gives an average CAPEX of $78,449 million (five-year average CAPEX). The most recent cash flow from operations is 139,514 (increasing consistently, so fine). Normalized free cash flow = 139,514 minus 78,449 = 61,065 (in millions), which he punches in. The rest is no different.

- Total debt: from the balance sheet, always the latest point in time, i.e. the latest quarter. Short-term debt plus long-term debt: (short-term debt 2,748 [filled from earlier pass; heard as "7", and 2,748 + 65,648 = 68,396]) plus 65,648 = 68,396.
- Cash: under assets, cash and investments, latest quarter: 123,329.
- Growth rate: assumptions of how much free cash flow grows over the next 20 years. For the next five years, look at three places and average (as done for MasterCard). Stock Oracle: company metrics, projected growth; the projected 3 to 5 year EPS growth rate is used as a proxy for the free cash flow growth rate because long term free cash flow and earnings should grow in line. Figure: 18.95% (the longer-term growth rate shown is 13.2%). FinViz: 18.42%. Zacks: 17.15%. Average of 18.95 + 18.42 + 17.15 = 18.17% for the next five years.
- Following five years: base case is the same growth rate (18.17), but remember the maximum is 15: anything above 15 is capped at 15. Last 10 years: 4%, as always.
- Shares: Overview, shares outstanding (diluted average) 10,863 million.
- Discount rate: depends on beta. Amazon beta 1.4, so discount rate 7.4%.

#### [15:10] Amazon result
Intrinsic value $206; current share price $214.33. On the base case, slightly overvalued by 3.99%.

#### [16:22] Using cash flow from operations instead of free cash flow
If a business has inconsistent CAPEX but very consistent cash flow from operations, sometimes he values with cash flow from operations instead of free cash flow. This can be done only if the cash flow from operations is very close to net income, i.e. not more than 20% above net income. Warren Buffett has said before that he doesn't deduct capital expenditures in his valuation if the capital expenditures are growth CAPEX and not maintenance CAPEX.

#### [17:03] Apple example
Apple's operating cash flow (orange) is very close to net income: 135 billion versus 117.78 billion (135,472 vs 117,77x); dividing 135,472 by the net income figure gives less than 20% difference, so operating cash flow can be used.

In the calculator: AAPL, method discounted cash flow from operations.
- Cash flow from operations: cash flow statement, annual, latest 12 months, net operating cash flow 135,472.
- Debt: balance sheet, quarterly, under liabilities, short-term debt plus long-term debt: 13,824 + 76,685 = 90,509 [as heard "13824 plus 76 685, 9509"; filled from earlier pass: total 90,509].
- Cash and short-term investments (under assets): 66,907.
- Growth: average of Stock Oracle, FinViz, Zacks. Stock Oracle (company metrics, next 3 to 5 years): 12.31%. FinViz: 11.23%. Zacks: 13.33%. Average 12.29%. Base case: same number for the next five years, 4% for the last 10 years.
- Shares outstanding (Overview): 14,810.36 (million). Beta 1.1, so discount rate 6.6%.

Result: intrinsic value $256; latest price 260.83. Slightly overvalued by 1.8%.

#### [22:33] Why Stock Oracle shows a lower value
Stock Oracle's own value is 190; the reason is very simple: it is probably using a much lower growth assumption. A more conservative valuation: instead of 12.29 use the lowest growth, 11.23, and assume that after five years the growth divides by two (11.23 / 2 = 5.61, "super conservative"). That gives an intrinsic value of 199.98, roughly about $200. So Stock Oracle's valuation is even more conservative. Intrinsic value is not one magical number; it's always a range based on your assumptions. The most important thing for him is to only buy if the share price is low enough to have a margin of safety. Would he buy Apple at $260? No, because even at the optimistic 256 it is not cheap. But if Apple drops below 190, much lower than the conservative or base case valuation, he'll be attracted to add more shares.

So far US listed stocks; now a non-US listed stock (Singapore Exchange, India, Hong Kong, Australian exchange). Singapore tickers are alphabets and numbers; Hong Kong tickers are numbers; all different.

Go to financials: income statement, balance sheet, cash flow statement. Look at the denomination: Singapore dollars and thousands, not millions. First check that the company has consistently increasing cash from operations, otherwise the DCF method can't be used. On Stock Oracle data reads left to right, on Yahoo Finance right to left: 2022, 2023, 2024, 2025 and last 12 months. Operating cash flow: 583, 446 (a slight drop), 615, 841, 826 (values in the order heard). Not up every year, but generally on an uptrend. Unfortunately Yahoo Finance only has four-year data, but good enough. Free cash flow: 539, 392 (slight drop), 551, 773: more or less on an uptrend, good enough. CAPEX: 44, 64, 68, 71: rising pretty nicely, not a sudden jump. If there were a sudden spike he would normalize by taking an average. For now, the normal discounted free cash flow method.

Calculator: name Singapore Exchange, symbol S68, method discounted free cash flow. In previous examples US companies reported in US dollars and stock price was in US dollars. Here the financial statement is in Singapore dollars and the stock price is in Singapore dollars too. This is not always the case. E.g. Bought on the Hong Kong Exchange (ticker 9988): price in Hong Kong dollars. Be clear about the currencies and put in the appropriate one. If they differ, check the exchange rate online and put it in. #### [06:13] Singapore Exchange inputs

- Debt: balance sheet, quarterly, under liabilities. Current (short-term) debt 326,360 [as heard "326-360"], long-term debt 299,667 [heard "29966"/"299667"]; total 332,303 [as heard; sum as stated], thousands, converted to millions = 332.303.
- Cash and short-term investments (under assets), cash and equivalents: 2,046,023 thousand = 2,046.023 million.
- Growth rate: the bad news is that for non-US listed stocks (Singapore, India, Australia) there isn't a lot of information on five-year or long-term growth; non-US listed companies don't tend to give very long-term growth projections. Best available: next one-year growth and historical growth. Yahoo Finance: analysis, growth estimates (scroll down). Only the next year growth: 8.22%. No FinViz or Zacks equivalent for non-US listed stocks. Enter 8.22% for the first five years, assume it continues for the next five years, and 4% for the last 10 years.
- Shares: Yahoo Finance, statistics, implied shares outstanding 1.07 billion; 1 billion = 1,000 million, so 1,070 million.

#### [11:57] Result
Intrinsic value $17.67; current share price 18.37 (heard "1837"). Slightly overvalued by about 3.9%. Would he buy at this price? No. But if it goes way below, e.g. $15 or $12, that's a deal and he may pick it up.

#### [12:13] Third method: discounted net income
He primarily prefers discounted free cash flow because cash is more real than accounting profits. However, certain companies, because of their business model, have cash flow that is not consistent and not always positive: finance companies, insurance companies, brokers and asset management companies. Their cash flow is affected by loans they give and loans redeemed, or customers putting cash into the funds they manage, which distorts the operations. For these he has no choice but to use the discounted net income method, but net income must be increasing consistently.

#### [13:24] BlackRock example
BlackRock is the world's biggest asset management company; not to be confused with Blackstone (more private equity). BlackRock is asset management, mostly ETFs and public assets. Ticker BLK. On financials (with a Pro subscription), net income in green is increasing pretty consistently; doesn't go up every year but generally an uptrend, and more consistent than operating cash flow, which is a lot more volatile. Always use what is more consistent.

Calculator: BlackRock, BLK, discounted net income method.
- Net income, last 12 months, from the income statement (not cash flow): 5,553 million.
- Total debt (balance sheet, latest quarter, short-term plus long-term): long-term debt 12,768; no short-term debt, so 12,768.
- Cash and investments, latest quarter: 11,468.
- Growth: company metrics, projected growth rate; Stock Oracle (data from FactSet) 3 to 5 year projected growth 11.53%. He could average with Zacks and FinViz but, already knowing how, just uses Stock Oracle's. Same growth for the next five years, 4% for the last 10 years.
- Shares outstanding (overview): 165.4 million; beta 1.49. Discount rate 7.7 (said "7.5, sorry, 7.7", the closest).
- Financial statements and share price are both in US dollars.

#### [18:36] Result
Intrinsic value shown as "$7.95" in the transcript [filled from earlier pass: $795]; current share price $967. About 21% overvalued. Stock Oracle's intrinsic value on net income is a lot higher than his calculation, 960 [as heard], because it uses a higher growth rate: the projected growth rate he used was 11.53%, but there's also a projected longer-term growth rate of 16% (16.07), which Stock Oracle probably used, more optimistic. You can use it too; it depends how optimistic or conservative you want to be. Punching 16.07 into the six to 10 year growth gets above $900. There's no right or wrong, it's a range. At the current $967, is it a really good deal? Not really; it still looks slightly expensive. He would probably only be interested if it goes much lower, to maybe 700 plus or even below $700.

### 40 - Price Book Ratio & Banks

#### [00:00] Method 4: price to book ratio, and when to use it
Method 4 of valuation is the price to book value method, using the price to book ratio. We use it when the company has no profits, when the company is making losses. Why invest in a company that is losing money? Ordinarily we only invest in companies where sales, profits and cash flow are going up consistently. But there are times when we may want to invest in a company that is losing money because we think it is losing money temporarily. Examples: banks losing money during the great financial crisis was a temporary situation; certain companies losing money temporarily during the COVID-19 pandemic. We know it's temporary and it will recover eventually; in the meantime it's losing money but we think it's still a good company.

Another situation: a company that is not making money but is deeply undervalued, i.e. the share price is way below the liquidation value. Liquidation value = if the business were to close down and sell everything and clear its debts, what's left for shareholders. If we can buy at a price 50% below the liquidation value, that's a good deal, because even if the business closes down we get more than we paid. These are exceptional circumstances.

How to value the stock then? We can't use PE ratio (no earnings), can't use discounted cash flow or discounted net income because they're negative. The only thing to compare the share price to is the book value of the shares.

When to use price to book ratio:
1. Companies that are losing money temporarily.
2. Banks. Banks are a very unique animal. For banks we don't use discounted cash flow or discounted net income; we simply measure the price to book ratio of the bank and compare it to its historical average to see if it's over or undervalued.
3. Property developers or REITs (real estate investment trusts). Buying a REIT is essentially buying a portfolio of properties; to know if it's expensive or cheap we find the book value: if the REIT closed down, sold all its properties and paid off the debt, what's left.

#### [03:38] What book value means
The price to book ratio compares the share price you're paying to the book value per share. Book value basically means the liquidation value of the company: if the company closes down it sells all its assets (property, machinery, equipment), collects the cash and pays off all its debts; what's left is the liquidation value, also known as book value.

Calculation: take total assets minus intangible assets like goodwill and brand (because that disappears when the company closes down) and deduct all the debt, all total liabilities. What's left is the book value of the whole company. Divide by the total number of shares to get book value per share: what's left behind for the shareholder per share. Formula: price to book ratio = share price divided by book value per share.

Example: if the company closed down, sold everything, paid the debt, and what's left is $100 million (liquidation value), and there are 100 million shares outstanding, the book value per share is a dollar. Theoretically, if the company closed down you should get a dollar back. If the share price is 50 cents, price to book ratio = 0.50 / 1 = 0.5. That's a pretty good deal: paying 50 cents for a dollar's worth of a share even at liquidation. When you invest in a company losing money there is a risk it could close down or go bankrupt; you want to make sure that even if it goes bust and sells everything, what you get is a dollar and you pay less than a dollar.

#### [07:17] Distressed businesses and the 0.5 rule
When using the price to book ratio to value a loss-making company, ideally we want the price to book ratio to be 0.5 or less. The first reason to use the method is valuing a company that is losing money (no net income, negative cash flows). These are distressed businesses, usually cyclical businesses currently unprofitable because of an industry recession or some crisis; as mentioned, during COVID-19 a lot of retailers lost money; you think it's temporary and they will recover. You want to pay less than the liquidation value; investors may see a good bargain if the price to book ratio is below 0.5, i.e. buying at 50 cents to the dollar.

Example: during the great financial crisis, back in 2009, he remembers buying US banks like Bank of America when the price to book ratio was 0.4, i.e. 40 cents to the dollar. Even if Bank of America closed down and sold everything, theoretically he would get a dollar for every 40 cents paid.

#### [08:30] Using it for profitable companies: banks
Price to book ratio is not just for unprofitable companies; also for profitable companies like banks. For some reason most analysts, including himself, use price to book ratio to value banks even when very profitable. The fair price to book ratio in general for banks is between 1.2 to 1.4; within that range the bank is fairly valued. If below 1.2 or below 1, the bank is really cheap, really undervalued. That is in general. All banks are different because they have different growth rates, so a more accurate way is to compare the current price to book ratio of the bank to its five-year average: above the five-year average means overvalued, below means undervalued.

#### [09:55] Bank of America example
Current share price 48.56 (heard "4856"). Overview, scroll down: book value per share 38.44 (heard "3844"). Price to book ratio = 48.56 / 38.44 = 1.26. Under valuation ratios, price to book for the most recent quarter is shown as 1.25; sometimes a rounding difference; more or less take 1.25 or 1.26. Expensive or cheap? Compare with the 5-year average: company metrics, scroll down to valuation or price ratios, price to book ratio. The last five years: 1.08, 1.01, 1.24, 1.43, 1.26; it goes up and down. The five-year average is 1.24. The current 1.26 is slightly above, so Bank of America is just slightly overvalued.

Intrinsic/fair value from price to book: take the last five-year average price to book ratio and multiply by the current book value per share. 1.24 x 38.44 = fair value of 47.67, the so-called intrinsic value. The current share price 48.56 is slightly above the fair value, so slightly overvalued.

#### [13:46] Singapore banks: dividend yield first
He is a very big fan of the three Singapore banks and tends to buy them mainly for dividend and not so much for capital gain, although they do give pretty good capital gain as well, especially DBS, a pretty high growth bank. Because he invests primarily for dividends, there are two things he looks at.

First, the dividend yield. It depends on interest rates, but recording in 2026 March he is looking for a dividend yield of at least 4.5% to 5%. Anything below that he is not going to take. Example: UOB, ticker U11 on the Singapore Exchange, on Yahoo Finance under statistics: dividend yield currently 4.3%, lower than what he wants (at least 4.5 to 5%). How much must the share price drop? Take the dividend per share based on the last 12 months, $1.56 (what the company pays in one year), and divide by the yield wanted: 1.56 / 0.045 = $34.67 needed to get a 4.5% yield. The current UOB share price as he records is $36, still too expensive; it needs to drop to $34 or lower to get at least a 4.5% yield.

#### [16:20] Singapore banks: fair value from price to book
Second, the intrinsic value based on price to book ratio: take the five-year average price to book ratio and multiply by the current book value per share, same as for Bank of America. Current book value per share is on Stock Oracle or Yahoo Finance (statistics, scroll down, under balance sheet): UOB book value per share 29.35 (heard "2935").

Five-year average price to book: calculate yourself, especially for non-US listed stocks (Hong Kong, Singapore, India, Malaysia anywhere). Get the data on TradingView: stock UOB Singapore U11, indicators, price to book ratio, set to five years; it goes up and down. Simple way for a five-year average: find the price to book ratio on the first day of the year for the last five years, add together and divide by five. Recording in 2026: January 2, 2026 (mouse roughly there) price to book 1.14; January 2025: 1.3; do it for 2024, 23, 22 and average. He already did it, shown on his slides (the other years' values were shown on screen, not stated aloud, except 1.09 mentioned later). The five-year average price to book ratio is 1.18. Multiply 1.18 by the current book value per share: fair value for UOB is 34.63 (heard "3463").

The current share price, even though it has dropped quite a bit, is still above the fair value, above $36 [as stated], a bit overvalued. But for Singapore banks he is willing to buy a bit overvalued, as long as he gets a good dividend yield of at least 4.5 to 5%. He doesn't mind paying slightly above fair value, but not too much; if too high above fair value, he won't buy.

#### [20:53] Price to book ratio calculator and standard deviation range
What is too high, what is too low? He uses the price to book ratio calculator, found under the intrinsic value calculator in one of the tabs. It doesn't just calculate fair value but also gives the plus one and minus one standard deviation. For UOB: book value per share 29.35 (from Yahoo Finance or any website), and the price to book ratio for the last five years: 1.14, 1.3, 1.09 (and two more years shown on screen) from TradingView on 1 January of each year. Key in the five numbers and the calculator gives the fair value plus the plus one and minus one standard deviation. That tells him he is willing to buy UOB above $34 but not more than 37; still willing to buy within this range, but only if he gets a dividend yield of at least 4.5 to 5%.

How often to update these values? Once a quarter is fine, but he does it every month. If you subscribe to the Ultimate Investors Playbook you get access to all his workings, research, buy levels, fair values, everything; every month he does an overview of all these stocks.

#### [22:43] OCBC example
His latest calculation for OCBC, based on exactly what he taught: current fair value 16.39 (heard "1639"), and the current share price of $20 is overvalued. Ideally he would buy when it gets below the fair value of 16.39, so it has quite a bit to drop. As said, if he gets a good yield he doesn't mind buying slightly above fair value. That is the plus one standard deviation price and the minus one standard deviation price: willing to buy at fair value but within one standard deviation, which is not more than $19.25 (stated); it is still above that, so way too expensive. His 5% yield line is $16.40: only when OCBC drops to 16.40 or lower will he get a 5% yield. He has identified four levels to add shares: 17.87, 16.35, 14.40 and 13.48 (heard 1787, 1635, 1440, 1348). These buy levels are based on technical analysis (technical support levels), taught in the next lesson, but the support levels must also give a good yield. Buying at 17.87 is within one standard deviation and he'd get probably about 4.5% yield. So he combines fundamentals and technicals to identify buy levels; more in lessons to come.

### 41 - REITs & Price Sales Ratio

#### [00:00] Valuing REITs
In the last video: price to book ratio can value loss-making companies, banks, and (this video) REITs, real estate investment trusts. Buying a REIT means effectively buying a portfolio of properties, and one of the best ways to value a portfolio of properties is the price to book ratio as well as the dividend yield. He tends to specialize in Singapore-listed REITs because they give very high dividend yield and are tax-free. To determine if a REIT is overvalued or undervalued he looks at two things.

Number one, the dividend yield: he wants at least 5% dividend yield in today's interest rate environment (this may change in the future). He takes the dividends per share, called DPU (distributions per unit) in REIT terms, and divides by 5% to give the maximum share price he is willing to pay to get a 5% yield.

Number two, the REIT's price to NAV ratio. NAV stands for net asset value. For Singapore-listed REITs, net asset value and book value are the same thing, used interchangeably, so price to NAV is the same as price to book ratio. US REITs are slightly different, but he focuses on Singapore listed because that's what he personally invests in.

NAV = same as book value: the estimated market value of the REIT's total assets minus the value of the total liabilities, i.e. market value of all the properties it owns minus all the liabilities. Net asset value divided by number of shares gives NAV per share. Price to NAV and price to book can be used interchangeably for REITs since the book value for REITs is marked to market. If price to NAV (price to book) equals one, the REIT is selling at the market value of its properties less the debt. At 1.2, the REIT is selling 20% above the market value of its properties less the debt. As a general rule of thumb, we usually don't pay more than a price to book ratio of 1.2 times. However, there are exceptions: certain REITs have high double-digit growth in dividends or DPU, and the price to book or price to NAV ratio could sometimes be higher, 1.2 going up to 1.5, which is still acceptable. That is just a general rule; the best thing is to calculate the average price to book ratio for the individual REIT and from there ascertain the fair value, exactly as for the banks in the previous video.

#### [03:50] Case study: CapitaLand Ascendas REIT
A very well-known Singapore REIT, CapitaLand Ascendas REIT. On TradingView put in this REIT, go to indicators and add price to book ratio; you see it over the last 10 years. He finds the 10-year average price to book ratio. The proper way: take the price to book ratio every year for the last 10 years like for the banks (25, 24, 23 ...), add the 10 years together, divide by 10 to get the 10-year average.

Shortcut (he is "a bit lazy" sometimes), which gives a very similar number: take the highest point of the 10-year price to book ratio, 1.61, and the lowest point, 1.03. Add the high and the low and divide by 2, giving 1.32, which is roughly, not exactly, the 10-year average. So for this REIT the average price to book ratio is 1.32. Below that, as now, it's cheap; above 1.32 it's expensive. A very simple way to value a REIT: is it above or below the 10-year average?

For a fair/intrinsic value: take the 10-year average price to book 1.32 and multiply by the book value per share (same as for the bank). Book value per share for this REIT, from Yahoo Finance, is $2.27. 1.32 x 2.27 = 2.99. The fair value is 2.99, marked in green; any time it's below that the REIT is undervalued. At the time of recording it is selling at 2.64, undervalued from a price to book perspective.

From a yield perspective: dividend yield from Yahoo or any financial website: the current yield is 5.759%, better than 5%, which means the current share price is attractive as well. In a nutshell, that is how he values it and checks whether it's an attractive price to buy.

#### [07:23] Last method: price to sales ratio
The last valuation method is the price to sales ratio method, as well as the price to sales growth ratio method. It values a company based on its revenue; not profits, not cash flow. We use it when a company doesn't have consistent profits or consistent cash flow, so the only way to evaluate is using sales revenue. Two circumstances:

1. Speculative growth stocks that are not yet profitable. Many companies in the early stages of growth have growing revenue but have not reached economies of scale, so are not yet profitable. One day they could be profitable and you could start looking at taking a small position first.
2. Companies highly cyclical in nature, like property developers and certain construction companies. Their profits go up, down, up, down depending on the economic cycle. With inconsistent profits you can't really use the price to earnings ratio or discounted net income; it doesn't really work.

Price to sales ratio takes the current share price and gives the price to sales ratio. You don't have to calculate it yourself; you can find it on any financial website, including Stock Oracle.

#### [09:01] Constellation Energy example
Example of inconsistent profits: Constellation Energy. In financials, bar chart, net profit in green goes up, down, down, up, down; very, very inconsistent, so you can use the price to sales ratio method. Price to sales ratio: company metrics, price ratios. From 2022, 23, 24, 25: 1.16, 1.79, 2.99, 4.13, and the most recent number is 3.71. The 5-year average is 2.52. The current price to sales 3.71 is above the five-year average, so it is currently overvalued; if below the five-year average, undervalued.

Fair/intrinsic value = average price to sales ratio (2.52) x revenue per share. Revenue per share for the last 12 months (Stock Oracle, overview): $85.77. 2.52 x 85.77 gives an intrinsic value of about $216. Current share price of Constellation Energy (CEG) is $317, above the intrinsic value, so currently overvalued.

#### [12:42] Price to sales growth (PSG) ratio
Related is the price to sales growth ratio method, not a very common method, but it can be quite useful for speculative growth companies with high growth of 50, 100% revenue growth but not yet profitable. It is like a cousin (of the PEG ratio): take the price to sales ratio and divide by the projected revenue growth rate to get the PSG ratio. If the PSG ratio is between 0.2 to 0.3, it is fairly priced; above 0.3 it is overvalued; below 0.2 it is undervalued.

Example: Nebius (ticker NBIS), a high growth speculative stock right now, very high revenue growth but not yet profitable (revenue growing like crazy, losing money). Company metrics, growth rates, projected revenue growth rate: very high, 144% (later 144.5%). Price to sales ratio, latest: 48.72. PSG = price to sales divided by revenue growth = 48.72 / 144.5 = about 0.34 (also shown ready-calculated). 0.34 tells you it is slightly overvalued, since ideally PSG should be between 0.2 and 0.3; above 0.3 it starts to be overvalued. When it gets below 0.3, that would at least be a safe price to buy.

#### [16:09] Summary of all valuation methods
- Primary default method: DCF, discounted cash flow; he always likes to use the free cash flow of the last 12 months, projected 20 years and discounted to present value.
- If the free cash flow is not consistent because CAPEX is not consistent, he uses cash from operations (discounted) [as spoken: "the discounted net income method" is used for companies where net income is consistent, such as insurance companies or asset managers; the transcript has a gap here]. [Earlier pass reads the sequence as follows: if free cash flow is inconsistent only because CAPEX is, use latest 12-month cash flow from operations minus the five-year average CAPEX, or use cash flow from operations directly only if it is not more than 20% above net income; all of this requires cash flow from operations to be increasing consistently. If it is not, use discounted net income, provided net income is consistently increasing, typically for insurance companies, asset managers, brokers and finance companies.] [from earlier pass]
- Banks are a special case: price to book. Calculate the five-year average price to book ratio and multiply by the current book value per share.
- REITs: same thing; take the 5 or 10 year average price to NAV ratio and multiply by the current NAV per share to get the fair value; also look at the dividend yield.
- Companies with inconsistent profit: price to sales, or price to sales growth ratio.

If you want it done automatically, subscribe to Stock Oracle and click on intrinsic value: it calculates the fair value by each of the methods, then using an algorithm tells you which valuation method is most appropriate, shown as the Oracle value or the intrinsic value based on the algorithm: 557.5 [heard "557... five"; earlier pass has $557], Microsoft is currently undervalued. [Earlier pass gives the Microsoft share price in that example as $405.] [from earlier pass]

#### [19:14] Quiz
Question 1: what type of companies tend to have inconsistent cash flow from operations? Answer: all of the above: property developers, banks and commodity companies. Question 2: a company has inconsistent, not-increasing cash flow from operations but consistently increasing net income; which method? Answer: the net income method (discounted net income).

# Part 8 — Lessons 43–46

### 43 - Reading Charts

#### [00:00] Lesson 6: timing your entries with technical analysis
Lesson 6 is about using chart patterns (technical analysis) to better time entries into the market, whether buy or sell, in order to maximize returns. Why learn it? Because in the short term the stock market is not always driven by logic; it is driven by emotions, or "sentiment". Prices are driven by demand and supply, which are in turn driven by the emotions of fear and greed. When people are excited they buy, buy, buy, and the price can keep going up even though it's overpriced: high can get even higher. When people are scared they sell, and the price keeps going down even though it's a good company and undervalued: low can keep going lower.

Technical analysis helps identify potential reversal points where price will tend to reverse back up. Those are the ideal points to get into. Ideally we don't want to buy a great, undervalued company whose price keeps going lower; we want to buy at a point when the price is going to reverse back up.

There is no 100% certainty in technical analysis. We can't predict the market for certain (said in lesson one). We are looking for high-probability reversal points: when we enter, there is a high chance the price will reverse up at that point. Could it still go lower? Of course. That's why we look for potential bottoms, and why we never go all in at once, since it could still reverse lower. We buy in tranches, three to four tranches, at these reversal levels. This gives a better edge compared to people who buy companies blindly without understanding the emotions driving the price.

#### [02:07] Technical analysis as psychology
Technical analysis is the study of emotional patterns captured on price charts. Looking at a stock chart is like being a psychologist looking at a patient: you read facial expression and body language, deduce emotions and anticipate behaviour. A chart is the facial expression and body language of the stock: are people excited, bullish, confused? What is the most probable short-term movement? By studying chart patterns we can tell if investors are getting more optimistic (bullish) or more pessimistic (bearish), which helps time entries and exits.

Reminder: technical analysis is not about predicting exactly where price is going, because no one can. It is about probability and identifying high-probability outcomes: are prices more likely to keep going up or keep going down? On the example chart he points to a downtrend (touched on in lesson two on ETFs, where he read trend changes using moving averages; this chapter goes more in depth). In a downtrend the probability is that prices keep going lower until the trend changes into an uptrend; once on an uptrend, price has a higher probability of moving higher. So if we want to buy a great, undervalued company, ideally we buy right at the point of reversal, so that the moment we get in it starts to reverse up. We can't always pick the exact bottom; we must be realistic. Sometimes we buy at the bottom and it goes even lower. So buy in tranches, but identify potential reversal points rather than buying randomly and watching price go a lot lower. Entering when price has a higher probability of rising in the short term will, over the long run, maximize returns.

#### [05:09] Investors who ignore charts
Many investors, such as Warren Buffett and Peter Lynch, don't look at charts at all. To them, as long as it's a good company and undervalued, they just buy it and don't care if it keeps going down in the short term, because they take a very long-term perspective: they'll be in the stock 5, 10, 15 years, so if price goes down for the next six months or one year they don't care since it will go up eventually. Nothing wrong with that; you can ignore charts too. What he finds is that combining chart patterns with great, undervalued companies can give better entry points. There's no perfect entry point, but better ones.

#### [05:59] Candlestick basics
Every weekday at 9:30 a.m. Eastern Time (New York time) the market opens; the first transacted price is the opening price. In the McDonald's example on screen, the opening price is $99.70. During the day price can go all the way up, down, up again; at the close (he says 4.30 p.m. Eastern; as transcribed) the last transacted price is the closing price, here $99.99 (transcribed "9999"). The high of the day was $100.30 and the low 99.45. So four prices matter each day: open, high, low, close. Candlestick charts represent these four prices.

How to read a candle: suppose price opens at $10 and closes at $20. Because it closed higher than it opened it's a bullish (up) day, drawn green (some people use white; same thing). The bottom of the body is the opening price ($10) and the top of the body is the closing price ($20). If during the day it went as high as, say, $25, that's the high of the day, shown by the line above the body, called a wick, shadow or tail. If it went as low as $8, that's the day low, the lower wick. The body shows open and close; the upper and lower wick/shadow/tail show the high and low of the day.
