Next day, price opens at $20 but closes at $10: a down, bearish day because it closed lower than the open, drawn in the other colour (red). The wicks again show the high of the day (could again be $25) and the low. (Transcript has a gap here.) For the bearish candle, the top of the body is the open and the bottom is the close; the day's low in this example is $7. [filled from earlier pass]

#### [09:50] The pin bar
For investing, candlestick patterns are not that important, so he won't go into detail, but one special candle to know is the pin bar: a candle with a very small body. In contrast, the body is very big in the other example candle he shows, where price tends to move up. (Transcript garbled here.) The bullish pin bar has a small body and a long lower tail, with the upper tail short or absent, and there is a higher likelihood that price moves up after it. [filled from earlier pass] A pin bar alone is not good enough; it has to appear at a strong level of support (or, for the opposite direction, resistance), which he'll teach later. At resistance with a long upper tail there is a higher chance the price will go lower.

For pin bars, whether bullish or bearish, the colour of the body doesn't matter because the body is so small. What matters is where the tail is. Even if it is red, a long lower tail makes it bullish; a long upper tail makes it bearish.

#### [12:41] Spotting pin bars on Amazon's daily chart
Exercise on an Amazon daily chart (each candle one day): he finds a pin bar; even though red, it's still a bullish pin bar because of a small body and a long lower tail, and price went up after. Another bullish pin bar shows up; again small body. Sometimes there's no upper tail, or a short one; the point is you need a long lower tail, and that's a bullish pattern. Again, a candlestick alone is not enough; it must be at a strong support level or a moving average, and he'll teach drawing support levels later. Another bullish pin bar, still bullish because of small body and lower tail.

#### [13:47] Which time frame and candles to use
You can look at daily candles (each a trading day), weekly candles, or monthly candles, and over different periods: 10-year, 5-year, 1-year. Common question: which period and which candles? Answer: as an investor you should look at all the time frames, each gives a different perspective. When he looks at a stock for the first time he always begins with the 10-year chart with monthly candles, then zooms into a one-year, or sometimes six-month, period looking at daily candles.

#### [15:09] Setting up TradingView
You can use any charting software; every broker has its own. He uses TradingView for demonstration, primarily what he uses. He will show different time frames (monthly, weekly, daily), how to enter stock symbols, how to identify support and resistance levels, and how to install indicators like moving averages. TradingView lets you put in stocks from around the world (US, Singapore, India) and other assets such as gold or even Bitcoin. Go to TradingView.com and open the charts; there's a desktop app for Windows or Mac, or the web version. There's a paid and a free version. The free version has limitations, e.g. only two indicators, whereas a lot of what they do needs more than two, but there's a workaround he'll show later, so you can use multiple indicators on the free version. He personally subscribes to the paid version for flexibility, but it's not a must. On the website go to Products and select Supercharts.

Top left: type the ticker, e.g. Google, G-O-O-G-L, and the chart appears. Your chart may initially not look like his because he's set colours and settings his way. Right-click on the chart (on a Mac, two fingers on the trackpad) and go to Settings. His settings (up to you whether to follow): under Symbol, candle body green and red, borders green and red, wick (upper and lower tail) green and red. Status line: logo, title, bar change values. Scales and lines: currency and unit always visible, scale modes A and L, visible on mouse over, scale placement auto, symbol name and value. Canvas is the background colour; he likes solid white and doesn't like a dark background, "that's just me"; crosshair settings can be copied. Events: he adds dividends (to know the ex-dividend dates), earnings, and latest news. The earlier pass also lists splits among the events, and text and lines set to black on the canvas. [from earlier pass] After OK the chart looks like his.

#### [19:26] Looking at time frames: 10-year monthly
When he looks at a stock for the first time he wants the 10-year monthly candles. Click "All" to show the stock from the beginning of time (could be more than 10 years); it goes to monthly candles. Then he drags it back to about 10 years ago. The current year is 2025, so 10 years back is about 2015, thereabouts. On the right he clicks log (logarithmic) charts, which he prefers, giving a very nice trend. For Google (chart shown), over the last 10 years it is on a long-term up, which is what he always wants to see for an investment.

If the 10-year chart is not going up, he's not interested. Example: Transocean, ticker RIG, oil and gas, whose chart for the last 10 years is not going up; the moment he sees this, he isn't even interested in looking at the business. Another example, from Singapore: Singapore Airlines (he catches himself, "not Singtel, sorry"), whose stock did not go up over 10 years. That tells him these are not long-term performers: "forget about it". He wants a stock that goes up over 10 years, a long-term performer, meaning over the long run the company makes more and more money.

#### [21:34] Reading monthly candles on Google
These are monthly candles, each representing one month. As he speaks (February; the earlier pass dates the recording 11 February 2025), the February candle is still forming and has not closed; look only at candles that have closed. The last closed candle was January. In January the price of Google opened at one level (pointed on screen), maybe went down to a low, went up to a high, and closed at another level; levels shown on screen, not stated aloud. January was a very bullish month, with price closing way above the open. The previous month (he says November, as transcribed) was a bit bearish: on the 1st it opened at the top of the body and closed lower, going as high and as low as the wicks show. (Transcript gap.) The month before that, December, was also very bullish: it opened on 1 December at the bottom of the body, ran up to the month's high, and closed on 31 December at the top of the body. [filled from earlier pass]

#### [24:00] Weekly candles
Now weekly candles, each representing a week. The previous week went up to a high, down to a low, and by Friday closed at a level (shown on screen, not stated). Looking at it as downtrend/uptrend: very clear going up. There's an uptrend, a downtrend, an uptrend, another uptrend, but overall the uptrends are longer than the downtrends.

#### [25:13] One-year daily candles
Zoom in: click one year and it automatically shows daily candles. Each candle is one weekday. As he records it is a Tuesday, market not yet open; the previous day's candle is Monday's, then Friday's, Thursday's, Wednesday's, Tuesday's. There's a big gap between Tuesday and Wednesday. On shorter time frames you will see a lot of these gaps, where the opening price (of Google) is far from the prior close. Sometimes when a company announces earnings and the market feels earnings are not as good as expected, price gets down; if better than expected, price goes up. It's not a guarantee. For example, yesterday McDonald's announced terrible earnings (revenue down), and the price [outcome garbled in transcript; earlier pass says the price went up anyway, as an example of the market being irrational in the short term] [filled from earlier pass]. [Earlier pass has the Google gap down between Wednesday's close and Thursday's open, where NEW has Tuesday and Wednesday.]

In the short term the market can be totally irrational, so don't be too concerned about short-term ups and downs because of news and earnings; it's really not important. But it shows this stuff happens, and it's an opportunity for investors to buy: if after the gap down you realize Google is now undervalued, then since in the long run Google is a great company and you'll make more money over the long run, these short-term drops are opportunities.

#### [28:45] Daily trends and summary of time frames
On the daily time frame you see shorter-term trends: a bit of an uptrend here, a bit of a downtrend there, another uptrend. He'll cover in detail later how to identify uptrends, downtrends and sideways consolidation patterns; for now just understand time frames. Summary: 10-year monthly, 5-year weekly, 1-year daily.

#### [29:13] Icons at the bottom of the chart
"E" means earnings: on this day the company reported earnings, and sometimes after earnings the price may jump up or down. You can see upcoming earnings if not already reported. For example, putting in Nvidia: it has not yet reported earnings and will shortly, so an E in a square shows, with earnings to be reported in a couple of days. If already reported, you get a green E. Hovering the mouse shows that Nvidia reports on 26 February, Wednesday. A particular sign means it reports after the market closes on that day; sometimes it reports before the market opens.

Back to Google: another icon appears when price suddenly has a drop or increase and you wonder whether news caused it; click on it and it shows all the news related to the stock on that day. Pretty useful as well.

A "D" icon marks a dividend, usually the ex-dividend date; you buy before the ex-dividend date to receive the dividend. [from earlier pass]

The earlier pass also notes the period buttons: "All" switches to monthly candles, "5Y" automatically shows weekly candles, and "1Y" automatically shows daily candles. [from earlier pass]

### 44 - Support & Resistance

#### [00:00] Adjusting for dividends
On the bottom right of TradingView there is a tab that adjusts the data for dividends. When analyzing an individual stock, he does not adjust for dividends. When comparing the performance of different stocks, he does adjust, because he wants to see the eventual performance of a stock relative to others after accounting for dividends.

Example: Google by itself, no adjustment. But comparing Google with DBS (a Singapore bank that pays a lot of dividends) over the last, say, five years: Google gained 152% versus DBS 92%. You might think Google is a lot better because it gave more returns, but DBS gives a lot of dividends whereas Google doesn't pay much. To be fair you must adjust for dividends. Clicking the adjustment, the 152% versus 92% changes and they are almost neck and neck: including dividends received, a DBS investor's returns are just 4% different from Google's. So it's very important to adjust for dividends when comparing two or more stocks, but not when looking at one stock by itself.

#### [02:05] What he looks for first: swing points and support/resistance
The first thing he does with charts is identify swing high and swing low points, and support and resistance levels, on all time frames, especially weekly and daily. Why? Because this tells him where the price is likely to reverse. If price is coming down, at a support level it is more likely to reverse back up; if going up, at a resistance level it is more likely to reverse down. Not a guarantee, not 100%, but high probability.

#### [03:08] Swing highs and swing lows
Price going up then coming down: that's a swing high point. Price going down then going up: a swing low point. On the chart in front of him he identifies several: an obvious swing high (price goes up and reverses down), a swing low, then another swing high, another swing low, another swing high, and more further back.

#### [04:22] Support levels
Price comes down then goes up: a swing low. If price comes down again and at the same swing low reverses up again, you have two swing lows roughly at the same price; you can draw a line across them, and that is a potential support level. If price goes down again to that same price and reverses up again, it becomes a confirmed support level. A support level is a price where every time the stock goes down it reverses back up. Why? Take $50 as the example: at $50 there are a lot of buyers waiting to buy, which he calls a demand zone. When price drops to $50 the buyers rush in, buy, buy, buy, demand exceeds supply and price reverses up.

Why identify support? It tells where price could potentially bottom after a wave down and reverse back up; not a guarantee, but a potential bottom.

When a stock is in a very strong trending phase, like Google right now (very strong uptrend, very strong downtrend, very strong uptrend), you won't see many support levels; they tend to happen when price goes into consolidation. Zooming back on Google, though, there were some support levels that could have been identified. Price drops to a swing low, goes up, drops to roughly the same point (doesn't have to be exact), goes up, and drops again to roughly the same point, about fifty dollars, and goes up again: a support level. Once you have two swing lows close by, you can connect them: using the drawing tools on the left, click horizontal line and draw it at the first swing low. Price comes back to $49.99, close to $50, and reverses up again. Two swing lows at roughly the same level, draw a line: a support level, a demand zone. Price waves down again and as it comes to this level ("4990" in the transcript; read as $49.90 [filled from earlier pass]; the earlier pass has $49.90 where the $49.99 above appears), it "magically" goes up again. Not magic: at that level there are many buyers queuing; the moment price drops to $50 a lot of buy orders come in, demand exceeds supply, price reverses up.

Why it matters: if investing in a stock with a support level, he waits for price to hit the support level and starts buying, to catch the reversal up, rather than buying while price is still coming down, knowing there's a high chance it goes down to the support level before reversing higher. No guarantees, but it's a better, optimal, probabilistic entry point. Price doesn't always hit the exact point; sometimes it goes close, buyers anticipate that support is near and buy early, and it reverses up, so it may fall short of the line; but it shows a strong level of buying. That's a support level.

#### [09:16] McDonald's example
Support levels he draws on the McDonald's chart: initially price dropped at about $230, reversed up, came down again to a similar level and reversed, again to that level and reversed. So a level of support, with many buyers waiting at 2.30 (about $230), creating a demand zone. The price was supported there too: dropped, bounced, bounced, bounced. Then price went down, made a swing low, went up, came down, and at that same point reversed up: another support level over there. A chart can have many support levels where buyers wait and price is likely to reverse. Later he'll show that when he buys shares he always buys at support levels, since these are areas where price is more likely to reverse.

#### [11:29] Thermo Fisher example
Thermo Fisher, weekly candles (daily also works; support and resistance appear on different time frames; weekly is usually a bit clearer). Price comes down to about 520 and reverses up; drops to 520 again, reverses up; comes down just below 520, reverses up; drops, reverses up. So somewhere around there, about 520, is a significant level of support, where price tends to reverse up again and again.

Support is not a line though we draw a line; it's more like a zone. Sometimes price drops to 520 and doesn't reverse right at 520; it may go a bit beyond it, "like a fence": it hits the fence and the fence moves a bit, a bit of a buffer, goes slightly below 520 then reverses up. Other times it doesn't touch 520, goes near and reverses. So think of it as a zone, with a buffer where price tends to be repelled.

Another support: price went down to about 439 and reversed up, telling you many buyers bought and demand exceeded supply. It went down again, hit about 439, went up again: a significant support. Many months later, when price goes down again, it gets to that support level and reverses up again: the market has a long-term memory. These support levels help us guess where price is going to stop, bottom and go back up.

#### [14:57] IWM (small cap ETF) example, and where to draw the line
IWM, the small cap ETF: price goes down, makes a swing low, makes another swing low near the first. Draw a horizontal line with the drawing tool. Price made a swing low, went up, came down, and when it approached the same level at about 162.70 (said "162, 70"), it reversed up again; down again, reversed up; down again, "right on the dot" reversed up again. That 162.72 is a significant level of support. There was also a bit of support elsewhere, and zooming into daily candles makes it much clearer, showing how significant the level was.

Where to draw the line (all the way down, or here?): he puts it at the point of best fit, where it touches as many swing lows as possible. A line touching only one swing low is not as significant. The chosen line touches this swing low, this one, is almost at another, touches this and this: all five swing lows. The more swing lows it touches, the more significant the line. That's how he decides where to put a support level. Price bounces off, bounces off, bounces off, bounces off, bounces off, and then comes down and right at 210 reverses up again. Not magic: the market has a memory that at 210 a lot of buyers tend to buy, and that drives the change in sentiment of that stock.

#### [18:39] Resistance levels, and flip of support/resistance
Review: support levels are price levels where price drops, hits the level and reverses back up, repeatedly; if at least two or more swing lows coincide at the same or similar level, you can draw a line called the support line or support level.

The opposite: price goes up and at a certain price drops back down (a swing high), goes up again and reaches a very similar swing high level and comes back down. Two or more swing highs coinciding at a similar level form a resistance level. Afterwards, when price goes up again, the resistance level has a tendency of reversing the price back down. Why? At this level there are many sellers queuing to sell; call it $100. When price reaches $100 the sellers rush in, sell, sell, sell, supply exceeds demand, and price reverses down.

Support becomes resistance and resistance becomes support. Example: price goes up, comes down, goes up, comes down, repeatedly: a resistance level. If it's a good company, eventually the price breaks out of that resistance. If after that price comes back down, when it reaches that resistance level there's a high chance it bounces off and goes up. "This resistance, once broken, becomes a support level. Very important concept. Please write this down... When a resistance level is broken, that previous resistance will become a support level."

#### [22:32] IWM resistance
Back on IWM, with the earlier support levels marked, there is a resistance level: price goes up and reverses back down (a swing high), goes up again, and at 198 comes back down again; this is a resistance level, and goes up again at 198 and reverses down again. As long as you have two or more swing highs coinciding at the same level, draw a line and mark it as resistance. When this resistance is eventually broken: price tried to break out but came back down, broke out, came back down again, but notice this resistance has now become a support level. Every time price goes down it hits the support, goes up, hits the support, goes up, and now it's beginning to trend up.

#### [23:53] Thermo Fisher resistance
On Thermo Fisher with support levels already drawn: over here was previously a resistance. Price went up to 520, reversed down; went back to 520 and reversed down. Once price breaks out of the resistance and comes back down, the previous resistance becomes a new support level. That's why it's important to look at where previous resistance and swing highs were: they could be future support levels where we add shares, a very good indicator of future support.

Another resistance: price goes up to about 604, reverses down, goes up to that same level, reverses down, same level again, then again. 604 is a very strong level of resistance; price has not been able to get above it for quite a couple of months, in fact for one, two, three years it's been below that level. Thermo Fisher, in his opinion, is a great company; over time it will make more and more money and increase in value, so eventually it will break 604. Exactly when he doesn't know. Once it breaks that level, this very strong resistance will become future support, supporting the stock price.

#### [26:44] Google: prior resistance became support
Back to the Google example with support levels drawn: prior to this support, it was a resistance level. Price went up, hit "4990" (as transcribed; read as $49.90 [filled from earlier pass]), came back down, went up, hit it, came back down, hit it again and was rejected a bit, then broke out. So it was a previous resistance, and once broken, that previous resistance at 4990 (i.e. $49.90 [filled from earlier pass]) became a new support level. Support and resistance levels can be identified and drawn on both daily and weekly charts; once in a while on monthly charts, but usually not, because on monthly charts price is normally on a very clear uptrend; this normally occurs on shorter time frames like weekly and daily.

#### [27:58] Mercado Libre (MELI), daily candles
Exercise (pause the video and try to draw a support level yourself). His support level is at about 280, "288 thereabouts" (as transcribed). It was a previous resistance: price went up to 280, reversed down, went up, reversed down: two coinciding swing highs, a resistance. Eventually it broke above, and when it came down again, that resistance line became a support. It went up, came down, hit that support and bounced up again. As investors, the purpose of identifying support is: before buying a stock, ideally a great company that's undervalued, it has dropped to a support level and we get in right at the support, giving a high probability of watching the reversal up.

Another support: a swing low with a corresponding swing low there. This support, when broken, becomes resistance. Support when broken becomes resistance; resistance when broken becomes support. And this resistance, once broken, becomes support again, and so on.

#### [30:27] Mercado Libre, weekly candles
Same stock on the weekly time frame; on different time frames you may notice support and resistance in different places. Exercise again. He identifies one: price goes up all the way to about $290 and gets rejected, comes down, goes up to the same swing high, reverses down, same swing high, reverses down. A resistance the price is not yet able to break out of. But if it's a good company with good fundamentals and undervalued, eventually it will break out; once it does, if it waves down again, that resistance becomes support. The support bounces off, hits the same support, bounces off, again, and goes all the way up.

#### [31:50] Walmart, weekly candles
Last example, Walmart on weekly candles (pause and try). He sees a resistance and support: price goes up, makes a swing high, comes down, makes a swing high at the same level, comes down: resistance. Once it breaks out, that resistance level becomes support; now it's hitting the support, hitting the support and eventually bounced back up: a significant level. Another support: price goes down, swing low, goes up, hits a similar swing low, goes up, and so forth: a level of support there as well.

### 45 - Moving Averages

#### [00:00] What a moving average is, and the 50 simple moving average
Besides drawing support and resistance levels, he uses moving averages to help identify potential buy levels. A moving average is a mathematical calculation that averages the price of a stock over a certain period of time. The charting software draws them. He runs through his favorite ones.

First: the 50 simple moving average. In the charting software click Indicators, type moving averages, and select simple moving average. By default it gives the nine-period simple moving average; he wants 50. Click the gear icon (settings), go to Input, and change it to 50; change nothing else. For style, always use blue for the 50 simple moving average ("why? because I said so, so follow it"), slightly thicker, usually the second thickness. That draws the 50 simple moving average.

#### [01:59] Moving averages act as dynamic support
Moving averages are really useful because they act as dynamic support levels. On the Meta chart (he says "matter", i.e. Meta, as the later transcript suggests): price goes up and down, and when it hits the blue line (the 50 moving average) it bounces off; hits the blue line again, bounces; again, bounces; again, bounces, "like magic". "Don't ask me why", but stocks have a tendency to wave down towards their moving averages and use the moving average as support, bouncing off it. So moving averages are areas where he likes to buy stocks, because that's when they tend to bounce and reverse back up after dropping.

Do all stocks hit the 50 moving average and bounce? No, not all the time. Sometimes they hit the 100 moving average, sometimes the 150. That's why he developed a set of favourite moving averages that act as support levels for most stocks.

#### [03:36] His full set: simple and exponential
There are two kinds of moving averages: simple and exponential. No need to worry about the mathematics; just know when to use which.

Simple moving averages he uses:
- 50, in blue
- 100, in orange
- 150, in green
- 200, in red

"Don't ask me why": over the years he found these are the most reliable simple moving averages.

For math geeks, how a simple moving average is calculated: the 50 simple moving average (the blue line) is plotted by taking the last 50 days of the closing price of the stock, adding them together and dividing by 50 equally. This repeats every day, plotting the blue line. Likewise the 200 simple moving average takes the closing prices over the last 200 days, adds them and divides by 200.

#### [05:25] Putting them in one by one
He puts in all the moving averages one by one: the 50 is in; next the 100. Go to Indicators, type simple moving average (default nine), click the settings icon, change to 100, colour orange, thicker line. Next 150: it has to be green, thick line. Finally the 200: red ("200 is always red"), thick line. That's the four simple moving averages: 50, 100, 150, 200. How they're used comes later; for now just put them in.

With the free software you can't put in more than two indicators. Must you pay? No, "we've got you covered": they created one indicator with all the moving averages in it, so on free charting software you can put them all in together. (He covers it shortly, below.) Advantage of putting them in individually: you can choose to look at only the ones you want, e.g. hide all but the 50. With the custom indicator you have to look at all of them together; you can't hide one or two. That's the only drawback.

#### [09:14] Exponential moving averages: 20 and 40
Recap: simple moving averages are the 50, 100, 150 and 200, four of them. He also puts in exponential moving averages. The difference is just the method of calculation. He uses the 20 and 40 EMA. For the 20 EMA the software takes the closing price of the last 20 days but doesn't add it all together and divide by 20; it assigns individual weights to the 20 days, with recent prices weighted more than prices further away, so it's a weighted moving average. He won't go through the exact mathematics because it's not important; all you need to know is how to use it. For the shorter-term moving averages he uses the 20 and 40 EMA.

To put them in: Indicators, type EMA, choose moving average exponential; the default is nine, which he doesn't want. Add one more so there are two to play with. For the first, click the gear icon and change to 20; style red. So as not to confuse it with the 200 simple moving average (also red), use a thin line versus the thick line. That's the 20 EMA. Next the 40 EMA: settings, inputs, change to 40, style blue as well, thin line rather than thick so you can differentiate. So 20 EMA is red thin, 40 EMA is blue thin.

All together six moving averages: the 20 and 40 EMA (the two exponential moving averages) and the 50, 100, 150 and 200 (the simple moving averages). You may be confused about how to use them; that's coming up.

#### [12:22] Saving a template
Don't redo this on every new chart. Save the template: click the Indicator Templates icon (four boxes), Save, name it whatever you like, e.g. "all moving averages". In the future on a new stock, say Apple, whose chart is initially blank, you don't have to re-key everything; just use the "all moving averages" template you saved.

#### [13:26] Free version: the Piranha Profits indicator
If you're on the free version and can't put in more than two moving averages: go to the Piranha Profits website where you're watching this video, Lesson 6, scroll down to "Lesson 6 course notes and resources" and click. The course notes and slides are there, and below them is "PP Multi MA", the Piranha Profits Multiple Moving Averages Indicator. Click the link, it brings you to a page; scroll down and click "Add to favorites". Then back in your charts, click Indicators and under Favorites the PP Multi MA has appeared (he was on Meta); click and all the moving averages are there, no need to put them in one by one. Drawback again: you have to look at all of them together and can't hide one. But on the free version you have to use this indicator.

Now that you've learned to draw support and resistance levels and put in moving averages, the next step is to combine these two powerful concepts to identify the trend of the stock (uptrend, downtrend or consolidation) and how he identifies levels to add shares.
### 46 - Buying on Uptrends

#### [00:00] Three kinds of trend
How do we identify the trend of the stock price? Price doesn't move completely randomly; one pattern is that prices move in trends or cycles. Generally three trends: an uptrend (price generally moves upward), a downtrend (price generally moves downward), and a consolidation pattern (price generally moves sideways). The first thing on a chart is to identify which of these it is.

Why important? An established trend in price is statistically more likely to continue from one interval to the next than to reverse. When a stock is on an uptrend, the probability is that price tends to move higher until the trend changes; on a downtrend, price tends to go lower until the downtrend changes. It's like identifying the river current: if it moves upwards and you jump in, you're more likely to be carried upwards; if it flows downwards, no matter how hard you swim you'll be swept down the river. The chart tells you where the current is flowing, i.e. where price is more likely to move in the short term. Once we identify the trend or its reversal, we can better time entries and exits.

#### [02:07] Uptrend definition
An uptrend is when the price generally moves upward. Do prices still drop in an uptrend? Yes. But every time price drops, it then makes a higher high, and again an even higher high, and again. So price makes a series of higher swing highs. And every time price drops it makes a swing low that's higher than the previous low. Definition: an uptrend is price making a pattern of higher swing highs and higher swing lows. Whenever you see this pattern, the market is bullish on this stock and the probability is that price will continue higher.

On an uptrend prices move in waves: wave up, wave down, wave up, wave down, like a breathing pattern, breathe out, breathe in, and it continues until the trend reverses.

#### [03:55] Where to buy in an uptrend
If the stock you want to buy is on an uptrend, is the optimal time to buy after a wave down or after a wave up? After a wave down, before it waves up. Because if you buy after it has waved up, the tendency after you buy is that it waves down. Ideally you wait for a wave down, buy, and right after you buy it begins to wave up, so at the onset you see a higher price than your entry. Not a guarantee, but it increases returns over time if you enter after wave-down patterns. The first golden rule: if price is on an uptrend, you always want to buy the dip of the uptrend.

Recall from earlier lessons: a dip or wave down of less than 10% is a pullback; more than 10% is a correction. Every year you'll have at least three pullbacks and at least one correction. Those are the times to buy the stock, provided it's a good company and, of course, undervalued.

#### [05:43] Finding the potential bottom of a wave down
How do I know it's the end of the wave down and it won't go lower? No way to predict the absolute bottom; no one can predict for sure. But we can make intelligent probabilistic guesses. We never know the actual bottom, but we can identify potential bottoms and add shares there; if it's not the bottom and it drops more, we add more at the next potential bottom.

How? Moving averages. Often when a stock is on a clear uptrend and waves down, it tends to find support at one of the moving averages.

#### [06:43] Microsoft daily chart: the 50 simple moving average
Microsoft on daily candles. On the daily time frame, pay attention mainly to the simple moving averages: the 50, 100, 150 and 200. Do not really pay attention to the 20 and 40 EMAs ("please take note"); the 20 and 40 EMAs only come into service at the higher time frames, weekly and monthly candles. So on daily candles, look at four moving averages: 50, 100, 150, 200.

In Microsoft during this period, every time price waved down it found support near the 50 moving average: hit the blue line, bounced up; again; again; again; again, "very beautifully". Once you notice Microsoft has the pattern of bouncing off the 50 moving average, a drop back near the 50 is a potential point of entry, provided it's undervalued and a great company and waves down to a support level; that's where he starts adding shares.

#### [08:29] Reading an uptrend from the moving averages
In an uptrend with higher swing highs, higher swing lows, wave up and wave down, notice the moving averages. Two clues:
- Slope: on an uptrend the moving averages tend to slope upwards, the blue line, the orange, green and red all sloping up. When moving averages are all sloping upwards, that indicates an uptrend; the slope of a moving average tells you the direction of the trend.
- Sequence: on a very clear uptrend, the 50 (blue) tends to be above the 100, the 100 above the 150 (green), and that above the 200 simple moving average. The shorter-term moving averages tend to be above the longer-term ones in this very nice sequence, another clue of an uptrend.

This is on the daily time frame; trends can be looked at on all time frames: daily, weekly and monthly.

#### [10:07] Microsoft on another daily period: the 100 moving average
Another daily period for Microsoft. There are times when price finds support at the 50 (blue line), but not all the time; sometimes it breaks below the 50, and then you find support at the lower moving averages. In this period, every time Microsoft waved down it found support at the 100 moving average, the orange line: hit the orange line, bounced up; again; again. The 100 acted as the support.

You may wonder: if price drops and hits the 50, is that the bottom, or will it go to the 100? Answer: we don't know where the bottom is, but we know these moving averages are potential support levels. What he does is simple: when price waves down and hits the first support level, say the 50, he buys a bit first, not all the shares. If it breaks below that moving average, he watches the next one, the 100; if it hits the 100 and bounces, he adds more shares. Every time it hits a support level he slowly adds more, always buying shares over four tranches, until once it bottoms it waves up again.

In the chart: when it waved up and down again, this time it found support at the 50 and bounced up; that could have been a point to buy. If you bought a bit at the 50 and it dropped to the 100, you could have added more. The whole idea is to add at these support levels. To be more conservative, wait for price to hit the support moving average and then wait for a green (bullish) candle first, looking like it's bouncing up, then add in.

#### [13:08] Weekly time frame: the 20 EMA
Same pattern on the weekly time frame, Microsoft again. On weekly and monthly time frames the 20 and 40 exponential moving averages begin to act as support levels. In this period, the strong support was the 20 EMA, the red thin line: wave up, wave down, hits the 20, bounces up, again and again, "bounce, bounce, bounce, bounce". A great place to add shares: every time it hit the 20 EMA, because historically it acted as a strong level of support.

Stocks are like people, each with a different personality. Certain stocks, when they wave down, like to bounce off the 20 moving average ("don't ask me why, it's just a phenomenon"); others don't bounce off the 20, they bounce off the 40. So before buying a stock he looks at historically which moving averages it bounced off, i.e. the personality of the stock. Once it's a good company and undervalued, he waits for it to hit its preferred moving averages where it bounced in the past, and adds shares to the position there.

#### [15:16] Monthly time frame: Google and the 20 EMA
The same on the monthly time frame. Google on monthly candles over a long period: every time it waves down it tends to find support at the red 20 moving average on monthly candles ("boing"). Every time it hits the red line, there's an area to buy, again provided it is undervalued. You must calculate the intrinsic value first, always ensure price is below the intrinsic value and hits a support level where it has a tendency to bounce in the past; that's where we add shares.

#### [16:10] Applying it: Microsoft, 10-year monthly
Type in Microsoft. For a stock looked at for the first time, he looks at the 10-year monthly candles: always the long-term time frame. Click "All" (goes to monthly by default), zoom into the last 10 years or so, then click log. First thing: ensure price is on a clear uptrend; it is. Next: over the 10-year period, whenever price dropped, which moving average did it bounce off historically? During these corrections, the price tended to bounce off the red line, the 20 exponential moving average ("excuse me, I love making sound effects"). So Microsoft has a tendency to find support at this red line during corrections. Does it make sense to draw a support level at the 20 moving average? Yes: with the drawing tool he places a line right at the red line.

Support levels you draw can be based on moving averages or on swing lows where two or more swing lows reverse. Either; as long as it's a support level, draw a line.

During a big crash, like 2022 when we got a bear market and the whole market crashed, Microsoft dropped significantly, went below the red line, but ultimately bounced off. This tells him that in a big crash Microsoft will ultimately be supported by the blue line, the 50 moving average on monthly candles. He draws another support level right at the blue line, the 50 moving average, which is at 324. So in the event of another big crash, 324 should be the bottom.

Interesting: there was a swing high, and another swing high of sorts, so a kind of resistance. Swing high came down, swing high came down, then broke the resistance; after price breaks resistance and comes down, that resistance becomes support. So one day if Microsoft crashes down, this 324 previous resistance would likely be support as well and it will bounce off, and it happens to coincide with the 50 moving average. So 324 is a very powerful, significant level to watch in the event of a crash; that is where it will likely bottom. No way to predict 100%, but these are probabilistic guesses using technical chart patterns.

#### [20:26] Microsoft 5-year weekly
Next, the weekly candles: 5-year, weekly. Any support levels? There was a swing low that bounced off, and it coincides with the line just drawn. That reaffirms that 390 is a relatively significant level of support. Also during this period price hit the 200 moving average, the red line, and bounced off, hit the 200 and bounced off. So the 200 is significant too, and it coincides with the 324 as well. Everything coincides: all the lines intersect to reinforce that these are very strong levels of support that would support the price in the event of a correction or crash, and that's where he wants to be a buyer.

#### [21:42] Microsoft daily (1 year), consolidation
Zoom to the daily charts, the one-year chart. Right now the stock is, in the short term, in a very choppy pattern, called a consolidation pattern (more on this later). In the short term Microsoft is not really on an uptrend; it is going through a sideways, very choppy pattern. There's a level of support he can visually identify: price came down at 407, reversed up; reached that level, reversed up; again; again; and right now it is at this level. So this 407 (transcribed "407, 80"; the earlier pass gives $407 / $407.80) [filled from earlier pass] is another potential support level where Microsoft has a tendency to bounce.

There's also a swing high and another swing high; swing highs are very significant levels. He draws another line connecting these two swing highs, because they could act as strong potential support if price drops all the way down there. He draws a number of support levels: usually for each stock he draws about four to five support levels, which tell him where to add shares when price comes down. He'll cover this more in lesson 7 where he puts everything together into his seven-step investor checklist; this is just the technical charting part.

#### [23:59] Intrinsic value line and where to add
Ultimately we only buy when the stock price is below the intrinsic value, calculated as taught in the previous lesson. He has already calculated it for Microsoft: $415 (the transcript reads "$450.415"; he then says "which I calculated to be 415"). On the chart he uses a dotted line: select horizontal ray, draw at the intrinsic value, 415; right-click (or two fingers on a trackpad on a Mac), go to settings, type "intrinsic value", select green ("I always use green for intrinsic value"), and line style dotted.

Would this be a level to add shares of Microsoft? Is it below intrinsic value of 415? Yes, slightly: Microsoft is at 412 right now. Is it at a support level? Yes, the 407 support. So could one add shares here? Yes. But would he buy a lot? No, he'd just nibble a small portion, because it is only slightly undervalued; he likes to buy more only when it's more significantly undervalued. If Microsoft breaks this support and goes down to the next support level, at 390, he adds more shares. If it breaks down to the third support level, a more significant long-term support at about "3.49 50" (as transcribed; earlier pass reads it as about $349.50) [filled from earlier pass], he adds more shares. That's how he builds an averaged-in position. Ultimately a great stock will go up over time: once it waves down to a support, whether this one or that one, eventually it will go higher, as the company makes more and more money and its intrinsic value goes up all the time.

---

# Part 9 — Lessons 47–51

### 47 - Buy Sell on Downtrends

#### [00:00] Recap: best time to buy on an uptrend
In the previous part he said that when price is on an uptrend it moves in wave patterns: wave up, wave up, wave down, wave up, wave up. The best time to buy is after a wave down, when price drops to a level of support. The support could be one you drew, or one of the moving averages, like the 50 moving average or the 150 moving average, that has acted as a previous support in the past. That is the ideal time to enter: you buy the dip on the uptrend.

#### [00:28] Worst time to buy: the parabolic move
The worst time to buy is when price waves up in a very, very sharp fashion. He calls this a parabolic move: price goes up 90 degrees, a very sharp move upwards. Usually on an uptrend price moves at a 45 degree angle (wave up, wave down, wave up, wave down). But there are times of what he calls panic buying, with a lot of enthusiasm, a lot of good news, crazy excitement, and price starts to go up 90 degrees, usually accompanied by very long bullish candles. When you see this, it is the absolute worst time to buy. In fact it is the best time to sell the stock, if you want to sell.

He repeats: it does not mean that once price goes up 90 degrees you have to sell. If it is still a great company, still growing and not that overvalued, and it goes up 90 degrees, he wouldn't sell; he'd still hold. However, if for whatever reason he wants to sell (he thinks it is really overvalued, or he needs the money to pay for something else or to buy another stock), then these sharp 90 degree moves up are a great time to offload the stock. Why? Because after price goes up 90 degrees on panic buying, usually (not a guarantee) price then has to retrace back down, back near the moving averages.

#### [02:39] Example: Broadcom (AVGO)
AVGO is initially on a very nice uptrend: wave up, wave down, wave up, wave down, wave up, wave down, wave down, wave down, nicely bouncing off the 50 moving average and going up at a 45 degree angle. Then there is some great news, some euphoria, people think it is the best stock in the world, and price goes up 90 degrees, a parabolic move. The candles are exceptionally big green candles, which he calls extended range candles. Price also gets quite far away from the moving averages. It is like a rubber band: pull price too far above the moving average and it tends to snap back. The moving average is like home base; like a pendulum pulled too far from its centre, it swings back. That is exactly what happens after a parabolic move: it tends to retrace back down to the moving average.

Just because a stock he owns does this doesn't mean he will sell. If it's a great company, he's holding for the long run and it is not crazy overvalued, he will hold. Even if it drops he doesn't really care; he'll just add more shares because it is going to continue going up eventually. But if for whatever reason he wants to sell (needs the cash to buy something else, or for personal reasons), the best time to sell is when a stock makes a parabolic move.

#### [04:21] Covered calls
For those who learn options (he will share some options strategies at the end of the course), the best time to sell covered call options is when a stock moves up 90 degrees, parabolic. But he tends to sell covered calls not on growth stocks but on more defensive and moderately cyclical stocks (ASR rendered "psychical") [filled from earlier pass].

#### [04:48] What the average retail investor does
This sounds like a lot of common sense, but does most people do it? No. The average retail investor does the complete opposite of what he has been teaching. Retail investors tend to buy when there's good news and sell when there's bad news. When price shoots up like that it is usually accompanied by good news ("the company had great profits, the company's going to grow"), people get really excited, jump in and buy right there. They always buy high. After a wave up comes a wave down, and that is when all the bad news comes up: "it's not as good as before", interest rates are rising, there are tariffs. People panic and sell after the price has dropped. So the average retail investor buys on the wave up, panics on the drop and sells. They always buy high, sell low, buy high, sell low, buy very high, sell low, and end up losing money even if they're in the right stock.

Now that you understand technical analysis you understand why: we don't buy and sell based on news. We ignore the news; it is there to confuse and distract you. We buy and sell purely on price action. Of course, once we have analyzed that it is a great business with great fundamentals and we know the intrinsic value, we then enter and exit based on these technical signals.

#### [06:35] More examples: Lockheed Martin and Monster Beverage
Lockheed Martin: price goes wave up, wave down, wave up, wave down, wave up, wave down, wave down, wave down on an uptrend, moving up 45 degrees. Then some good news (whatever news, we don't care) and price goes up 90 degrees, followed by big green candles, far from the moving average, like a stretched pendulum. Eventually it snaps back down. If you want to sell for whatever reason, that's the time to start selling. We can't always sell right at the top; sometimes after you sell it can still go up a bit more, but it's better to sell near the top than near the bottom.

Monster Beverage: on an uptrend, wave up, wave up, wave down, wave down, wave up, wave down, wave up, wave down, nicely bouncing off the 50 moving average. That is a great time to buy: here's the 50 moving average, buy; here again, buy; here again, buy, provided it is undervalued (a couple of times below intrinsic value). Then some good news and price goes parabolic, 90 degrees, with green candles. That's the time you could sell covered call options, or start to scale out of your position if for some reason you want to raise cash to buy something else.

#### [08:10] Downtrends: definition
So far this was what to do on an uptrend. But price is not always on an uptrend; there will be times it goes on a downtrend. A downtrend is price generally moving in a downward fashion. In a downtrend prices still go up, yes, but every time price goes up (a short-term rally) it then goes even lower. A downtrend is defined as price making a series of lower swing highs: the initial swing high, then a lower high, a lower high, and a lower high. At the same time it makes lower lows: the initial low point, a lower low, an even lower low, and an even lower low. The definition of a downtrend is a series of lower swing highs and lower swing lows.

When the market is on a downtrend, it tells us the market is bearish, pessimistic about the stock. On a downtrend the probability is that prices will continue to move downward. Even if it's a great company and undervalued, because of the downward momentum, "cheap can get cheaper, low can get lower". It's like a river current: if you try to swim against the current it's difficult and you keep getting swept downstream until the trend begins to shift.

#### [10:24] Moving averages on a downtrend
On an uptrend the moving averages slope upwards; on a downtrend they are all sloping downwards: the 50 (blue line), the 150, the 100 (not drawn on the chart, but sloping down as well) and the 200 day. The sequence is also the opposite. On an uptrend the smaller moving average is above the bigger one: the 50 is above the 150, the 150 above the 200. On a downtrend the 200, the bigger moving average, is above the 150 and the 150 is above the 50. You always see this on a downtrend.

Downtrends can appear on any time frame: daily, weekly. But normally you don't see a downtrend on the monthly candle, because the monthly is the long-term trend and that's usually always going up for a good company. If a company's long-term trend is going down, you don't want to touch it. You want companies where the long-term monthly is going up, but the daily and weekly go through periods of downtrend once in a while; that's fine, he doesn't sweat it.

#### [12:08] Do you sell a great company when it enters a downtrend?
A common question: if I'm in a great company on an uptrend and it goes into a downtrend, do I sell? In the past, many years ago, that's what he used to do: the moment he saw the stock in a downtrend he'd sell and wait for the new uptrend to buy it back. He doesn't do that anymore. Nowadays, when he's invested in a great company and it goes on a downtrend for whatever reason (maybe an overall bear market or some macroeconomic recession, but the company itself is fine and keeps growing in the long run, only short-term negativity), he doesn't sell. Why not?

#### [13:03] Refresher: how to identify a change in trend (lesson two)
In lesson two, under investing in ETFs, he taught how to identify a change in trend using moving averages, specifically on the daily timeframe. When a stock is on an uptrend, the 50 moving average (blue line) is always above the 150 moving average (green line), and both are sloping up. An uptrend reverses into a downtrend when two conditions are met. First, the 50 moving average has to flatten or slope downwards, and the same for the 150; both cannot be sloping up. If one is still sloping up, it is not a change of trend; they must both be flat or sloping down. Second, the 50 moving average has to cross below the 150: a crossover where the blue line crosses below the green line signals that the uptrend has reversed into a downtrend.

On a downtrend the 150 is above the 50 and both slope down. A downtrend reverses into an uptrend by the opposite: the 50 must flatten and slope up, the 150 must flatten and slope up, and the 50 must cross back above the 150. Review lesson two, where he covered this step by step.

#### [14:53] Why he stopped selling on downtrend signals
In the old days, when he noticed the downtrend he would sell, then wait for the new uptrend and get back in. He stopped because he found that many times, when he sold at the start of a downtrend, the downtrend didn't last very long and reversed up very fast. By the time he bought back on a new uptrend signal, he ended up buying back at a much higher price. By jumping out and in, out and in, he found that over the long term he did not make as much money as if he had stayed in the stock and ridden the up and down trends. That is his experience, and why he doesn't do it anymore.

#### [16:10] Amazon examples
In 2021 Amazon was on a very nice uptrend. In 2022 there was a bear market because the Fed raised interest rates very rapidly, so the uptrend reversed into a downtrend; the 50 moving average (blue) crossed below the green line, both sloping down. If you followed the signal you would have sold it all at, say, $165. It kept going down and down, and you bought back only when the new uptrend started (the 50 crossed back above the 150 and was sloping up), at $106. Sell at 160 (he says "160" here, after "$165" earlier), buy back at 106: brilliant. But this doesn't happen that often; it happens only in a bear market, which happens on average once every six years.

More often it goes like this: the stock is on an uptrend, then goes into a downtrend, the 50 crosses below the 150, you sell at, say, $74. After you sell, it reverses back up, the 50 goes back above the 150, and you buy it back at about $92. Selling at 74 and buying back at 92, you miss out, and this happens very frequently.

Another example of getting shaken out of Amazon: an uptrend, then a downtrend with the 50 crossing below the 150, and you sell at about $15 [as stated]. The downtrend lasted a few months, then reversed into an uptrend, the 50 back above the 150, and you buy back at, say, $19. You got out at 15 and bought back at 19, losing about $4 out of $15, and 4 out of 15 is 27%. That's a lot. This is what he calls whipsaw: you get whipped out of the stock. When you try to jump out and in of stocks based on trends, eight or nine out of ten times you get whipsawed and end up buying it back 20, 30% higher, and all this reduces your returns. So over the years, if he had just held on to great companies through these up and down trends, he would have made a lot more than he did jumping out and back in.

#### [19:41] If you haven't bought yet: a stock on a downtrend
What if you have not bought the stock yet? You identified a great company that is undervalued but on a downtrend; should you just buy? Someone like Warren Buffett doesn't look at chart patterns at all. If he finds a great company that's undervalued, even on a downtrend, he would still buy and won't care about the downtrend, because he knows it won't last that long. Downtrends last a couple of months, but eventually reverse into an uptrend and go a lot higher. If you are a long-term investor like Buffett looking five to ten years ahead, these short-term trends won't bother you; even if you bought and it went lower, in the long run it will reverse up anyway.

Having said that, personally, if he finds a great stock that's undervalued and on a downtrend, he wouldn't jump in blindly. There are two things one could do when to enter. Number one: wait for the downtrend to end and wait for the new uptrend to buy, i.e. wait for the 50 to cross above the 150 and make sure they are not sloping down. Or number two: buy during the downtrend, but don't buy when price is moving up on a downtrend. On a downtrend price moves wave down, wave down, wave up, wave down, wave up, wave down, wave down. For goodness' sake, don't buy after a wave up; that's the dumbest time to buy, because after you buy it is going to crash back down. (He repeats: "same thing here, wave up, and you go and buy... That's the dumbest time to buy.") So if you want to buy on a downtrend, you want to buy only when you see signs of a potential bottom: you buy at the bottom of a downtrend, anticipating the reversal to an uptrend before the uptrend starts.

### 48 - Catching Bottoms

#### [00:00] Two options for a great, undervalued company on a downtrend
If you identify a great company that's undervalued but on a downtrend, there are two things you can do: A, wait for the downtrend to end and wait for a new uptrend confirmation to buy, or B, buy even during the downtrend but look for signs of a bottom. He starts with the first option.

#### [00:24] Option A: wait for the uptrend (Mercado Libre)
Mercado Libre was on a downtrend back in 2022 because of the bear market; everything was on a downtrend. There was nothing wrong with the company; the whole sentiment was bearish. The 50 moving average is below the 150, the 150 below the 200, all sloping downwards. The uptrend would have been confirmed where the 50 crossed back above the 150 and they both started to slope upwards. The blue crossing above the green is visible earlier, but that is not valid because the green is still sloping down, so that is not an uptrend confirmation. We have to wait for the green and blue lines to flatten and slope up. The earliest you would have bought on uptrend confirmation is somewhere there, at about $800 to about $920 (as he says: "about $800 and about $920"). You'd have gotten in at the start of this uptrend, which is great. The only problem: the stock bottomed at $600. If you had waited for the uptrend you would have gotten in after the price had already moved 50% from the lows.

The main disadvantage of waiting for a new uptrend is you cannot get in near the bottom; you get in after price has already moved, usually 30, 40, 50% higher. Is there a way to get in a lot earlier, near the bottom? Yes: identify signs of a bottom during a downtrend, so we get in even before the uptrend is confirmed. There are two signs of a bottom: first, a parabolic drop to a strong support level; second, a double bottom pattern.

#### [03:00] Sign 1: parabolic drop to a strong support level
In past videos, on an uptrend, price goes up at 45 degrees and then 90 degrees, a parabolic top with extreme buying greed, and that's usually when it tops and comes back down. The reverse is true in a downtrend. Price normally drops at a 45 degree angle (wave down, wave up, wave down, wave up...). What tends to happen when the stock bottoms (not 100% all the time, but pretty frequently) is that before it bottoms it drops 90 degrees: a parabolic drop, a sharp drop. It's like a roller coaster at the theme park: before the ride ends there's usually a drop at the end. That induces panic selling.

A stock can only bottom when there are no more sellers left, i.e. everyone who wants to sell has already sold. That is induced by capitulation or panic selling. When price drops 90 degrees it induces retail traders to throw in the towel, panic and get out, and that's when price bottoms. For this we need price to drop in a 90 degree fashion and see longer than usual red candles. After the drop, we want the price to hit a support level previously identified, which you've learned how to draw at the monthly, weekly and daily timeframes. When price drops to a support level there, there's a very high probability price then reverses back up and starts an uptrend. Not 100%, but a pretty good guess of where the bottom is.

If you want to be more conservative, wait for a green bullish candle to appear: after the big red candles drop, wait for a big green candle or a bullish pin bar before getting in. That gives a bit more confirmation it has reversed up.

#### [06:25] Sign 2: the double bottom pattern
This is very similar to the first except you see a double dip in price. Drawn on the chart: the stock is going down on the downtrend at 45 degrees and initially makes a drop to a level, the first swing low. Imagine this low is, say, $50. After price drops to that first swing low it rallies up and then makes another sharp drop. This drop has to be a 90 degree drop as well. They can both be 90 degrees, or if not, the second one should be. During this second 90 degree drop, price has to go below the first swing low: if the first swing low was $50, the second low has to go below $50, e.g. $49.50 or $49, whatever. Once it goes below the swing low, within about three candles we should see price close back above that swing low, close back above $50 with a bullish candle. That completes the double bottom pattern. When he sees this bottom form, he starts buying there, because it's a good signal of a reversal. Alson Chu (ASR: "Alsin") [filled from earlier pass], who teaches price action manipulation, calls this a bear trap pattern. It's a very powerful pattern.

#### [09:00] Mercado Libre: both signs
Could we have gotten in earlier on Mercado Libre than the uptrend confirmation? Yes: both a parabolic drop to support and a double bottom appeared. Price was initially dropping at 45 degrees, then a 90 degree parabolic drop that induces panic selling, with bigger than usual red candles. At the end of the red candles it hits a strong support level previously identified on the charts. To be more cautious, wait for a green candle, the first bullish candle, and one could have bought there. That doesn't mean it is the absolute bottom, just near the bottom. Sometimes after you buy it could go a bit lower, nothing wrong with that, but eventually you would catch the very strong reversal up. It's a lot better than buying when price had already moved up 50%.

If you don't want to buy at the first drop, wait for a double bottom, which may or may not happen. In this case: the first drop is the first swing low, somewhere about $640 (estimating). Price went up and then dropped below that 640; in a double bottom the second bottom has to go below the first. After going lower it went back above 640, ideally within three candles, forming a bullish candle. That is exactly when he'll buy, right near the bottom before the uptrend.

That is how he catches bottoms. No guarantee; sometimes it could go even lower, which is why we always buy in tranches and never go all in at one go. It is a potential bottom; he keeps saying potential bottom.

#### [11:45] More examples: Apple, Monster Beverage
Apple was on an uptrend, then a very short-lived downtrend, then reversed back into an uptrend. Imagine you identified it as a great company, undervalued, on a downtrend, so you waited for the uptrend: the 50 (blue) crossing above the green to confirm. You'd have gotten in at 209. The bottom was at 164. So 209 minus 164: you'd have gotten in only after the price had reversed 27% up, giving lower returns. Apple actually made a double bottom: the first bottom was the first swing low; price went up, then a parabolic drop, a 90 degree drop with big red candles, went below the first swing low, and within one, two, three candles formed the first bullish candle. Ideally wait for the first bullish candle and buy, catching the stock near the bottom.

Monster Beverage: a downtrend, then an uptrend. Waiting for the uptrend confirmed by the 50 crossing above the 150, you would have gotten in only after price moved 37% up from the bottom. Could you have bought nearer the bottom? Yes, it's a double bottom pattern: a 90 degree drop, double drop, double bottom, and you get in right there, very early.

#### [14:41] Parabolic drop to support: Google and Mercado Libre
Google was on a downtrend during the 2022 bear market (he says "2020, 2020 bad market" in the transcript [as heard]). There's the parabolic drop, a 90 degree drop with long red candles, hitting a support level of $83. The low was hit on the 3rd of November, at 83.34 (stated "8334"). Where did the support level come from? It was identified on a higher timeframe. When looking at a stock, always start with the monthly timeframe and draw support levels there, go down to the weekly and draw support levels, and finally go to the daily. This daily drop of 90 degrees was to a support on a higher timeframe. On the monthly candles of Google, the support level at the 3rd of November is on the 50 moving average of the monthly candles (blue line); historically price hit the blue line and bounced. That's where the parabolic drop on the daily occurred; he bought there and caught near the bottom. That's why it is important to draw your support levels on the monthly and weekly.

One more: Mercado Libre on the daily timeframe, on a downtrend with moving averages all sloping down. Then the parabolic drop, 90 degrees, big red candles, hitting a strong support level at 666 on the 12th of May. The support level was identified on the monthly candles, where it was a swing high, resistance. After resistance is broken, resistance becomes support. This level identified on multi-timeframe was 666, drawn beforehand, and when price hit 666 with a 90 degree drop he thought, "that's the bottom". It was beautiful. Again it doesn't always turn out 100%, but the probabilities are pretty high.

#### [17:54] Consolidation (sideways)
Recap: on an uptrend, we buy the dip. On a downtrend, wait for the reversal to an uptrend or look for signs of a bottom. Last scenario: the stock is in a consolidation, where price goes sideways. On an uptrend price makes higher highs and higher lows; on a downtrend lower highs and lower lows; in consolidation it makes same highs and same lows. In the example price goes up, makes a high at $292, goes down, goes up to the same high at 292 and goes down, and again the same high. The moment you see three same highs, that confirms a resistance. If price makes three same lows (e.g. at 272, then another at the same point, and another), we are sandwiched between a resistance and a support. He calls that a channel; price is stuck within this range.

If a stock is within a range and is a good company and undervalued, can you buy? Yes. You buy when price drops near the support level. Here the support is at 272, so the moment price waves down near 272, he would add. In a consolidation: buy near support, because if it's a good company, eventually it will get out of the range and begin its new uptrend.

Another example: Bank of America, stuck within a resistance of $30.84 and a support of $26.61 (transcript: "$30, $84" and "Twink 661"; read as such, [unclear]) and bouncing within the channel. In a consolidation the moving averages move sideways and criss-cross each other, going back and forth. On an uptrend they slope up and act as support; on a downtrend they slope downwards and sometimes act as resistance. In consolidation he ignores the moving averages because they are useless there; they don't act as support levels. All he looks at is the support and resistance he has drawn. As long as price drops near the support, he adds shares, building his position until he has a full position, and lets it eventually run and break out of the consolidation.

That concludes this lesson on technical analysis. In subsequent lessons he combines everything (fundamental analysis, technical analysis, valuations) into a step-by-step checklist to invest in any stock.

### 50 - VMI Formula Part 1

#### [00:00] Lesson 7: putting it all together
So far you've learned fundamental analysis (how to identify a great business and all the financial ratios), technical analysis (chart patterns, support levels) and stock valuation (the intrinsic value of the shares of the business). Some of you would be wondering: there are so many things to look at, which one do I look at first and which is the most important factor? In this lesson everything is put together into a formula, a checklist; all the pieces of the jigsaw puzzle. The formula has seven steps. Before you invest in any stock you put it through these seven steps. If it passes the seven steps, you invest. If it does not pass, you don't invest. It's as simple as that.

#### [00:47] The three questions
He divides the steps into three questions. First: is this a great business? When you buy a stock you are buying a piece of a business and you only want to buy great businesses. There are thousands of businesses in the stock market, so why settle for anything less than the best? We don't want average companies, lousy companies or just good companies; only great businesses.

Once you know it's a great business, you put it in your watch list and watch for the time to be right to pull the trigger, which only happens if you're getting a great price, only when the price is right. A great business can be a lousy investment if you pay too high a price for the shares, if you're overpaying. But a great business becomes a great investment if you're buying the shares at less than what it is worth, when it's undervalued. That doesn't happen that often, but when it happens, that's when you buy.

The last question: is this a great point of entry? By looking at the charts we can ascertain historical patterns of support so that we buy at optimal points that increase our returns over time.

#### [02:34] The seven steps in overview
Step 1: a great business grows its sales, profits and cash flow in a very predictable, very consistent manner, and is resilient. Rain or shine, pandemic or no pandemic, recession or economic growth, the business should keep growing consistently every single year. So the first step is to look at the historical track record to ensure the company consistently increases its sales revenue, net income and cash flow from operations for at least the last 5 to 10 years. Out of all the companies in the world, what percentage pass the first step? From experience, less than 5%. So just the first step eliminates 95% of the companies in the world.

Step 2: ensure the business is projected to grow in the next 10 to 20 years, i.e. a positive growth rate. This comes from the business having certain growth catalysts, like being in a growth industry, opening more outlets, creating more products, having a lot more customers it can sell to. Later he'll run through the data on how to find these projected growth rates.

Step 3 is the most important step of all: only invest in businesses with a competitive advantage, meaning the company has protection from potential competition. Competitors cannot easily take away their customers and market share, so the company is able to keep growing its sales, profits and cash flow over the long run and grow its intrinsic value. This is called an economic moat. Always ensure you invest in companies with an economic moat. Not many companies have a moat. Later he'll go through how to check if the company has a wide moat, a narrow moat or no moat. If it's a no-moat company, we never invest. A narrow-moat company maybe, but primarily he invests only in wide-moat companies with little or no competition.

Step 4: invest in companies that are highly profitable and operationally efficient. Later he'll show all the financial ratios that tell whether the company is truly profitable and operationally efficient, things like return on equity, return on invested capital and so on.

Step 5: invest in companies with a conservative debt structure. Some companies make a lot of money but take on a high level of debt to do so. That can be risky: if interest rates rise too much, these companies suddenly pay a lot of interest expense, which lowers their profits. You've read of companies that have gone bankrupt or are in the process of going bankrupt, like Evergrande Property in China, or previously companies that have gone bust like Hyflux in Singapore. What did they have in common? Very high debt. If you invest in companies with little or no debt, it can't go bankrupt; you can only go bankrupt with high debt. That's why step five is very important, especially to ensure the businesses you buy would not go bust.

If the company passes the first five steps, it's a great business. In the world out there, less than one percent of companies can pass these five steps. So running through these five steps eliminates 99% of the businesses in the world and you only invest in the best of the best.

Passing the first five steps does not mean it's a buy, because it may be at the wrong price: a great business but too expensive. You only put it in your watch list and be patient until the price is right.

Step 6: how do we know the price is right? Only when the share price sells below the intrinsic value, i.e. when the stock is undervalued. The lower the share price is versus the true value, the greater the margin of safety we have to acquire the shares. Later he'll run through that as well.

Step 7: it's a great business at a great price (undervalued); finally look at the charts and ensure we enter at an optimal level by looking at chart patterns. Ideally we buy when price is making a dip on the uptrend: price is on an uptrend, wave up, wave down, wave up, wave down, wave up; wait for a wave down, a dip to a support level like a moving average, so we add shares before the next wave up. By intelligently timing entries we catch the stock before it starts waving up and buy near the bottoms of corrections. Or we buy when price is at the support level of a consolidation: sometimes after a strong uptrend a stock consolidates, going sideways for a couple of months, and we buy when price is near the support level of the consolidation. Or we buy when price reverses into a new uptrend: for example price is currently on a downtrend, going down and down ("cheap could get a lot cheaper"), and you wait for a reversal from lower highs and lower lows to higher highs and higher lows, and start to get in at the start of a new change in trend. We'll look at a lot more examples as we go on.

#### [10:31] Step 1 in detail: consistent sales, net income, operating cash flow
Step one: ensure the company has a history of consistently increasing sales revenue, net income and cash flows from operations for the last five to 10 years.

Of these three metrics the most important is of course sales revenue: when it's increasing, the company is getting more and more customers, selling more goods and services. The second most important is cash flow from operations, the actual cash generated from business operations, which needs to be increasing consistently. "Increase consistently" doesn't mean it has to go up every single year; there could be years it drops temporarily. But over the last five or ten years there must be an uptrend in sales and cash from operations. One or two years of a dip is fine.

Net income is obviously important as well and must be increasing consistently. If net income is not increasing consistently, then look at operating income, which should be increasing consistently. As the fundamental analysis lesson covered, net income may have a sudden drop because of increased taxation, or a one-off loss the company sustained in an investment, or a lawsuit; or suddenly go up because of one-off activities not related to core business operations. That's why we look at operating income: it doesn't include non-operating expenses (taxation, lawsuits, mergers and acquisition costs) nor non-operating income (Forex gains or gains on investment income). By removing these non-core items we see the actual income from business operations. Revise the fundamental analysis lesson if you don't recall. If net income is not increasing consistently, it's okay as long as operating income is increasing consistently.

Also, as mentioned in the fundamental analysis lesson, gross profit margins and net profit margins should be consistent over the last five years or, better still, increasing. If profit margins have been falling for five years, that's a warning sign to avoid that company.

#### [14:24] Exceptions to step 1
This step one rule applies to all businesses except certain exceptions. One is banks: by the nature of their business, their cash flows from operations are usually inconsistent; in fact some years banks may have negative cash from operations. For banks you look only at sales revenue and net income and ignore cash flows from operations. The same is true for property developers and commodity companies, which tend to have very inconsistent cash flows from operations.

Another exception: speculative growth companies, which are fairly new, with revenue growing at double digits, like 50 to 100% revenue growth, but not yet profitable because they're still new. He does invest in them once in a while, but they are more risky, so he tends to make a very small investment only. For these you just have to ensure revenue is growing consistently; usually net income and cash flow from operations may still be negative. But he wants net income and cash from operations to have started turning positive in at least the last two quarters. Examples: Meituan from China (ASR "May-Tuan"), where when he invested about two years ago the profit and cash flow had just turned positive; and Palantir from the US (PLTR), a speculative growth company with revenues growing very strongly, net income still negative, but cash flows from operations recently turned positive [the transcript cuts off here; filled from earlier pass, which says the cash flow from operations turned positive on the quarterly view].

#### [16:50] Consistency makes the future predictable: GM vs J&J
The companies we invest in have net income and cash flow from operations increasing consistently for at least five years, or better 10 years.
