On the chart, General Motors and another company (ASR: "Mobile Company"; [unclear], possibly ExxonMobil) over a six-year period: in one year they made a lot of money, high profits; the next year they broke even; then made profits, more profits, less profits, and lost money. That is inconsistent and unpredictable. These are companies we never want to invest in, because if historical sales and profits are so inconsistent and unpredictable there's no way to predict next year's profits or the next five years of profits; we can't project it. On the other hand, Johnson & Johnson over the same period: earnings increase year after year after year, so you can better guess that next year or the next five years will probably be higher. When historical earnings, sales and cash flow are consistent, future sales, earnings and cash flows are more predictable.

#### [18:41] Examples that pass: Visa and Microsoft
Where to find this: go to Stock Oracle and look at the financial trends chart, or go to the financial statements: the income statement for revenue growth and operating income growth, and the statement of cash flows for cash flow from operations growth.

Visa, which he owns in his portfolio: click financials and look at the financials trend chart. Revenue is growing very consistently over the last five to ten years; operating income (orange) very consistently; net income very consistently. You can look at net income, but if it is not consistent, look at operating income, which is fine as well. On the right, operating cash flow (orange) is increasing very consistently over the last five to ten years. Especially during past recessions and crises he wants to see revenue continue to grow and cash flow continue to increase; this tells him the company is resilient, predictable and consistent.

Besides this, we look at profit margins: go to company metrics. On the trend chart you see gross profit margins; make sure they've been very consistent in the last five years at least. Operating margins also very consistent. If consistent or increasing, good; if declining consistently, a cause for worry. For Visa, gross profit margins are 78%, very high; operating margins like 67%; net profit margin 49%, very, very strong. Scrolling down you can also see the current figure versus the five-year average: you want the current to be more or less close to the five-year average, or higher is better. Hence he owns Visa; very strong company, passes the first step.

Microsoft, also in his portfolio: in the financial trend chart sales revenue grows very, very consistently, net income very consistent, operating cash flow (orange) increasing very consistently. A high quality business. Profit margins in company metrics: gross profit margins pretty consistent, operating margins very, very consistent, and pretty high: gross margins at 68%, operating margins at 46%, net profit margins at 39%. Remember that in the whole market, less than 5% of companies pass the first step with consistent, predictable revenue growth and profit growth.

#### [22:16] Industries he avoids: oil and gas
Many companies have very inconsistent revenue and profit. There are certain industries he tends to avoid because in a short term they could do very well, sometimes in certain periods really well, but in the long run revenue and profit growth is very inconsistent, and as a long-term investor he avoids them. Oil and gas companies tend to be very cyclical (ASR: "psychical"). Example: ExxonMobil (XOM), one of the biggest. He's not saying it's a bad company, but it is a very cyclical business. Looking at the financials, is it growing consistently? No, it goes up and down like a roller coaster: revenue goes up, down, down. Operating income and net income are very choppy: profit goes up, goes down, makes a loss, goes up, goes down. Why? Oil and gas profit depends a lot on oil prices. In certain years when oil prices go up, like in 2026 because of the war [earlier pass has 2016], they make a lot of money; in certain years like COVID, when oil prices go down, they lose a lot of money. So it's very inconsistent. He's not saying you can't make money in these companies, you can, but your timing must be very good: you must be very lucky to buy before oil prices go up and get out before oil prices go down. In the long run, you want your Microsoft or your Visa. The keyword is unpredictable: they do well sometimes, not well other times, and it's hard to predict these companies, so he tends to avoid them. Cash flow goes up, down, up, down, very inconsistent. To him these are a different type of animal from the investments he makes ([partly unclear in ASR]).

#### [25:02] Airlines
Another group he avoids is airlines. Singapore Airlines is one of the best airlines in the world, very well managed, but the whole airline industry is affected by oil prices, by tourism, by too many things. Looking at the financials, revenue goes up, goes down, up, then down again, very cyclical (ASR "practical"). Profits are very choppy, even operating income: up, down, loses money, down, up. Net operating cash flow goes up, down, negative, up, down, very choppy, and as a result profit margins are choppy too: gross profit margin goes up, down, up, down, and gross margin is only 16%, net profit margin only 9.6%. Very low profit margins because it's so competitive and a very high cost business: cost of labor, fuel costs, maintenance costs and so on. Although he flies Singapore Airlines, he won't invest in the business, even though it has great management.

#### [26:58] Early-stage growth companies, and Palantir as the example
Some students ask: how about early-stage growth companies, with revenue growing very strongly but not yet profitable because still early stage, maybe profitable in the future? The answer: sometimes he invests, but not often; in fact he rarely does. He only invests if he is very convinced they'll be very profitable in the future because they have a very unique business model and an economic moat that can protect them from potential competition.

A good example is Palantir. He first invested in 2022. At that time revenue was already growing very, very strongly but profits were still negative. Why invest? He studied the business model and found it very unique: at that time it was the only company using AI to help enterprises, before AI became popular; they were the first to use AI within enterprise software. So he thought they had a great [moat/business; word dropped in ASR] and started investing. But he only started investing when they showed at least their first one quarter of positive cash flow [transcript cuts off; filled from earlier pass]. On the quarterly view (click "quarterly"): on a full-year basis they were not yet profitable, but on a quarterly basis back in December 2022 they started to become positive. That's when he started investing. Because these companies don't have the five-year track record, they are more risky, so he invests half of his normal investment allocation. Since he invested, about four years ago, revenue has continued to grow and profit has been increasing for the last four years. It has not yet reached a five-year track record, so he still treats it as a speculative growth company; once it has at least five years of consistency he upgrades it to a normal investment. The majority of the time, 90% of the time [earlier pass has 95% of the time, with a 5% exception], he doesn't make exceptions for speculative growth companies, and when he does, he buys a much smaller position because it's a lot more risky.

### 51 - VMI Formula Part 2

#### [00:00] Step 2: positive growth rate
In the second step he wants to ensure the business has certain growth catalysts that would allow it to grow in the next 10 to 20 years. Where to find the figure: a few websites he goes to, as shown before under stock valuation. First Stock Oracle itself, where he can see the projected growth rate in the next three to five years and a long-term growth rate. He can also go to finviz.com, Yahoo Finance and Zacks.com for projected growth rates. As long as the company has a positive growth rate it passes step two. Obviously the higher the growth rate the better.

Example: Microsoft, which passed the first step. Does it have a positive growth rate? Go to company metrics, scroll all the way down to projected growth. Microsoft is projected to grow its earnings at 19.62% for the next three to five years, and beyond that at 8.9%. Positive? Yes. Good? Not bad. That passes step two. It's as simple as that.

#### [01:32] Where to get growth information
The best source is to read the company's quarterly reports, annual reports, and listen to or read the earnings call transcript. On Stock Oracle, under earnings, each quarter the company announces its earnings and you can download the press release and click on the earnings call transcript, where the entire management team talks about their future growth plans. From there you understand where growth is coming from: what new products, services, markets. A very powerful tool under Stock Oracle: AI Insights. Click AI Insights and scroll down to see the growth drivers. For Microsoft in the next 5, 10 years: AI-driven cloud expansion, enterprise AI and Copilot monetization [ASR: "enterprise says"; earlier pass has "enterprise SaaS"], proprietary AI infrastructure, commercial bookings and contract backlog, global cloud market share gains, and Windows 11 adoption and OEM growth. It's all there under AI Insights.

#### [02:39] The six high growth industries
Ideally he wants the companies he invests in to be in high growth industries, within a very strong secular growth trend over the next 10 years. Six main ones he likes to focus on:
- AI and robotics, the first major growth trend.
- AI energy infrastructure.
- Healthcare, because the world has an aging population; people are growing older and need more healthcare. A long-term growth industry.
- Digital payments, the fourth major growth driver in the next 10 to 20 years. Companies like Mastercard and Visa: high growth as people now pay digitally rather than with physical cash.
- Cyber security: as more and more companies use AI and are all online, there will be a lot of hacking and cybercrime, so cybersecurity is a huge growth industry.
- The sixth is something very new: the new space economy. He has not yet invested in it, but is watching it. It is potentially a big business in the future, but he hasn't invested in any of it yet because many of the companies involved are not yet profitable. He's keeping a close look. Not just space tourism: there are commercial applications like data centers in space, the missile defense shield, and low Earth orbit satellite communications.

Many of the companies he invests in are in cybersecurity, digital payments, healthcare, AI and robotics, and AI energy and infrastructure. One thing he likes about Microsoft is that it is in two of these high growth industries: AI and robotics, and cybersecurity.

#### [05:14] Step 3: the economic moat
To him step three is the most important step of all. It ensures the business has a sustainable competitive advantage that protects it from competition, so competitors cannot easily take away its customers or market share. Warren Buffett calls this an economic moat.

What's a moat? In the old days kings lived in castles, and other kingdoms were always thinking of attacking. To protect the castle they built a moat around it: a water barrier where they put crocodiles and piranhas so attackers can't reach the castle. Same in business. Step one tells us the business has a track record of increasing revenue, profits and cash flow. Step two tells us the industry is growing. But can the business continue to grow revenue, profits and cash flow in the next 10, 20 years? The only thing that can stop it is competition taking away its customers and market share. So we have to ensure the business can protect itself against this competition. He likes to invest in monopolistic businesses with ideally little or no competition. When a company has a very strong economic moat it maintains pricing power: it can raise prices consistently without losing customers or market share. It protects its market share and so generates consistent growth in sales, net income and cash flow for the foreseeable future, the next 10 to 20 years.

A business can have a wide moat (a lot of crocodiles), a narrow moat (few crocodiles), or no moat (no protection; anyone can attack the castle tomorrow). He only invests in wide-moat and narrow-moat companies; the difference is explained in more detail shortly. About 70 to 80% of the stocks he buys are wide-moat companies. He buys some narrow-moat companies as well, but avoids no-moat companies at all cost. A no-moat company doesn't mean the price can't go up in the short term: it can still go up, revenue can still grow, it can still be profitable. But he wouldn't invest because it can be easily disrupted by bigger competitors.

Examples: when Zoom first came about, about 10 years ago, it was doing very well, especially during the pandemic with people going online, and revenue was growing. But he didn't dare buy Zoom because, although growing, it didn't have a very strong moat and could easily be disrupted by bigger competitors like Microsoft and Google. Same reason he didn't dare buy PayPal: although it was getting traction, popular and growing, he felt the business could be easily disrupted and replaced by much stronger competitors. Good thing he didn't buy it, because after many years, after going up initially, the shares have collapsed and he still wouldn't buy it. To him the moat is very important.

#### [09:05] The five sources of a moat
Altogether five things he looks at.

Number one: a very strong brand people love and are very loyal to, such that even if the company raises price, people still buy, and even if competitors are cheaper, people still buy the brand because they love it. Examples: Apple, McDonald's, Coca-Cola, Nike, Microsoft. Ideally he likes to buy companies with what he calls a brand monopoly: when you mention a particular product, the brand is top of mind recall, the first thing people think about. A game: he mentions the product or service and you say the first thing that comes to mind.
- Office software, the software you must use to be productive: Microsoft. A brand monopoly with no close competition. Key in Microsoft and look at the last 10 years: it goes up consistently because it has a very wide economic moat.
- Search, which we do every day for work or play: Google. We say "Google this, Google that"; it's part of our vocabulary. We don't say "Bing this" or "DuckDuckGo this". He can't live without Google Maps, Google Docs, Gmail and Gemini; it's part of everyday life. Google is a brand monopoly, and brand monopolies in the long run always go up because they have little or no competition.
- Watching videos online: if you think free, YouTube, which Google owns. If you think paid, Netflix, again a brand monopoly. Put in Netflix and in the long run, over time, it always goes higher. It's a cultural icon phenomenon.
- Wherever you are in the world (Singapore, London, New York, Africa), early in the morning or late at night, you're hungry and many restaurants are closed, but you know there's a fast food restaurant nearby where the food is not too bad: McDonald's. Kids are brainwashed from young with the Happy Meal. A brand monopoly, and in the long run it always goes up.
- Social media: WhatsApp, Instagram, Facebook, all owned by Meta Platforms. Another brand monopoly. In many parts of the world, especially Europe and Asia, you can't live without WhatsApp, Instagram or Facebook; everyone is using it. Fun facts: there are about 6 billion people in the world with internet access, and out of the 6 billion, 3.5 billion use one of Meta's products. That's 60% of the entire world on the internet. No other company comes close, not Twitter/X or Snapchat or Reddit.

Number two: very high customer switching costs. This creates a very strong moat of protection too. Once a customer uses the product or service, it becomes very expensive or very inconvenient to switch to a competitor or another brand. It locks in the customer for a couple of years, normally on a subscription basis.
- Apple: once you're in the Apple ecosystem it is very hard to leave, like Hotel California: you can check out but you can never leave. His entire family and most of his office staff use all Apple products: Apple Watch, iPhone, MacBook, iPad, his house camera system and lighting system connected to iCloud using the iPad, all devices synced seamlessly on iCloud. Every time he creates a video, takes a photograph, or does a document on his computer, it is instantly synced to his iPhone, iPad and iCloud. Even if Samsung or Xiaomi or whatever competitor has a much better phone, cheaper, more powerful, more features, would he buy it? No, never. A Samsung phone can't sync to his Apple Watch or MacBook, which makes life very inconvenient and frustrating. He's got hours and hours of family videos and work synced to iCloud; would he make the effort to download everything from iCloud and transfer to, say, an Android or Google cloud? No, too much work. So what gives Apple the moat is not better products (he admits some companies may have better phones or tablets) but the ecosystem: once you're locked into iCloud it's very hard to get out. If you've never used an Apple product you won't understand. That's high switching costs.
- Many enterprise software companies have high switching costs. When an enterprise uses ServiceNow or Salesforce it's embedded into all their workflows, so it's very hard to switch to a competitor or to create their own software system.
- Cyber security companies: when a company engages a vendor like Palo Alto, CrowdStrike or Fortinet [ASR: "Fortinac"], it's integrated into the entire company, and switching to another vendor is very risky and very, very difficult. It locks in the customers.

Number three: the network effect. The more customers use the product, the better the product gets, and the more people want to use it because everyone is using it. Outside the US, like Asia and Europe, you cannot live without WhatsApp because everyone is on it; if you're not, you can't collaborate with friends or in the workplace. In his part of the world you've got to use Facebook and Instagram or you're left out of the loop. In the US it's a bit different: you don't really use WhatsApp, you use iMessage, which creates another network effect in the Apple ecosystem. In China it's different too: they cannot live without WeChat, owned by Tencent. Different parts of the world have different network effects. To sell something online, in most parts of the world you've got to be on Amazon, because of its massive network effect: everyone is selling there, everyone is buying there. In China you've got to be on Alibaba, on Pinduoduo. In Latin America you've got to be on Mercado Libre. Google also has a network effect: the more people use Google search, the more people use Gemini, the more intelligent their searches and AI become, and the more powerful it becomes. Network effect means the more people use it, the stronger the product becomes and the more people want to use it, a flywheel.

Number four: high barriers to entry. For example a patent: a pharmaceutical company invents a drug and patents it, protected for 20 years; but it doesn't last forever, it lasts for 20 years. Regulatory moats: a company like Palantir or Lockheed Martin has a lot of contracts with the US military, the Pentagon. Can new competitors come in and compete? No, because they need very high-level security clearance with the military. That regulatory restriction creates a moat and protects them against competition. Barriers can come from regulatory requirements, intellectual property, patents and trademarks.

Number five: economies of scale. When a company becomes so big it's almost impossible to compete with them: they can buy in bulk, run things more efficiently, and spread their fixed costs over a larger business. As a result they can temporarily lower their price and kill competitors who cannot survive; or even if they keep the same price they earn much higher profit margins than competitors, and use the extra profits to reinvest in R&D and become even stronger. Companies like Amazon, Walmart and Costco have huge economies of scale; it's almost impossible for anyone to compete with them at that level.

#### [20:21] Wide moat vs narrow moat vs no moat
A business can have a wide moat, a narrow moat or no moat. A wide moat is when a business has a very strong and sustainable competitive advantage that we are pretty certain can't easily be disrupted for the next 20 years; a very monopolistic business. A narrow-moat company has some competitive advantage that is probably sustainable for the next 10 years; beyond 10 years we are not so sure. Not as strong as a wide moat, but still strong. The worst is a company with no economic moat: it could be growing profits and revenue now, but can easily be killed by a stronger competitor in the near future, in the next one or two or three years. It has no clear competitive advantage.

Wide-moat companies have no competitors or insignificant competition. Narrow-moat companies have several significant competitors. No-moat companies have many, many competitors all selling the same product or service, which looks very, very close, almost a commodity.

A wide-moat company can raise prices without affecting demand very much; people will still buy. A narrow-moat company is limited in its ability to raise prices: if it increases price too much, customers will go to the competition. For no-moat companies, price is the ultimate decision: people buy on price, they don't care about your brand or quality, they just buy the cheapest one. They sell a product where price is the most important factor in the buying decision.

Why buy wide-moat companies? They have very high profit margins, a consistent increase in sales and profits, and a predictable long-term future growth outlook. Narrow-moat companies have average profit margins (not bad, pretty good, but not super), and increasing sales and profits over the next 10 years; beyond that we are not too sure. He would still invest in them. But he avoids no-moat companies because they tend to have low profit margins, inconsistent sales and profits and unpredictable future growth; beyond five years we're not sure what will happen. He's not saying you can't buy no-moat companies, you can, but more for short-term speculation, not something you can buy and close your eyes and feel safe in the long run.

#### [23:25] Examples of wide, narrow and no moat
Wide-moat companies have brand monopoly, network effects, switching costs and so on; no-moat companies sell a commodity product or service. Wide-moat examples: Meta Platforms, Amazon, S&P Global, Alphabet (Google), Netflix, Nvidia, Mastercard, Visa, Apple, and many, many more. Narrow-moat companies: Cisco Systems, FedEx, Expedia, Dow Chemical, Alcoa, Chevron, Schlumberger, Tesla, ExxonMobil, Royal Dutch Shell.

When he mentioned Tesla, some will ask: are you sure it has a narrow moat, not a wide moat? A simple test: can Tesla raise the price of its cars every year and not lose market share? No. In fact it faces increasing competition from so many electric vehicle makers in China, so much so that not only can they not raise prices, they have to keep cutting prices and discounting to avoid losing too many customers. They don't have that pricing power, hence no wide moat; they have a lot of competition, so it's more of a narrow moat.

No-moat companies: the majority of commodity companies, airlines, auto manufacturers, property developers, construction companies, shipping companies, oil and gas companies; most of them, not all, but most. Whatever they're selling, there's so much competition, a bloody red ocean of competition. He stays away from those industries.

#### [25:05] The five moat questions
To summarize, before you buy a stock, ask:
1. Does it have a strong brand? Is it a brand monopoly, like McDonald's, Visa, Mastercard, Microsoft, Google, Coca-Cola, Pepsi?
2. Does it have high barriers to entry, where it's not easy for people to do the same thing because of regulatory barriers, intellectual property or patents? Companies like Lockheed Martin, Airbus, Palantir.
3. Does it have huge economies of scale, doing things on such a big scale that smaller competitors cannot compete, like Amazon, Walmart, Airbus?
4. Does it have a network effect, where the more people use it the better the product becomes and the more other people want to use it, like Google, Meta, Apple, Alibaba, Tencent?
5. Does it have high switching costs, where once people use the product it becomes very inconvenient or expensive to switch to another provider? For example Microsoft, Palo Alto, CrowdStrike, Amazon Web Services (companies that build their business on AWS find it not easy to migrate to another cloud provider).

If a company has at least three out of these five moat sources, he would want to invest in the company; it tells him the company has a strong moat.

#### [26:38] Checking the moat on Stock Oracle Pro
If you are subscribed to Stock Oracle Pro you can analyze the moat easily. Put in Meta Platforms and straight away it's assigned a wide moat. Click Oracle IQ to read more. There are three perspectives on the moat. First the Oracle moat, assigned by their research team based on an algorithm: wide moat. Second a community moat, where you can vote on what you think (narrow moat, wide moat), built on the community's vote. Third the AI moat, developed by AI. Click AI moat: Meta has a score of 9 out of 10, a very, very wide moat, with an explanation: Meta operates the largest global social media and digital advertising ecosystem through its family of apps, Facebook, Instagram, WhatsApp, Messenger and Threads. It competes primarily with Alphabet, ByteDance and Amazon and emerging platforms. Meta holds a dominant share of global social media usage and advertising revenue, benefiting from scale and network effects that smaller competitors struggle to match. It breaks down the moat sources: brand loyalty and pricing power 9 out of 10, barriers to entry 9 out of 10, switching costs 8 out of 10, network effect 10 out of 10, economies of scale 9 out of 10. Straight away you know this is a very wide-moat company.

Contrast with Zoom, which he mentioned used to grow initially but he didn't dare invest in because it didn't have a very strong moat. Put in Zoom Communications: narrow moat. Oracle IQ, AI moat: moat score of six out of ten. It does have some moat but not very, very strong. He doesn't often invest in six out of ten; not strong enough. Seven, eight or nine out of ten gives him more confidence. Zoom has some brand loyalty and pricing power, six out of ten; barriers to entry only five out of ten because bigger competitors can always replicate a similar platform; switching costs six out of ten (you can easily switch to another platform); network effect six out of ten; economies of scale five out of ten. So-so.

Many years ago there was a company called Beyond Meat, which was very, very hot, and he said he's not going to invest in it. Thank God, because it collapsed. It has no moat; no moat is the most dangerous. It explains why: it only scores 2 out of 10. No pricing power, no brand loyalty, no barriers to entry, no switching costs, no network effect, and low economies of scale. This gives a shortcut to analyze the moat of a company.

# Part 10 — Lessons 52–54

### VMI Formula Part 3

#### [00:00] Step 3 shortcut: the 10-year chart
Still on step three, the sustainable competitive advantage. One good visual indication of whether a company has a wide moat or narrow moat is the long-term chart of the last 10 years. If the share price is on a clear uptrend over the last 10 years, that is usually a sign of a strong economic moat. If the chart instead is generally on a downtrend over the long run, the company doesn't have a competitive advantage. That is another shortcut you can use.

#### [00:36] Step 4: Is the company profitable and operationally efficient?
Business passes steps one, two, three; now step four: check whether the company is profitable and operationally efficient. He looks at four metrics.

**Return on equity (ROE).** Covered in the fundamental analysis lesson; now it is being brought together into this framework. ROE tells you how well the company makes money from its shareholders' equity in the business. Ideally he looks for an ROE of at least 12%. ROE of 12% or more is a very profitable business; more than 15% is a super exceptional business.

**Return on invested capital (ROIC).** Why look at ROIC if you look at ROE? Because some companies use a lot of debt to finance the business; by using a lot of debt they need less equity, which makes the ROE look very high (looks very good) but they have a lot of debt, which is not good. A company with a lot of debt will have a very low ROIC. So you want both high ROE and high ROIC, to ensure profitability is not being pushed up by too much debt. He won't run through formulas again (covered in the fundamental analysis lesson), just the numbers. For ROIC, ideally 12% for a good business; 15% or more is an excellent business. Certain companies don't use ROIC because by nature they are highly leveraged businesses or have high physical capex that requires a lot of debt: banks, insurance companies and regulated utilities. For these he doesn't look at ROIC, just ROE, because with ROIC they would all fail.

**Working capital efficiency.** Compare sales revenue to accounts receivables; you want sales revenue growing faster than accounts receivables (all covered in the fundamental analysis lesson).

**Cash conversion cycle.** For companies that sell physical goods, like Apple, like Nike, look at the cash conversion cycle. You want it consistent or declining, not increasing too much. It tells you how many days it takes, for every $1 the company spends, to get the money back; the faster the better. A negative cash conversion cycle is fantastic because the company collects cash up front from customers before it even pays suppliers.

#### [04:21] Where to find the data: Stock Oracle (Nvidia example)
All of this is on any financial website. Example: Nvidia. Go down and click on company metrics to get ROE and ROIC. The trend chart of ROE and ROIC is shown; as long as it is consistent or rising that's good, but consistently declining is something to watch out for. For the specific numbers, scroll down to profitability ratios: Nvidia's ROE is 101%, way above 15% and even above the five-year average; ROIC is 93%, way above the five-year average as well. So it clearly passes high ROE and high ROIC.

For sales revenue versus accounts receivables, click on financials. You don't need the paid subscription; the basic free site shows it. Revenue is growing faster than accounts receivables. The cash conversion cycle is generally moderately rising, but nothing to be concerned about.

#### [06:16] Alternatives: Morningstar and TradingView
If the stock isn't listed on Stock Oracle (maybe a foreign stock), go to morningstar.com and put in the ticker symbol; it doesn't have to be a US stock (he uses Nvidia again for consistency). Click key metrics, then profitability and efficiency; you can see ROE and ROIC for the last six years.

For the cash conversion cycle and the accounts receivables versus revenue chart, use TradingView, for any stock, e.g. a Singapore stock like Singapore Telecoms (Singtel). Click indicators, type "cash conversion cycle", select it, and look at the last five years. For Singtel it's been mostly going sideways, and it is negative, which is a very good thing: Singtel collects cash up front before paying its suppliers, the best scenario.

For accounts receivable versus revenue, there is a special indicator from the course resources. Under the course notes of the fundamental analysis lesson, click "course notes and resources" and you'll see the special indicator called Total Revenue versus Accounts Receivable. Download the script; it opens on the TradingView chart. Once downloaded, go to indicators and find it (the name as transcribed: "Pirata profit total revenue and accounts receivables charts"; the script is called "Piranha Profit" [filled from earlier pass]). Over five years, green is the revenue and red is the accounts receivables.

#### [09:39] Step 5: Strong balance sheet, conservative debt
Once the business passes step four, step five: make sure the company has a strong balance sheet and a conservative debt structure. Too much debt becomes too risky; he avoids companies with too much debt. There are many ratios but he looks at three main ones (a review from the fundamental analysis lesson):

1. **Current ratio.** Ideally more than one, meaning more current assets than current liabilities. As said before, some companies intentionally have a low current ratio because they collect sales upfront, like subscription companies such as ServiceNow, or Apple. Money collected up front before delivering the service is recorded as deferred revenue under current liabilities, which makes the denominator bigger and the current ratio less than one. So where the current ratio is less than one, don't be too alarmed; it's because of this.
2. **Debt to EBITDA ratio.** Compares total debt to profits (earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization). You want it to be three or less, meaning the company can pay back all its debt in three years using its profits.
3. **Debt servicing ratio.** Should be less than 30%. It is interest expenses divided by cash flow from operations times 100%.

Must you calculate this yourself? No, it's on Stock Oracle, also on the basic account. Go to company metrics, scroll down to debt ratios (debt and liquidity ratios): current ratio, debt servicing ratio, debt to EBITDA. You can compare against the five-year average. Nvidia: current ratio 3.91, way above one; debt servicing ratio zero, fantastic, less than 30%; debt to EBITDA less than 3, in fact 0.06. Also the interest coverage ratio mentioned before: more than 3 is okay, more than 5 is very safe, and Nvidia's is 503. Nvidia has a solid balance sheet.

#### [13:11] Banks and REITs
These three metrics do not apply to banks or real estate investment trusts. For banks, check balance sheet strength with the common equity tier 1 ratio, also on Stock Oracle. Ideally over 10 is pretty safe. For REITs, check the gearing ratio: total debt divided by total assets. Find it in the quarterly and annual report of the REIT, or on the REIT's website under investor presentation. Singapore-listed REITs report this as the gearing ratio. Ideally 45% or lower; 45% is the minimum requirement, but below 40% is the safest.

Finding the CET1 ratio: put in a bank like JP Morgan (also Bank of America, DBS Group Holdings), click overview and scroll all the way down to financial strength. JP Morgan's common equity tier 1 ratio is 14.1%, way above 10%. DBS Group Holdings: 17%. Singapore banks are very, very, very conservative and very safe.

#### [14:56] Watch list, then value the business
Once the business passes the first five steps, it's a high quality business. Can you buy straight away? No; put it on your watch list and make sure the price is right. You have to value the shares. Ideally you only buy when the share price is selling below the stock's fair value or intrinsic value; that is the margin of safety. If you buy a great business when overvalued and pay too high a price, even though it's a great business it may take you a long time to break even if the share price drops way below the intrinsic value after you bought it.

#### [15:49] Getting the intrinsic value
Simplest way: Stock Oracle does it automatically. Click on intrinsic value and it shows the value using different methods. For Google, the discounted cash flow method gives an intrinsic value of $190. But this valuation doesn't normalize the capex: recently Google's capex went up very high and free cash flow dropped a lot, which is why the number is lower. So he likes to use the Oracle value, which adjusts for these issues and normalizes the capex if free cash flow is not consistent recently. It gives the most appropriate intrinsic value, $302. So Google right now is selling slightly above the intrinsic value. If you want to calculate manually, use the intrinsic value calculators from the last lesson.

**Primary method: DCF (discounted cash flow).** He projects how much cash the business will generate for the next 20 years and discounts it to present value. Use the intrinsic value calculator, or for automatic, Stock Oracle's pro account with the Oracle value. The assumption: cash flow from operations must be increasing consistently.

**If cash from operations is inconsistent:** usually he doesn't like to buy those companies, but if he wants to for whatever reason, he uses the discounted net income method, ensuring net income is increasing consistently, at least for the last five years. If both cash flow from operations and net income are inconsistent, he won't buy it; it wouldn't pass the first five steps in the first place. For a high quality business, one of them has to be increasing consistently.

**Banks** (JP Morgan, Bank of America, DBS, OCBC): price to book ratio method. Take the last five years' average price to book ratio, multiply by the current book value per share to get fair value. The numbers are on Stock Oracle.

**REITs:** similarly, the price to NAV ratio. Take the average price to NAV ratio for the last 5 or 10 years, multiplied by the current NAV per share, to get fair value. Buy when the price is below fair value. He buys a REIT essentially for dividend yield, so the yield must be appropriate; personally, for Singapore-listed REITs he wants at least a 5% dividend yield.

**Speculative growth companies.** Early stage growth companies where revenue is growing very strongly but not yet profitable, without a track record of consistent profit and cash flow. He rarely buys these. If the moat is very strong and the opportunity huge, he will invest, but not too much, usually a small allocation. Examples: Palantir; another more speculative stock he bought is Celsius, the energy drink. With no profits and no consistent cash flow, use a price to sales method. One way: take the five-year average price to sales ratio and multiply by current revenue per share to get intrinsic value (covered in the previous lesson; revise it). Or use the price to sales growth ratio: price to sales ratio divided by the projected revenue growth rate; ideally between 0.2 and 0.3 is fair value. Above 0.3 is overvalued, below 0.2 undervalued; between 0.2 and 0.3 is the sweet spot.

### VMI Formula Part 4

#### [00:00] Step 7: Charts, buy on waves down to support
The last step is to look at the charts and buy when price retraces, or waves down, to a support level. In general, when we invest in great businesses the price always goes up over the long run, but not in a straight line. It always moves in wave patterns: wave up, wave down, wave up, wave down, repeating, because of greed, fear, greed, fear. It's a cycle. As investors we always want to buy at the lowest price, but you can never catch the exact bottom. If you're hung up on always buying at the bottom, you'll be disappointed and miss out a lot because you always think it's going to go lower and never get in. As long as we buy near the bottom we've done our job, and as long as we don't buy near the top we're doing the right thing.

Rule of thumb: never buy during a wave up. Never chase the stock; wait for the stock to come to you. Always wait for a wave down to a level of support, and buy as long as the price is near or at the support level. That's good enough.

#### [01:37] Scenario 1: buy the dip in an uptrend
A stock goes through cycles. Sometimes it is on an uptrend, making higher highs and higher lows. During an uptrend, wait for the wave down, and once it hits a support level, like a moving average (the 50, 100, 150 or 200 moving average; any one is fine because they act as natural support levels), add shares. You can never tell how deep a correction will get; sometimes it breaks that first moving average and goes to a lower one. That's why we buy in tranches. If you intend to buy 100 shares, you don't buy all 100 at the first support. You buy, for example, a third or a quarter of your planned allocation: 25 shares at the first support level; if it breaks and goes to the second support level, buy the next 25 shares; the lower it goes, the more you buy, until you have bought your entire allocation. Then leave it, don't add any more. Buying in tranches averages in your cost and gives a very good average; you may not pay the exact bottom price, but you know that good companies bounce back up in time (maybe in a month, three months, a year). As long as you buy near the bottom while undervalued, you'll do very, very well. Generally on an uptrend we buy the dip when it hits a support level.

#### [04:04] Scenario 2: consolidation
Sometimes a stock goes from an uptrend into a consolidation, a sideways pattern. For example, Amazon is now in a sideways pattern, and for quite a while TSM was too. During consolidation, notice where the support level is; every time the price goes back down near the support, keep adding shares, buying in tranches, one quarter or one third at a time over the year until you reach the full allocation.

#### [05:04] Scenario 3: downtrend
Sometimes the stock is on a downtrend, e.g. during a bear market or big correction. We are referring to the daily candles, the shorter term time frame. Usually you can wait for the downtrend to reverse into an uptrend before starting to enter your allocation: buying the reversal of the downtrend into the uptrend.

#### [05:33] Chart example: Apple (uptrend)
Apple at this time was on an uptrend on the daily candles. The 50 moving average is above the 150 moving average and both are sloping up; the 200 moving average is sloping up too, so it's not a very, very clear uptrend. Watch the wave patterns: wave up, wave up, wave down, wave up, wave down. Optimal times to add shares: when it waves down to a support level. Wave down to the support level was an entry; wave up, wave down, enter there; as long as you enter near the support levels, that's a good enough job. One wave down hits the 50 moving average; you could add shares there. Is it possible that after you add, it breaks lower and hits the red moving average? Of course. Then you add a second tranche. You can't always know how deep a correction will get, so you add in stages. In this case it went all the way down to the 200 moving average.

Always add shares on a wave down, never a wave up. On a wave up, would you want to add? Of course not. Whenever the price gets too far from the moving average, like a rubber band it will snap back eventually. So don't get in when it's waving up. After a wave up, never get in; wait for a wave down. Very simple.

#### [07:44] Chart example: Boeing and TSM (consolidation)
Boeing is in a sideways consolidation. In a consolidation the moving averages are useless; ignore them. They tend to criss-cross. Moving averages are only useful in a clear uptrend or clear downtrend: on an uptrend they slope up and act as support; on a downtrend, sloping down, they act as resistance. In a sideways pattern they are totally useless, so rely on support and resistance levels that you identify and draw. For Boeing there's a clear resistance level at 375 and a support level, identified from the pattern, at about 326. Every time the price drops near 326, just add shares.

TSM has been consolidating for about a year. It was on a very nice uptrend and then went sideways: resistance, resistance, resistance, resistance and support, support, support, support. Last year he identified the support at 108; every time the price was near there, he added shares. He added a few times, and now it looks like it is breaking out of that resistance, that consolidation, and starting a new uptrend. Whenever a stock goes into a consolidation, eventually it will break out into a new uptrend, so you want to accumulate during the consolidation.

#### [10:12] Third scenario: downtrend on the daily chart
On a downtrend, usually the 50 moving average is below the 150 moving average and sloping down, and the price is below the 200 moving average, which is sloping down too. Wave patterns: wave up, wave down, wave up, wave down, wave down; lower highs and lower lows. You may say on a downtrend "low can get lower, cheap can get cheaper", so you want to wait for the downtrend to reverse into an uptrend before adding. You could do that. How do you know it's an uptrend? When the moving averages start sloping back up, as learned in the technical analysis portion: when the 50 moving average (blue line) crosses above the 150 moving average (green line) and they are sloping up, that's a change in trend and where you could start adding shares. Or the price goes above the 200 moving average (red line) and the 200 slopes up; that's also an indication of a change in trend.

You can also see it in price action: lower highs and lower lows is a downtrend; higher highs and higher lows is an uptrend. Without the moving averages you can see the trend change from the wave patterns: a higher high and a higher low is a change in trend, and you could start adding to catch the new uptrend.

The only drawback of waiting for the trend to change is that you end up buying higher than buying earlier. Some ask, could I get in even before the trend changes? Actually you could, because when you buy good companies they will go up eventually; over a one year, three or five-year view they will be a lot higher. If you don't mind it going down a bit more, you can jump in even on a downtrend for fundamentally good businesses. He does buy on the downtrend sometimes, under special circumstances explained shortly.

#### [13:20] Pre-entry before a confirmed trend change
Sometimes he enters even before a confirmed change in trend on the daily candles. Example stock: downtrend; the 50 moving average has not crossed above the 150 yet and is still sloping down. By the time the blue crosses above the green and you buy, you'll be buying at about 46, a lot higher than now. If you believe in the fundamentals and know it'll be higher eventually, you could get in early, preemptively, as long as you start to see higher highs and higher lows in the price action. Here: high, lower high, lower high; low, lower low, lower low (downtrend); but then a high makes a new high (higher high) and the low makes a higher low. Once you see a higher high and higher low, the trend looks to be changing, especially the moment it crosses above the 50 moving average (blue line) and the blue line is flattening; that could warrant an entry. This is a pre-entry before the actual confirmed changing trend.

#### [15:21] Buying on a downtrend: special conditions
He may add shares on a daily-timeframe downtrend if a few conditions are met. First, the price is at the low of the downtrend after a sharp, 90 degree drop. Second, the price is at a strong support on a higher timeframe (above daily: the weekly and monthly).

**Example FICO.** On the daily time frame FICO is on a downtrend: wave up, wave down, wave up... He started buying right at a sharp drop. Why buy on a downtrend? Because after a sharp drop, it tends to reverse back up. Not always, but pretty high probability. It was at the low of the downtrend after a sharp drop, and on the weekly candles there's a strong support at the red line, the 200 moving average. Historically the price plunged, hit that red line and bounced back up; now it plunged, hit that red line and bounced back up again. That gave him the confidence to say it's the bottom, so he bought right there, and sure enough it's now bouncing back up.

**Example Mercado Libre (MELI).** On the daily time frame it's on a clear downtrend, moving averages sloping down, and there's a sharp drop at the bottom of the downtrend. On the monthly candles: wave up, wave down, hits the blue line; again; again. The blue line, the 40 EMA on the monthly candles, has historically been a strong support. That gave him the confidence to add shares right there before it bounced back up. Even though the daily candles are on a downtrend, as an investment you could still add shares as long as the weekly and monthly timeframe show a strong level of support.

#### [19:24] Which time frame? All three (Alphabet example)
Students ask whether to look at daily, weekly or monthly candles. He looks at all three, because each time frame shows different levels of support. Example: Alphabet.

*Daily candles* (short-term): look for historical support. In the recent retracement, wave down, wave down, wave down, every time it drops it goes near the orange line, which happens to be the 100 moving average for this stock. Every stock has its own character; some like to bounce off the blue line, some the green line. Alphabet recently likes the orange line, so it looks like a support. He draws a support level there, which is 833 (as transcribed; read as 2833 [filled from earlier pass]), his buy level one. If price goes near that level, he's adding shares, provided it's undervalued, which it is, because Alphabet's intrinsic value is like 3,008 thereabouts (as transcribed; the earlier pass also has about 3008 [filled from earlier pass], though the video 54 case study gives a range of 292 to 304 for the same value). Could it go lower and break the support? Sure; you never know how deep a correction can go. Where's the next support? On the daily candles there's another: remember a support level can come from a moving average or from you drawing it based on previous swing low patterns. Based on the previous two swing lows there's another support at 2670 (as transcribed), so he draws a second level. This is a bit subjective, depends on you identifying what makes sense, doesn't have to be perfect.

*Weekly candles*: is there another support? The blue line, the 50 moving average, is a support as well; historically whenever the price hit that blue line it bounced off (it's pretty far back so not visible). He draws another support level at that blue line, which is 24779 (as transcribed; read as 2479 [filled from earlier pass]). Three support levels now.

*Monthly candles* (very long-term): there's also a support at the red dotted line, the 20 EMA on monthly candles; price hits the red line, bounces off, bounces off. There was a big crash when it hit the blue line, but generally during a normal correction it could drop to this red line. Another support at this red dotted line, at 2-261 (as transcribed; read as 2261 [filled from earlier pass]). He thinks that makes sense: his fourth buy level. Could you draw more? Sure, then you'd have many buy levels. Usually he draws three or four buy levels and that's enough. From the daily, weekly and monthly candles he has four levels of support.

#### [24:03] Buying the tranches; averaging down vs averaging up
What do the four levels mean? Every time price hits a level he adds a tranche of shares. He never buys a full position all at once; he spreads purchases over three to four tranches. If he intends to buy 100 shares, he'd buy 25 shares each time, or 33 shares, up to you. In four tranches, he buys 25 shares each time: when price hits the first level he buys 25 shares; if it breaks lower to the second level, he adds 25 more, and keeps adding as it goes lower. Is he happy? Yes, because he gets to buy more shares at a bigger discount. Is it possible that it hits the first level and goes up and you never get to buy the rest? Of course; then you only have a quarter position. Don't worry; eventually it will come back down again and you take your next quarter position.

Buying more at a lower price is **averaging down**. **Averaging up** means buying more at a higher price, and that's fine as well: sometimes the price hits the first support, you buy, it doesn't go lower but goes up immediately, then comes down again, hits the support again, you buy the next quarter, goes up again, drops again, buy the next quarter, and so on. In that instance you are buying more at a higher price. You can average up or down depending on how it plays out. Which is better? Averaging down is better: you buy more shares at a lower price, your average cost is much lower, and you make a lot more money over time. But psychologically a lot of people don't like averaging down; it doesn't feel good when the price drops, they freak out, but it's actually good. Averaging up is not as good because you buy more at a higher price, although it's still undervalued and fine, and people tend to feel better because it's going up. He leaves it up to you. He's fine either way but prefers averaging down.

#### [27:39] What's next
They have gone through the seven steps in detail. Next, the lesson will have a lot of different case studies on US stocks, China stocks, different companies, so you can see how this is practiced in reality. For those who subscribe to the UIP, the Ultimate Investors Playbook, every month he does a deep dive research into the seven steps of two great companies and presents them every month: two great ideas a month with the UIP Playbook subscription.

### VMI Case Study (GOOGL)

#### [00:00] Setup: why Alphabet
A case study on Alphabet, ticker GOOGL, to see if it passes the seven-step investment analysis. People always ask where he finds ideas for stocks to analyze. As a new investor especially, always start by looking at businesses where you use the products and services yourself. He watches YouTube every day, creates YouTube videos, uses Google Search, Gemini, Google Maps, Gmail. So naturally he wants to analyze Alphabet, which owns all these products.

Alphabet has two stock tickers, GOOGL and GOOG. Same company, different classes of shares: GOOGL is class A shares, which give voting rights; GOOG has no voting rights. Most of us don't bother voting, so it doesn't matter which you buy, and the share prices are very close anyway. He uses class A for this case study.

#### [01:31] Step 1: consistent revenue, net income, cash flow
Does the company have a history of consistently increasing sales revenue, net income and cash flow from operations? Using Stock Oracle, go to financials. You can look at the trend chart, the actual financial statements, or both.

Income statement: revenue over the last five years at least: 257, 280, 307, 349, 402. Revenue growing consistently. Net income: 76, 59 (a drop), then 73, 100, 132. A general uptrend, although one year dropped; it doesn't have to go up every single year. Cash flow statement (annual figures): net operating cash flow 65 billion, 91 billion, 91 billion (flat this year), 101, 125, 164. Generally on an uptrend.

With the pro account paid subscription, just look at the trend chart: revenue in blue increasing very consistently, net income in green on a clear uptrend. If net income is not increasing consistently, ensure operating income is increasing consistently, as it is here. Net operating cash flow in orange is increasing consistently, very consistent.

As part of step one, also check gross profit and operating margins are consistent or increasing. Go to company metrics: gross profit margins over the last five years have been more or less quite stable; operating margins over the last five years have also been pretty stable. That passes step one.

#### [03:59] Step 2: positive growth rate and catalysts
Step two: the company has positive growth rate and growth catalysts. First place to look is Stock Oracle. He puts what he looks for under his favorites: projected three to five year earnings growth rate 15.3%; projected longer-term growth rate beyond that 10.13%. You can customize your own 10 favorites: click the three dots, edit favorites; there are 479 metrics altogether to choose from. His 10 favorites may differ from yours.

Originally: the projected growth rate is based on FactSet, the main data provider for Stock Oracle. Go to company metrics, scroll all the way down to projected growth: projected growth rate for three to five years 15.3%, projected longer-term growth rate 10.13%. The cash flow growth rate specifically is 17%, but usually he takes these two to be more conservative. He writes them down on screen: 15.3 and 10.13. As long as it has a positive growth rate, it passes this step.

He can also look at other websites for a different number to take an average. Finviz for GOOGL: projected growth rate for the next five years is 12.88%. Zacks: projected growth 14.72%. All positive, all double digit, great. Later, when computing intrinsic value, you could use the lowest figure to be very conservative, the highest to be more optimistic, or an average. There are many ways to value a stock whether optimistic or pessimistic, and we take a range. A valuation is always a range.

**Qualitative: where is growth coming from?** Alphabet is so big, everyone uses their products, they have 90% market share in search. One of the simplest ways is AI Insights on Stock Oracle, which lists the growth drivers: explosive AI and cloud infrastructure expansion; dominance in digital advertising and search with AI monetization; enterprise AI adoption through their Google Cloud backlog (Google Cloud being one of their core businesses); subscription and YouTube ecosystem expansion; strong free cash flow and financial firepower; global reach and ecosystem integration; and finally they own Waymo, the market leader in autonomous driving. "Waymo and other bets" is long-term optionality. If you think there are a lot of growth drivers and a lot of opportunity for the company to continue compounding your wealth, great. That's step two.

#### [09:11] Step 3: moat (most important step)
Step three, which he always says is the most important step of all: does the company have a sustainable competitive advantage that protects it from competition, an economic moat? He wants wide moat or narrow moat companies, and avoids those with no moat. Simplest: Stock Oracle. Go to Oracle IQ, then AI moat analysis, which gives the moat score. You may or may not agree; do your own research, but it summarizes and brings together the research you could find on your own. Alphabet's moat score is 9 out of 10, a very, very strong moat.

Sources: brand loyalty ("Google this, Google that"; the first thing we think of for search; 90% market share in search) and pricing power (advertising on Google or YouTube costs top dollar): 9 out of 10 for brand power and pricing power. Very high barriers to entry: 10 out of 10; it's not easy for anyone to start a website and compete with Google search given their history and data. High switching costs: 8 out of 10. Network effect: 10 out of 10. Economies of scale: 9 out of 10. The written analysis: Alphabet competes across multiple high technology sectors in search and digital advertising; Google holds an overwhelming global market share of over 90%, dwarfing rivals like Microsoft Bing at 4% and others like Yandex and DuckDuckGo. In cloud computing, Google Cloud is a fast-growing but distant third behind Amazon Web Services and Microsoft Azure. In AI, Alphabet's Gemini platform competes with OpenAI's ChatGPT and others, supported by massive R&D and infrastructure investments. The moat is rooted in its dominant global advertising platform anchored by Google Search and YouTube, which together generate unparalleled user intent data and advertiser reach. (He skims the rest: "blah, blah".) Does it pass step three? Yes.

#### [11:41] Step 4: profitable and operationally efficient
Check ROE and ROIC for profitability; for operational efficiency, sales revenue growth versus accounts receivables growth, and the cash conversion cycle. Alphabet doesn't sell a physical product, so the cash conversion cycle is not as applicable. ROE and ROIC are in his favorites; otherwise go to company metrics. The chart shows ROE in blue and ROIC in orange; as long as they are stable or increasing, great. Ideally 12% or more is a great business; over 15% excellent. Under profitability ratios: ROE latest is 35.7%, really high, and above the five-year average of 30%. ROIC 32%, above the five-year average of 27%. High ROE, high ROIC.

Sales revenue versus accounts receivables is under financials: revenue in blue is growing a lot faster than accounts receivables, great. If accounts receivables were growing faster than revenue, that's a red flag. The cash conversion cycle doesn't really apply to Alphabet (no physical product) but it's there; it has been more or less consistent over the last five to ten years.

#### [14:08] Step 5: conservative debt
Three ratios: current ratio, debt servicing ratio, debt to EBITDA ratio. Under company metrics, debt and liquidity ratios: current ratio right now is two, more than one, very, very safe. Debt servicing ratio zero, great (usually want less than 30%). Debt to EBITDA: want three or less, it's only 0.33. Interest coverage ratio should be at least 3 to 5; it is 112. On all metrics Alphabet is a very, very financially strong business.

#### [15:22] Passing five steps; step 6: valuation
It passes the five steps: a great business, high quality company; put it on the watch list and watch for the time to buy. Buy when the share price is below the intrinsic value, buying the margin of safety. Method: default DCF (discounted cash flow). To use it, cash from operations must be increasing consistently: it is (orange operating cash flow increasing consistently). Calculate manually with the intrinsic value calculator shown earlier in the course, or use the automated calculator on Stock Oracle (intrinsic value, scroll down); it pulls all the numbers and you adjust them. Using the discounted free cash flow method, it calculates intrinsic value of $190.89. This is based on the latest free cash flow, latest total debt, latest cash and investments, discount rate, shares outstanding and projected growth rates, all pulled for you. He won't just take it; he makes adjustments, because the calculator just takes the latest free cash flow without adjusting.

**Normalizing capex.** Review: free cash flow = cash flow from operations minus capex. For the hyperscalers (Meta, Microsoft, Amazon, Google) recently investing a lot in AI infrastructure, capex went up a lot, so free cash flow dropped a lot. Will this last forever? No, it's temporary. Taking the latest capex makes free cash flow unusually low and underestimates intrinsic value. For these hyperscalers right now, take the average capex of the last five years to normalize it for a more accurate free cash flow.

The latest free cash flow in the calculator is 73-66 (73,266 in millions; he later states 73-266). Derivation: financials, cash flow statement, annual: latest cash from operations is $164 billion and capex is $91 billion. Double-check: 164,713 minus 91,447 = 73,266, exactly what the calculator showed. The capex over the last five years: 24 billion, 31, 32, 52, then a big jump to 91, which artificially brings down free cash flow because of the sudden surge in AI investment. Average: 24,640 + 31,485 + 32,251 + 52,535 + 91,447, divided by five = average capex of 46,471.6. Normalized free cash flow = latest operating cash flow minus average capex: 164,713 minus 46,471.6 = 118,241.4. Put 118,241.4 into the calculator (intrinsic value, discounted free cash flow calculator) and recalculate: intrinsic value is $304.13. This is the **base case valuation: $304.13**.

**More pessimistic valuation.** The 304 is based on growth rates of 15.3% and 10.13% and 4% (from Stock Oracle). Finviz gives 12.8% (12.88%) and Zacks 14.72%. To be more conservative, take an average of the three figures (FactSet, Finviz, Zacks): 14.3% growth for the next five years. Change 15.3 to 14.3 and recalculate: the more conservative intrinsic value is 292.41. Valuation range: 292 to 304.

For those who are very lazy and don't want to do the adjustments: just look at the Oracle value, which gives a kind of median between this range, 294. You can simply use the Oracle value or manually do the adjustments.

#### [24:22] Step 7: charts and support levels
The first five steps show Alphabet is a great company that deserves a place on the watch list; the intrinsic value range is between 292 and 304. The last step: technical charts to identify potential support levels where he would add shares in tranches, and these support levels should be below the intrinsic value.

On TradingView, when he looks at a stock for the first time, he looks at the long-term perspective: a 10-year timeframe on monthly candles (roughly back to 2016). If it's a great company you should see a long-term uptrend; if you see a long-term downtrend, think twice. Alphabet shows a long-term uptrend with wave patterns: wave up, wave up, wave down... a big wave down (the COVID crash); wave up, wave up, wave down, wave up, big wave down, wave down, wave up (the 2022 bear market); wave up repeatedly, then a big wave down, wave down again (the trade war unleashed by Trump); then wave up, and now we're here. That's the general pattern you want on the long-term time frame.

**Draw the intrinsic value.** The range is 292 to 304; he can take the more conservative, 292. Use the drawing tool, horizontal ray (dotted line), mark at 292. He uses green to denote intrinsic value. He only buys when the price goes below this green line.

**Monthly supports.** On the long-term timeframe there's a very strong support at the 50 moving average (blue line): during a bear market or market crash, if price hits this blue line there's a high chance it bounces off, because historically it has acted as a very strong support. These are technical patterns. He draws a horizontal line at the blue line: 163-94 (163.94, as transcribed "1, 63-94" / "16394"). Is he predicting the price will drop to 163.94? Of course not; he can't predict where the price will go in the short term, and it may never drop to that level. But if there's a big crash and price drops there, there's a high probability it would bounce off the 50 moving average because that has been the technical pattern; it doesn't mean it's guaranteed. Also, the red line, the 20 exponential moving average on monthly candles, has also acted as support during normal wave downs (toing, toing). He draws another potential support level at the 20 EMA (the price was not stated aloud).

**Chart settings aside.** On TradingView he clicks L for logarithmic charts, and sometimes A to readjust the chart. When drawing support levels he does not click ADJ (adjusting for dividends). He only adjusts for dividends when comparing performance of a few stocks; for just one stock he does not adjust.

**Weekly (five-year).** After identifying supports on the long-term timeframe, zoom into the medium-term, weekly candles: click five year, which automatically goes to weekly candles. Price action: wave up, wave down, a downtrend during the 2022 bear market, an uptrend, a sharp correction, an uptrend again. The price finds support at the 50 moving average (blue line) on the weekly candles, which coincides with the support already drawn, because the weekly 50 moving average is equivalent to the 20 EMA on the monthly candles. This double confirms a pretty strong support level. When the price dropped it also found support at the 100 moving average (orange line) on the weekly candles, previously tested as strong support, which also coincides with a previous swing high: two points of confirmation. He draws another support level at 207 because the orange line has shown previous support and coincides with a previous swing high. Three support levels identified so far. Support levels can be subjective; two investors can identify different support levels.

**Daily (one-year).** Click one year to get the daily time frame (started with 10-year monthly, five-year weekly, now one-year daily). The current trend: an uptrend with wave up, wave down repeatedly during the entire period, then recently it waved down, then wave up, wave up, wave up; it looks like it's at the early stages of a short-term downtrend (as he says). How long will this downtrend last until it reverses into an uptrend? No one can predict.

He's already invested in Alphabet, for quite a number of years. Is this a reason to sell? No, of course not: it's a great company and in the long run it will always go higher; it doesn't go up in a straight line, in the short term it can go on a short-term downtrend, but eventually the trend will reverse back up. He doesn't sell what he has, but he's always wanting to add to build his portfolio, when it's below the intrinsic value. Intrinsic value is 292. With three support levels identified, if it hits them he definitely adds shares. But we can't predict whether price will go all the way down; it may never get there for many, many years, it could retrace and bounce back up and keep going higher. So he identifies another support level closer to the intrinsic value, using the closest swing lows (this is a swing low, this is a swing low, this is a swing low). Could he draw a support level right here? Yes, support, support, support, and the price bounces off. But personally he won't buy at this support level, because although it could bounce off, it is not below the intrinsic value: he'd be paying the fair price, not buying undervalued with a margin of safety. Nothing wrong with that, but he is very conservative and likes to buy at least below the intrinsic value. So although there's a support level there, he personally won't add shares there; he'll move down to the next swing low, there's another swing low, and through experience he places his support level at about 270.

#### [37:22] Summary of approach
For every stock he thinks is a great company, he identifies the intrinsic value and then below that identifies at least four to five support levels where he will add shares if the price drops to that level. He keeps adding until he has a full position. Once he has a full position he won't add more but lets the position grow organically. What is a full position? That's covered in the next lesson on how to build a winning portfolio (portfolio allocation). This concludes the case study on applying the seven-step analysis to Alphabet.
