# Part 11 — Lessons 55–58

### VMI Case Study Part 1 (MSFT)

#### [00:00] Introduction and the thesis for buying
In this video he does a case study on how he analyzes a stock and puts it through the seven-step VMI research. The stock is one of his favorites and is in his portfolio: Microsoft, symbol MSFT. It is listed on the NASDAQ stock exchange, in the technology sector, specifically the software industry.

Before you buy a stock, you must be very clear on your thesis for why you are buying it. You may be buying it for growth. Some stocks have low growth but you buy them for predictability: a very predictable company with predictable cash flow. Some you buy because they are defensive: during a recession these companies still generate high profits. A later lesson covers the eight investment categories (buying for growth, for dividends, for defensiveness). Before analyzing a stock, ask why you are buying it and what category it fits. For Microsoft, his main thesis is both growth and predictability. It is a growth stock that grows at high double digits, and at the same time a very predictable company, recession proof and pandemic proof.

#### [01:58] Understand the business: be the customer
Before putting any stock through the seven steps, it is really important to understand the business, products and services well, and the best way is to be a customer yourself. He keeps telling students to only invest in businesses where they are the customer, because only then do you truly understand the business. One reason he invests in Microsoft is that he uses its products: Microsoft Office for home and office computing, LinkedIn, Microsoft Teams (a more sophisticated version of Skype), and the Xbox, which he plays with his kids.

To really understand the business, also read the annual report, go to the company website, and do an online search of the company description. Quick description: Microsoft develops, licenses and supports software, services and solutions worldwide. They offer productivity software, cloud computing services and advertisement campaigns.

#### [03:12] Three business segments and revenue breakdown
- Intelligent cloud: cloud solutions for enterprises. In the market they are number two in cloud enterprise solutions, number one being Amazon, but Microsoft's Azure (their enterprise cloud solution) is fast catching up. Also Visual Studio and Windows Server.
- Productivity and business processes: Office 365 (Excel, PowerPoint, Word), LinkedIn, and Microsoft Teams for collaboration. Most enterprises tend to use Teams rather than Zoom (Zoom is more individual level).
- Personal computing: for the average person, Windows ("if you don't use a Mac, you're using Windows"). He uses a Mac and runs Windows on it too because a lot of apps only work on Windows. Also the Xbox, and Microsoft has huge potential in the upcoming Metaverse because it owns Microsoft HoloLens, a virtual reality and augmented reality headset.

Revenue breakdown: 34% from personal computing, 30% from cloud enterprise, and 35% from enterprise productivity and business processes (as stated).

#### [04:55] Step 1: consistent growth in revenue, net income and operating cash flow
Step one: does the company have a history of consistently increasing sales revenue, net income and cash flow from operations for at least the last five to ten years?

For historical financial performance he goes to two websites. First, Yahoo Finance: type the ticker (MSFT), click Financials, and look at the income statement and later the cash flow statement, always making sure he is looking at the annual figures. Looking at 2018, 2019, 2020, 2021 and the last 12 months, revenue is 110 billion, 125 billion, 143 billion, 168 billion, 176 billion: increasing consistently for the last four years. Net income: 16 billion, 39 billion, 44 billion, 61 billion, 67 billion: again increasing consistently.

Cash flow: click on cash flow, ensure it is on annual figures, and remember the denomination is in thousands and in US dollars. Cash flows from operating activities (same as cash flows from operations): 43 billion, 52 billion, 60 billion, 76 billion, and the last 12 months 81 billion. Increasing consistently. Always ensure free cash flow is consistently positive: it is. That passes the first part of step one.

For a longer history than four years, go to GuruFocus (gurufocus.com), put Microsoft in the search and scroll down for a visual representation. Revenue grows very nicely over the long run; net income also grows nicely long run. Certain years may show a drop in net income (one dip, around seven years back, in the earlier pass's reading) [from earlier pass]; don't freak out, it doesn't have to go up every year as long as there is a general uptrend. If you want to know why profit dropped, go to the annual reports and read the income statement. What you will find here is that it dropped because of changes in taxation, which is fine because it is not a core business expense that caused the drop; it is a non-core operating expense, so the business itself was still growing. Operating cash flow (the gray one) is increasing consistently in the long run, and free cash flow in green is also positive. That passes step one.

#### [08:58] Profit margins (related to step one)
Related to step one, also look at profit margins: ensure the gross profit margin and/or net profit margin is consistent or rising. For that, go to Morningstar (morningstar.com), type in Microsoft, go to operating performance. It shows the last couple of years' gross margin and net profit margin; scroll to the end to see the five-year average. Gross profit margin five-year average is 65%, and the last couple of years look like 65, 67, 68 (read from the screen): pretty consistent, and recently higher than the five-year average, which is really good. Net profit margin five-year average is 26%, and the last couple of years 30%, 36%, 38%: rising as well. As long as it is consistent or rising, that's great, as long as it's not falling. So step one is passed.

#### [10:31] Step 2: positive growth rate, projected to grow
Step two: does the company have a positive growth rate, and is it projected to grow in the future? Get growth projections from a few websites.

First, FinViz (which also states the sector, technology, and industry, software, and ticker MSFT). Once you type in the stock symbol you can check growth projections: in the last five years Microsoft grew at 25.7% per year, and it is projected to grow at 16.53% in the next five years. Note that down (he opens a sticky note). Second, Zacks (zacks.com): expected earnings growth for the next three to five years is 12.04%. Third, Simply Wall St (simplywall.st): another growth projection at 10.8%.

Different websites give different growth projections, so which one do you use? You can take an average of all three; if you are optimistic take the highest, if pessimistic take the lowest. Many ways to do it. What's important is that growth is positive; if positive it passes step two, since we want a growing company. Negative growth fails step two.

The average: 16.53 plus 12.04 plus 10.8 gives a 13.12% average (he says "13% average. 13.12% average"). He will use this figure later to calculate the intrinsic value. Bear in mind that sometimes one website gives a very different figure, for example 16% and 14% and 3%, where 3% is way out there; then he ignores it as an outlier, whether ridiculously low or ridiculously high, and takes the average of the websites that have more or less the same growth projections. That passes step two.

#### [14:17] Step 3: sustainable competitive advantage (the moat)
He has mentioned many times that step three is the most important step of all: the company must have a sustainable competitive advantage that protects it from competition. It must have an economic moat, ideally a wide moat, that fends off competition. Step three is more qualitative: there is no number to look at. It is understanding the business and, through research on competitors, figuring out whether it has a wide moat. The best way to understand a business is to be a customer yourself; the problem when you invest in a stock whose product or service you don't use is that it's really hard to understand the company's competitive advantage. He uses Microsoft and is sure you do too.

Sources of a company's moat (covered in earlier lessons):
- Brand monopoly
- High switching costs
- Barriers to entry
- Economies of scale
- Network effect

Ideally it has at least one or two of these moat sources.

Brand monopoly: when you mention Office software, does Microsoft come up as top-of-mind recall? To him the answer is yes. Word processing: Microsoft Word. Spreadsheets: Microsoft Excel. Presentations: PowerPoint. Operating system: Windows or Mac OS, though Windows has the greater majority of the world. Definitely a brand monopoly.

High switching costs (once people use the product, is it very difficult to change to another brand, are you locked in?): he argues Microsoft has high switching costs for both individuals and companies. For individuals, he is so used to PowerPoint, Excel and Word; even though alternatives exist he still uses them because he's used to them, having learned them all his life since he was a kid. At company level, if your company uses Microsoft Azure as its cloud enterprise provider, it is very costly, expensive and frustrating to change to another solution like Amazon once you're locked in.

Network effect: he uses a Mac and works on Keynote (the Mac version of presentations), but still has to convert to PowerPoint to share with colleagues and collaborators because many people use PowerPoint. That is the network effect: everyone is using it, so you have to use it. Another example is LinkedIn, which Microsoft owns and which has this network effect. So Microsoft definitely has three of the economic moat sources, and he says it definitely has a sustainable competitive advantage.

#### [18:30] Clue: the 10-year chart
Usually a company with a wide economic moat has a long-term uptrend in the stock price. Take the 10-year chart with monthly candles; if the price goes up like that over 10 years, it usually has a wide economic moat, because only companies with no close competition perform so well. Microsoft definitely has that: the 10-year chart is on a clear uptrend.

#### [19:24] Look at the competition
Another thing: look at the competitors by searching online "Microsoft's competitors". Microsoft has different competitors in each segment. In cloud, it competes with Amazon (not stated in the article, interestingly, so look at other sources) and also Google/Alphabet. In enterprise IT solutions, it competes with Oracle and SAP (and not really with IBM anymore; this is a pretty old article). So: Oracle, SAP, Amazon and Alphabet in cloud/enterprise. In personal application software, it competes with Apple, but Apple has a 14% market share in the desktop operating system and Microsoft Windows has an 86% market share.

#### [20:35] TradingView competitor comparison
On TradingView you can compare the stock's performance with its competition. He puts in Microsoft, looks at the last five years, and uses comparison with close competitors. Compared with Oracle and SAP in enterprise IT solutions, Microsoft performs a lot better, and obviously you want to invest in the strongest performer. Compared with Amazon and Alphabet it is not a direct comparison, because Amazon is not only in cloud but also e-commerce, and Alphabet is not only in cloud but also search advertising and YouTube; they compete in only one part of the business. Even so, in the last five years Microsoft has outperformed Amazon and Alphabet, so it is a very, very strong contender. (He owns all three companies, so he doesn't care which wins.) Compared with Apple, which competes more specifically in the desktop PC operating system and is not a direct competitor in many other segments (Microsoft doesn't really make much hardware), the two are pretty much neck and neck, very close, with Apple slightly above Microsoft. Based on the competitor comparison, Microsoft is a very, very strong business.

### VMI Case Study Part 2 (MSFT)

#### [00:00] Step 4: profitable and operationally efficient
The four metrics for step four (profitable and operationally efficient):
1. Return on equity (ROE)
2. Return on invested capital (ROIC)
3. Sales revenue growing much faster than accounts receivables
4. The cash conversion cycle

#### [00:46] ROE and ROIC
Go to Morningstar, click Operating Performance. Ideally ROE and ROIC should be above 12%; that's a great business. For the last 12 months, ROE is 49% ("Wow") and ROIC is 33% ("double wow"), way above 12%. The five-year averages are 34% and 20%. It definitely passes these two metrics.

#### [01:36] Sales revenue vs accounts receivables
Use TradingView to compare growth trends. Click Financials, click revenue, total revenue, annual (revenue for the last five years). Then go to indicators, search for accounts receivables, click annual. Revenue is in one window and AR in another; to merge them, click the three dots (more) on one and choose "move to existing pane above". They then have different price axes, one on the left and one on the right, so shift them all to the right: right-click the axis (two fingers on the trackpad on a Mac) and choose merge all scales into one on the right. Now revenue (blue) is going up very nicely and AR (magenta) is going up slower. That is how this step is passed.

#### [03:29] Cash conversion cycle
The cash conversion cycle mainly applies to retail companies that sell physical goods and have physical inventory, so it doesn't really apply to Microsoft much since it sells software, but he looks at it for practice. Click financials again and type cash conversion cycle. You want it to be declining or consistent. Here it is going down, very good. The shorter the cash conversion cycle, the faster the company makes its money back; if it is rising, that's a bad sign. It passes the four metrics.

#### [04:44] Step 5: conservative debt
Three ratios check debt level: the current ratio, the debt to EBITDA ratio, and the debt servicing ratio. The first two can be pulled off a website. On GuruFocus (bottom right corner), the current ratio is 2.16; as long as the current ratio is more than one, it's safe. Debt to EBITDA: we want three or less; it is 0.7, very safe as well.

The third ratio, debt servicing ratio, you must calculate yourself since he doesn't find it on any website; it is very similar to what we have in our own personal finance. Take the net interest expenses (how much the company spends on interest payments every year) and divide by the company's cash flow from operations (how much cash it generates from its business), multiplied by 100%. Net interest expenses are under expenses in the income statement (annual figure, last 12 months): the figure on screen reads 2,296,00[unclear] in thousands, mind you, so actually 2,296 million. Cash from operations from the cash flow statement for the last 12 months: $81 billion, $81.945 billion. 2,296 divided by 81,945 gives 2.8%. Very, very low, less than 30%, so it passes the conservative debt step.

#### [08:29] Conclusion: great business, and how often to re-do the analysis
Microsoft passes the first five steps, so it is a great business. Once you know it is a great business, put it on your watch list and watch for the price to be right, cheap enough, below the intrinsic value, to add shares. Next is to calculate the intrinsic value and see if the price is below or above it.

How often to do this analysis? You could do it once a year. Once a business is a great business it doesn't change that easily; it remains a great business for many, many, many years, for the next five, six, seven years, so you don't really have to do this that often. Once a year is more than enough. It takes a lot of work, but similarly, if you're buying a refrigerator, a TV or a car, you go through checklists to make sure you buy the best one; before you get married, you date that person enough to make sure it's the right person. When buying a stock you're buying a piece of a business, so think of yourself as buying a business and becoming an owner, so put in the research and time to find out it's a great business. Once you buy a great business you have no more worries: in the short term the price may go up or down, you don't care, because over time the business will keep growing in value and the share price will be a lot higher.

#### [10:07] Step 7: intrinsic value with the DCF calculator
His primary method is always the discounted cash flow method, so he uses it for Microsoft. Before using DCF, ensure cash flow from operations is increasing consistently; if not, you can't use it. Last couple of years: 43 billion, 52 billion, 60 billion, 76 billion, and last 12 months 81 billion. Consistent increase: yes, so the method can be used.

Calculator: key in the white boxes, ignore the shaded boxes because they have formulas built in, and changing them would change the formula. Inputs in order:
- Name of stock: Microsoft. Stock symbol: MSFT.
- Valuation method: discounted cash flow (options include discounted free cash flow and discounted net income). Use discounted cash flow for Microsoft.
- Statement currency (the currency the financial statements are in): US dollars. Always double check. For a Chinese company like Alibaba the statements would be in RMB and you'd change it to RMB.
- Stock listing currency (what currency the stock price is listed in): US dollars for Microsoft. The exchange rate between one US dollar and one US dollar is one; ensure this is correct. For Alibaba, statement currency is RMB (he says "Chinese yen"), and the listing currency is US dollars if you buy BABA on the US exchange, or Hong Kong dollars if you buy it on the Hong Kong exchange. Get all currencies right, and if it reports in RMB and is listed in US dollars, put the appropriate exchange rate. That is covered in the Alibaba or Tencent case study; for now keep US dollars.
- Most recent cash flow from operations: the idea is to find how much cash the company generates now and project it into the future. Last 12 months is 81,945,000 in thousands (three zeros, and three more zeros is six zeros, a million), so 81,945 million dollars, which is 81 billion dollars. Caution: ensure the figure you see now is similar to or higher than previous years, a gradual increase. If cash flow has been going up and up and in the last 12 months there is a big jump, do not take that number because it may not be sustainable; he would not use it. He would use the previous year, or take an average of the last three or four years, because he doesn't want an exceptionally high number that may not be sustainable. Likewise for companies where, maybe because of the pandemic, cash was growing and growing and then in the last year there was a big plunge: he doesn't want that figure either since it won't last very long and once the pandemic is over it will go back to trend growth. Instead of the latest figure, take the average of, say, the last three or four years for a more normal cash flow. For Microsoft growth is very consistent, so take the last figure, 81,945 million (the calculator is already in millions).
- Total debt: not total liabilities; it is short-term debt plus long-term debt. Go to the balance sheet and always use the latest quarter. Under liabilities: current debt (same as short-term debt) plus long-term debt: "3249, plus 5039" as read aloud, which he says gives 50,288 and plugs in. (The sum as spoken does not match; values were shown on screen.) [filled from earlier pass: the earlier pass reads long-term debt as 50,039 and the sum as 53,288 million, i.e. 3,249 + 50,039; the spoken "5039" and "50,288" appear to be a slip, and the raw transcript shows only the spoken figures.]
- Cash and short-term investments: an asset on the balance sheet, latest quarter. Cash and equivalents plus short-term investments gives total cash, $130,584 million.
- Growth rates: three of them (first five years, next five years, last 10 years). For the first five years, recall step two: Finviz 16%, Zacks 12%, Simply Wall Street 10%. You could take an average; if optimistic take the higher, if pessimistic take the lower, if unsure take the average; it really depends. He takes the average, 13.12%, for the first five years. For the next five years, usually the same growth rate as the first five years, subject to a maximum of 15% (the calculator says maximum 15%). If it were 20%, he would not put 20% but a maximum of 15% to be conservative. Here 13% is already below 15%, so 13% as well. For the last 10 years, he puts the average GDP growth rate of the US economy plus 1%; the last he checked it's 4.18%; you can just fix it at 4%.
- Number of shares outstanding: Finviz states it pretty clearly, 7.51 billion. One billion is 1,000 million, so multiply by 1,000: 7,510 million shares (put in millions).
- Discount rate: use the table below the calculator, based on the beta of the stock (this table is for US stocks; there's another for China stocks). Microsoft's beta is 0.88. In the table, 0.8 is 4.6 and 1 is 5.6 (as he states), so 0.88 is somewhere in between; you don't have to be exact, a number somewhere there is fine. You could take the higher one (5.6%), that's fine, or 5%, arguing 0.88 is closer to 0.8; he says "fine, we'll use 5%. Doesn't have to be exact."

#### [23:37] Result and range of valuations
The intrinsic value is $398. The current share price "as of today, 15 of January 2020" (as he says) [earlier pass has 15 January 2022] is $310; put in the current closing price of 310. Based on that, the stock is undervalued by 22%. This is based on the growth projection of 13%, and the main thing that can affect your valuation is your growth projections.

He always likes to have a pessimistic scenario: what's the worst that can happen? Assume Microsoft doesn't grow at 13%. Looking at the sticky note, the worst growth projection was 10.8% from Simply Wall Street. Put 10.8%: the valuation is 337, so at the current share price of 310 you still get an 8% discount, not bad. So 337 is a pessimistic valuation, while 398 is the base case, which is expected. It's always good to have a range of valuations; if the current share price is below even the pessimistic one, that gives a great margin of safety.

Another pessimistic valuation: assume the five-year growth is 13% but that after five years the growth drops by half. Half of 13.12 is 6.56; putting 6.56 drops the valuation to 324.99, "which is about 325" and still undervalued, a pretty good deal. This is "Pessimistic Part 2". So there is a range, and the current share price is 310: "do you feel pretty safe buying at this price knowing that the company is worth anywhere between 325 to 398? Yeah, I am."

#### [27:26] The last step: the entry point on the charts
It's a great business and undervalued, but is it at a great point of entry? Look at the charts and buy when the price is making a dip on the uptrend, or at a support level of a consolidation, or reversing into a new uptrend. He has drawn a green line for the intrinsic value of 325, the most pessimistic intrinsic value (you could put the more optimistic one as well). He looks at all time frames, always starting with the longest, the 10-year monthly candles. He wants a clear uptrend; if there isn't one, he's not interested in the stock in the first place. Then on the long-term chart he looks for where there has been very strong support, i.e. whenever there's a crash, where the price ends up. The price hit the 20 moving average (red dotted line) in each crash, so the red dotted line is strong support. He draws a support level at the red line: "260, 262", i.e. this level is 262. If Microsoft crashes for whatever reason (bear market, nuclear war or whatever), it will hit this support and you want to be a buyer at 262. That doesn't mean we only buy at 262; we can start buying already because it's undervalued, and you never know when it will hit that: this year, next year, three years from now. If you wait only for it to reach there, you may buy maybe once every few years.

Next, the five-year weekly candles, again looking for historical support patterns. The price hit the 50 moving average (blue line), which is pretty strong, so he draws another support there: 283. It was also a previous swing low (wave up, wave down); in a deep correction you tend to find support at the previous swing low. Also, the 20 moving average is a pretty strong support on the weekly candles, so another support is drawn there, giving three supports, all based on observing historical support patterns.

Finally, the daily candles, the short-term time frame, with the wave pattern again (wave up, wave down). Right now it has waved down and found support at the green line, the 150 moving average, which also coincides with previous resistance becoming support, so another level is drawn there. That makes four support levels: 316, 305, 283 and 262. Every time the price gets near one of them, he adds shares. After this wave up it waved down, hit the first level, and was undervalued: he could have bought one quarter of his planned allocation. At the second level he buys another quarter, so he could have bought half of the intended Microsoft shares he wants in his portfolio. If it drops further he adds more until he has a full position. It may not go all the way down; it could bounce from here, wave up and wave down again, at which time he'd buy another position if it does that.

### VMI Case Study Part 1 (AMZN)

#### [00:00] Introduction and thesis
Case study on Amazon, ticker AMZN, listed on the NASDAQ stock exchange in the US markets. Before investing you must understand the investment thesis. For Amazon, the main purpose is that it is a large-cap growth company: investing for growth. Usually when a company grows more than 20%, we classify it as a growth company.

It is within the consumer discretionary sector and the retail industry. Some people think Amazon is a technology stock; no, it's not. It is a retail company within the consumer discretionary sector. The seven steps: is it a great business at a great price, and what do the charts say.

#### [01:00] Understand the business
The best way is to be a customer; he is himself an Amazon Prime customer. If not, read up from the website, annual reports, and online searches. Quick overview: Amazon is in several businesses.
- Retail: most well known. It is one of the highest-grossing e-commerce retailers in the world. He says "one of the highest grossing", not the highest: the biggest-grossing e-commerce company in the world is actually Alibaba. Amazon is smaller than Alibaba in terms of gross merchandise value, but Amazon is a lot more profitable, with much higher margins, because of its cloud business. In retail (including Amazon Go) they compete with Alibaba, Walmart, Target and eBay. Amazon also owns Whole Foods, a supermarket that sells healthy stuff.
- Retail is not their most profitable business. Their most profitable business is actually their cloud services business. In cloud, Amazon is the market leader, competing with Microsoft Azure, Alibaba Cloud and Google Cloud.
- Game streaming: they own Twitch, which competes with YouTube Gaming and Mixer.
- Amazon Prime Music competes with Spotify and Apple Music. Amazon Alexa competes with Google Home.
- Smaller businesses: logistics (Amazon Logistics competes with Federal Express); Amazon Pay (competes with Stripe, Apple Pay, PayPal and Google Pay); Amazon Kindle and Audible books; Amazon Prime (competes with Netflix).

#### [03:30] Step 1: revenue, net income, cash flow from operations
Does Amazon have a history of consistently increasing sales revenue, net income and cash flows from operations for at least the last five years? Go to Yahoo Finance, put in Amazon, go to financials and start with the income statement, double checking that it is annual, in thousands and in US dollars. Only the last few years of data are available: revenue 232 billion, 280 billion, 386 billion, and last 12 months 457 billion. So four years including the last 12 months, an uptrend in revenue. Net income: 10 billion, 11 billion, 21 billion and 26 billion: increasing consistently, as is revenue.

Cash flow from operations (operating cash flow): 30 billion, 38 billion, 66 billion, and in the last 12 months a bit of a drop to 54 billion, but generally an uptrend. Free cash flow needs to be consistently positive: it has been, but the last 12 months it turned negative. Why? Something out of the norm needs to be looked at.

Free cash flow is equal to cash from operations (operating cash flow) minus capital expenditures (also known as PP&E, property, plant and equipment): whatever cash the company generates from the business after deducting the capital expenditure. If free cash flow turns negative it is for one of two reasons: either the operating cash flow is negative or the capex is very big. Here operating cash flow is still very healthy, so the only reason is huge capital expenditure in the last 12 months. When something is out of the ordinary you need to do more research: go online, find what Amazon is spending on, read their reports to find out if it is a good thing, whether they are spending on good stuff. Capital expenditure is under investing cash flow; opened up, you see a huge amount spent on PP&E, a huge jump from previous years.

He did his own research on why they spent so much in the last 12 months. His students ask him all the time when unsure, and he posts answers on the chat groups; that is the benefit of being in the chat groups, you can ask questions and he and his team reply. The reply: in the last 12 months Amazon spent a whopping $45 billion on capital expenditures, more than twice what it spent in the prior 12 months and more than it usually spends. The company is investing aggressively in a wide array of businesses, including new Amazon Fresh supermarkets, cloud infrastructure and voice-activated technology, but the biggest driver of the growth in capex has been logistics. This huge jump in capex obviously caused free cash flow to drop to exceptionally low levels, negative.

Is it good or bad? To him, good: the investments may cause free cash flow to turn negative temporarily for one year, but investing heavily in new businesses like cloud infrastructure and logistics will lead to higher growth and higher profits in the future. So one year of negative free cash flow is fine. But if it persists for more than one year, like two or three years, you've got to be careful.

#### [09:15] GuruFocus long-term view
For a longer trend in revenue, net income and cash flow, go to GuruFocus (typing Amazon). In the longer run there is very nice growth in revenue, which is what we're looking for. Net income looks really small compared to revenue, but further down you can see it clearer: net income (blue) has been increasing consistently in the last five years; operating cash flow (gray) increasing consistently; free cash flow consistently positive except the last 12 months because of the high capex investment.

One thing a bit unique about Amazon: its operating cash flow is much higher than its net profit/net income. Net income is going up, but operating cash flow is a lot higher. Why? He'll talk more about this later in the intrinsic value calculation; just bear it in mind for now.

#### [10:20] Profit margins
Also within step one, consistent gross profit and net profit margins. Go to Morningstar, type Amazon, operating performance, scroll all the way to the right. Gross margins for 2020, 2019, 2018 etc.: 22%, 25%, 26%, 24% (as read): more or less consistent, pretty okay. Net profit margins: 1% ("that's really low"), 4%, 4%, 5%: growing and consistent. Last 12 months: gross margin 25%, net margin 5.7%. Margins look like they are growing, and in the last 12 months (TTM) they are higher than the five-year average gross margins and five-year average net profit margins. Profit margins look healthy. Passes step one.

#### [11:43] Step 2: growth driver and projected growth rate
Does the company have a growth driver / growth catalyst, and what's the projected growth rate? The business is primarily in e-commerce and cloud computing, both in huge growth industries. More and more people are spending online, so it is a long-term secular growth industry, and cloud computing is also a secular growth industry. A lot of growth catalysts drive the growth of the business.

Growth rate projections: Finviz (symbol AMZN): Amazon has grown 101% over the last five years; this 101% is the average per-year growth, not total over five years. Phenomenal. Projecting forward, growth in the next five years is expected to moderate to 36% growth per year. Zacks: expected earnings growth for the next three to five years is 24.68%. Simply Wall Street (third opinion): 29.8%. They are all pretty similar, so take as usual the average of the three: 36 plus 24.68 plus 29.8 gives an average of 30% growth rate for the next five years. Keep this handy for the intrinsic value calculation later. It passes step two.

#### [15:09] Step 3: sustainable competitive advantage
Step three is as always the most important step of all: only invest in companies with a sustainable competitive advantage, an economic moat that protects it from competition. As of Q2 2020, Amazon Web Services has a 33% market share of the cloud market, a clear market leader.

Questions for a wide moat:
- Brand monopoly? When you think of e-commerce, does Amazon come to mind at the top? To him, yes. In cloud infrastructure, also yes.
- High barriers to entry? Is it easy for another company to compete with Amazon, especially Amazon Prime with same-day free delivery? It's going to be really difficult because of their scale and size; they can do it faster and cheaper than anyone else. So they have this barrier to entry.
- Huge economies of scale: you bet.
- Network effect (the more people use it, the more people want to use it)? Maybe not.
- High switching costs: not at the individual level. If you buy on Amazon today you could buy on Alibaba tomorrow and eBay the next day, so you can switch easily as an individual; not much switching cost in e-commerce. But for the cloud business there are higher switching costs: once a company hosts its stuff on Amazon Web Services, it is expensive and difficult to switch to Microsoft Azure, for example. So there are high switching costs in cloud enterprise solutions, but for the individual, not really.

He would say Amazon definitely has at least two or three of these sources of a wide moat.

#### [17:28] Step 4: profitable and operationally efficient
Four metrics: ROE, ROIC, sales revenue growing faster than accounts receivables, and a cash conversion cycle that is consistent or declining.

ROE and ROIC from Morningstar, operating performance. ROE for the last couple of years: 12%, 28%, 21%, 27%, consistently above 12% as read; last 12 months 25%, higher than the five-year average. Looks good. ROIC: 14%, 11%, 14%, 13%. Last 12 months it has been above 12% and above the five-year average. It has been slightly below 12% in 2017 and 2019, but last 12 months it's way above 12% and above the five-year average, so that's fine for him.

Sales revenue vs accounts receivables, on TradingView: put in Amazon, go to financials, revenue, annual revenue (last five years); then indicators/financials again and accounts receivables, annual. They are in two separate windows; merge by clicking the three dots (more) and "move to existing pane above". Then put the two axes to the right: right-click on the axis (two fingers on a trackpad on a Mac) and choose merge all scales into one on the right, so both have their scale on the right and can be compared directly. On the five-year chart, revenue is growing a lot more than accounts receivables.

Cash conversion cycle: indicators, financials, cash conversion, click quarterly as well, and look at the last five years. We want it consistent or declining; it looks like it is declining, which is good. The shorter the cash conversion cycle, the more efficient the company is at turning around its cash. That passes step four.

### VMI Case Study Part 2 (AMZN)

#### [00:00] Step 5: conservative debt
Three metrics: current ratio, we want it more than one; debt to EBITDA ratio, we want three or less; debt servicing ratio, we want less than 30%. Debt servicing ratio is the net interest expenses of the company divided by the cash flow from operations.

Current ratio can be found on any website: on GuruFocus, scroll down, it is 1.12. Debt to EBITDA, also on most websites, e.g. GuruFocus: 1.76. That's less than three, and current ratio more than one.

Debt servicing ratio must be calculated yourself. Formula: net interest expenses (how much the company spends on interest payments) divided by cash flow from operations, multiplied by 100%. Interest expenses come from the income statement (Yahoo Finance; he checks the data, saying quarterly). The company made 430 million in interest income but paid 1.7 billion in interest expenses; the net operating interest is 1.3 billion, or 1,311 million; put in 1,311 million. Divide by cash from operations: from the cash flow statement, operating cash flow for the last 12 months is 54,672. 1,311 divided by 54,672 is only 2.4%, obviously less than 30%. Only 2.4% of the company's cash from operations is used to pay its interest expenses, which is very, very low. It passes step five.

#### [03:25] Great business; choosing the valuation method
Amazon passes the first five steps: it's a great business, so put it on the watch list and add shares when it's a great price, when the share price is selling below the intrinsic value.

Which method to value Amazon? His primary method is discounted cash flow from operations, but recall from step one that for Amazon the cash flow from operations is much higher than net income, by more than 1.5 times. When cash from operations is a lot higher than net income, he finds it more conservative to use the discounted free cash flow method instead. Net income for the last 12 months was 26 billion, and in previous years 21 billion, 11 billion, 10 billion. Cash from operations: in 2018, while net income was 10 billion, cash from operations was 30 billion, then 38 billion, 66 billion, 54 billion: a lot higher than net income by more than 1.5 times.

Rationale: why is cash from operations so much higher than net income? Because of depreciation and amortization, a non-cash expense. When you calculate net income you deduct depreciation and amortization, but for cash from operations you add depreciation back. Depreciation, $31 billion, $24 billion (as read from screen), makes up a huge chunk of the expenses; adding it back to net income makes cash from operations very, very big. This tells us the company has high capital expenditures or high depreciation costs. For such companies it makes sense to use free cash flow, which is cash from operations after deducting capital expenditure. Free cash flow is calculated for you on the website: Amazon's free cash flow was 17 billion, 21 billion, 25 billion, and in the last 12 months suddenly turned negative, because of the huge jump in capex from Amazon making a lot of investments to grow bigger in the future.

#### [07:12] Normalizing the free cash flow
If we use free cash flow in the calculator, does it make sense to take the latest number? No: it is exceptionally low because of a one-time increase in capex. As mentioned before, when using the calculator and you see cash flow or free cash flow with a sudden dramatic increase or a sudden drop, do not take these extreme numbers because they will skew and distort the value of the business. Whenever there's a big change in the last year or so, ignore it and take the year before, before the skew happened: a more normalized free cash flow. So he will not take the last 12 months but the number before that, which is more normal: 25,924 (million), the end of 2020. That is the free cash flow he uses to project for Amazon.

#### [08:26] Intrinsic value calculator inputs
- Stock: Amazon, symbol AMZN. Method: free cash flow.
- Latest normalized free cash flow (not exceptionally low or high): 25,924 million.
- Total debt (short-term plus long-term): balance sheet, quarterly (always the latest quarter), under total liabilities. There is no short-term debt, so it isn't there. Long-term debt: $50 billion, 50,055 million.
- Cash and short-term investments: balance sheet, latest quarter, under assets. Cash and equivalents plus short-term investments gives total cash: 78,988 (million).
- Growth rate for the first five years: from before, Finviz 36%, Zacks 24%, Simply Wall Street 29%; take the average, 30%. For the next five years, the same as the first five years subject to a maximum of 15%; since 30% is above 15%, take 15%. For the last 10 years, 4%, the GDP growth rate of the US economy plus 1%.
- Shares outstanding (Finviz): 507 million shares.
- Discount rate: depends on the beta; Finviz shows Amazon's beta is 1.1. Based on beta 1.1, the discount rate is 6.3%. This table is slightly different from the one used for Microsoft because there has been a recent update. The table is based on the formula: discount rate equals the risk-free rate (the five-year average of the 10-year treasury bond rate) plus the beta multiplied by the market risk premium. The table is updated once in a while, so always ensure you're using the latest calculator; updates are posted on the chat groups and uploaded to the course website. Those calculating Microsoft may want to use the latest one too; it would be just slightly different. Use 6.3%.

#### [14:22] Result and range
Intrinsic value: 3,312 (as read, "3,312, 332"). The current share price of Amazon, as of Friday's closing price, is 3242.76, so Amazon is slightly undervalued by 2.1%. That is based on the average growth rate used (30%).

If more optimistic, believing Amazon will grow at 36% (Finviz's projection), changing only that gives an intrinsic value of $4,071 "about there". Intrinsic value is never one number; it is always a range, because we can't be absolutely sure what the company's growth will be and can only do projections. You can do a more optimistic and a more pessimistic projection and get a range. So Amazon is roughly worth 3,300 to 4,000 [he says "3,0003 to 4,000"; unclear] [filled from earlier pass: reads the range as about 3,312 to 4,071, consistent with the two values just given]. If the share price is below this range you are getting a margin of safety; currently the share price is below this range, at about $3,200.

Final reminder: always ensure currencies are correct. Statement currency is US dollars and the stock trades in US dollars, so the exchange rate is 1 (he says "1.001 is to 1.1" [unclear]) [filled from earlier pass: the exchange rate is 1 (USD to USD, 1:1)].

#### [17:05] Entry point on the charts
Now that it's a great business and we know the intrinsic value, accumulate shares at a great point of entry. Prices move in wave patterns, wave up, wave down, so always wait for the wave down to a support level. Buy either at the dip of an uptrend, at the support level of a consolidation, or when price reverses into a new uptrend. Look at daily, weekly and monthly timeframes to identify support levels and add shares in tranches.

Start with the 10-year monthly candles, wanting a clear uptrend, which we see. Next identify support levels from historical patterns: historically during a wave down it hit support several times at the 20 EMA (red dotted line), so it's a pretty strong support for Amazon; draw a support level there, at 3116 (as stated). On the weekly candles there is a strong support at the 50 moving average (blue line), so also a support line there. A support level can also be a moving average that showed previous support: the orange 100 moving average has acted as a strong support, and that is where the last support level is, a pretty significant level, "3-116" (as read). Another support at a previous swing low (swing low, swing low, swing low): a pretty strong support at 2881. So three support levels identified: "351, 3116 and 2881" as read aloud (the first value is unclear; shown on screen). [filled from earlier pass: the earlier pass reads the weekly 50 moving average support as 3,351; the raw transcript has only "351".] On the daily candles, the shorter-term time frame, there is a bit of support as well (support, support, support), so draw another: four support levels.

#### [20:20] Splitting the allocation into tranches
Four support levels means you can buy your planned allocation in four tranches. Amazon is a high-priced stock, so buying 100 shares is difficult for most people; with a small budget you may only be able to buy one share, costing $3,242. With certain brokers like Interactive Brokers you can buy fractional shares, e.g. 0.25 shares at a time, so each time you invest about 3,200 divided by four, or 800 dollars. You can apply for fractional shares through brokers like Interactive Brokers; if you've got a small account that's something you could do. So buy 0.25 shares at one level, another 0.25 at the next, another at the next, and if it drops all the way to the very strong support, buy the last 0.25 and you have your full one share of Amazon. For bigger accounts (a million-dollar account or a few hundred thousand), you could buy one share at each level, or three shares at each; it's up to you how to divide it.

#### [22:08] Is today a reasonable point to add?
The intrinsic value calculated had a range from 3,312 to optimistically 4,070 (as stated), a pretty big range. The current share price is below the more pessimistic valuation. On the daily candles the price is more or less moving sideways in a consolidation, and in a sideways move you want to buy near the support of the consolidation, a pretty strong support (hits the support, goes up, support, support, support). Right now it just waved down to the support level at 3198, so "yep, I think this is a reasonable point to add shares of Amazon". You could add more if it goes lower, so there are three support levels here; the first support identified is really below the first one. If you have no Amazon shares yet, add your first third position here, another third, another third (as he says).

#### [23:38] Second opinion: Morningstar fair value
For a second opinion on intrinsic value you can look at Morningstar, but it's a paid subscription (Morningstar Premium) to see their intrinsic value calculation, which they call fair value, at 4,001. Morningstar's valuation most of the time tends to be higher than his, because they tend to be more optimistic, or rather he tends to be more pessimistic. He likes to look at their valuation to get a sense of where his valuation is relative to theirs. He feels very, very comfortable because they value the stock at 4,001, "way above even my optimistic valuation of 4,070" (as transcribed; the two figures as stated are inconsistent). Right now the stock is at "3,002" (as transcribed; unclear) [earlier pass has the current price as about 3,242.76, consistent with the roughly $3,200 earlier], which is why he is really, really comfortable adding shares even at this level.
