# Part 12 — Lessons 60–62

### 60 - How to Diversify

#### [00:00] Lesson 8: building a winning portfolio
So far you have learned what stocks to buy (only stocks of great companies) and exactly when to buy (when these great companies sell below their intrinsic value and the price has retraced to a level of support). The third thing, even more important at times, is knowing how many shares to buy of each stock you want to own.

Some will ask: if I identify a great company, why not go all in with 100% of my money? Never ever do that. No matter how great a business is or how confident you are, never put all your eggs in one basket, because things can happen that you cannot anticipate or predict.

#### [00:52] The Boeing example
Boeing used to be a high-quality company passing all the criteria of great businesses. For years revenue, profits and free cash flow went up, and it was a duopoly with Airbus. In 2020 everything changed. First there were two air crashes that caused the 787 Max planes to be grounded (as stated in the transcript). Then the unthinkable, which no one could have predicted: a global pandemic in 2020. Borders closed, airlines couldn't fly and postponed or cancelled orders with Boeing. For the first time in its history Boeing, a great company, lost money, and its share price dropped 80%. If all your money were in that one stock and your entire wealth dropped 80%, "you jump out the window".

Did he own Boeing before the pandemic? Yes; he thought it was great and couldn't predict the pandemic, and his Boeing stock dropped 80%. Despite that, by the end of 2020 his portfolio was up 44% (first portfolio) and 41% (second portfolio); he has two portfolios and both owned Boeing. Why? Because he diversified properly. With several great companies, even if one, two or three have a massive problem, his wealth still grows because the returns of the rest make up for the short-term drops. That is why diversification is very, very important.

#### [03:07] 2023 example
Same thing in 2023. Some great companies he owned were whacked for certain reasons: Estée Lauder, Boston Beer and Disney. He thinks Estée Lauder and Boston Beer are still great; Disney is having problems transitioning from cable TV to streaming. These three dropped like crazy in 2023, and Alibaba also dropped like crazy. Despite that, his portfolio in 2023 was up 48% in the first portfolio and up 43% in the second, and he made a total gain of two million US dollars in profits across the two portfolios. His wealth still grows consistently because of proper diversification.

He knows people who also bought Alibaba, Disney, PayPal and lost so much money, whereas he made a lot. The difference: they didn't diversify; they went all in on one stock "and they died".

#### [04:24] The aircraft engine analogy
Would you sit on an aircraft with only one engine? He wouldn't dare. Even if it were the highest-quality engine made by Rolls-Royce there is still risk, e.g. a bird strike can make it malfunction and the plane could crash. But if the plane has eight Rolls-Royce engines, what is the chance of all eight malfunctioning or being hit by birds? Almost zero probability. Even if one or two fail you still have six. Likewise you invest in a portfolio of several great companies, so even if one or two malfunction temporarily, your portfolio keeps going.

#### [05:30] Capital preservation
To be a successful investor, the most important thing is not focusing on making the greatest gains; it is making sure you never lose money. The portfolio goes through ups and downs, but over two, three, four years you never want to lose money. In the words of Warren Buffett, if you buy a group of great companies and buy them when they are selling for less than what they are worth, it is impossible to lose money. "I repeat, it is impossible to lose money" if you buy a portfolio of great, undervalued companies and hold them over a period of time. Put everything into one stock and you could lose money if something unanticipated happens. It is all about capital preservation as well as growing wealth consistently and sustainably.

Our main objective is not just high returns, double-digit returns, but to get them in a very low-risk way. Collectively he sees it as zero risk: in the short term prices can go down, but over time collectively the portfolio will always grow in value.

#### [07:06] Rules of diversification
Rule 1: always divide your capital into a portfolio of minimum eight stocks to comfortably about 30 stocks. If you have very small capital, like only $1,000, still divide it among eight stocks. Whether you invest $1,000, $100,000 or $10 million, divide it into at least eight different stocks, minimum, to comfortably about 30. This is called concentrated diversification.

Can you go beyond 30, e.g. 100 or 1,000 stocks? That is called broad diversification; nothing wrong with it, but with too many stocks you can't monitor or keep track of them well and you won't do as well. Personally he has about 40 stocks right now, but he is a full-time investor whose only daily job is looking at his stocks. If you're not full-time, cap it at about 30.

There are altogether 6,000 stocks in the US market and we only want the top 1% highest-quality companies. 1% times 6,000 is 60, so the rule of thumb is no more than 60 stocks; beyond 60 you go outside the top 1%. For most people not doing it full time, comfortably 8 to 30 is a great number.

#### [09:20] Why diversify
Diversification reduces the risk of one bad investment damaging your capital. You can do all your research and find a great company, but things happen you can't anticipate. Boeing is one example; Alibaba and many China companies are another. They were great companies, but the Chinese government introduced regulation that changed a lot of their business models. Put all your money into Alibaba or one China stock and it drops 50%, 80%, and you would cry. He owned Alibaba and it dropped like 50, 60%, and it didn't bother him at all; he is still growing his portfolio year after year because one or two that didn't work out don't matter. The ones that did really well, like Nvidia, Microsoft, Amazon and META, more than make up for the ones that underperform in the short term.

No matter how good you are, there is no sure-win stock; you can and will have losing investments. Warren Buffett, arguably the greatest investor in the world: did he make bad investments over the last 58 years of running Berkshire Hathaway? Yes. He invested in airline stocks and lost money; Occidental Petroleum, still losing money; IBM, also lost money. But he is the greatest investor because he also invested in Apple, Mastercard, McDonald's and Coca-Cola, which made so much money they made up for the mistakes. If the world's greatest investor makes mistakes, so will you; the aim is not to make no mistakes. [The transcript has a gap here: a sentence beginning "But the 90% of the time," is cut off at [10:55]-[11:48].] The earlier pass reads the point as: the key to a winning portfolio is not being right all the time but winning something like 80 to 90% of the time and minimising the losses from the 10 to 20% of investments that turn bad. [filled from earlier pass]

#### [11:48] Diversify across sectors and markets
The eight to 30 stocks should not all be from the same industry or sector; have stocks from different sectors, different industries, even different markets at times. His current portfolio pie chart: about 27% technology, 22% consumer discretionary (sectors discussed shortly), 15% healthcare, 13% financials, 7% consumer staples, 2% industrials and 12% communication. Roughly about 90% of his stocks are listed in US markets, 4% or in fact 3% from China-Hong Kong, and the rest from the European market.

Separately he has a dividend portfolio focused only on collecting dividends, holding only Singapore-listed stocks: the banks and the core REITs (real estate investment trusts). His capital gain portfolio is 90% US, 4% or 3% China right now, and the balance European.

#### [13:05] Rule 2: equal allocation
As far as possible, divide capital as equally as possible among core long-term investments. Example: you invest $10,000 to begin with and put in another $10,000 every year; you divide the $10,000 equally among the total number of stocks you plan to own. [Part of the transcript is missing here; the 10-stock case, 10% each, is implied by later text.] If you plan 20 stocks, each has a 5% allocation. With 30 stocks, 100% / 30 = 3.33% each. He currently has 40 stocks, so 100% / 40 is about 2.5%; each stock he buys gets 2.5% of his total investment.

For more speculative investments (turnaround companies or speculative growth companies, discussed later) he would typically allocate only half or even one third of the normal position. With 10 stocks each has a 10% allocation, called a full allocation; a riskier company gets only half, which is 5%.

When does he add shares? (1) The stock is undervalued: share price below intrinsic value. (2) Not only undervalued, but the price has retraced to a level of support, as learned previously. (3) He does not yet have a full position. "This is really important." Once a stock has a full allocation, he adds no more. With a 20-stock portfolio each is 5%; once he has bought 5% worth, he will not buy any more. "That is the discipline." Never go beyond your full allocation.

#### [16:16] Worked example
Start a portfolio with $10,000 and aim for 10 stocks, so each gets a 10% allocation or $1,000. You can't buy exactly $1,000; it will be somewhere around there. Amazon at $150 per share: roughly $1,000 / $150, so six shares (about $900, since another share would take you over about 1,050). It can be slightly less or slightly more, roughly a 10% allocation. Alphabet: 7 shares; Meta: 3 shares; a high-price stock like Nvidia at $350: three shares. Roughly $1,000 each, a 10% allocation.

Per the previous lesson, you don't buy all at once; you buy in about four tranches. At the first support level buy one quarter, at the second level another quarter, averaging in. For six Amazon shares, when the price is undervalued at the first support level you buy maybe two shares first (or one share); if it drops to the second support level, buy another two. You buy in three to four tranches until you reach the full six-share allocation. Once you have a full 10% allocation, you don't buy more, until next year when you add another $10,000: divide again by the 10 stocks and add accordingly.

At any point in time the portfolio will not be equally balanced, because not all stocks are at a buy point. Nvidia may be overvalued, so you can't buy it until undervalued; it may sit at 0% allocation. Amazon may drop all the way to undervalued so you can build a full allocation. Apple may drop only to the first or second buy level, so you buy half (5%) instead of 10% and the rest only if Apple drops further. The idea is eventually a balanced allocation, but while building you will not always be balanced.

#### [19:42] His own portfolio
Over 40 stocks, so a full position is 2.5%. Allocations differ: Apple 1.7% (the transcript also reads 1.77% later), ServiceNow close to a full 2.5%, Meta 6.9%. Apple is only at 1.7% because it didn't drop enough to his buy levels; it hit the first buy level, he bought one quarter, then it flew up and he had no chance to buy more. The discipline is to wait for it to come back down to the buy level and eventually reach a full 2.5%.

On the chart for Apple, his calculated intrinsic value is the green line, about $170 (a bit blurred). The buy levels are 165, 157, 150 and 141. Every time it hits a level he buys more until full. Apple only dropped to the first level, so he bought one quarter of the full allocation, which then grew organically. The new stock "map page" [unclear] he only bought a quarter of, about 0.96%; that is a bit more than a quarter because it went up after he bought it.

Why does Meta have 6.9% rather than 2.5%? The allocation grew organically as the share price kept going up: when first bought it was 2.5%. Would he sell Meta shares to rebalance back to 2.5%? No; "we don't do that as investors". As long as Meta is a great company, still growing and not crazy overvalued, he keeps holding and lets it increase organically, but he will not buy more than the 2.5% full allocation.

Later he will also share that if you buy a stock at 2.5% and the price drops by half, so it is now 1.25%, will he add shares to bring it back to 2.5%? No. Once he buys 2.5%, that's it; it either grows organically or drops organically.

He mentions the Ultimate Investors Playbook (UIP), enrolled at inside.pranaprofits.com, with daily buy and sell notifications; as a subscriber, every month he does a deep-dive review of his portfolio showing the stocks, percentage allocations, a monthly update of the intrinsic value he calculates, and updated buy levels.

### 61 - Favourite Sectors

#### [00:00] Three main types of stocks
Different people want different stocks. If you're younger or don't mind more volatility, focus on high-growth stocks that can give huge returns but are a lot more volatile. If you're older or dislike volatility, go for stocks with lower returns but less volatility. Stocks are businesses, all different, meeting different objectives. All stocks can be broadly divided into three main types.

#### [00:28] Defensive stocks
Defensive stocks are companies selling a usually lower-priced product or service that is a necessity. Good or bad economy, you have to buy them: detergent to clean your house, toothpaste, sanitary pads for ladies, batteries, shaving cream or razor blades for men. So they have much more consistent sales and profits regardless of the economic cycle.

These are also known as boring businesses. Toothpaste, batteries, potato chips: boring. But boring doesn't mean a bad investment; Warren Buffett loves boring companies because a boring company is very predictable, sales and profits are very consistent, and you don't often get new competitors wanting to enter a boring industry. Boring businesses usually have low to moderate growth, growing sales and profits in single digits, 5 to 10%, but very consistently, with not much volatility even in recessions. Defensive stocks are great for investors who don't mind moderate returns with low volatility.

Defensive stocks tend to outperform, doing very well during a recession or when people anticipate one: people buy safe, boring companies, so defensive stocks go up during a recession or bear market. It is always good to have some defensive stocks; during market crashes they tend to hold your portfolio up. They tend to underperform when the economy is doing very well: in a boom people sell boring stocks and use the money to buy more cyclical ones. He likes to buy defensive stocks when they are down, cheap, when no one wants them, and then they protect his portfolio during the next recession or bear market.

Of the 11 sectors, three are defensive: healthcare, consumer staples and utilities. You could simply buy the sector ETFs: healthcare XLV, consumer staples XLP, utilities XLU.

#### [03:50] Healthcare
One of his favourite sectors: defensive, and some healthcare stocks also have pretty good growth potential because the sector is in a long-term megatrend due to the global aging population.

- Biotechnology (biotech): the most speculative and riskiest healthcare stocks. Most don't make money or consistent profits, but one or two make a lot of money: super high risk, super high return. Great for speculation; for long-term investing he stays away.
- Pharmaceuticals: most don't have consistent profits. The two that do are Eli Lilly and Novo Nordisk ("Novo Nordic" in the transcript). The trouble is patents on drugs last only about 20 years; after expiry they need new drugs in the pipeline, so there is a limit to their economic moat. So he normally prefers to avoid them. He broke his own rule last year by buying Novo Nordisk because it was very cheap, and it is still below his average cost: "punished by the market gods". Generally he avoids pharmaceuticals.
- Hospitals: he loves them; very predictable. The one he invested in is HCA Healthcare, which has done very well for him.
- Medical equipment companies, like IDEXX Laboratories and Thermo Fisher: medical supplies and equipment, more predictable, no patents running out after 20 years.
- Health insurance and managed healthcare companies, market leader United Health Group. Recently, as a group, they have been hit by many regulatory issues, but he thinks they're short-term; in the long run they should do well, especially the leader.

#### [06:13] Consumer staples
Companies manufacturing food, beverage and personal products, also retail/trading services companies. Think of them as essentials bought regularly every month at the grocery store: generally low-price items like toiletries, soft drinks, energy drinks, snack foods. They grow very slowly, like Pepsi and Coca-Cola. Generally he doesn't hold them long term, but he does buy them when very undervalued, and once overvalued he sells out and buys back again when undervalued. Unlike growth stocks, which he does not buy and sell repeatedly (he holds growth stocks through the ups and downs). There are exceptions: some high-growth staples such as Monster (pretty high growth) and Celsius (an up-and-coming energy drink in the health niche). Pepsi and Coca-Cola are very low growth; also Walmart and Costco. These will unlikely beat the S&P 500 in the long run, but are useful to buy when cheap because they protect your portfolio in a recession; staples tend to hold their price.

#### [07:58] Utilities
Companies providing electricity, gas and utilities to residential homes and organizations. Generally very low growth; he doesn't touch them. Demand for electricity grows less than 2% every year. People buy utilities more for dividends, and they are fairly defensive.

Recently, because of the AI revolution, there's big demand for energy to drive data centers, and certain utilities grow double digits, though not all: the independent power producers (IPP), which can set their own market price. Examples: Constellation Energy, Talen Energy and NRG Energy (the earlier pass also lists Vistra) [from earlier pass]. The problem is that as he presents this, many are quite overvalued and lack a consistent profit track record, so he doesn't own them individually but through an ETF, ticker AIPO [earlier pass also reads AIPO; filled from earlier pass]. Other utilities such as NextEra Energy and Southern Company are regulated utilities whose price is set by the state government, so they are very low growth and he tends to avoid them. Among the three defensive sectors, he usually doesn't buy utilities, but buys a lot of healthcare and some consumer staples.

#### [09:45] Cyclical stocks
The complete opposite of defensive stocks, with big ups and downs; he calls them the mood-swing stocks. Cyclical companies sell higher-priced products and services that people buy more of when the economy is good: new cars, new houses, construction and renovation, industrial equipment, borrowing more from banks. They make a lot of money, generating high sales and profits, during economic expansions, but in recessions and slowdowns they make less profit or may even lose money. Investors who buy cyclicals are willing to experience higher volatility for higher gains.

They outperform during an economic boom: when people expect the economy to do well, cyclicals are the first to fly up. Conversely, when a recession or bear market is expected, they drop the most, "like flies". There are five cyclical sectors: basic materials (commodities), financials, energy, real estate and industrials.

#### [12:08] Basic materials
Companies manufacturing chemicals, building materials, paper, and those engaged in commodities exploration and processing (gold, silver, steel, aluminum, fertilizer). Names given include Steel Dynamics, Nucor, Alcoa, DuPont, Freeport and Linde [from earlier pass]. Earnings depend on commodity prices: some years gold and silver go up a lot and they make a lot of money; other years prices fall and they make less or lose a lot. Revenue and profits are very unpredictable and very cyclical, and he likes consistency and predictability. Most commodity companies have very low profit margins (lots of competition), lower return on equity, and pretty high debt (highly leveraged). So he stays away. Exception he invested in: LIN plc, ticker L-I-N, an industrial gas company. It is not like natural gas; it produces industrial gas in factories, so its price isn't affected by normal commodity cyclicality. Although under basic materials, as a company it is pretty predictable.

#### [14:12] Energy
Oil and gas companies. Biggest: ConocoPhillips, Chevron, British Petroleum, ExxonMobil, to name a few. For the same reason he stays away: the long-run share price is very up and down and unpredictable. Certain years, like 2026 because of the Middle East war, when oil prices go up, these stocks do very well; in years like COVID when oil goes down they collapse. Very inconsistent. They are good for speculation, swing trading, a bit of short-term momentum trading, but he avoids them for long-term investing.

#### [15:06] Financials
Generally pretty cyclical: when the economy does well financials do better and in a recession they don't. But some companies within it are less cyclical and more consistent, with high single-digit rather than double-digit growth. Certain companies, especially in FinTech, do have high growth rates. The sector includes banks, savings and loans, asset management, credit services, brokerage firms, financial ratings and analytics firms, payment technology companies and insurance companies.

- Banks: be careful. Very highly leveraged, they could easily go to zero if they hold toxic stuff on their balance sheets, hard to tell unless you're a professional bank analyst. He avoids banks unless they are the top banks in the world, like J.P. Morgan and Goldman Sachs (he may invest at a good price) and stays away from regional banks. He is a big fan of the three Singapore banks, which he sees as very conservative and paying very good dividends.
- Asset managers: okay, not bad investments. Two main ones, BlackStone and BlackRock; high single digit growth, not super high double digit. Worth investing in when undervalued, but he currently owns none (he used to).
- Financial ratings and analytics: his favourite within financials, e.g. S&P Global (financial data analytics and ratings agency) and MSCI. The earlier pass adds that this group, along with companies that own stock exchanges, are "toll-booth" companies with little or no competition in their region. [from earlier pass]
- Payment technology: favourites Visa and Mastercard.
- Banks: just the top ones, JPMorgan or Goldman Sachs. BlackRock and Blackstone are the asset managers.
- Insurance: he likes it, though not all, only the strong ones. One of the strongest insurance companies in the world is Chubb (the earlier pass also names Arch Capital, ACGL) [from earlier pass].

#### [18:13] Real estate
He doesn't typically invest in this sector: most companies have high debt, low profit margins, no strong economic moats and no very strong return on equity, because it is a very competitive sector. It includes mortgage companies, property management companies and REITs. He does invest in REITs but for dividends, not capital gain, and only in Singapore-listed REITs because they have some of the highest dividend yields in the world and the dividend is tax free. US-listed REITs have 30% withholding tax on dividends, which doesn't make sense to him after deducting the tax, and they tend to have much higher leverage or debt. Top real estate companies: Regency Centers Corp, Extra Space Storage, Iron Mountain, Essex Property Trust. He personally avoids this entire sector.

#### [19:15] Industrials
Companies manufacturing machinery, tools and industrial products, including aerospace and defense, transportation and logistics services. Generally he doesn't invest much: higher debt, lower profit margins, economic moats may not be as strong. Exceptions exist; he invests in some, but not too many in his portfolio. Leading industrials: Deere (ticker DE), Honeywell (HON), Caterpillar (CAT). Deere and Caterpillar are very strong, very big companies, but very cyclical with inconsistent profits, so he doesn't like to hold them. He does invest in Waste Management (it clears waste), a very predictable company with consistent revenue and profits; one of the rare few industrials he owns. He also owns Pool Corporation, the largest manufacturer and wholesale distributor of swimming pools and pool [supplies] (transcript: "pool manduess"), and Copart, a leading vehicle auction/salvage company. [A gap precedes the next sentence.] Lockheed Martin is one of the leading military defense companies [name inferred from "Martin"; the earlier pass also reads Lockheed Martin, filled from earlier pass], and there are others like Raytheon and General Dynamics, but he doesn't own them right now.

Of the five cyclical sectors, the one he focuses more on is financials; the rest he buys a bit but not much. The majority of his stocks are not from the defensive or cyclical sectors but from the growth sectors.

#### [21:29] Growth sectors
Companies with high growth that are also moderately cyclical: whether recession or good economy, revenues and profits tend to be more consistent. Three more resilient, higher-growth sectors: technology, communication services and consumer discretionary.

- Technology: the biggest part of his portfolio. Companies engaged in design, development and support of computer applications and manufacturing of computer equipment and components. Leading tech he owns: Apple, Microsoft, ServiceNow, Palo Alto Networks (cybersecurity), Nvidia (semiconductors), Palantir (AI enterprise company), and a couple more.
- Consumer discretionary: retail stores, auto and auto parts manufacturers, residential construction (that part he doesn't buy), lodging, restaurants and entertainment. Higher-priced branded goods you have to think before buying, as opposed to consumer staples like soft drinks or snacks, which are lower-priced and need no thought. Favourites: Booking.com (the world's biggest online travel agent), Mercado Libre (the Amazon of Latin America), and Amazon itself, the biggest e-commerce company in the US and around the world; Amazon is not just e-commerce, it is also big in digital advertising and AWS (which is also under technology). Also Lowe's and Home Depot, and Nike, some of the stronger retail brands.
- Communication services: in the old days AT&T phone lines, which he doesn't invest in. He invests in social media, which falls under it: Meta Platforms (owns Facebook, WhatsApp, Instagram and Messenger) and Alphabet (Google, which owns YouTube). Pretty recently he added Netflix; he never owned it in the past, "which I do regret", but once it got cheap again he added it.

#### [24:23] Sector performance versus the S&P 500
Comparing sector ETFs against the S&P 500 benchmark over the long run: technology has the highest growth, followed by consumer discretionary (ETF XLY); healthcare is very much on par with the S&P 500. The rest, especially real estate, energy, utilities and materials, he avoids because they underperform the S&P over the long run. The only underperformer he owns some of is consumer staples, which is useful because it is defensive in a bear market and recession.

#### [25:07] His portfolio sector construction
Before buying, have a strategy for what sectors your portfolio will be made of; it is up to you. His preference right now: technology 27% (including Salesforce, Apple, [unclear: "Lever Systems"; the earlier pass lists Veeva Systems among his tech holdings, filled from earlier pass], Microsoft), his biggest holding. Second biggest, consumer discretionary: Nike, Amazon, Booking.com, Mercado Libre, to name a few. Third largest, healthcare (good growth as well as defensive): United Health, Thermo Fisher, Abbott Laboratories. Fourth largest, financials and communication services: communications include Meta Platforms and Google, financials include Visa and Mastercard. Industrials very small, only 2%. Consumer staples relatively small at 7%: Hershey's chocolates, McDonald's and Pepsi. Plus a small 3% allocation to China ETFs.

So far this has worked pretty well: last year in 2023 [as stated] his portfolio gained 45% versus the S&P 500's about 24%; in the last five years it has gained 145% versus the S&P 500's only 83%. He will continue to focus his portfolio this way.

### 62 - Dividend & Predictable Stocks

#### [00:00] The eight stock investment categories
Besides sector allocation, think about the thesis for why you're buying a stock. There are altogether eight reasons, which he calls the eight stock investment categories. If your main objective is the highest growth, focus mainly on large growth companies. If you don't want the volatility but want a consistent dividend for constant cash flow, focus on dividend stocks. If you're older, like some growth but want stocks that protect you in a recession, that won't drop much when the market collapses because they are very predictable, those are predictable companies. If you think a company isn't doing well now but will turn around and you could make a lot of money, that is a turnaround situation.

Analogy: a well-balanced diet of protein, carbohydrates, vitamins, fibre. Only protein (meat, meat, meat) is fine if you're younger because it grows muscles and bones; these are like growth stocks. Too much meat, especially when older, may not be healthy (uric acid and so on). You also need carbohydrates: like predictable stocks, to balance growth stocks. Some like spices, a bit more risky. Vegetables and fibre are like dividend stocks paying consistent dividends. It depends on your objectives. Generally people who are older with a lot more funds want more dividend stocks and less growth; younger people want more growth and maybe no dividend stocks.

#### [03:05] Category 1: dividend (income) stocks
Stocks you buy to collect dividends. If you're younger you may not be interested: they don't grow wealth much, have low capital appreciation, but give constant dividends. More suitable for older people, those about to retire, or retirees. He is 50 years old this year, so they are for him as well; he has a separate portfolio just for dividend stocks.

Criteria are those of a great company plus a few additional metrics:
- Dividend yield of at least about 5% as a rule of thumb, given today's interest rate environment (this could change over time). Dividend yield = dividends per share / current share price.
- Consistently increasing dividend per share for at least the last five years. "Very important."
- Consistently increasing net income and cash flows from operations for the last five years.
- Stable share price, either trending upwards or ranging, for the last five years. It doesn't need to go up much; if you buy for dividends, sideways is fine since you get 5%, as long as it isn't going down consistently.
- Avoid very cyclical companies like airlines, commodity companies, shipping companies, property companies: in a recession their profits drop and they cut dividends. You want a fairly defensive company able to keep increasing dividends even in recessions.
- Conservative debt: debt servicing ratio less than 30%, current ratio more than one (learned earlier).
- Dividend payout ratio between 20 to 100%, but not more than 100%.

#### [06:42] Payout ratio
Payout ratio = dividend per share / earnings per share. Earns a dollar EPS and pays 50 cents: 50% payout, very good. Earns a dollar and pays more than a dollar, like a dollar 20: payout above 100%, not good, because dividends aren't coming from profit but from somewhere else, which is not sustainable. Make sure dividends come from the company's earnings per share.

#### [07:38] Dividend yield
Dividend yield = dividend per share paid in the last 12 months / current share price. Example: share price $50, dividend per share $2.50 over the last 12 months: 2.5 / 50 = 5% yield. We want higher yield, the higher the better, but a common mistake is looking at yield without considering dividend per share growth. Can dividend per share fall while yield is high? Yes: if dividend per share drops but the share price drops even more, yield becomes very high, which is not good. So the yield should be relatively attractive but, more importantly, dividend per share should be increasing consistently for at least the last five years. Payout ratio between 20% and 100%, but not more than 100%.

#### [09:18] Singapore versus US for dividends
He prefers Singapore-listed stocks for dividends. Every market has its advantages: the US is the best for capital gain, not the best for dividends, for two reasons. One, most US stocks have very low dividend yield. Two, dividends from US-listed stocks are subject to 30% withholding tax for foreigners; capital gains are tax-free for Singaporeans, but dividends are taxed 30%. So he never buys US stocks for dividends; he buys them for capital gains. Dividends from Singapore-listed stocks are tax-free. Of Singapore stocks, he focuses on very few for dividends: the three Singapore banks DBS, UOB and OCBC, and selected high-quality REITs.

#### [10:29] Screening US dividend stocks (finviz-style screener)
Though he doesn't do it himself: for US stocks, most high-quality companies won't give 5% even in this high interest rate environment; most of the time a 3% yield is considered pretty good. In the descriptive tab, select a dividend yield of at least [the transcript gap; set at over 3%]. For himself he only buys at 5% yield, but you can't find that in high-quality US companies; the most is about 3%. Under fundamental add: earnings growing positively for the last 5 years, sales growing positively for the last 5 years, earnings projected to grow at least 5% in the next 5 years, and current ratio over one. Under descriptive, invest only in mid to large companies for dividends (over $2 billion). Out of over 6,000 US stocks, only 49 meet the criteria. Not all should be bought for dividends: avoid the very cyclical sectors such as real estate, commodities and energy. In the financial view, dividend yield above 3%: stocks like Bank of America, Cisco and Gilead Sciences (if he's not wrong).

For Cisco, go to Yahoo Finance, enter Cisco and click Statistics for dividend information: trailing dividend yield (based on the last 12 months of dividends paid) is 3%; forward dividend yield (based on projected dividends over the next 12 months) is 3% as well.

#### [14:18] Stock Oracle
For more in-depth dividend information, use Stock Oracle. Take Pepsi, a great dividend payer: the overview page shows standard dividend info similar to Yahoo Finance. In the dividend section you see the dividend payout ratio, current dividend yield, forward dividend yield, and the dividend growth rate for the last three, five and ten years, plus when the company pays next: the next pay date is 31st March 26, and the ex-dividend date was [unclear: transcript says "six months"; the earlier pass reads 6 March 2026, filled from earlier pass]. The earlier pass adds the point that you must hold the shares before the ex-dividend date to receive the dividend on the pay date. [from earlier pass] The metrics tab shows how dividend yield has changed over 10 years; the dividend per share (in purple) should be going up consistently, with earnings per share also going up, supporting the increase. It also shows the dividend payout ratio and a dividend history table (when paid, ex-dividend dates).

#### [16:01] Alternatives for income investors
For much higher yields you have more options than dividend stocks. US-listed dividend stocks give at the very most two or three percent dividend yield, and you deduct 30% withholding if you're not from a country with a tax treaty with the US. Singapore-listed stocks are not too bad, but yields are probably 4 or 5%. Another option is Singapore-listed REITs. Beyond these, non-stock instruments give higher yields: corporate bonds about 4.4%, Treasury bonds 4%, and private credit BDCs.

BDCs are business development companies, special companies designed for private lending. They are the only kind of US-listed stock he buys for dividends, but they are not analyzed like normal companies because of their unique structure: legally required to pay out 90% of their net investment income as dividends, and because of that not subject to the 30% withholding tax. (With a broker like Interactive Brokers they do tax you 30% but refund it the next year.) He invests personally because they give pretty good yields of 8% to 9%; at the time of recording about 12%, because of a lot of bad news in the private credit market, but he invests in the highest-quality private credit BDCs, so he gets very high yield with long-term safety.

There are also high-yield bond funds; he just bought one, shared in the Ultimate Investors Playbook alert to subscribers, with a yield of about eight to nine percent [the transcript also garbles a figure "5.77 percent"; the earlier pass reads it as a yield quoted for high-yield bond funds in general, filled from earlier pass], and other income funds. These are alternatives beyond normal stocks. He points to the Income Investor course, which he created focused on bonds, private credit and real estate investment trusts.

#### [18:58] Category 2: predictable companies
Companies that sell a product or service you use regularly, so revenue and profits are very predictable. Usually not very high growth, but profits are predictable. His mascot is a loyal dog. They are large companies with wide economic moats, predictable earnings and cash flows from operations, selling a product or service that never goes obsolete (hamburgers, chocolates). [Part of the transcript missing; he mentions consumers and that they can be in the finance or consumer discretionary sectors.] Large-cap predictables have very low uncertainty in net income and cash flow and slow but steady capital appreciation: not double-digit 20, 30% growth, usually single digit or high single digit, 5 to 10%.

Examples he has invested in:
- Pepsi (PEP): beverage and snack company, defensive.
- Chubb: insurance company, moderately cyclical.
- McDonald's: very defensive.
- Waste Management: very defensive.
- Monster Beverage (energy drink): defensive.
- S&P Global: global ratings and data analytics agency, very predictable, but a finance stock so moderately cyclical.
- Thermo Fisher: healthcare medical devices and instruments, defensive.
- Mastercard: payment technology, moderately cyclical.
- The earlier pass also lists Visa and Apple (predictable technology) among the moderately cyclical predictables. [from earlier pass]

It's either defensive or moderately cyclical depending on how you categorize; the intention is the same: profits are predictable. Not high growth, but they bring stability to your portfolio.

Although many have lower revenue growth than high-growth companies, those that do a lot of share buybacks can still increase earnings per share by double digits. As an investor you can still get pretty high double-digit returns and beat the market with predictable companies, especially buying at big discounts during temporary crises. And when the price is way over-extended or over[valued], he combines it with selling covered call options to create extra income that increases returns (transcript cut off). For how he sells covered calls, see lesson 12 on turbocharging the portfolio using options.
