# Part 13 — Lessons 63–67

### 63 - Growth Stocks & More

#### [00:00] Category 3: large growth companies
The third investment category makes up most of his stocks: large growth companies. He uses a "cheater" (a stand-in) to represent this type. These are big companies that still have huge growth potential, growing at above 20 to 25% a year. The majority tend to be technology-related companies, but not all, though most. They are also companies in secular or long-term growth industries like cybersecurity, e-commerce and software. They have been profitable for at least 5 years; that is the dividing line versus speculative growth stocks. [from earlier pass]

Usually the stock price of growth companies is overvalued, but there will be times when, if you are patient, they get undervalued during corrections, pullbacks and even bear markets, and that is when we can really build a strong position.

Growth companies could have a wide economic moat (brand monopoly, high switching costs, network effect that lasts for at least 20 years), such as Microsoft, Nvidia, Google and Meta Platforms. Or they may be narrow-moat growth companies, which also have a competitive advantage that lasts at least 10 years. Investing in either is fine as long as it has a moat. In general narrow-moat companies are a bit more risky, so he usually takes smaller positions in them, especially initially.

Take note: growth companies, especially in their early stages, usually don't pay dividends most of the time, and may not do share buybacks. They may in fact issue more shares and give a lot of stock-based compensation. Once they reach a certain maturity, like Microsoft, they start doing share buybacks and paying dividends. Whether they do buybacks or pay dividends, the cash flow will make up the free cash flow per share. [The transcript is garbled at this point; the names listed include Google, Nvidia, Salesforce and others, partly unclear.] The names in this category include Google, MercadoLibre, Nvidia, Veeva Systems, Salesforce and ServiceNow (and Microsoft, Meta, Amazon, Adobe among the wide-moat ones); narrow-moat examples are Booking.com, IDEXX and Tesla. [filled from earlier pass] The high growth in revenue, profit and free cash flow outruns the dilution from new shares, so free cash flow per share and EPS still grow at double digits. [from earlier pass] He says Tesla is the only one among them he doesn't invest in, because he is a bit concerned that recently their pricing power has not been effective. They have to cut prices, which is usually a red flag, and that is why he has avoided Tesla even though it is a narrow-moat growth company.

#### [02:58] Category 4: speculative growth stocks
The fourth category is speculative growth stocks. He very rarely invests in these. They are usually relatively new growth companies that have the potential to become wide-moat stocks that dominate their industry, but currently only have a narrow moat or may not even have a moat yet; they are still building it. Their revenue is growing at very high rates, usually over 100% revenue growth, but because of high investment costs and lack of profits [partly garbled in the transcript]. Sometimes you can make a lot of money from them, but you can also lose a lot, so be careful. For example, Amazon used to be a speculative stock; for the first seven years after listing [the transcript is garbled here, referring back to the lesson on stock valuation]. Amazon did not make any money in those first seven years, but its revenue grew like crazy and the share price rose about 15-fold during that period. [filled from earlier pass] The caution: for every one Amazon that made it, 99 others didn't. [from earlier pass] Also, large growth stocks are already profitable for at least 5 years; speculative ones are not. His own entry rule is to buy once the company can turn a profit for at least the first year, not requiring the 5-year track record, while acknowledging it is a lot more risky. [from earlier pass]

#### [05:21] Valuing speculative growth stocks and position sizing
For companies that do not yet have consistent profits and cash flow, value them with the price to sales growth ratio: the price to sales ratio divided by the projected revenue growth. Ideally it should be between 0.2 to 0.3, which is fair value. More than 0.3 is overvalued; ideally below 0.3, or below 0.2 is really, really undervalued.

Companies in this category do not yet pass all the steps of a great business, so they are much higher risk. He does invest in them, but rarely; if he finds a great one, he invests. Because it is riskier he does not invest his normal allocation. If his normal allocation is, say, 5% of the portfolio, he invests 2.5%. If his normal allocation is 2%, he invests 1%. He always invests half or one third of his usual position, because it's higher risk. If it doesn't work out and the worst happens and it goes to 0, he loses 1% of the portfolio. But you rarely go to zero; normally those that don't work out lose half their money and don't come back, so at most he is down 0.5%, which is okay. Those that do work out, because they are high growth, could go up 5 to 10 times the money put in. So this category is super high risk but very high returns, so keep a small allocation.

#### [06:55] Examples: Palantir and others
In 2022 he bought Palantir. At that time it did not yet have one year of net profits or free cash flow. The moment it had one quarter of profits, he started to invest because he was very impressed by the business model. This was before ChatGPT, before AI became popular; they were one of the very first AI enterprise companies able to help other companies transform their business. He thought it had huge potential, so he invested just 1% of his portfolio into Palantir. After about four years, it went up [the transcript is garbled here; the size of the gain was not captured] about 20 times (also stated as "24"). [filled from earlier pass] The business he cited as an example of its customers is Airbus. [from earlier pass] You invest a small percentage, but because these stocks can go up so much they can eventually make a big impact on your portfolio. He sold some along the way when it got really overvalued, but still holds a sizable position.

Another speculative growth stock he bought about a year or so ago is [unclear; sounds like "South years"] Celsius. [filled from earlier pass] He bought it before it had the five-year track record of consistent profits and cash flow. It has gone up quite a bit, but not yet double or triple his money; he believes it will in the near future. One he didn't buy is Uber; he didn't have the same conviction, although he thinks it's not a bad company. Two interesting ones are AST Space Mobile and Rocket Lab, which could be huge winners in the new space economy. The only reason he hasn't bought them yet is that he thinks they are way overvalued on the price to sales growth model. If they get fairly priced or undervalued, he may start to take a position.

#### [08:55] Calculating price to sales growth in Stock Oracle (AST Space Mobile)
You can calculate the ratio yourself, but Stock Oracle also pre-calculates it. Using AST Space Mobile as the example: under company metrics you can see sales growing, but it is still losing money and unprofitable, so you use the price to sales growth ratio. Scroll down to projected growth: the projected revenue growth is 127% (about 127.6 shown on screen). The price to sales ratio currently is 324 (as stated aloud). Dividing the price to sales ratio by the revenue growth rate of roughly 127% gives roughly a price to sales growth ratio of 2.54 as stated, which is still way overvalued, since 0.2 to 0.3 is fairly priced. That's why he has not yet entered a position.

#### [10:40] Category 5: deep cyclical stocks
The fifth category is deep cyclical stocks. Personally he very rarely invests in these, but mentions them because many investors focus on them and once in a while they make an attractive investment. These are companies in capital intensive industries, for example shipping, real estate, property development, commodities, oil and gas. They are highly sensitive to the economic cycle and unable to respond to demand changes quickly. They make significant profits during economic booms but smaller profits or even losses during recession. They are very cyclical: make money, lose money, make money, lose money, and the stock price is like a big roller coaster.

With normal great companies he can hold for the long term and keep compounding wealth. [The transcript is garbled here.] But with deep cyclicals you can't hold for the long term; you have to get in and get out fast. If you hold for the long run you don't make much money because of the big, volatile nature of the business. Buy near the bottom of the cycle when they are generating low profits and are very undervalued; sell when they are overvalued. You do the opposite of the crowd. [Partly garbled.] Buy when profits are low or in a temporary loss and the stock is very undervalued; sell when they are making a lot of money and are overvalued. [filled from earlier pass] Their share price tends to be near certain chart levels, so look at the weekly and monthly charts; that's when you can start to sell and take profits. He shows an example shortly.

#### [14:00] Example: Caterpillar
Unlike consistent companies, for deep cyclicals like Caterpillar revenue goes down, up, down, up; very cyclical. Same with profits. He mentions real estate developers, industrial companies, offshore and marine companies, and commodity companies. Caterpillar's share price is very volatile: up, down, down, up, down, down, and every time it drops, it can drop as much as 50%. If you just hold through the ups and downs, over time you don't make much return because of the big drops. So get in after a big drop, after it has dropped like 40, 50%, when it hits a very strong support level, in this case the 200 moving average on the monthly candles; then you can buy. Once it waves up and starts making a lot of money, get out. It crashes again 50%, get in again, waves up, get out, crashes, get in. You play the cycles. He says it's not really his cup of tea and he doesn't usually do this, but if you want to play deep cyclicals, that's the way.

#### [15:43] Category 6: deep value stocks
The next category/style is deep value stocks. This was made popular by Benjamin Graham, the father of value investing, as well as Warren Buffett; when Buffett first started, his approach was buying deep value stocks. His own style is to buy very good companies; he mentions this because some investors use this style, and he goes through it quickly to give an idea.

In deep value stocks the share price is selling lower than the net [asset value, garbled] on their balance sheets. If you buy these stocks, you basically get the business for free. It involves buying distressed companies selling below their net working capital less the long-term debt. Net working capital is current assets minus current liabilities, then you deduct the long-term debt, and you divide by the shares outstanding. This number has to be more than the current share price. If you can buy at a share price below this number, even if the company winds up and liquidates you are getting more than what you paid. Bear in mind many of these companies may not have strong economic moats; in fact most have no moat.

However, do not invest in sunset industries or companies whose products are becoming obsolete. Avoid these lousy companies even if very cheap, as the value of the company could continually be eroded. But we can buy deep value stocks if they are still profitable and the balance sheet is not deteriorating. Don't expect the company to recover immediately; you need long-term patience. A deep value stock is usually in a difficult situation and it may take time, maybe several years, for the company to recover and the share price to rise. That's why he doesn't like these stocks: it takes a long time to get your money back. He does not use this style and owns nothing in this category. [from earlier pass]

They are also called cigar butt stocks, Buffett's image of picking up a cigar butt with one free puff left, but it's soggy, wet, dirty: a dirty company but with some money left.

#### [19:17] Criteria for deep value stocks
If you decide to buy these companies:
- Positive cash flow from operations for the last 12 months.
- Low debt; current ratio must be more than 1, and [a threshold of "30%" is stated but the transcript is garbled; unclear] (the earlier pass reads this as debt-to-equity below 1 and a debt servicing ratio below 30%, the standard VMI rule, noting the transcript says "more than 30%"). [filled from earlier pass]
- Net working capital less long-term debt divided by shares outstanding must be higher than the current share price.
- Wait for the price to be above the 150 moving average, i.e. wait for an uptrend pattern; if in a downtrend, don't buy, only in a consolidation [garbled]. The earlier pass records the entry as buying at a support level in a consolidation, or when it is reversing into a new uptrend, defined as the 50 MA above the 150 MA, and never in a downtrend; the exit is when the 50 MA crosses back below the 150 MA, or when the company starts to report a loss. [filled from earlier pass] Some people use this approach, but personally he doesn't like it too much.


### 64 - Maximizing Portfolio returns

#### [00:00] Category 7: turnaround companies
Category number seven is turnaround stocks. These are great businesses hit by some kind of temporary bad news, like a lawsuit, a boycott, a scandal, mismanagement, recession; something that caused the company's profits to drop temporarily. He classifies a great company as being in a turnaround situation if profits drop more than 25% in one year, or profits decline for two consecutive years. Either situation, he calls it a turnaround company. The company may be making temporary losses because of the bad news, but their economic moat and competitive advantage remain intact. In such cases the stock price could fall 40 to 50% below intrinsic value, and that could be a time to buy these great companies.

He cautions that turnaround stocks are more risky than usual growth stocks or predictable stocks, because some may take a long time to turn around, and there could be cases where it never turns around because the short-term problem becomes a long-term problem, or the economic moat deteriorates because of the problem, which can happen. He gives examples of turnaround stocks he bought over the years: some turned around really well, some have not yet, and some he thinks have long-term structural issues.

#### [01:44] Turnaround examples: Meta, Boeing, Boston Beer, Estee Lauder, Disney
Meta Platforms (Facebook and Instagram): in 2021 and 2022 Facebook had problems because Apple tweaked its privacy settings, so Meta couldn't target customers properly. At the same time they spent a lot of money on the Metaverse. Because of the heavy Metaverse expenditure and the inability to target due to Apple's privacy changes, Meta's profits dropped about 40%. People panicked, and the share price dropped 70%. That was a turnaround situation where you could buy a great company at a 70% discount. He bought more Meta shares, and it went from $70 to $80 per share to close to $400 per share. Meta is now one of the biggest stocks in his portfolio and has given him the most profit. A successful turnaround.

Boeing: in 2020, profits went from making money to losing money because of the plane crash, the grounding of planes, and the pandemic causing airlines to cancel orders. A short-term problem for a great company, so he bought Boeing; he already owned it and added more shares when it dropped like 70%. When it rebounded he eventually sold it for a small profit, and decided not to buy it back, because since that crash Boeing has had more and more problems, like the door falling off. He thinks Boeing is one of the rare cases where it is no longer a great company: its brand image has been punished by execution problems and its moat has been deteriorating. So he sold at a profit but won't hold it or buy it back.

He is still holding Estee Lauder and Boston Beer, which are also turnaround companies. Boston Beer had a very good product, hard seltzer; they over-projected revenue and built a lot of capacity, but demand for the beverage dropped, so they suffered short-term profit losses and the share price dropped more than 60%. He believes it is beginning to turn around now. Estee Lauder was hit by supply chain problems because of COVID as well as the weak Chinese consumer, so profits dropped two years in a row. Neither has turned around yet, but he thinks they will in the next one to two years.

He also mentions a stock he bought because he thought it was a short-term problem but sold at a loss, because after reviewing it he realized the short-term problem may be a longer-term problem (the stock is not named clearly in the transcript). The lesson: when you invest in turnaround companies they can take a longer time to turn around, or in rare cases never turn around, as in the case of Disney, for example. Disney's problem was the struggling transition from cable TV to streaming TV, which he judged a long-term structural problem rather than a short-term one, so he sold at a loss. [from earlier pass] So exercise more caution and put in a smaller than usual allocation.

#### [05:33] Category 8: ETFs
The eighth and last category is ETFs, covered in lesson two. You can buy index ETFs that track the entire market, like the China ETF or the S&P 500 ETF; a sector ETF, like the healthcare ETF XLV or the technology ETF XLK; or specific industry ETFs, like cybersecurity ETFs IHAK or HACK (two cybersecurity ETFs). The great thing is you're buying a basket of many companies, so no need to calculate intrinsic value and no company-specific risk. So for ETFs you just focus on buying based on technical analysis: trends, moving averages, support and resistance. Some ETFs show the PE ratio, which gives a rough idea of valuation; for certain ETFs you can take the PE ratio and divide by the earnings growth rate to get the PEG ratio, and if the PEG ratio is less than about two, it's reasonably priced. You can buy country-specific ETFs, sector and industry ETFs, and other kinds like bond ETFs and commodity ETFs, though he would avoid commodity ETFs except for short-term speculative trades, not long-term investments.

#### [07:34] Why the eight categories matter: investment thesis
The eight categories: growth stocks, predictable stocks, deep cyclical, speculative growth, turnaround companies, dividend plays, deep value stocks and ETFs. Before you buy a stock, ask what your investment thesis is: why are you buying it? It has to fit into one of these eight categories. If it doesn't fit, you shouldn't buy it.

For example he owns Nvidia because it is a growth company; DBS Bank he buys for dividends, not growth. When people ask "Adam, which is better, Nvidia or DBS Bank?", you can't compare a steak with an apple: one is protein, one is a vegetable. Likewise you can't compare Nvidia, a growth company, with DBS, more of a dividend stock. Nvidia, a growth stock, must be compared with other growth stocks such as ServiceNow, Salesforce, Microsoft. DBS, a dividend stock, is compared with other dividend stocks like OCBC, UOB, CapitaLand Ascendas REIT and other high-dividend stocks.

A stock can transition from one category to another. Apple for many years was a company growing over 20%; now its growth has slowed to less than 20%, but its sales and profits are more predictable because of recurring revenue from its services business, so Apple went from a growth stock to a predictable stock. Meta was a growth stock, was hit by temporary bad news in 2021 and became a turnaround, and now that it has turned around and problems are resolved, it is a growth stock again. A speculative growth stock like Palantir, once it achieves five years of consistent sales and profits, may eventually upgrade into a growth stock.

#### [10:23] What Adam's own portfolio looks like
Different investors have different styles; it's up to you, and he shares what he does. Almost half his stocks are growth companies: no problem with revenue, growing 20%. That is how his portfolio is able to beat the S&P 500. He also has quite a number of predictable companies: defensive ones (healthcare and consumer staples), moderately cyclical ones like predictable technology companies such as Apple, and predictable financials like Visa and MasterCard. These are more defensive; in a recession or bear market they tend to be more resilient and balance the portfolio.

He has some ETFs in specific industries (the earlier pass also lists an AI-power ETF, AIPO, and China ETFs at about 3% of the portfolio) [from earlier pass]: the healthcare medical devices ETF IHI, and the semiconductor ETF called SOXX (transcribed "FOX"; unclear). Deep value companies: none. Deep cyclical: none. Speculative growth: only one, Palantir; he tends to be rare with speculative stocks. Turnaround: a few right now but not too many, Estee Lauder and Boston Beer; he does his best not to buy too many turnarounds because they are more risky. For dividend stocks he has a separate portfolio altogether, mentioned earlier, investing primarily in Singapore-listed banks and high-quality REITs.

#### [12:44] Questions to ask before buying a stock
Should I buy DBS Bank, Nvidia, Apple? Different investors have different account sizes, risk profiles, objectives and portfolio strategies. Before you buy, ask:
- Question 1: Is this a great business? Put it through the fundamental criteria; if it passes the five steps of a great business, it passes question one.
- Question 2: Is the stock undervalued? If the share price is below intrinsic value and the price has retraced to a level of support, it passes question two.
- Question 3: What type of stock is this, and what investment category (growth, predictable, dividend, speculative growth)? What sector (healthcare, financial)? Is this the kind of stock I want in my portfolio? For example, if you only want growth stocks you wouldn't buy DBS Bank because you're not interested in dividends. If you want dividends, you won't buy Nvidia or ServiceNow because these are growth stocks that don't pay dividends. Buy a stock that fits your portfolio objectives. You could have a mixture, e.g. 50% growth, 20% dividend, 30% predictable; know your strategy and pick stocks that fit it.
- The most important question: do I already have a full allocation for this stock in my portfolio?

#### [14:59] Allocation and why not to rebalance
If you plan to have 20 stocks in your portfolio, each stock should be a maximum of 5% allocation. Once you have bought 5% of a stock, do not buy more than a 5% allocation.

Once you buy it and the price goes up, e.g. Nvidia up two, three, four hundred percent, from 5% it may grow organically to 6, 8, 9, 10%. Should you rebalance, i.e. sell the additional 5% and use the cash to buy other stocks so everything becomes 5% again, as many fund managers do? The answer is no. What he learned from the greatest investors like Warren Buffett and Peter Lynch: if you have a great company whose share price doubles and triples and now makes up a big percentage of your portfolio, as long as it is still a great company, still growing, and not crazily overpriced, it doesn't make sense to sell. Let it grow to its fullest potential. "Do not cut your flowers."

Likewise, if you put in 5% and the stock drops by half to 2.5%, should you top it up back to 5%? No, don't. The common mistake is selling stocks that have gone up a lot and using the money to buy the shares that went down a lot to rebalance; that is like cutting your flowers and watering the weeds in your garden.

#### [16:51] The 5% / 95% rule
Many people think successful investors only pick winning stocks, that all their picks outperform the index. Wrong. Even the best investors follow what he calls the 5% 95% rule: over time, 95% of your profits will come from only 5% of the companies you buy.

Example: Warren Buffett has run Berkshire Hathaway for over 58 years. During that time Berkshire grew 170,000% versus the S&P 500 growing 3,900%, so Buffett outperformed the S&P 500 by 43 times. But in his annual letter to shareholders he wrote that over the years he has made many mistakes; consequently their extensive collection of businesses currently consists of a few enterprises that have truly extraordinary economics, many that enjoy very good economic characteristics, and a large group that are marginal. Meaning, of all the businesses he owns, most are so-so, but it's the few that account for all the profits.

A closer example: Buffett's stocks over the last five years: Apple, HP, American Express, CVX, Bank of America, Coca-Cola, Occidental Petroleum, NU, KC and PARA (as transcribed, partly unclear; read as NU, KHC and PARA [filled from earlier pass]). Only Apple did very well, only one. HPQ and AXP did slightly better than the S&P 500. Most did not do well: CVX, Bank of America, Coca-Cola and Occidental made positive gains but underperformed the S&P 500. And he had three stocks with negative returns: minus 30%, minus 35%, minus 69%. So even the world's greatest investor makes boo-boos and has stocks that suck, yet he is still the greatest investor and made billions. Over the last 58 years he has bought and owned over 210 stocks and businesses, but only about a dozen (12 of 200), which is about 5%, contributed to 90% of all his gains.

#### [20:51] The same in Adam's portfolio
He can't show all 40 stocks, so he shows a sample of 10 from his portfolio: Nvidia, ASML, Apple, Microsoft, Lowe's, Meta, Amazon, Estee Lauder, Disney and Alibaba. Over the last five years, not all did well or beat the S&P 500. Roughly, out of 10 stocks you buy, two to three will do very well and outperform exceptionally, four to six will do well, and you always have two to three that suck, at least in the short term.

In his sample the very good ones: Nvidia up 700%, ASML up 281%, Apple 257%, Microsoft 237%; these four way outperform the S&P 500. Lowe's, Meta and Amazon did not badly, pretty good, slightly above the S&P, or in Amazon's case slightly below the S&P. And the ones that stank: Estee Lauder down 15%, Disney down 26% (which he already sold), Alibaba down 41%.

When you initially buy these 10 stocks you never know after 5 years which will outperform and which underperform; you only know after it happens. That's why you have to keep all of them, so the massive gains from the winners more than offset the temporary drops from the losers. Net net, you are still growing your portfolio and beating the S&P 500. This concludes lesson eight; the next lesson covers how to manage your portfolio, the exit rules and how to consistently monitor your portfolio in order to beat the market.

### 66 - Finding Great Stocks

#### [00:00] Lesson nine overview
Lesson nine has several parts. The first part focuses on how to look for investment opportunities and find great companies that can pass the seven-step criteria. The next parts focus on portfolio management: once you have built the portfolio, how to regularly monitor the stocks, looking at earnings reports and company announcements, and then most importantly the exit rules: when do you sell your stocks, under what conditions.

#### [00:28] Where do the best stocks come from? Buy what you patronize
Some people think the best ideas come from an insider tip or a secret report you pay a lot for. No. The best stocks he owns, which have made him the most money, come from businesses he patronizes himself, whose products and services he uses regularly, and he bets it's the same for you. The best place to find great stocks is all around you. Ask: what products or services do you use regularly and pay for all the time? As you do, you notice what we call consumer monopolies.

His biggest positions are in Microsoft, because he uses Microsoft every single day (Excel, Word). He uses Facebook, Instagram, WhatsApp every day, so he owns Meta, which owns all these social media brands. He uses Amazon, orders through Amazon, watches Amazon Prime Video, so he owns Amazon. He can't live without Google (search, Google Maps, Google Docs, Gmail, YouTube), hence he owns Alphabet. Every day when buying stuff he uses his Visa and MasterCard, so he owns Visa and MasterCard stock. He uses Adobe a lot in designing (his daughter is learning design in college), so he owns Adobe. He has an iPhone and Apple Watch, and his entire family has MacBooks and iPads, so he owns Apple. It is really that simple; common sense.

#### [02:25] What Buffett said
He learned this from watching a video of Warren Buffett many years ago; in an interview someone asked Buffett how he found his greatest investments. Buffett made his fortune buying Coca-Cola, American Express, Visa, MasterCard, McDonald's, Bank of America and other great stocks. His answer was very simple: he buys stocks of businesses which he patronizes all the time. Buffett bought Coke because he loved drinking Coke (he admits to drinking four to six cans a day). He bought Gillette because he shaves with Gillette blades. He bought American Express because he uses their credit cards (he used to use their traveler's checks). His reasoning: if I use these products every day, so do millions of people around the world, which proves these companies have a brand monopoly and are market leaders, and these are the companies you want to own.

What convinced Buffett to buy Apple, now his greatest investment, was not an analyst research paper but his granddaughter, who told him she can't live without her iPhone, and Buffett was like, wow. Many people can't live without their Apple products, and you want to own that business, because such businesses have very predictable future revenue and cash flow.

#### [04:17] Caveat: still run the seven-step criteria (Netflix example)
Just because you use a product doesn't mean it's a great company; you still have to put the stock through the seven-step criteria. His family subscribes to Netflix and he watches once in a while, but he doesn't own the stock, because after the seven-step research he found Netflix is a good business, not bad, but not a great company. So you have to do the research and not just say "I use the product, I'll buy it."

One thing he doesn't like about Netflix is that it has to spend hundreds of millions of dollars to generate its own content, so there is a huge amount of maintenance capex: they keep spending so much to maintain content and customers. And making content is hit-and-miss: some movies are big hits, some flop and could lose millions. Because of that he doesn't find Netflix very predictable. He prefers Alphabet, which owns YouTube, where YouTube doesn't generate its own content; users generate it, so there is very low maintenance capex.

#### [05:19] Building a list of stocks to analyze
Ask yourself, from when you wake up in the morning until you sleep at night, what products do you use? What soap and shampoo do you use? What toothpaste? [from earlier pass] Check out those brands and research them if they are listed companies. Which pay (payment) service do you use? Where does your breakfast come from, where do you buy groceries? What car do you drive? Where do you go for lunch and dinner: McDonald's, a restaurant? If it is a listed company, this is how you build a list of stocks to analyze. What tools do you use every day: Microsoft like him, Google, other software to do your work? That's how you build a list.

#### [06:20] Great companies hit by temporary bad news
No matter how great a stock is, there will be times it gets hit by bad news that causes the share price to drop 20, 30, 40, 50%, and that could be a great opportunity to buy a great company at a huge discount. Sometimes the bad news hits short-term profits: profits may drop for one quarter, two or three quarters or even a year. Sometimes profit doesn't even drop; revenue is the same, but the bad news caused negative sentiment or emotions towards the stock. That is the best kind of example, where the business itself is not affected and the share price dropped purely from irrational selling, and he loves these opportunities.

The most important thing is to ensure the bad news is temporary: it may hit profits short term, but ultimately the company's business model and competitive advantage (economic moat) remain intact, so over the medium to longer term it can keep growing revenue and profits and make back whatever short-term losses the bad news caused.

#### [07:43] Example 1: Meta in 2022
In 2022 Meta, which owns Facebook, dropped almost 70% because of two pieces of bad news. First, CEO Mark Zuckerberg decided to start spending money building the Metaverse, not yet profitable in the short term, leading to a lot of increased expenses and profits dropping temporarily. Second, Apple changed its privacy settings so you can tell the app not to track you, preventing Facebook from targeting its advertisements and causing a drop in advertising return on investment. Facebook dropped 70%; many people panicked and sold. He was holding his shares and bought more at a 60, 70% discount, because he knew these problems were temporary.

What gave him confidence: first, he did his research and found Meta still had its competitive advantage and was building an AI engine that would circumvent Apple's privacy changes, so it was a short-term problem. More importantly, he uses Facebook every day and asked himself if he was using less; no, he kept using the same amount of Facebook, Instagram and WhatsApp. Second, he is himself an advertiser on Facebook; his company advertises every year and spends millions of dollars on ads there. He asked whether there was an alternative to advertise on; not really, Facebook gave him the best return on his advertising dollars. Was he planning to spend less next year? No, they planned to spend more. Because he was a customer himself, an advertiser, he understood the business, and that gave him the confidence to keep adding more shares at a huge discount while people who didn't understand the business sold in panic. That is how Facebook today is one of his big positions and gives him one of the highest returns in his portfolio. It is really important that the biggest stocks you own are ones whose business you really understand, and you truly understand the business only if you yourself are a customer of that business; that's why it's important to buy what you know.

#### [10:28] Example 2: Ozempic fear in 2023
In 2023 there was a big hoo-ha about a drug, Ozempic, taken by Kim Kardashian and Elon Musk, an injection that causes you to lose weight and control diabetes, stopping you from feeling hungry often. The moment news came out, many food stocks like Pepsi, Hershey's and McDonald's (and Yum Brands [from earlier pass]) dropped 20%, because people feared that if everyone takes the drug they will eat less chocolate, cookies and potato chips; food stocks dropped 20, 30%. Did their revenue and profits actually drop? No; it was an irrational fear, people freaked out. He used it as an opportunity to buy more McDonald's at a big discount, and bought Hershey's and Pepsi.

Healthcare stocks also dropped because people thought that if people lose weight they won't get heart disease or medical problems as much, so they sold medical stocks: Thermo Fisher, HCA Healthcare, Abbott Laboratories dropped 10, 20, 30%. He said great, I'll buy those. You can take advantage of short-term bad news. Ultimately nothing changed with these companies: still making money, still had their wide moat, still passed the seven-step criteria.

#### [12:16] Example 3: Booking.com in 2020
Sometimes business profits actually plunge significantly, but if you know it's a temporary reason and it will bounce back, that can be a great opportunity. Booking.com is the largest online travel agent in the world; it owns Priceline, Agoda and many travel brands. During the COVID-19 pandemic in 2020, global borders shut and people couldn't travel; revenue dropped like 90%, profits dropped 90%, and the share price plunged significantly. While everyone sold in panic, he bought aggressively at huge discounts, because it was a great company that passed all his criteria and sales and profits dropped temporarily for a reason that was not its fault. He asked himself if the pandemic would last forever; no, eventually it would be over and the stock would rebound significantly.

He could have bought many other travel businesses like hotels, airlines and cruise ships, but didn't because they didn't meet his criteria, whereas Booking, an online business with a very strong balance sheet, did. So do not buy blindly just because you read bad news on a stock; it may not be a good business in the first place. It has to pass the investment criteria. The decision of when to buy is based on what you learned previously: is it undervalued, has the price retraced to a strong level of support, is there a reversal pattern. The decision to buy is based on fundamentals and technicals. We don't buy based on the news; the news points us to the opportunity, but we have to do the research, and the fundamental and technical entry criteria must be met before we pull the trigger.

Most bad news is temporary. However, if a company is accused of financial fraud or any financial irregularity, don't touch it, because usually where there's smoke there's a big fire behind it. Other than that, any short-term bad news is, he would say, an opportunity.

#### [14:59] Using stock screeners: Finviz (free) and GuruFocus (paid)
You can sometimes find great companies using stock screeners. He finds the best free one is on finviz.com, and the best paid screener is gurufocus.com. He starts with the free Finviz screener: click screener. There are all together 9,500 stocks and ETFs in the database (not all US-listed; some non-US). You can filter these down to the top 50 or top 20 based on criteria you put into the scanner. There are three types of criteria: descriptive, fundamentals and technicals.

Fundamentals first. You can set the PE ratio, e.g. less than 15, or over 0, over 50, under 5, over 20, whatever. Which PE should we set, high or low? Any PE, because PE ratio by itself is completely meaningless: a stock with a high PE could be cheap and a good company, and a stock with a low PE could be expensive and a lousy company. PE and forward PE are not important. What's important is earnings growth for the next five years (the projected growth of the company); we want to invest in companies that are growing. If looking specifically for high growth companies select over 20% growth; the software then filters from 9,000 stocks down to 492 stocks with earnings growth of more than 20% in the next five years. If you are looking for stocks not necessarily all high growth, as long as growing, set a lower rate, e.g. over 10%, and you get more candidates. The stricter, the fewer candidates; the more lenient, the more candidates. It depends whether you want growth stocks or more predictable stocks.

Next, return on equity: ideally 12% or higher. Finviz doesn't have 12%; it has over 10 and over 15, so select over 10. From 9,000 stocks they are now down to 550 stocks with two criteria; the more criteria added, the shorter the list.

Sales growth over the last five years: we want revenue growing over the last five years, a consistent good track record. Up to you: over 20% for very high growth, over 10%; he puts over 5%, as long as it has historically been growing.

PEG ratio: a very rough way of valuing the company. Theoretically a PEG of more than one is overvalued, but in reality many great companies have a PEG of more than one, so he usually puts under two to be a bit more lenient.

Current ratio: more than one. Earnings growth next year: you want a company growing next year; over 5% or over 10% depending on the kind of stock; he puts over 5% for now. Earnings growth for the last 5 years: over 5%. Return on investment: over 10% (similar to ROIC).

#### [20:36] Results and refining
With all these fundamental criteria they are left with 69 stocks, which you then put through the seven-step analysis: does it qualify as a great company? If 69 is still too many, increase strictness, e.g. earnings growth for the last five years over 10%, which shortens the list to 55 stocks. Familiar names on the list: Applied Materials, Arista Networks, Copart (a great company). On the second page: Alphabet, MasterCard, MercadoLibre, Netflix, Winnebago (also a great mid-cap company), ZTO Express. So some great ones do come out.

You can also add technical criteria, e.g. only stocks above their 200-day moving average; usually if a stock is above the 200-day moving average the overall trend is going up. Setting price above the 200 day moving average reduces the list to 37 stocks. Under descriptive criteria you can sort by market cap: e.g. only large companies, over 10 billion market cap, or small companies and above. If you select only large companies, 37 stocks fall to only 15. If you select all kinds, big and small, you get the bigger list of 37.

If looking for dividends, put in a dividend yield criterion, e.g. paying dividends of at least 2%; that gives only two stocks (US stocks are not known for their dividends), so he sets it to any for the longer list. You can view the list as charts to quickly look at what the charts look like; again familiar names: Applied Materials, Booking.com, Copart, and on the second page Google, JBL and others.

### 67 - Stock Screeners

#### [00:00] Limits of Finviz and why GuruFocus
Finviz screener is not bad but has limitations: it is more US-centric (you can't really sort by India, China or European stocks), and you can't set the exact criteria you want; for example for ROE you can't say exactly 12%. A more sophisticated screener is on gurufocus.com. It is a paid service, so you must subscribe, but he thinks it's worth it; it's a pretty good screener, which he uses as well.

#### [00:28] GuruFocus screener: presets and the all-in-one screener
On gurufocus.com, click screener. There are many preset screeners, for example Ben Graham's lost formula screener (screens using Ben Graham's deep value criteria), and screens for fast growers, good companies, high quality companies, all kinds. But you can create your own customized screener based on what you've learned in this course, the seven steps. Go to the all-in-one screener. He has already created his own set but shows how to customize one.

First you select the regions to find stocks from: if you only want US stocks, click US only and uncheck Asia, Europe and the rest; or click all three for US, Asia, Europe; you can add Canada, the UK, Oceania, whatever. Then you see the number of criteria they have, which is really, really comprehensive. You don't have to use every criterion (you could, but it's not necessary). Ultimately he still has to do his seven-step analysis; the screener is just a short list of stocks he knows meet some of the basic criteria.

#### [02:08] The 12 criteria he uses (saved as "Adam VMI")
He keeps it really simple, with just 12 criteria. Under Fundamental:
- Current ratio at least one (one and above).
- Interest coverage ratio three and above.
- Debt to EBITDA ratio three or less. (These are things learned in the seven-step analysis, applied to the screener.)
- Return on equity (ROE) 12% or more.
- Return on invested capital (ROIC) 12% or more.
- Five-year average ROIC 12% or more.
- Share buyback ratio 1% or more, meaning he wants to focus on companies that buy back shares; not a must, but great to have.
- He also uses some of GuruFocus's own internal ranking system, a financial ranking system of 1 to 10 using their proprietary metrics: financial strength ranking of 7 and above, profitability ranking of seven and above, and a predictability ranking of four to five stars. GuruFocus ranks whether a company is very predictable based on past revenue, profits and cash flow.

Under valuation ratio: price to GuruFocus value ratio of 0 to 1. GuruFocus has its own way of valuing stock, which he doesn't always agree with; he finds they are sometimes a bit too optimistic, a bit too aggressive, so his intrinsic value is always below theirs. For the purpose of the screener he puts price to GuruFocus valuation from zero to one, meaning he only wants stocks that are not overvalued based on their valuation; ultimately he has to do his own valuation to confirm.

#### [04:29] Results and routine
Using just these 12 criteria (11 fundamental, one valuation ratio) it filters 21 stocks. As of now the list includes Alphabet, Visa, Johnson & Johnson, ASML, Cisco, Nike, Infosys, Edwards Lifesciences, Agilent, Wipro and others (the rest of the list was shown on screen). Many of these he already owns. He screens at least once a month, because new stocks may come out and some stocks may be removed from the screener; that's how he also looks for new investment opportunities.

#### [05:18] Watching super investors: Dataroma
Could you find great ideas by watching what other super investors buy and sell? Sometimes you could get good ideas, but not all the time. Dataroma.com is a free website that tracks the 13F filings of well-known investors managing billions of dollars, some of the biggest hedge fund managers and super investors in the US, such as Bill Ackman, Warren Buffett, Li Lu, Chase Coleman and so on. It shows their top 10 holdings and their most recent purchases. For example Bill Ackman, a very successful fund manager and one of the few he respects: in the latest filings, quarter three of 2023, he recently added to Google, Hilton and Howard Hughes. (The free site pops up ads.) For Warren Buffett, Berkshire Hathaway: largest holding is Apple, which makes up 50% of his portfolio. Recent buys in quarter three: LLYVK (Liberty Media Corp), Sirius Holdings and Atlanta Braves Holdings; the previous quarter, Capital One Financial, Occidental Petroleum and so on.

Very important: just because you respect someone, a great investor, doesn't mean you blindly follow their stock picks. He respects Buffett tremendously, but there are many stocks Buffett buys that he would never buy. For example in the last few years Buffett bought a lot of Occidental Petroleum (OXY), which he will never buy because it doesn't meet his investment criteria and he doesn't like to buy energy stocks; since he bought it, it has gone down significantly. Buffett also famously bought a lot of airline stocks in 2019, and in 2020 during COVID the stocks crashed and he cut his loss; airline stocks are something he would never buy. So many of these people are great investors he respects, but he won't buy what they buy. Don't follow blindly; you can look at what they're buying, but ultimately you must do your own research, put it through the seven-step analysis and ask yourself whether it meets your criteria. Many times it may not. Very, very important.

More useful than looking at what they do individually is to see which stocks they are all buying, the most popular stocks among super investors. That gives him ideas to then do his seven-step analysis. He never buys blindly just because a lot of them bought it, because they could all be wrong.

#### [09:16] Dataroma home page: most-owned and top buys
On the home page, on the right, the top 10 most-owned stocks by the super investors are currently: Google, Microsoft, Meta Platforms, Amazon, Visa, Berkshire Hathaway, Wells Fargo, UnitedHealth and MasterCard (as transcribed, nine names listed for the top 10). Of those 10 he owns eight, and he didn't buy them because the super investors bought them; he bought them from his own analysis previously. There is a very similar list by percentage ownership. The top 10 buys in the last quarter: Alphabet, Meta, Amazon, Disney, Microsoft, UnitedHealth, CVS Health, Thermo Fisher, Dollar General and Charter Communications. Disney was one of the most bought stocks by super investors in the last quarter, and he sold his Disney shares because he no longer thinks it meets his investment criteria. Many times when they buy, he would sell; when they don't buy, he buys; and when they buy, he may buy. Use it as a reference, but don't just follow it.

#### [10:30] Ultimate Investors Playbook (UIP)
Finally, you can subscribe to UIP, the Ultimate Investors Playbook, which he manages. He finds the best stocks in the world for you, does all the research, shows exactly when he is buying and selling, and updates the intrinsic value and buy levels every single month. Subscribe at insight.piranaprofits.com (as spoken; the URL is transcribed inconsistently). As part of UIP, every month he presents two stocks: one picked by himself personally, and another voted on by the community. For each he does in-depth research using the seven-step analysis criteria.

Example: in May of 2022 (as he says "last year") he presented a stock called Intuit. The deep-dive research covers everything: the investment thesis (why invest, in terms of its market potential and how it beats the S&P 500 and the overall market); the business overview (what it does, products, services); the seven steps: revenue and how it has grown over the years, net profit, cash from operations and how consistently they grow, gross profit margins and profit margins analysis, return on equity and return on invested capital, how revenue is rising faster than accounts receivable; the economic moat (wide or narrow, and sources of the moat; in this case market leadership, network effect, high switching costs); a direct competitor study comparing each stock with its competition (for Intuit: Sage Group, Xero Limited, H&R Block, and relative performance over the 10-year and 5-year timeframes); growth drivers; debt structure; how he values the stock using the intrinsic value calculator to come up with the intrinsic value, with the valuation updated again every month; and investment risk and so on.

This stock was presented in May (stated as "2020" in the transcript) when the price dropped to the strong support level, so within the buy zone, and right now it's up to $594. It consolidated and is now back up in a new uptrend. Every month he also shows how to update the intrinsic value and buy levels of each stock. For example, one stock in his UIP portfolio and watch list is Fortinet, FTNT (transcribed "FDN"): the latest intrinsic value is within the range of $73 (conservative) to $90 (base case); the current share price is $55, so it is still quite undervalued. His four buy levels, updated every month based on strong support levels, are $61, 56, 52 and 47.

There is also a special notification channel on Discord where he shares his transactions every single day: the moment he buys or sells something he takes a screenshot and shares it with subscribers. For example, on the 24th October he bought Keppel DC REIT at $1.80 (transcribed "$5,600 shares"; the quantity is unclear, read as 5,600 shares [filled from earlier pass]); following that on the 25th he added Thermo Fisher on the US market at 427, and he shows the allocation increase in his portfolio as well as a screenshot of all transactions. This helps students see how he actually invests and trades every day; by watching, you become his apprentice, the fastest way to learn live. If you don't already have a subscription, enroll at the UIP site (spoken as "inside.piranaprofits.com").
