# Part 14 — Lessons 68–71

### 68 - Monitor Your Portfolio

#### [00:01] Building a watch list
As you look at investment opportunities, either through the stock screener or through the products and services you use every day, and you do your seven-step analysis, once you find a great business that passes the first five steps, you put that stock into your watch list. (He says he will show later how to create a watch list on charting software or a brokerage platform.) The idea is to build a watch list of roughly about 20 to 30 great companies that you watch every single day. Take five minutes a day to look at those 20 to 30 stocks, waiting for the right time to buy. The right time is when the price drops below the intrinsic value (it is undervalued) and at the same time the price drops to a level of support that you have identified. The whole point is to be patient and wait for the right point to pull the trigger.

#### [00:58] Creating a watch list on TradingView
You can create a watch list on your charting platform, e.g. TradingView, or on Think or Swim (which is also a broker), or at Interactive Brokers. On TradingView.com (the charting software he uses), open the panel on the right using the icon, click "create new list" and name it whatever you want, e.g. US stocks. If you invest in various markets you can create multiple watch lists: US stocks, Asian stocks, European stocks. You can also create multiple watch lists by investment category: one for dividend stocks, another for growth stocks, another for more speculative growth stocks. Once named, save it. Then put in the stocks you want to watch. These are all great companies you have already analyzed; they must pass all the criteria except for price. Click the plus sign to add stocks, e.g. Abbott Laboratories ("a pretty good company"), BlackRock, Essential, Pepsi, Procter and Gamble, Fortinet, Microsoft: a mix of technology, consumer staple and financial stocks. Put in your best 20 to 30 companies. It is like a hit list: you watch for the price to be right, then hit it and buy it into your portfolio. You do not buy all at once; as he has said, you add shares in tranches and average in your position.

#### [03:35] Watch list on Think or Swim
Another place is your brokerage platform, e.g. Think or Swim by TD Ameritrade, which is also charting software. He has his watch list on the left. Every day when the market is open he takes just five to ten minutes to run through the watch list, waiting for the right price. For Abbott Laboratories, the intrinsic value is shown as $11 [as stated aloud; likely truncated, the buy levels below are around 100] [earlier pass has $111]. He marks intrinsic value with a green line he draws, and for every stock he puts in his buy levels: significant support levels below the intrinsic value. He usually has three to four buy levels per stock. For Abbott his current buy levels are 104, 101, 96 and 88. Recently the price dropped through level one, level two, level three and almost to level four; he could have been adding shares at each, and it has now reversed back up. Currently Abbott is still undervalued, but eventually it will go up and become overvalued, and then you can't buy it any more until the intrinsic value goes up when you revaluate, or the price drops again below intrinsic value.

Another example: Essential [earlier pass reads this name as Accenture]. Last year the price dropped below intrinsic value all the way to the last support level. Its intrinsic value is 314 and it dropped all the way down to 242. It was a great time to add shares of this great business. His four buy levels are 301, 282, 262 and 242, and it dropped to the last buy level. As an investor you would add one quarter, one quarter, one quarter until you have a full position, usually by the last support level, and then eventually it reverses back up. A great business will always rebound; it's a matter of time until market sentiment shifts.

#### [06:23] Watch list on Interactive Brokers
In Interactive Brokers: go to new window, then watch list. You can place it anywhere on your workstation (unlock it, drag it left or right, then lock it); he put his on the right and his chart on the left. On Interactive Brokers you can also put in TradingView charts for free. The version provided there has some limited functions, but you can put in all the moving averages for free, whereas using TradingView outside Interactive Brokers you have to pay a subscription for many of these moving averages. To get the chart: new window, scroll down to chart, then advanced chart; the TradingView chart pops out and you can put it onto your workstation. On the right in the watch list put all the stocks you're watching that have passed the criteria of being a great company, e.g. Apple (AAPL), Essential, Abbott Laboratories, Microsoft, and so on.

#### [08:49] Monitoring the portfolio
Once you have bought a stock it goes from your watch list into your portfolio. Eventually you want a portfolio of great companies, anywhere from 10 to 20 to 30 to 40 stocks. Currently he has 40 stocks in his portfolio. Must you monitor the portfolio regularly? Yes. He spends less than 10 minutes a day: a quick look every day when the market is open. He is checking whether prices have retraced or dropped to a buy zone; if he doesn't yet have a full position, he adds more shares. The buy zone must be below the intrinsic value and near or at a support level you have identified.

#### [09:41] Do not worry about daily fluctuations; drawdowns and Mr. Market
Do not be concerned about daily fluctuations in the share price. It goes up one day, down another; that's volatility, and volatility is normal. Many students are paranoid: they log in, the price dropped the next day, "why did it drop?"; the price goes up and they get excited. No: be chill, be zen. The majority of the time, any price drop very often has nothing to do with the value of the underlying business. Don't freak out if your great company drops 10, 20, 30%; it's normal. Expect ups and downs. Temporary drawdowns are normal. A drawdown is a temporary drop in the market price of the stocks in your portfolio. You have to be psychologically prepared; if not, when it happens you'll freak out and do dumb things, like selling in fear and finding when it rebounds later that you're worse off.

The market is like a person, Mr. Market, like a drunk psychotic person with bipolar disorder: very happy one day, very sad the next, which is why the market goes through big ups and downs. Market price every day is driven not by logic but by emotions, sentiment and manipulation. When Mr. Market is in a bad mood and everything is red and the price keeps dropping, there are only two things you should do as an investor: take advantage of him by buying more shares of great companies at a depressed price, or ignore him. What you should never do is be influenced by him: when he gets scared you get scared, when he gets excited you get excited. That is the way to financially ruin your investments. On days of panic when prices drop, drop, drop: if you already have a full position, just ignore the market, go fishing, don't even look at it. If you have not built a full position, use it as a chance to add more shares.

#### [12:17] Prices move in waves
New investors get overly concerned with day-to-day fluctuations and keep asking "Adam, why did the price drop?" Prices don't go up in a straight line. A great company's price will always go up, but it moves in wave patterns: wave up, wave down, wave up. His answer to "why did it drop" is simple: after a wave up you can have a wave down; after breathing out you've got to breathe in. It's part of the pattern.

#### [13:00] The 10 reasons a stock price drops
Sometimes there is news after a price drop where analysts try to explain it. He compiled all the possible reasons: 10 in total. The first nine are not a cause for concern.

1. Not company specific: something happened to the macro environment or geopolitics, e.g. a war announced (Middle East war, Ukraine war), panic selling, people think a recession is coming, or the Fed raises interest rates. This can cause the share price to drop temporarily, not just of that company but all companies.
2. A bear market: happens on average once every six years, as mentioned. The overall market turns negative, everyone gets fearful and pessimistic; good companies and bad companies all drop for emotional reasons, nothing to do with the company's business.
3. Corrections: happen on average once a year, and during a correction the market drops about 10 to 15%. Good companies drop as well.
4. Sector rotation: money tends to rotate between sectors (healthcare, technology, financial, etc.). For example, in 2023 there was a rotation of money out of healthcare: many institutional investors sold healthcare stocks and put the money into technology stocks. All healthcare companies, good or bad, drop because money is going out of the sector; when money goes into technology, most technology stocks go up, good or bad. Don't be surprised that a very good healthcare company keeps dropping [he says "In 2020" in the transcript, in context of the 2023 rotation]; there's nothing wrong with the company. Take advantage and buy more shares at a discount, because eventually the rotation will reverse. His suspicion is that next year, 2024, there is a rotation back into healthcare where healthcare stocks go up, and then something else goes down; they all take turns.
5. Industry-specific news: news affecting one industry drops all companies in it together. E.g. in 2023 the Ozempic drug that helps people lose weight got very popular, and the entire food industry of stocks dropped: McDonald's, Pepsi, Yum Brands, Hershey's, because of fear that people will eat less. A great opportunity to buy those food companies when the whole industry is sold down.
6. Manipulation by market makers and algo traders, which can drop stocks for reasons not related to the company.

All these reasons are temporary and great opportunities to add more shares; if you have enough shares, ignore it, do something else and don't be emotionally affected.

#### [17:12] Company-specific reasons
7. Missing earnings estimates or lowering forward guidance. Companies announce earnings four times a year. Before then analysts have a projection (e.g. "we think McDonald's will earn $5 per share"). When a company beats the estimate the price often jumps after earnings; if it misses, the price gaps down. Can you predict whether it will beat or miss? No; it's like tossing a coin, 50-50. Don't be concerned when your company misses in a quarter and don't be too happy when it beats, because a great company will have bad quarters and good quarters; it can't have a good quarter every quarter. If it misses and the price drops on panic selling, it's an opportunity to add more shares because it is still a great company. A company may also lower forward guidance (future projections). Example: just yesterday Adobe announced earnings that were better than expected, but said next year it expects revenue lower than projection, and the stock dropped 6%. Even if a company beats its current report but projects lower revenue or earnings next year, the price could drop 5 to 10%. Anything to be concerned about? No. An opportunity to buy? Yes.
8. A cyclical slowdown in demand for their products and services. Even for a great business, take a semiconductor company like ASML, Texas Instruments, Nvidia or AMD: in the long run demand for semiconductors will keep rising so revenue will rise, but not in a straight line; in the short term you can have a strong run-up, a temporary drop, and a run-up again. Same with advertising: online advertising keeps growing in the long run, but at times advertising expenditure drops during an economic slowdown. In 2022 there was a slowdown in ad spending because of fear of a recession, so Google (Alphabet) and Meta's revenue dipped a bit last year, but this year has re-accelerated. Same with semiconductors: a bit of a cyclical dip last year, then re-acceleration. Short term it may affect revenue and profits, but long run it's not a problem because it is a short-term cyclical issue.
9. Other temporary bad news: a product recall (e.g. Tesla recalling many cars because of a manufacturing fault; could hit short-term profits, but a great business bounces back), a lawsuit causing short-term dropping profits, a sex scandal, whatever. All temporary. The one thing he worries about is a company accused of financial fraud; in those cases he wouldn't hold it and would sell the stock immediately. All other reasons are a short-term phenomenon. A stock price can also drop because a major shareholder was forced to sell for whatever reason, e.g. a major holder of McDonald's in trouble forced to sell because of personal problems; that drops the stock temporarily. Great time to buy, because it isn't the company, it's that "idiot" who sold because of his situation. Again, sometimes manipulation by market makers and algos drops the stock too.

#### [22:40] Reason 10: structural problems
99% of the time the reason is one of the first nine, and none is cause for concern: they are short-term and opportunities to buy more shares. The only time he gets concerned is the 10th reason: the company has a structural or long-term problem causing the drop. This is very, very rare; if you buy a company that meets all his investment criteria as a wide-moat company, it is very rare, but it can happen, so watch out. Examples of companies with long-term structural problems that permanently damaged their business model: in the future they will never grow revenue again, or profits will never re-accelerate, or they suffered a permanent deterioration of competitive advantage; their economic moat has been disrupted so they can no longer fend off competition. In those situations you look to get out. Examples:
- Nokia, which used to have a 90% market share, was disrupted by the touchscreen phone from iPhone, Apple and BlackBerry; it is nowhere near where it was and will never get back there.
- Blockbuster Video, disrupted by video streaming from Netflix; never recovered.
- Kodak, disrupted by digital photography; never recovered.
- Yahoo, once the number one search engine, disrupted by Google; never recovered.
- AMC, which owns movie theaters, disrupted again by video streaming; will never go back to where they once were.

These were great companies that were no longer great because of a permanent problem: economic moat deteriorates, permanent disruption to the business model, and permanent change in consumer behavior (people don't go to physical movie theaters much any more, they watch streaming). A change in consumer behavior can totally wreck a company. If you're not sure whether it's a short-term or permanent problem, bring up the question in the Discord private community chat, directed at him or at everyone, for feedback.

#### [25:21] Quarterly reports and earnings dates
Pay attention to the quarterly financial report. Companies announce earnings four times a year, usually during earnings season, which is April, July, October and January. To find out when your company announces: he finds it most convenient on his TradingView charts (also on the Think or Swim chart). Below the chart are letters; E is earnings. Historically, for Apple, earnings were reported in November, August and May. The next one is shown in purple; clicking it shows it will come out on the 25th of January 2024, with estimates: analysts project Apple will earn $2.8 per share for the quarter [earlier pass has $2.08]. If Apple beats that the price would usually jump; if it misses, the price would drop. D is dividends: the ex-dividend date, meaning if you buy the stock before that date you get the dividend from the shares you own at payout. You can also go to finance.yahoo.com/calendar/earnings to see the earnings calendar for the next days, weeks or months, and search by company.

#### [27:32] Reading reports and conference calls
When a company announces earnings it's useful to read the report. Download the quarterly report from the company's website under investor relations (e.g. apple.com or adobe.com), including the latest quarterly and annual reports. After results there is a conference call where the CEO and management team take questions from analysts; it's useful to listen to the audio or read the transcript. Google e.g. "Adobe conference call" and it comes up in search results, with the transcript as well. You can also go to Seekingalpha.com and search for conference calls and earnings results for all the major stocks. It is especially useful in the transcripts to find out how management is executing on their plans; if profits or revenue drop temporarily, they explain what happened on these calls. Don't be overly concerned about short-term beats or misses. You are only concerned about long-term structural damage to the company's competitive advantage or business model, and that doesn't happen overnight; it takes years for a company to lose its competitive advantage, so you can see it coming.

### 69 - Dealing with Drawdowns

#### [00:00] Psychology and drawdowns
This is very important: how to psychologically prepare for drawdowns. One reason many investors fail is not lack of intellect or knowledge. He knows many very smart people with financial degrees and CFAs who make terrible investors because they make bad decisions, unable to control their psychology and emotions. He'll go so far as to say that 70% of investing success comes down to managing your psychology, and one of the most important parts is being psychologically prepared for drawdowns. A drawdown is a temporary drop in the price of the stocks you own and a temporary drop in the market value of your portfolio. Drawdowns are inevitable. No matter how great an investor you are, your portfolio cannot go up in a straight line; it goes through ups and downs. Like a pilot: no matter how great, you can't fly a plane without turbulence. You prepare for it, and when it happens you don't panic; you ride the wave, enjoy the journey, and use it as an opportunity to further build positions in great companies during these temporary drawdowns.

#### [01:12] His own portfolio over five years
Looking at his portfolio over the last five years: a very challenging period with two bear markets in five years (very rare, but it happened), a recession in 2020, the Ukraine war, super high interest rates, super high inflation. Despite that, his portfolio (in blue) grew 141% over five years, beating the S&P 500 (yellow) by quite a wide margin. But it did not go up every day, week or month; it went through drawdowns. In 2020 his portfolio had a drawdown of 25%. In 2022 it went through a 30% drawdown. It doesn't matter, because as long as you hold great companies you keep compounding your wealth over time. You can't avoid drawdowns because you never know when they will happen; no way to predict when a bear market or correction will happen. Analogy: the stock market is like a roller coaster, and the only people who get hurt are those who jump off halfway. As long as you stay on, seat belt fastened, through the ups and downs, you will reach the destination, which is a lot higher than where you started.

#### [03:00] Warren Buffett and Peter Lynch
Warren Buffett has been building his business for the last 58 years investing in the stock markets. Over the last 10 years Berkshire Hathaway returned 312%, beating the S&P 500, with a compounded annual return on average of about 12%, as shown on the chart. Did it go up in a straight line? No; even the greatest investor in the world goes through drawdowns: 18%, 16%, 30%, 27%, 9% and 11% drawdowns.

Peter Lynch is known as the greatest fund manager that ever lived. He managed the Magellan Fund from 1977 to 1990, when he retired to spend more time with his family. During those 13 years he grew the fund 604% versus the S&P 500 at 223%, beating it by over three times, with a compounded annual return on average of 29%. Here's the kicker: more than 50% of clients who put money into his mutual fund lost money. The best mutual fund in the world with a 29% return a year, and more than 50% of his clients lost money. The reason: the fund did not go up in a straight line. Over 13 years it went through big drawdowns: 56%, 27%, 42% and 32%. It doesn't matter because at the end of the day he beat the S&P 500 by over three times. The problem is that most investors buy into a fund after it does well. After the run-up people get excited ("it's a great fund") and buy. Then what goes up must come down; after wave up, wave down, like high and low tide, like the seasons. After people put their money in, it drops 56%, and they panic ("it's a lousy fund, I put it in the wrong fund") and sell. Then the fund goes up, people get excited, buy again, it drops 27%, they panic and sell; up again, buy, panic, sell. Buy there, sell there, and you end up losing money.

#### [06:18] The point: buy low, sell high
You can put your money into the best mutual fund in the world run by the best manager, or into the best stocks in the world, but you will still lose money if you keep buying high and selling low: buying when you get excited, when everyone gets excited, and selling when you panic, when everyone panics. That's why 70% of investing success is psychology. You've got to buy great companies when they have collapsed, when people are panicking and no one wants them. When everyone wants it and it keeps going up, that's the time you don't buy more. You could always sell a bit to take profits, or simply hold through the ups and downs because eventually it will go higher.

#### [07:05] Company announcements: share buybacks
As you hold great companies, several announcements will come along the way. A company may announce it is buying back shares. As mentioned before, buying back shares is usually bullish news because there are less and less shares outstanding, which increases the value per share. But it's very important to note that a company should only use its money to buy back its own shares if the shares are undervalued; then it's a good move. If a company buys back shares when its share price is at an all-time high, when it's expensive, it's not acting in the best interests of shareholders, which is a bad thing. Also a company should only buy back shares if it is itself doing well, with revenue and profits growing; that benefits shareholders. If the company is not doing well (revenues falling, profits falling, losing competitive advantage), it should not be buying back shares; it should use its cash to improve its business, improve its products and services, and hire better people. Example: many years ago IBM was not doing well and its business was deteriorating; instead of using money to improve the business, the CEO kept buying back shares to artificially inflate earnings per share. In the long term that is detrimental to the business. So buying back shares is good, but only if the shares are undervalued and the company is doing well.

#### [08:55] Selling new shares and rights issues
The opposite is a company selling new shares, usually when it needs to raise money to pay off debt, make acquisitions, or fund working capital. Good or bad news? Most of the time the companies he buys have a lot of cash and "never, never" have to sell new shares because they generate enough cash from operations. If a company you own sells new shares, find out what it is raising the money for. To pay off debt: not a very good thing. Short of money and needing working capital: also not very good. To make acquisitions: check the company being acquired, is it a good company, are they overpaying? Many questions to ask. Generally, we don't like companies that have to sell new shares, because it raises capital but dilutes existing shareholders. When the company sells new shares, you as an existing shareholder have the right to buy the shares first at a discount; that's why it's called a rights issue. If you exercise your rights you buy the new shares at a discount, putting more money into the company. If you don't want to buy more shares, you can sell your rights for some money or let the rights expire. Generally this is bearish-to-neutral news.

#### [10:35] Stock splits and reverse splits
A company may announce a stock split. It does not change the value of the business; it's exactly the same business, neither better nor worse; it simply makes the shares look more affordable. Example: about a year ago Amazon was selling at $3,000 per share. Say you owned one share: its value is one share times 3,000 = $3,000. Amazon did a share split, splitting one share into 20 smaller shares, like taking your pizza and cutting it into 20 smaller pieces. The next day you have 20 shares instead of one, but each is a smaller piece: each share is $3,000 divided by 20 = $150 per share. The value of your shares is still $3,000; a split doesn't make you better or worse off. The intrinsic value is split accordingly: he calculated Amazon's intrinsic value before the split at about $3,500; the number of shares increased 20-fold, so the new intrinsic value is $175. The only reason to split is to make shares look more affordable so more retail investors buy: at $3,000 not many can afford one share, but at $150 more people may buy, creating more demand, which could temporarily increase the share price. So it is bullish news.

The opposite is a reverse stock split: the company finds its share price too small, so it combines 10 shares into one to make the share price go up and look more decent. That happens too, and normally is bearish news.

#### [13:38] Cash dividends and where to read announcements
A company could announce cash dividends, with an ex-dividend date: if you buy the shares any time before that date you get the dividends; if you buy after that date you don't. There is also a payout day, the exact day the dividends are credited into your account. Places to read company announcements: FinViz (type the ticker, e.g. AAPL, and scroll down to see recent news about the stock and the most recent company announcements). You can also go to Yahoo Finance (search "yahoo finance apple", go to Yahoo Finance and scroll down to press releases; companies issue a press release when they make an announcement). Companies also make SEC (Securities and Exchange Commission) filings; if there's a share split, a dividend issue, or company results, they have to file. You can sort by financial reports, corporate changes, tender offer acquisition reports, other proxy statements.

### 70 - When to Sell Part 1

#### [00:00] Selling rules: the six valid reasons
So far the course covered what to buy, when to buy and how many shares to buy; now the last part: knowing when to sell. In investing, as opposed to short-term trading, you want to hold shares of a great business as long as possible, for years and years, allowing it to compound and grow 5x, 10x, 50x, or even in cases 100x. This compounds your wealth at a very powerful rate. However, there are times when we should sell. There are only six valid reasons to sell a great business.

#### [00:28] Reason 1: the business is no longer great
You sell if the business is no longer great because the fundamentals have changed permanently: changing consumer preferences, disruption by technology, or the economic moat has deteriorated permanently. This is quite rare. Usually if you buy a great company with a wide moat, the company should sustain its moat for at least 20 years; a company with a narrow moat can usually sustain its competitive advantage for at least 10 years. So it's very rare, but it does happen. Has it happened to a great business he invested in? Not really, but it happened to a REIT he bought: Manulife US Office REIT, in about 2018, which invests in offices in the US. At the time it was a great business. During the 2020 pandemic people started working from home, and after the pandemic this consumer behavior did not really change much; people continued to work from home or in a hybrid work model. Occupancy in the offices dropped from 95% to 30% and looked like it was not coming back. Revenue and profits started dropping and could not get back to pre-pandemic level. To make things worse, the Fed raised interest rates very, very fast in 2022. When rates rise too fast and the REIT can't earn enough rental income, the property is devalued, which increases the debt-to-asset ratio (gearing ratio). The REIT's gearing ratio exceeded 50%, which means it was in breach of its financial covenants; the banks could withdraw the loans and the REIT could go bankrupt. The business model changed permanently: no strong balance sheet, and its ability to generate future income and rentals was hampered by COVID changing business practices to working from home or a mixed working culture. He sold because it was no longer great, and took a loss on that investment. The good thing was it was only a small percentage of his dividend portfolio, so it didn't really affect the portfolio that year and he still had a very positive year.

#### [03:41] Reason 2: you made a mistake in your initial analysis
You thought it was a great company and undervalued and bought, but afterwards realize you made a mistake: it's not as great as you thought, or not as cheap, because you were too optimistic in your evaluation. This has happened to him; the example is Disney. He first bought Disney as a turnaround situation. In the past Disney did very well, with revenues and profits increasing consistently. Then Disney decided to transition from the cable TV networks business, a sunset industry (people watch less cable TV and more streaming because of Netflix), to Disney Plus. During the transition its profits dropped by more than 60%, because streaming was not yet profitable and the cable business was in decline. He thought Disney was very cheap and that after the transition profits could go back to very high levels, so he invested. After holding about two years, and doing more and more research, he realized the streaming business was getting more and more competitive (Amazon Prime, Apple TV, Netflix), that the content Disney created was not as great as before, and, worse, that they spent millions and millions of dollars creating content that would be a hit or miss: good movies make a lot of money but bad movies could lose millions. Disney was not as predictable a business as he wanted to own. He sold all his shares, "took the loss like a man", took the proceeds and immediately reinvested in other businesses he thought were great and more undervalued, to make back the losses in Disney.

#### [06:17] Reason 3: management does not act in shareholders' best interest
Many things fall in this category, e.g. management buying back shares when the share price is at an all-time high and overpriced; management buying back shares when the company is not doing well; or the company issuing new shares to raise capital when it already has a lot of cash on the balance sheet which it is not deploying. These are red flags; you want to sell. Has this happened to him? No, never, "at least not yet. So who knows in the future?"

#### [06:57] Reason 4: grossly overvalued
It's still a great company, an amazing business, but the share price has gone up to a grossly overvalued level. Even if the price gets overvalued by 10%, 20%, 30%, he'll still hold it, because with a great company eventually the valuation will increase and catch up to the share price. But if the share price gets ridiculously overpriced, more than 50 to 100% overvalued, then he will start to reduce his position: maybe sell a third, then half, then the rest, to reinvest in another business just as great but at a more reasonable valuation. Example: if McDonald's intrinsic value is, say, $250 per share and McDonald's goes crazy to $500, which is 100% overpriced, he'll start to sell some shares or all the shares even though McDonald's is still a great company.

#### [08:22] Reason 5: you find a better stock
You are invested in a great company doing well, but you find another stock just as great or even better, maybe a better deal as it's more undervalued. There is nothing wrong with selling to buy the better one, if you don't have extra cash to buy the second. If you have extra cash, just hold the first and buy the second too. Only if you need to raise the cash to buy the second one you really love is it fine to swap, or maybe sell half of the first and use half the proceeds to buy the second.

#### [09:09] Reason 6: you need the cash for personal expenses
Some people get worried: why is Jeff Bezos selling shares of Amazon? Isn't Amazon a great company, won't it grow a lot more? Or why is Mark Zuckerberg selling shares of Meta? Many times when founders or the CEO sell shares it doesn't mean they are bearish or think the company is not great; many times they sell to raise cash for other projects (Jeff Bezos is building his Blue Origin rocket [transcript says "Blue Horizon"], Mark Zuckerberg is selling to give to his charity). Same for us: sell if you need to fund personal expenses like sending your kids to college, buying a new car, renovating your house. If you don't need the cash, leave it there.

He reminds you that you should only invest with money you know you don't need in the short term. If you need the money in the next one or two years, do not invest it in the stock market, because in the short term anything can happen: markets could drop 50%, could go up 50%, you never know. If you need the cash, put it into short-term bonds that mature by the time you need the money, money market funds, or a time/fixed deposit. The worst thing is to invest money you need into the stock market. If the market suddenly drops 50% and you need the cash, you are forced to sell great companies at a 50, 60% discount and sustain huge losses. You only want to sell stocks when they are overvalued, at an all-time high, to raise cash to spend.

#### [11:28] Invalid reasons to sell
1. Thinking the stock price is too high, or that you have high unrealized percentage gains you want to lock in in case you lose them. One of the biggest mistakes: you buy a stock, it goes up 20, 30, 40%, you think "I've got 40% of unrealized profits", get nervous and sell to take profit before it drops and the gain is gone. That is a very short-term trading mentality. A trader can do that, but if you are an investor who wants to grow your portfolio by huge amounts, it's the wrong way of thinking. Many people want a stock that increases fivefold, tenfold, or fiftyfold, and great companies deliver that over time; but to get 5x, 10x, 50x you must resist the temptation to sell when the stock is up 50%, 100%, 150%, or 200%. There's no such thing as the stock price being too high; high can get even higher, especially for a growing business whose intrinsic value keeps increasing. You'd be surprised: the stock can keep running for years and decades. Do not cut that huge gain potential short too early.

2. Selling while it's high to buy back when it's lower (trading in and out). He used to do this many years ago; he has been in the markets 30 years, 31 years this year. The moment a stock went up and looked really high he'd sell, then buy back when it dropped. He jumped in and out and made money doing that, on Apple, Microsoft, Meta, years ago. But he found that if he had held great companies like Apple, Meta, Microsoft, Amazon for the last 20 plus years instead of jumping in and out, today he'd probably be five times richer, or even 10 times richer. Concretely: a stock (call it Apple) waves up, waves down, waves up, and at some point he thinks it's going to go down and sells. After he sells, it goes down a bit and then flies up without him; to buy back he'd have to pay a much higher price, having missed a load of gains while it continues up 100, 200, 300% without him. This happened many, many times, countless, with great companies like Microsoft, Apple and Amazon. Another pattern: the price goes up, comes down, goes up, he thinks it looks really high and sells to take profits; it comes down ("I felt so smart selling it"), he plans to buy back when it touches a trend line, but before reaching the trend line it suddenly zooms up and takes off without him. He experienced this many, many times and left so much money on the table. It sounds like a great idea, but there's a very high risk of mistiming. You can pull it off once in a while, but over the long term there will be many times you mistime it, it takes off without you, and you get much lower returns than if you had just held the great company.

3. Selling because you predict a crash or recession is coming, or you listen to some expert's prediction. "That is the most stupid thing you can ever do." No one can predict when the next recession will hit or for certain when the next crash will come. Classic case: 2023. At the start of 2023 and end of 2022, the majority of economists and analysts said a recession was inevitable in 2023 and most people predicted a crash. Most investors sold all their stock near the bottom at the end of 2022, or worse, shorted the market predicting the crash. In 2023 the S&P 500 went up over 20% and the NASDAQ went up over 40%. Those who sold at the bottom missed the huge gains, and those who shorted made huge losses. Never ever sell because of a prediction of the economy or the market.

4. Never sell because there's bad news out there: bad macroeconomic news ("the yield curve is so inverted, I better sell", "GDP is negative", "interest rates are too high") or geopolitical news ("the US has entered a new war"). The most stupid reason to sell, because often you will regret it: the market continues going up, or dips slightly and goes up without you.

5. Never sell in panic during a correction or bear market. During a correction the market can drop 10, 15%, or in a bear market 20, 25%, and it's natural to panic. People who don't understand the company they own think "the price keeps dropping every single day, what if it goes to zero". Panic sets in and they sell near or at the bottom. He knows so many people who sold at the bottom of the 2020 bear market in March and knocked their heads against the wall as the market zoomed up 80% one year later. Many in 2022 panicked and sold in September, October 2022 because everyone said it would get worse, then in 2023 pulled their hair out when the market zoomed up 20 to 40%. Stay invested in great companies, ride the ups and downs and don't be afraid of temporary drawdowns.

#### [18:58] 6. Do not sell a great stock just to reduce allocation (portfolio rebalancing)
Many professional fund managers practice portfolio rebalancing at the end of the year. A balanced portfolio has roughly equal percentage allocation across all stocks. Example: a portfolio of 10 stocks, each ideally a 10% allocation, as learned in the lesson on building a winning portfolio. Investing $10,000 into 10 stocks means $1,000 each. Year one: all 10%. Extreme example: one year later one of the stocks is a huge winner, like Nvidia, and doubles in price while the other stocks don't move at all. In market value that first stock would now make up about 18% of the portfolio, and the other nine would make up 9% each. Most fund managers would say this stock takes up too much allocation, sell shares of it to reduce the allocation, and reinvest the cash into the other nine to balance back to 10% each. That's portfolio rebalancing. Warren Buffett and Peter Lynch say this is really stupid: if stock number one continues to be a great business and keeps growing, you shouldn't sell it even though it has a big allocation. When Warren Buffett bought Apple, it was a normal-size allocation in his portfolio, but because Apple did so well, Apple now makes up 50% of Buffett's entire public portfolio. Does he sell Apple to reduce the allocation? No. He says if Apple remains a great company, keeps growing, keeps dominating and is not too overpriced, it makes sense to only hold, and we may even want to buy more. That's the difference between super investors and normal fund managers, and why over 90% of fund managers cannot beat the market: they do dumb, stupid things like that. Peter Lynch says the same: you should not use the normal rebalancing method; as long as your great stock remains great, you keep holding it. If you sell your great stock and use the money to buy the other stocks, it's like cutting the flowers in your garden and watering the weeds.

### 71 - When to Sell Part 2

#### [00:00] Exit rule 1 in detail: fundamentals deteriorate permanently
The first exit rule is to sell when the fundamentals of the business deteriorate permanently. Examples: the company loses its competitive advantage; the economic moat weakens or deteriorates. Causes include number one: the business is disrupted by new technology or competition, so it no longer has the ability to generate increasing revenue, profits and free cash flow.

Singapore Press Holdings (his Singapore context) used to be a monopoly in the media industry in Singapore but was disrupted by social media, Google and Facebook. He knows it firsthand because he used to advertise 100% on Singapore Press Holdings, the local newspapers. As people stopped reading news from newspapers and read online, he could see the effect of his advertising on the papers diminishing, so he shifted his entire ad spend to Google and Facebook, as did many other advertisers. That was the start of the demise of Singapore Press Holdings; the share price collapsed and eventually the shares were delisted.

Nokia: in the 1990s Nokia had a 90% market share, selling mobile phones ("they weren't really smart at the time"). 90% of the people in the world, including himself and his friends, used a Nokia phone. Then came the iPhone and BlackBerry [the transcript is partly garbled here: "and we had BlackBerry. So all these were more advanced than a Nokia"]. Nokia was very slow to provide this new type of smartphone. It wasn't that Nokia couldn't do it; they made a strategic error: in the old days mobile phones cost $100, $200, and when Apple came out with the first phone at like 500 bucks, Nokia said that's insane, nobody would pay $500 for a phone, and kept making $200 phones. A strategic blunder, because eventually everyone bought $500 [he says "$500,000"] iPhones and Samsung phones. Nokia's market share dropped from 90% to, "I don't know, 1% right now", and Nokia's share price dropped from $42 to like $2. It literally collapsed. He never owned Singapore Press Holdings or Nokia shares but was watching all the way. The moment he saw people changing their Nokia for an iPhone or BlackBerry, he knew it was the end of the company. It did not happen overnight; it took several years to lose market share. If you had owned Nokia at $42 you had a lot of time to get out; even before Nokia reported really bad financial results you could see around you that people were no longer buying the next Nokia phone.

People ask: Adam, how do I know when the fundamentals of a business deteriorate? Very simple: look around you; the people around you are no longer buying that product or service, which you used to. That's why it's really, really important to invest in companies where you are the customer yourself. He owns McDonald's because he loves it, goes about three times a week for breakfast, and the day he stops eating McDonald's is the day he'll sell the stock, because that means a lot of other people will do the same. If he didn't eat at McDonald's he wouldn't understand the business and wouldn't know when to sell. Same reason he never owned Starbucks: even though it would be a great company, he doesn't like Starbucks coffee; he's from Singapore and drinks local coffee for a dollar fifty and refuses to pay more than three bucks for coffee. Since he's not a customer he has no advantage in understanding the business. He owns McDonald's, Yum Brands, Hershey chocolates because he eats these all the time ("you can tell I'm really healthy, right?"). Same with Singapore Press Holdings: he never owned shares but, as an advertiser, the moment he stopped advertising and moved to Facebook he knew it was the end. At that time the share price hadn't collapsed yet and the financial results were still okay, so if he'd owned shares there would have been a lot of time to get out. Never worry "what if it's too late": you will know way beforehand, because you can see the business losing its competitive advantage around you.

Kodak: years ago cameras were film cameras; his father was a photographer so his house was full of film cameras. You had to develop the film, and Kodak was the market leader in film. When digital cameras were invented, "there's no more film", and when the iPhone came out and you could take pictures with a phone, he said "that's it, the end of Kodak". At that time Kodak's share price was still very high and Kodak was still making a lot of money, because it took years for everyone to adopt digital cameras; not overnight. So there was a lot of time to get out before the share price collapsed.

Intel: 10, 20, 30 years ago, any computer you bought (Apple, IBM, Compaq) had Intel chips, and everyone advertised "Intel inside". Slowly these computer companies stopped using Intel chips: Apple said we're going to use our own chips, Samsung the same. Slowly the ads no longer said Intel inside, and the moment he noticed, he said that's the end of Intel. The share price hadn't collapsed yet. It's very easy to tell if you understand the business, which is why you must buy businesses you understand, whose products you use yourself, or at least know people who use the products or services very well.

Blockbuster Video: in the old days you rented a videocassette and returned it in three days or got fined. In the US Blockbuster was a monopoly, all over the place, the market leader. When Netflix first came out with video streaming, Blockbuster did not bust overnight; it took many, many years as people slowly changed their preferences from videotapes to online. You could see the slow demise as it lost its competitive advantage.

#### [08:10] Moat erosion from changing consumer preferences
A company can also lose its moat because of a change in consumer preferences. In the old days when he went shopping with his parents as a boy you'd buy a lot of canned baked beans and unhealthy food or ketchup; Kraft Heinz (cheese, ketchup, baked beans) were doing very, very well. As people got more health conscious (when he started to raise his own family they'd avoid processed food and buy fresh food), you could see the shift in consumer preferences, and that's when Kraft Heinz began to lose its dominant moat as a food company. A company's economic moat does not change overnight. If you buy a great company, don't freak out just because the share price dropped for one day or after one earnings report; it takes years for the moat to erode.

#### [09:19] Fundamental signs of moat erosion
The best sign is to observe what consumers are doing. You can also look at the quarterly financial report:
- Profit margins declining consistently for at least two [the transcript is garbled here, appears to be "two ... years"; he continues about the company having to cut prices/discount to attract more customers] [filled from earlier pass: the decline is due to lower prices, the company being forced to discount to attract customers; earlier pass says "2-3 years"]. That means the company is losing its pricing power. A great business does not need to give discounts to keep its market share; if a company is forced to keep discounting, it has no pricing power, is facing a lot of competition, and that is a sign of a deteriorating business.
- Lower profit margins caused by higher cost of sales, which also makes margins contract.
- Declining market share. If a company is continually losing market share year after year: one quarter fine, three quarters fine, one year maybe okay, but if it loses market share for two years straight, it's a sign the business is not the same as it used to be. When you observe customers switching to competitors [garbled in the transcript] [filled from earlier pass: customers visibly switching to competitor brands is another sign].

#### [10:53] Financial fraud
[Partly garbled: "views of financial fraud or financial irregularities"] If the company is accused of financial fraud or financial irregularities, sell the stock, because if there's smoke there's usually a lot of fire, and it could be detrimental to the entire business. For those old enough to remember: WorldCom and Enron were among the biggest companies in America at the time (Enron an energy company, WorldCom a communications company). No one thought they could go bust, but both went to zero because of accounting fraud. When initially accused, people said no, it can't be, they're such big companies; eventually they went bust. He didn't own shares in those companies, "thank God", and learned his lesson: no matter how famous or big the company, the moment it is accused of financial fraud, get out. Don't even look back.

#### [11:49] Exit rule 3 in detail: still great but ridiculously overvalued and over-extended
Usually if he finds the stock price is selling more than 50% to 100% [overvalued; the transcript is cut here], he would start [reducing] his position [filled from earlier pass: the threshold is more than 50-100% above intrinsic value, and he reinvests in an equally great business at a more reasonable valuation]. He never sells all at once, because you can never pick the top (it could go even higher), just as we can never pick the bottom and so average in our position. So he sells in tranches too: to sell 100 shares, he'll sell maybe 25 shares first, a few days later another 25, a few days later another 25, slowly averaging out. Usually when a stock is extremely overvalued you also notice the stock price is far from the moving averages. [Garbled: he introduces "parabolic" moves.] A sustainable uptrend moves in a 45-degree fashion: wave up, wave down, wave up, at a 45-degree angle. Usually as the price goes up it is at least near the moving averages; in this context look at the 20 exponential moving average (20 EMA), usually the one closest to the price if the price is on an uptrend. The red line on the chart is the 20 EMA; at a 45-degree angle the price is near the 20 EMA and even near the 50 moving average. But when the price goes from 45 degrees to vertical, going up in a 90-degree fashion with very long green candles, we call this a parabolic move (90-degree moves). When you see that, [the transcript drops a clause; context: get out] [filled from earlier pass: the parabolic move is driven by panic buying, everyone rushing in, and is usually the end of the uptrend]. Usually that is where poor retail investors are lured to the party: they buy because they heard it's a great stock from a neighbor or friend, they jump in right at the top, and it collapses. The same happens with the overall index. Warren Buffett said many years ago that a bull market is like sex: it feels the best just before it ends. So when you see this climax, "boom, it explodes". If a stock he owns goes up at 45 degrees, fine; the moment it goes up 90 degrees with big long candles, especially if it's really [overextended], "I'm getting out", because usually it will collapse.

#### [15:25] Example: Meituan
A stock he bought, Meituan from the Hong Kong market, at $70 per share (he bought way down there; check his YouTube video, type Adam Khoo Meituan stock, where he shared this investment at the time). His calculated intrinsic value was $220 at the time. After he bought, it went up and up and crossed the intrinsic value. If a stock is slightly overvalued, like 10, 20, 30%, he'll still hold it. But then it started going parabolic: 90 degrees. He sold [a quarter] of his shares because it was already overvalued, selling at about 330 [he says "3.30"] against intrinsic value of 220, i.e. 50% overvalued; he started selling a quarter of his shares. After he sold, it initially came back down, and he thought maybe he could buy back when it got back below intrinsic value, but then it went "whoa" and reached $440, where it was 100% overvalued, and he said "I'm out" and sold all his shares. He showed the notification he sent to his UIP (Ultimate Investors Playbook) subscribers; back in the old days he used Telegram, now a Discord channel. They had 3,000 members at that time, and he made a 400% return. He also sold another stock, Ping An Insurance, at the time, and sold the remaining half later, right above 440. Subscribe to UIP and you get his immediate buy and sell alerts for when he gets out of a stock and when he starts buying. After he sold, he was really lucky, as there's no way to predict this: it later collapsed all the way down. People asked if he would buy it back; he said no, because the Chinese government came up with a lot of new regulations that totally changed Meituan's business model, so it is no longer a great business. No matter how low it goes, he isn't buying it back; he just moves on to another great company.

#### [17:42] Which stocks he rarely sells
Secular growth stocks and large-cap growth stocks: he doesn't usually like to sell them; he holds them for many, many years unless they get ridiculously overpriced. Stocks like Meta, Microsoft, Amazon, Nvidia: he will very rarely sell them, because in the long run they just keep compounding his wealth. Same with defensive companies: very, very rarely sells unless they get crazy expensive.

#### [18:18] Deep cyclical stocks
He very rarely buys deep cyclical stocks (mentioned in the investment categories), but if he does, he won't hold them for very long. Deep cyclical stocks include banks, insurance, certain industrial companies, real estate companies, property developers, shipping companies, commodity companies. Their profits go up and down; when the economy is booming they make a lot of money, when it's not doing well they make less, and their share price is also very cyclical, like a big roller coaster. Example: JP Morgan, very cyclical. He doesn't like to buy these, but sometimes he will if they are near or at the bottom of the cycle and very cheap, and then he gets out near the top of the cycle. This is only for cyclical stocks.

How to tell the bottom or top of the cycle: look at the price-to-book ratio, especially for banks, insurance companies and real estate companies. In TradingView put in the indicator "price to book ratio". The blue line is JP Morgan's price-to-book ratio. Looking at the last 10 years, the highest point is 1.89 and the lowest is 0.64. He takes the highest and lowest and roughly adds them together and divides by two to get the midpoint range; for this bank the midpoint is 1.26 [as stated; (1.89+0.64)/2 = 1.265]. Below the midline is a buy; that is one way to tell when it's near the bottom. To get out, he sells usually when it reaches the midpoint of the 10-year high and the 10-year median: take the high 1.89, add 1.26, divide by 2 to get 1.56. So 1.56 is the midpoint between the 10-year high and the 10-year median; when it reaches that, it's a sell. After you sell it can still go higher; you can never sell right at the top. As he speaks, JP Morgan is hitting that high again; if he owned JP Morgan (which he doesn't) he would already have sold it, and he wouldn't want to buy here. This is uniquely for cyclical stocks.

Caveat: he owns a lot of Singapore bank stocks, and they are cyclical over the long run, but he never sells them; he holds them through the ups and downs, because he buys them not for medium-term trades but to collect dividends. Singapore bank stocks give very good dividends: when he buys them when they are very low, at about five, six percent dividend yield, he locks in the yield, tax free, and holds to collect passive income year after year. For US banks, by contrast, he sells them, because the dividends are just too little for him to want to hold them.
