# Part 15 — Lessons 72–76

### Reasons Not to Sell

#### [00:00] Short-term traders and why I stopped being one
Many people are very short-term focused as investors. When the price goes up a lot they think "I'm going to sell it and buy it back lower", or they hope to buy it lower. Or they say "this great stock is now on a downtrend, so I'll sell it, and when it reverses into an uptrend I'll buy it back." They are always thinking sell high, buy back low, and they get in and out on stops. As I mentioned earlier, I used to do that. Looking back, if I hadn't, and had held on to these great companies, I would have made a lot more money; my wealth would have compounded at a much higher rate.

#### [00:29] Illustrative example: Amazon 2018 to 2020
Take Amazon, a great business, over a certain period, let's say 2018 to 2020. Suppose you traded in and out by the trends. You buy at 1006 (before the stock split); it goes on an uptrend, then reverses into a downtrend. By the time you get a downtrend signal (the 50 crossing below the 150) you sell, at 1330, and the transcript gives the result as a "70% loss" [unclear; figure as heard; later in the same passage he adds "minus 17", and the earlier pass reads this first leg as a 17% loss] [filled from earlier pass]. It reverses back into an uptrend (50 above the 150 again), so you buy back at 1009. It reverses into a downtrend again and you sell at 1776, losing 6%. It reverses up again, you buy at $2,000, and it goes up to 3352, making 62%. That is what many traders do: in and out, in and out. At the end do you make money? Yes. Totalling it up: 62%, minus 6.5, minus 17, and you are still up 24% in two years. But if you had just bought at the start and held through the ups and downs with your eyes closed, you would have gained 108%. So it always pays to stay invested in a great company.

#### [02:12] The great-spouse analogy
Finding a great company that compounds your wealth over time is not easy; they are very rare. In the entire US market, less than 1% of companies are great companies to hold on to: your Microsofts, your Nvidia, your Amazon, your Meta. It is the same in relationships. Is it easy to find the man or woman of your dreams, beautiful, funny, loyal, intelligent, kind? No, it's really difficult. Once you find that great spouse you want to hold on to them and grow together and enjoy life together for years, through their ups and downs. The problem is some people think "they're going into a bad mood, I'll avoid being there; when my wife goes crazy I'll run away to the pub with my friends and come back when she's in a better mood." If you keep doing that, one day by the time you come back your wife is no longer there, because she ran off with the best friend who was there to comfort her. Same with a great company: if you leave it, it will leave you behind.

#### [03:48] Time in the market
Hence the famous quote: time in the markets as an investor is more important than attempting to time the markets. If you just hold Amazon over an extended period, over a 5-year period it is up 610% and over a 10-year period up 2,176%. Many great companies do this, but very few investors capture a fraction of these gains. Why? They freak out too easily. They sell the moment it drops a bit, or once it goes up a bit they want to take profits, and they get thrown off the ride and don't stay on long enough for it to make them really wealthy.

#### [04:33] Never sell because of macroeconomic concerns
Another reason you should never sell: never sell because of macroeconomic concerns, such as "the yield curve is inverted and a recession is coming", "M2 is declining and the Fed is doing QT", or a war, an earthquake, a virus. Look at the S&P 500 over the 10 years from March 09 to December 2019 (you can take any period). The S&P 500 gained 495%. Did most investors enjoy that close to 500% return? No, very few captured a fraction of it, because along the way they got scared out of the market by news that scares them. The news gives you bad news every day; that's how they get views and eyeballs. Write this down: there's always something to worry about. The bull always climbs a wall of constant worry.

What was the news at the beginning of this 500% bull market? 663,000 jobs lost in March; Chrysler and GM filed for bankruptcy; BP oil spill; stock market flash crash; most powerful earthquake to ever hit; S&P downgrades US debt; European sovereign debt crisis; US government shutdown; Ebola virus contagion fears; then Brexit, Charlottesville, S&P 500 enters a bear market, US government shuts down again, a trade war, yield curve inversion, Iranian bombing, emergency Fed injection into the repo market. Reading all this you would have sold early and been thrown off the ride.

#### [06:35] Words of wisdom
In the words of Peter Lynch, your ultimate success or failure will depend on your ability to ignore the worries of the world long enough to allow your investments to succeed. These are words of wisdom that changed my life and allowed me to compound millions in the market. The first one is by Charlie Munger [the start of the quote is missing in the transcript]: the money is not in buying and selling stocks; it's in waiting, sitting on your ass and being patient, and few people can do that. Peter Lynch said that his best stocks have been the third year, the fourth year, or even the fifth year that he's owned them; it's not the third week or fourth week. People want their money very rapidly; it just doesn't happen. You can make small money fast in a few weeks or a few months, but you will never make big money, never make millions, with that short-term mentality. Munger again says the first rule of compounding your wealth is never to interrupt it unnecessarily. And one of my favorites: the stock market is a device for transferring money from the impatient people to the patient people.

#### [07:43] What to do when the market and your portfolio are near an all-time high
As you invest for many years there will be times when the overall stock market and your portfolio are at an all-time high (portfolio goes up, down, up, down, and now sits near an all-time high). The stocks you own are no longer cheap; they could be fairly priced or starting to be overpriced. What do you do? Near the all-time high there are always two groups: those who chase and say "I want to buy now because I think it's going to go higher", and those who say "I better sell before it comes down again."

If you're already invested, I would say at this point you definitely do not want to buy more. Remember, follow the rules: only add more shares when prices drop to undervalued levels and to support levels. But does it mean that you should sell? In the old days I used to sell when I thought the market or my portfolio was at the top. There were times I sold and it did come down a bit, but then it rose a lot higher and again I was off the ride. Or it came down and I was going to buy it back, but before I could, it reversed up and again I was thrown off the ride. Looking back through my history, I found that if I simply held on through those inevitable ups and downs I would have ended up a lot richer, because if you try to get out and get back in, you could mistime it. Mistiming means you get out too early and it runs off without you, or you get out at the right time but don't get in fast enough and the market runs off without you.

The risk of being out of the market is worse than the risk of being in the market. If you're in and it drops, no worries, you'll come back and you're still in. If you get out and the market runs off without you, you can't get back in except at a very high price in the future. Write this down: the risk of staying out of the market over the long run is worse than the risk of staying in the markets. That is one of the lessons I learned.

#### [10:23] Three options near a new high
Option one is to simply close your eyes and hold it. Psychologically expect that yes, there could be a correction coming, but you never know exactly when it's going to happen. If it does, add more shares (if you don't already have a full position) once it gets undervalued again, once it reaches the support levels you have identified, and be prepared for any temporary drawdown on your portfolio.

Option two, what I did in the past: sell your shares and hope to buy back at a lower price when it gets nearer the moving averages or support levels. I'm not saying you can't, but it's not always easy to time selling the exact top and buying back the exact bottom. The risk of selling and not buying back at a lower price in time is very, very big in the long run.

#### [11:29] The cost of mistiming: missing the best days
This chart shows how timing the market can be very costly in the long run if you mistime it, which is very easy even with all your technical analysis skills, because the market can't be predicted 100%; it sometimes goes up when people least expect and down when people least expect. This chart was life-changing for me and really changed my perspective, because I used to time the market a lot as an investor. Nowadays I do my best not to time the market; I just stay invested in great companies.

Imagine you invested $10,000 into the S&P 500 index for the last 20 years, from January 03 to 2022. If you just held through the ups and downs, your money would have grown 548% in those 20 years (individual companies would give much higher, but this is just the index, and it applies to great companies as well). If during those 20 years you jumped out and back in and missed the best 10 days, your return from 548% would be only 197%: you lose more than half your returns by missing just 10 days. Miss the best 20 days and your return is only 78%, a fraction of the buy-and-hold return. Miss the best 30 days: only up 17%. Miss the best 40 days: negative 19%. Miss the best 50 days: you would have lost 42%. Miss the best 60 days: you'd have lost 57% of your money.

If you're trying to time the market, you're telling yourself you can get out and back in without missing those crucial days, and you never know when the biggest winning days will appear. In fact, seven of the 10 best days in the entire 20-year history occurred during bear markets, and what do most investors do during bear markets? They get out, so they are not there to capture the 10 best days in 20 years, and that is why they end up with huge losses. Some of you may be thinking "what if I miss the 10 worst days?" Of course, with a crystal ball that would be fantastic, but in your attempt to miss the 10 worst days you may unknowingly miss the 10 best days. So it's better to stay put and not miss any of those days, because you never know.

#### [14:37] My screenshot of a best day
On average, in a year, the market goes up about 10%, but it doesn't go up slowly through the year; sometimes the entire annual return can come in one day, and you'd miss that one day. This was one of those days. I can't remember the exact date, but I took a screenshot of my two portfolios at Interactive Brokers: in that one day one portfolio was up 10.5% and the other up 10.8% (transcript reads "10.8.3%"). One portfolio gained 175k and the other gained 231K, so in that one day my portfolio grew 396,000 US. What if I had missed that one day? My entire portfolio returns would be very different that year and for the last five years.

#### [15:34] Peter Lynch on preparing for corrections
Next life-changing quote, by Peter Lynch: far more money has been lost by investors preparing for corrections or trying to anticipate corrections than has been lost in the corrections themselves. Meaning: many people try to predict that a crash is coming and so get out of the market or do not invest, and they end up losing more in opportunity cost than those who just stay in and ride the short-term ups and downs.

#### [16:09] Option three: hedging with options
Option one is just hold through the ups and downs. Option two is try to sell high and buy back low, and good luck to you. Option three: hold on to your shares and don't sell them, but if you can see we're going through a correction or bear market, sell covered call options to generate extra income as you expect the price to correct and consolidate, and at the same time buy protective put options to generate profits as the market drops temporarily, against drawdowns. If you learn how to use options with your portfolio, they can allow you to earn extra income during those short-term corrections and bear markets. We cover this in detail in our options trading course, which you can also take from Piranha Profits.

Here is a screenshot from my portfolio during one of the past bear markets. The stocks were all dropping, so my investments were dropping in price temporarily (they rebounded later), but notice I used a lot of options, calendar spreads, which allowed me to generate short-term profits to make up for the short-term drop in my investment portfolio. We call this hedging your portfolio using options, using protective put options. Note that learning to hedge is not a must; it is nice to have, but not a must. If you don't know how, that's fine: you just hold it and you'll make a lot of money anyway. If you can do it, it's just a bonus. You will still do very well simply by holding great companies through their short-term ups and downs, because eventually they will go much higher over time.

#### [18:06] Another portfolio and a student message
This is another of my portfolios: I used a bear put spread combo and made profits here, put option profits, during the bear market, when the stocks were going down but my options were making me money in the short term. These are some messages from students as I guided them through the bear market. Michael said: "Me too. I discovered Adam on YouTube and knew I had to have him as my mentor. I bought all the courses on Black Friday last year. Even though the market is going down, my account is about staying the same because of hedging. I'm no longer worried about the market falling when I'm sleeping."

That concludes Lesson 9, which covered finding investment opportunities as well as portfolio management, how to monitor your watch list and your portfolio, and finally exit and selling rules.

### The Psychology of Winning Investors, Part 1 (Lesson 10: Rules 1 and 2)

#### [00:00] Strategy is only 30%; psychology is the rest
This is one of the most important lessons in the entire course. To achieve success in anything it takes two factors, and the first is the right strategy. Most people focus only on strategy ("how do I do it?"), but I can tell you that strategy only makes up 30% of a person's success. The rest comes down to your psychology or mindset, and most people leave that to chance and think they have the right psychology when they don't. Success is really about psychology: the emotional discipline, focus and self-motivation to apply what you've learned and follow the rules.

Take weight loss. Learning how to lose weight, the strategy, is pretty simple: you can read all kinds of Atkins diets and exercise regimes on the internet. But most people know what to do and don't have the discipline to follow through, to exercise consistently and eat certain foods and avoid others. That's why less than 5% of people who attempt to lose weight actually lose weight. And the sad thing is that out of the 5% who lose weight, 3% put the weight back on in the next couple of years. Again, psychology.

Same in business. You need the right strategy: marketing, finance, accounting, logistics. But if strategy were all you need, everyone with an MBA would be able to run a multinational company, and that's not true; many people with MBAs can't run a business, and many who dropped out of school become successful entrepreneurs. If strategy were all you need, everyone with a PhD in finance would be a billionaire, and we know that's not the reality.

For the beginning of this course you've focused a lot on strategy: fundamental analysis, technical analysis, how to identify a great business. You now have the knowledge and the strategies. So now I'll focus on what's really important, which is the 70%: the psychology and mindset it takes to succeed as an investor and build your wealth over time.

#### [02:41] Rule 1: Absolute discipline
The first step in the psychology of successful investors is to have absolute discipline. Discipline means you only buy a stock and only sell a stock based on predetermined rules in your investment plan. These predetermined rules must be objective rules based on fundamentals and technical analysis, which is what we've learned. For example, we only buy very good businesses with strong fundamentals (consistently increasing sales, earnings, cash flow, low debt and so on) that are undervalued, and we want to buy stocks when they're on a clear uptrend. Do most people invest based on these rules? No. Most buy and sell based on rumors ("I heard this company's going to be taken over", "I heard a rumor this stock's going to go up because someone's playing up the stock"), or on emotions, and the two emotions are fear and greed: buying out of greed and selling based on fear. Many also buy and sell on the opinions of other people. If you do all these things, I guarantee you will never make money in the long run.

Another common mistake is buying because of FOMO, the fear of missing out: everyone's buying it, I don't want to miss the train. They jump into something they don't understand and get slaughtered like lemmings, which follow each other down the cliff. So never buy based on FOMO, never buy something you don't understand that's not in your investment plan, and never sell in panic just because you read in the papers that some expert said the market's going to crash or there's a trade war, only to see the stock keep going up after you sell. Always have that discipline.

#### [04:42] Warren Buffett and the dot-com bubble
Warren Buffett is the greatest investor in the world, and by his own admission he's not the most intelligent person in the world, but he's the most disciplined investor. I like this example because the method I use is very similar to his: we only invest in profitable businesses that are undervalued and avoid unprofitable businesses that are overpriced and going up because of hype.

This happened during the dot-com bubble, 1999 to 2000. The stock market was irrational. Companies that were unprofitable and overpriced kept going up: Netscape, AOL, Pets.com, all hype. On the other hand companies that were making money, like McDonald's, Procter & Gamble, Coca-Cola, were undervalued but kept going down. Imagine you followed your investment plan, bought good undervalued companies and the price went down, while your friends who bought lousy unprofitable companies saw the share price go up. How would you feel? Most people start to doubt themselves and say "forget my investment plan, I'll buy the stocks that are going up." Buffett refused. In 1999, while the S&P 500 went up 18%, Berkshire Hathaway went down 23%. Imagine being down 23% while the market is up 18%. Most people would abandon their plans and be seduced by FOMO and jump in with the crowd. Buffett avoided those loss-making technology stocks, and sure enough the market kept going up and his portfolio kept going down, but he didn't care because he had discipline.

Everyone was criticizing and ridiculing him for sticking to his rules of buying good companies. Barron's, a very well-known financial publication, ran an article that said "What's wrong with you, Warren? After 30 years of unrivaled investment success, Warren Buffett may be losing his magic touch": you're a dinosaur, you don't understand tech stocks, you're sticking to old-economy stocks. Berkshire Hathaway fell from $80,000 per share down to $54,000 per share. He was unfazed and stuck to his rules because he knew that in the short term the stock market is irrational, but eventually stock prices always go back to reality and reflect the fundamentals of the business. One year later the bubble burst and all those unprofitable high-priced tech stocks collapsed. The NASDAQ dropped 72% in the next couple of years and the S&P 500 lost 28%. Eventually everyone lost money, but Buffett made all the money because he stuck to his rules of holding good, profitable, undervalued companies. In that short period Berkshire was down 23% while the market was up 18%; over the long run the market collapsed and Buffett outperformed it for many, many years. His stock went from $80,000 down to $54,000 (a 23% drop in the short term) but in the long run, from 54,000 it is now at $443,000.

The lesson: when you stick to your investment plan and only buy good undervalued companies, in the short term the market may not reward you. You may find everyone going up while you're going down. Do not abandon your plan. In the short term the stock market is irrational and manipulated, but eventually it goes back to reality. Those holding good undervalued companies end up making all the money, while those holding hyped-up unprofitable companies eventually lose all their money.

#### [09:50] History repeats: 2020 and 2021
Does history repeat? You bet. Last year was 2020, and a lot of companies that were not making money and were overpriced kept going up, like Roku, Zoom, Tesla, Peloton, Fastly. I refused to buy them because they're not making money and they're overpriced. I was criticized: "Adam, why aren't you buying these stocks? They're going up." I said it won't last. Sure enough, now in 2021 these stocks have collapsed 50, 60%. The companies I bought, which make money and are undervalued, like Microsoft, Alphabet, McDonald's and Yum Brands, are holding their price, and some are still going up in this correction. So always stick to your investment plan no matter what, even though in the short term other people may outperform you, because in the long run you will always outperform the market by sticking to great undervalued companies and your plan.

#### [11:07] Rule 2: Invest only in what you understand (circle of competence)
Psychology rule number two is to only invest in what you truly understand and what you have researched yourself: staying within your circle of competence. No matter how smart you are, you can't be an expert in everything and you don't have to. You don't have to win at every game, only at the game you understand. For me, I don't really understand energy companies; energy prices are so unpredictable, and I never invest in commodity companies because commodity prices are very unpredictable. The price may go up without me and that's fine, because I make money in businesses I understand, which are predictable and have very consistent cash flow.

I've been criticized for not investing in Tesla because I think it doesn't make sense: it's overpriced and the electric vehicle market is very competitive. People said "Adam, you don't understand Tesla. It's not just a car company, it's a software company, a battery company, a company that will change the world." No matter how much research I've done, I just don't understand the business, so I refuse to invest in it. People can make all their money in Tesla, but it's not my game. I don't have to make money in every game; I make a lot of money in what I understand, like Facebook, Amazon, Alphabet and Microsoft, and that's good enough.

#### [12:58] Never blindly rely on experts' tips
When you invest, never blindly rely on recommendations and tips from experts. Imagine I'm invited to dinner with some of the greatest investors in the world: Warren Buffett, Carl Icahn, Bill Ackman and others. During dinner all of them mention they're investing in a stock called Micron, say. Just because they're all investing in Micron, and I admire all of them, would I invest in Micron? No, because I have not done my own research. But I'll take their idea and put it through my seven-step investment analysis. Does it meet my criteria and my investment plan? If it does, I'll invest, not because they have invested but because it meets my criteria. If it doesn't, I won't invest even though people I admire or experts invest.

I truly admire Warren Buffett and have learned so much from his work, but there are many things he invested in that I would never invest in because they don't make sense to me: General Motors, the airlines, Occidental Petroleum, IBM. I refused to buy all those, and I don't know if it's luck, but these are stocks he lost money in. Thank God I did not follow some of his investments. Always do your own research for every investment, monitor your stocks regularly and keep investment records with your portfolio tracker and investment journal. There's no free lunch in this world; no one gives you money for free. To create wealth you've got to put in the work and effort, and the same is true in investing.

#### [15:09] The Nobel Group example
Many of you receive analyst reports from your broker or private bank, and I read a lot of these myself. You can leverage on their research, there's a lot that's useful, but never blindly follow their recommendations. Always check whether it meets your checklist. I won't name the broker because I don't want to embarrass anyone, but this is one of the top brokers in Singapore, and they send me research reports regularly. This was back in November of 2011. They sent a report recommending a buy on Nobel Group, one of the largest commodity companies in the world, listed on the Singapore stock market and in the Straits Times Index, a blue-chip company. A long article said it's a great company: buy Nobel at $1.56, the current price, target price 2.05 (as heard, "205"). The moment I read it I said let me do my research. First, I never ever buy commodity companies, so straight away I wouldn't buy it, but I checked anyway, and going through the seven steps it failed most of them. Friends asked "Adam, should I buy Nobel Group?" I said I won't, because it failed my steps. Which ones?

Number one, negative cash flow from operations: it showed increasing profits but the cash flow was negative, so why generate profits on paper but no cash coming in? Second, profit margin of less than 0.5%, very thin, so the moment cost rises it could swing to a huge loss. Third, very high debt: debt to EBITDA ratio of more than 12 (it should never be more than 3, but this was 12) and debt servicing ratio over 50%. Debt too high, profits too low, cash flow negative: not a great company by my criteria, so I didn't buy.

What happened after? A week after the recommendation the price dropped from $1.56 to $1.08 (transcript reads "$1.8"), a 31% drop in a week. Imagine you bought it and realized you made a mistake, it's not a good company. What should you do? Cut loss, get out, sell, take your loss like a man or woman. Do most people? No. Most wouldn't sell, because "if I sell I'm going to lose money; if I hold on, maybe it might come back, I hope." Remember, in the markets hope is for the hopeless. Never have hope. Once you know it's a lousy business, get out. If it's a good business and the price goes down temporarily, that's fine, you can buy more shares, because it's about the business. In this case it's a lousy business, so the moment you know, you get out, win, lose or draw. Many refused and held on for blind hope. From 108 a couple of years later it dropped all the way to 45 cents, then I think to five cents, and now it's been delisted and the company has collapsed and people lost everything. A newspaper headline had investors complaining "I was cheated: tales from the collapse of commodity giant Nobel Group."

The lessons: first, sometimes we end up buying lousy companies by mistake or because we weren't aware; the moment you understand it's a mistake, get out. Second, don't listen to experts and analysts blindly; always do your own research. And the funny thing is that when people lost all their money in this stock, instead of learning to analyze businesses they blamed everyone except themselves: the broker, the company, their luck. As long as you keep blaming others you don't take responsibility, keep going in circles, never learn your lesson and will always lose money.

#### [20:29] The opposite example: Meituan Dianping
Here is an opposite example, where I wanted to buy a company and the analysts said "don't buy, it's a crap company." This was September 2019, a China stock called Meituan Dianping [transcript: "Meituan Tianping"]. I did a whole YouTube video on it (still on YouTube) where I did the research and said this is a great company with multi-bagger potential, that it could double or triple in the next year or so. In my chat groups I shared that Meituan is approaching my ideal buy price of $70, "getting my dry powder ready". Many students watched it with me, and on the 10th of September the price hit $70 and we all bought. I bought at 70 bucks; students posted "I bought at 70", another "bought in at 73", and so on. Students said "But Adam, I saw this article on CNBC, published in March 2019, a couple of months earlier: analysts said investors should avoid shares of Meituan Dianping. Why are you buying?" My reply: ignore these analysts, they may not know what the hell they're doing; trust your own research. Sure enough we bought at $70 on the 10th of September, and within the next 17 months the price went from $70 to $440, up 528% (as heard, "$528%") in 17 months. If I had listened to those analysts and not trusted my own research, I'd have missed out.

When did I sell? The moment it reached 320 I started selling because it was overvalued; my intrinsic value at the time was 240. I took some profits off the table, kept selling more and more, and at 440 I sold the majority of my stake. This was published on the UIP channel, the Ultimate Investors Playbook, which you can subscribe to for my live alerts. A screenshot from my account shows me up close to 400%, and I said I'm looking to sell half of my balance that I'm holding, up 400%, as it's quite overvalued.

#### [23:25] Goldman Sachs says short: June 2012
One last example, June 21, 2012. Goldman Sachs, one of the top investment banks on Wall Street, announced it was recommending clients build short positions in the S&P 500 due to a weakening domestic economy. You've seen on financial websites all these experts and bankers saying short stocks, or stocks are going up, making recommendations. Should you listen? No, never. Follow your investment rules. They said short stocks, but did stocks go down? No, they went up. On the chart, on the 21st of June they said short the market. Do the charts suggest an uptrend or a downtrend? An uptrend: the 50 moving average is above the 150 moving average. Even though the 50 crossed below the 150 for a while, the 150 was still sloping up. Sure enough the market didn't go down; it went up 7% in the next couple of months. People who listened to these experts in the media lost money, or lost the opportunity to stay in the market for another 7% gain. So never listen to the opinions or advice of analysts, bankers or experts. Instead follow the rules of your investment plan, based on the fundamentals of the businesses and the chart patterns you've learned in this course.

### The Psychology of Winning Investors, Part 2 (Rules 3, 4 and 5)

#### [00:00] Rule 3: Be patient and think long term
Psychology rule number three is to be patient and think long term, and this is one of the most difficult things people grapple with. Most people want to get rich quick, get rich fast, and I'm here to tell you it just doesn't happen; it doesn't exist. That's why most people end up going for get-rich-quick schemes or buying speculative hype stocks that look like they could double or triple really fast. Once in a while you could make a lot of money buying speculative stocks, just as once in a while you could make a lot of money buying the lottery, but in the long run whatever money you make you will always lose back. What I found over the years is that the only way to build sustainable wealth that grows consistently over the long run is to invest with a long-term time frame, be patient, and allow your investments to compound and grow in value.

Two of my favorite quotes: the stock market is a wonderful mechanism that transfers wealth from the impatient to the patient. And in the words of Charlie Munger, the big money is not in buying and selling stocks; the big money is in waiting. It's in buying great companies and giving them the chance to grow and compound over time.

The trouble is that most investors start excited to get wealthy, then abandon their investment strategy and fail because they grow impatient when they don't get rich overnight, or when the good companies they bought don't go up in the short term while they see other stocks or cryptocurrency going up. They get seduced by those quick profits, abandon the good companies and jump into those that look like they're going up; eventually those crash and the ones they abandoned go up. So remember that successful investing is not a get-rich-quick method but a get-rich-slow, get-rich-sure method. It takes years for small money to grow and compound into huge sums.

#### [02:14] Prices don't go up in a straight line
Stock prices, and your portfolio, don't go up in a straight line; they go through ups and downs and cycles. There are many times when you buy a good company that gives, for example, an annualized return of 26%, but it doesn't mean the price goes up 26% every year. There are times when the price goes flat for a year or even down for a year, but eventually it rises so that the annualized return is 26%. For example, you buy a stock at $100 and in three years it doubles to $200. Would you be happy with that? That's not bad; it's equivalent to a 26% annual return. But the price may not go up in a straight line: sometimes the first two years it goes sideways and nowhere, and the third year it doubles. That happens very frequently. The trouble is that after two years of seeing it go nowhere, most people lose faith and patience, say "I'm going to sell it and buy something else", and the moment they sell it, it doubles. So you've got to be patient. When you buy a good undervalued company, know it will go up, but you never know when it will double or triple, and you've got to be there when it happens.

#### [04:08] The power of compounding: the golf game
Compounding is what makes small money grow into huge amounts, and that's how you get really wealthy over time. Many people think "Adam, my money grows at 20% a year, that's not exciting, I want 100%, 200%." Like I'm saying, that doesn't exist; there's no quick money overnight. But if your money can grow at 20%, 30% a year and you compound that over the long run, it becomes millions and millions of dollars, even hundreds of millions if you're starting from a bigger base.

A great illustration is a game of golf (you play 18 holes). We bet 10 cents on the first hole, and on every following hole we double the bet. Second hole 20 cents, third hole 40 cents, fourth hole 80 cents, fifth hole $1.60. Without using a calculator, give a gut guess: what will the bet be on the 18th hole? Think of a number or write it down. Let's look at the next few holes: hole six, $3.20, then 6.40, 12.80 (as heard "640, 1280"), and at hole 9, halfway, the bet is $25.60. Do you want to change your guess for the 18th hole? Hole 10, $51; hole 11, $102; hole 12, $204. Hole 13, $409; hole 14, $800 (as heard); hole 15, $1,600. We're three holes from the 18th and at $1,600; change your guess or keep it? Notice that on hole 18 the bet is now $13,000 [as heard]. In 18 holes, starting with only 10 cents. That is the power of compounding, where small money grows into a huge amount over time.

Notice that for the first few holes it grows very slowly: 10 cents, 20 cents, 40 cents. It's like investing where in the first few years your money grows so slowly it's like watching grass grow and you get impatient and think "forget it, I'll do something more exciting." But when you stick to it, you come to a point where your money explodes exponentially. Between hole 15 and hole 18 there's only a three-hole difference [earlier pass has "only 4 holes"], but from $1,600 to $13,000 is a huge difference. Likewise if you start investing one year late, or four years later than someone else, where you end up over the long run is hugely different. So start as early as possible to give your money the runway to grow and compound.

#### [07:57] Rule 4: Buy slowly and sell even slower
In most things in life, activity equates to performance: the more active you are, the more results you get. Not so in investing; sometimes the less activity you do, the more money you make.

Be very patient before you make a buy. A mistake many of my students make, after taking my course or reading a book, is getting too anxious to buy something and trigger happy. They see something and start buying because they're afraid the market will go up, jumping in when a company may not be undervalued enough or isn't at the right buy point. One thing I've learned: if there's no great business at a great price at this point, do nothing. Sometimes great companies are all overpriced and nothing is undervalued. Then hold cash. Don't invest for the sake of investing; sometimes it's better to hold cash than buy stocks just because you want to buy something. If it's not at a good price, don't buy; wait for the price to be right, no need to rush. It's like baseball: you only swing when you have the perfect pitch, and if there's no perfect pitch you don't have to swing. Investing is not a race, it's a marathon. When the time is right, when the market is in fear, in correction, and great companies are on sale, that's when you buy aggressively. But when markets are high, things are expensive, and people are euphoric and excited, that's the time not to buy.

Be patient in buying, but be even more patient in selling. Another mistake that keeps people from getting really wealthy is that they take profits fast: buy a stock, it goes up 20%, "let me grab the profits before I lose them." By doing that you will make small money but never big money. The best stocks are the ones you hold forever. Then don't I ever sell? When would I use the money? It is very difficult to find a great business; there are very few great companies. Once you find one and are lucky enough to buy shares at a good price, hold it as long as possible and don't sell out too early. When I buy a great company I hold forever. I only sell if I need the cash to buy something, for example a new house or a car; fine, I'll sell. But if I don't need the cash, leave it in and let it grow and compound.

#### [11:34] The Fidelity study
There was an interesting study on the clients of Fidelity, one of the largest brokerages in the US, which looked at all client accounts to see which made the most money over the last 30 years. The accounts with the biggest gains and most profits were inactive accounts: either the client opened the account, put money in and forgot they owned the stock, or the client died and no one claimed the stock. Those made the most money. The accounts that were actively traded, where clients kept buying and selling, performed the worst. The irony: in investing, lower activity results in greater returns.

#### [12:27] My Facebook screenshot
A personal example: to this day I carry on my phone a screenshot of when I first bought Facebook, many years ago, I think about six or seven years ago, at $19 per share. Today Facebook sells at about $350 per share. A great investment? Yes. Did I hold it all the way to today? No, because I was an idiot. When I started investing I used to jump in and out of stocks: buy, it goes up a bit, jump out or buy something else. Yes, I made money, but I realized that if I had just bought great companies and held without getting in and out, today I'd be 10 times richer. I keep this picture in my phone as a reminder: when you find a great company, never sell it unless you need the cash to buy something. If you don't, let it grow and compound in your account.

#### [13:47] The spouse analogy again
It's like relationships: it's so difficult to find a great spouse, loving, caring, beautiful, kind-hearted, and the moment you find that top 1% of people in the world you want to hold on to them for life. During those years they'll go through good moods and bad moods, and in a bad mood you want to be there for them. For stocks, you want to buy more shares at a discount so you compound even faster. When your stock is temporarily down, that's a good opportunity to add more shares before it compounds higher again. In the marriage, when your spouse is in a bad mood you're there for them, you score brownie points, you listen, and the marriage gets even stronger.

Some say "if the stock's going to go down, why not sell it, let it go down further and buy at a lower price?" Sounds great, but in reality it's impossible to time selling at the top and buying back at the bottom. More often than not you sell a good stock before you have a chance to buy it back, it goes up and you've missed it; that happened to me many, many times. It's like your spouse is in a bad mood so you run away to the pub for a drink with friends, and by the time you get back she's run off with your best friend. So never, never leave a great company, never leave a great spouse; hold on, let it compound, and when it goes through bad moods and corrections, use that as an opportunity to add more shares. That's how you grow your wealth, and that's why Charlie Munger said the big money is not in buying and selling; the big money is in waiting, in inactivity. It sounds difficult and counterintuitive, but it's true.

#### [15:27] Rule 5: Focus on business performance, not the stock price
Psychology rule number five: focus on the business performance and not the stock price in evaluating the success of your investment. Many people judge their investments by short-term price movement: buy a stock, it goes up next month, "good investment"; it goes down for a month, "bad investment." That's not true. For example, many people bought Alibaba and Tencent and other Chinese companies that are fundamentally good businesses, but because of emotions, bad news and the Chinese crackdown the share price went down, and they think it was a mistake to buy Tencent or Alibaba. Is it really an investment mistake? No. If the business itself is doing well, making money and growing, even if the share price goes down temporarily it does not mean it's a bad investment. Judge the success of your investment by the business itself: as long as the business is growing and making money, it's a good investment regardless of short-term price movement.

In the short term the price movement is not driven by logic or fundamentals; it is driven by emotions and manipulation, and there's no way to predict for certain where the price will go. So if you buy a good company and the price goes down, don't feel regret and don't beat yourself up, because there's no way you could have predicted it. As long as it's a good business and fundamentals remain great, any short-term weakness in price is an opportunity to add more shares. In the short term the stock price can be vastly different from, even divorced from, the value of the business. So judge your investment by the performance of the underlying business and take advantage of the market's short-term mispricing: let the market serve you, rather than letting the market instruct you on what is a good investment.

#### [18:25] Buffett and the Washington Post
A great example is how Warren Buffett turned $10 million into $1.5 billion with one stock, the Washington Post. He bought it at about $20 per share when it was worth $100 per share, that is, 80% undervalued. How could he buy it 80% undervalued, and why didn't everyone do what he did? Here is an interview where he explains the psychology of his investment (Buffett's words as on the video):

Interviewer: In 1973 maybe your first big run at the Washington Post; you began to buy shares. How did you come to the conclusion? Buffett: I'd looked at the prospectus in 1971, when Charlie and I first met Kay Graham, and they were going public right at the time of the Pentagon Papers. In 1973 the stock got very cheap because their television licenses were under challenge by Bebe Rebozo, Nixon's friend, and the stock went from, I think, like 38 to 16 or so in a very short period. When it got down to around 20, just in a few blocks, we bought almost 10% of the company, and that now is about 18% because they've repurchased shares. When it got to about 20, the whole Washington Post company, with 4,800,000 shares out, was selling for $100 million. They owned the Washington Post, Newsweek, four big network television stations including CBS in Washington, D.C. and Jacksonville, Florida, everything, and they owed no money. If you took those separate parts, you could literally have been out in the middle of the Atlantic Ocean at midnight in a rowboat, and you could have sold them for $500 million; people would have been swimming out in shark-infested waters, if necessary. And you could have had all of it for $100 million. Interviewer: Here's what I don't understand, how come 95 people didn't see that? Buffett: It was amazing. We were buying it from big institutional investors, and they were bailing out of it. If you'd asked any one of them what the pieces were worth, they would have said something close to what I would say. But they thought the stock was going to go down. So what? And the stock did go down; in fact, after we bought it, it went down a little more. So what? I'm not looking at the price of the stock, I'm looking at the value of the business; I'm looking at the properties and at the Graham family. That stock we paid about $10 million for is probably worth a billion and a half or more today.

What I found most intriguing was not that he bought it 80% undervalued, but that all the other analysts and big investment houses agreed the stock was worth $100 and the business itself $500 million, and yet they were not buying; they were selling it at $20, and Buffett was buying at $20. Why? Because most of them were concerned that the share price would go down next year; they were concerned about short-term price movement. So they passed up the opportunity to buy a great business at an 80% discount. Because Buffett thought long term and not short term, he did the deal and turned $10 million into $1.5 billion.

#### [21:42] Does this happen today?
It happens all the time. A most recent example is the good Chinese companies like Tencent or Alibaba. Ask most analysts and investors and they'd agree Alibaba is worth about $300 a share, but right now it's a hundred dollars per share and no one dares to buy it except a few people like me. Why? People are afraid: what if it keeps going down? Because of short-term focus you miss the opportunity to buy that great company at an 80% discount. When would the share price rebound and reflect the true value of the business? No one can predict; it could be one month, three months, six months, a year or even three years, but you've got to be patient. And if you buy a great company and the price keeps going down, don't be disheartened: Buffett bought the Washington Post at $20 and it went down further to $16. You can never catch the bottom, but you don't need to buy at the absolute bottom. As long as you bought when it's undervalued, have the patience to hold on and know the value of the business, you'll be richly rewarded over time.

### The Psychology of Winning Investors, Part 3 (Rules 6 to 10)

#### [00:00] Rule 6: Think independently
Psychology rule number six is to think independently. One reason many people don't succeed in the markets is that they are creatures of conformity. They get excited when other people are excited and afraid when others are afraid; they follow the crowd. If you do that you always tend to buy high, because you buy when everyone is excited and the market is high, and sell low, because everyone is scared when it's low. If you keep buying high and selling low you will always lose money, even though you may buy the right companies or the index. To profit from the market you must train yourself to think and feel opposite of everyone else, to go opposite of the masses. That's the only way to beat the market. Learn to be fearful when others are greedy and greedy when others are fearful. It's easier said than done, which is why you have to train yourself.

Only you control your emotions. No one can make you feel scared except yourself and no one can make you feel excited except yourself. You create your emotions based on what you choose to focus on and the meaning you give to the experience. The best way to train yourself is to participate in the community and the chat groups, which strengthens your ability to think against the crowd.

#### [01:35] "Worst day" headlines and the fear and greed index
An interesting observation: every time the market has a big drop, the next day you see an article with a person with a very sad face and the word "worst day" in the title, for example "Dow craters in worst day" in the new lockdown. Whenever you see the words "worst day" and a picture of a very sad man, usually that's the best time to start adding shares, because that's when the media creates a lot of fear. This article came out on the 28th of October; after the drop you see a big article full of fear, and that's the time to add shares, usually just before the market starts to bounce back up. This pattern has repeated time and time again. Here's another example: after a big drop you again see the article with "Dow's worst"; you have to have the word "worst" and a man with a very sad or fearful face, and that's usually when you catch the market before it goes up again. So be greedy when people are fearful. It's easy to tell from these articles: a fearful face with the word "Wednesday" is a sign that people are fearful.

There's also the fear and greed index, at money.cnn.com fear and greed (as heard, "money, CNN.com, fear and greed"), which measures the amount of fear in the markets, swinging from extreme fear to extreme greed. As I'm talking, it's the 21st of December 2021 and the market is at extreme fear (it can get even more extreme, but it's already at extreme fear), which is why I've been adding shares the last few days. Remember we can't predict the market with certainty; it doesn't mean the market will go up next day or next week. But it tells you that when people are scared, good companies are selling at discounts, and you can only buy a good company at a discount when people are scared.

Scrolling down, you can see the market is like a pendulum, going from greed to fear and back. Currently it is at level 25. During the COVID crash last year it went all the way down to 10, and it also dropped during the trade war. The point is that every time people get scared, the market rebounds. And once there's extreme greed and everyone is buying, that's the time you don't want to buy, because when everyone has bought, the market tends to drop. So go opposite of everyone's emotions: the best time to buy is during extreme fear and the best time to sell is during extreme greed.

#### [05:14] The boom and bust cycle of emotions
The market does not go up in a straight line; everything is in a cycle, the boom cycle and the bust cycle. Near the top of the cycle people have all the positive emotions, thrill and euphoria: "I'm a genius", "we're going to be rich", "I've got to tell everyone to buy". When everyone around you is talking about stocks and buying, the market is near the top. That's when you don't want to buy; that's when you want to start taking profits. You want to buy when prices are low, and prices are low when people feel angry, panicked, depressed, in disbelief. When people around you are saying "don't touch stocks, this company's really bad, we're going to die", that's the time to buy stocks (of good companies, of course).

I'll end this with a quote from John Templeton, another legendary investor: bull markets are born on pessimism. When a bull market starts, the general public is very pessimistic, thinking the market will crash. They grow on skepticism: as the market goes up, people are skeptical, "no, it won't go up", and the more people say it won't, the more it goes up. They mature on optimism: when everyone around you says "we're going to do well", the bull market is nearing the end. And they die on euphoria, when people think the market will keep going and they'll be rich. So the time of maximum pessimism is the best time to buy stocks and the time of maximum optimism is the best time to sell stocks.

#### [07:37] Probability, not prediction
When the market is on an uptrend there's a higher probability prices go up; on a downtrend a higher probability they go down; and when you see a sad face with "worst day" there's a probability prices bounce. It's all about probability: by studying chart patterns we can guess the probability of where price is likely to go. But no one can predict short-term price movement with absolute certainty. Prices are driven by demand and supply, which is driven by emotions and manipulation, which is driven by news; we can't predict tomorrow's news or how millions of people will react.

#### Rule 7: Never attempt to predict the market
Your decision to buy and sell should never be based on predictions but on the fundamentals of the business: if it's a good business, undervalued, retraced to a support level, you add shares. That's it. And you never buy all at once; you buy in tranches to average your cost. Warren Buffett said: we have long felt that the only value of stock forecasters is to make fortune tellers look good. At the beginning of every year analysts and economists predict this year the market will go up, or down; all that forecasting is as good as fortune telling, as good as the weatherman. You can only guess, not predict for certain. If you make decisions based on predictions, over the long run it leads to bad performance, because a predictive mindset causes you not to follow your predetermined rules. For example, if you predict "the market's going to crash this year", you won't buy a good undervalued business because you're afraid of a crash, and then it goes up and you miss the opportunity because of your prediction. I do look at the market and its trend, but I always tell myself never to make decisions based on predictions.

As investors our job is not to predict short-term price direction, because it's impossible to get right all the time. Our job is to buy businesses, great companies that would thrive and grow under all economic conditions. I built a bulletproof portfolio of great companies, so whether there's high or low inflation, high or low interest rates, recession or no recession, my group of companies would collectively grow over time. That's the only thing you've got to do as a successful investor.

Every day on CNBC or Bloomberg, experts make predictions, and for every expert who predicts the market will go up there's another who predicts it will go down. Sometimes one is right, sometimes the other; your chance of being right is 50-50, so you might as well toss a coin. Back in October 2009 the Dow Jones was at 9,800 points, and people wondered whether it would continue up or crash back down. On CNBC on the 6th of October the headline said "Experts position themselves for Dow 10,000", i.e. bullish. The day before, on the 5th of October, the headline said "Dow will fall to 6,003 by year-end" [earlier pass has 6,300], per a survey of portfolio managers. So who do you listen to, CNBC or CNBC? They said opposite things on the 5th and 6th of October. The answer: don't listen to anyone, it's all crap, they're like fortune tellers. Instead, follow the trend. If the market's on an uptrend, the probability is prices go up; on a downtrend, prices keep going down. If you hold good companies they go up and down in the short term but in the long term they always go up.

On that 6th of October chart, half predicted up to 10,000 and half a crash back to 6,000. What would you do, buy or sell? Look at the trend: it's an uptrend. How do you know? The 50 moving average is above the 150, so the probability is that prices keep going up. What happened? Not only did it go up to 10,000, the Dow Jones went all the way up to 11,000, 20,000, and now it's at 23, 24,000 (as heard). Imagine you'd listened to those clowns who said it would crash to 6,300: you'd have shorted the market and lost money as it went up, or avoided buying good stocks and lost the chance to make money. That's why I keep telling my students to stop reading the mainstream news and its predictions and forecasts, because they do nothing but distract you, cloud your mind and screw you up psychologically. Follow the rules instead.

#### [14:24] The S&P 500 ETF and the news of the last 10 to 12 years
Something mind-blowing, at least to me: the S&P 500 ETF from 2008 to 2019, about 10 years, went from 80 points to 290 points, up 262% (I didn't update the slide; today SPY is at 400, about 450 points thereabouts [as heard]). So since the beginning of 2009 the market has gone up like 470%, 400%. Even if you didn't know how to pick the right stocks, if you just bought the ETF and closed your eyes for 10 or 12 years you would have made 400% on your money. Even a blind, one-legged baboon could have made money in the stock market in the last 12 years. But did everyone? No; many people in the last 10 to 12 years did not make money, and some lost money. How can people lose money when the market keeps going up (for the last "120 years", as heard)? The only reason is that they read the stupid news and stupid predictions that caused them to sell low and buy high.

Here's the news from the last 10 to 12 years:
- New York Times, July 3rd, 2010 (2010 is right before this monster 400% bull run): "A market forecast that says take cover." With the stock market lurching again, plenty of investors are nervous and some are downright bearish. Robert Prechter (transcript: "Preacher"), a market forecaster and social theorist, "is in another league entirely": he is convinced that we have entered a market decline of staggering proportions, perhaps the biggest of the last 300 years. If you read that, would you buy or hold stocks? No, you'd sell. If you'd listened and sold, you'd have missed out 400% growth in your wealth, or worse, if you had shorted the markets you'd be destroyed. (So don't listen to preachers. I'm just kidding.)
- 2011, USA Today: "S&P lowers its outlook. Could US default on its debt?" People said to me "Adam, how can you invest in US companies? The government's going to default on the debt and collapse." Sure enough, the market went up.
- 2012, CNBC: "Europe debt defaults are poised to rattle stocks": not only is the US in trouble, now the Eurozone sovereign debt crisis, so sell all the stocks.
- 2013, Bloomberg News: "A US default seen as catastrophe, dwarfing Lehman's fall" (the 2009 crash from Lehman Brothers; this time worse). People get freaked out, sell all their stock or don't dare to buy and miss the market going up again. That same year another white-haired, smart-looking man: "87% risk of stock crash by year end." Did the market crash in 2013? No, it went up 20%.
- 2015: David Stockman said stocks and bonds will crash soon. How can you not listen to a guy called Stockman, born to talk about stocks? Even if you took my course and Adam says it's a great company and undervalued, but Mr. Stockman said we're going to die, who do you listen to, Adam Khoo or David Stockman? If you listened to Stockman you wouldn't have made money.
- 2016: "Here comes the biggest crash in a generation." It didn't crash; it went up again.
- 2018: "Why markets are still heading for a crash?"

If you read the financial media, every bloody year they tell you the market is going to crash. Why? Because bad news sells newspapers, articles, clicks and advertisements. Will the market crash? Once in a while, of course. Every six years the market will go through a bear market; it's a statistic. Once a year the market will go down at least three to five percent. Once in two years it will go down 10%. Once in six years it will go down 20%. Markets will go down, but when they do you don't fear them, you take advantage of them. Exactly when, no one knows or can predict. So stop making predictions, stop reading this garbage, focus on building a portfolio of great companies on your watch list, and start adding shares whenever you see those corrections, and you'll do very, very well.

#### [21:28] Rule 8: Pay no attention to macroeconomic or geopolitical events
Many people think that to be a successful investor you've got to know about the economy, GDP, interest rates, yield curves, inflation, and read all this data to know where the economy is going. Absolute crap and rubbish. Rule number eight: pay no attention to macroeconomic or geopolitical events. Number one, there's no way to predict macroeconomic events; no one can predict when they're going to raise interest rates or predict inflation. Everyone predicts, but getting it right is a 50-50 chance. Number two, you can't predict how the market will react to economic events. Since you can't predict all this, why waste your time? Also pay no attention to geopolitical events: the moment an event occurs, like the Omicron virus or the government shutdown, the market reacts instantaneously, and there's no way you can make money by reading all this macroeconomic news.

Although in my newsletter I do write about interest rates, GDP and inflation, and talk about these in my YouTube videos, understand that I do so purely for intellectual and entertainment purposes only. I pay zero attention to any of these when making my investment decisions to buy or sell. It's all market noise that is unpredictable. Peter Lynch said it best: if you spend more than 13 minutes analyzing economic and market forecasts, you've wasted 10 minutes of your time. All the great investors have said this and I repeat it: focus on the business. If the business is growing and making money, that's all you need to know. Ignore interest rates and inflation; it sounds intellectual and smart to talk about, but it has no use at all in being a great investor.

The only reason to sell a stock is if the business itself is no longer a great business. If it is a great business, never sell because of macroeconomic or geopolitical reasons. A quote I love, again from Peter Lynch: your ultimate success or failure will depend on your ability to ignore the worries of the world long enough to allow your investments to succeed. The problem with most people is they get so worried about what's happening in the world that they don't allow the companies they buy to reach their full potential. Take the 10-year period on the S&P 500 where it grew almost 500%: most people would never have enjoyed that growth, because look at all the reasons to sell that freak people out from the news: 600,000 jobs lost in March (as heard), Chrysler and GM file for bankruptcy, BP oil spill, flash crash, European sovereign debt crisis, powerful earthquake, S&P downgrades US debt, fiscal cliff talks, US government shutdown, taper tantrum, Ebola contagion fears, Brexit, Charlottesville, Dow falls the biggest point decline ever, US government shutdown again, S&P enters bear market, trade war, yield curve inverts, Iranian bombing, emergency Fed injection. There are so many reasons to cause you to sell. Ignore all these, focus on great companies, and you would make millions and millions in the market.

#### [25:51] Rule 9: Risk management and capital protection
Before they buy a stock, most people think about how much money they can make, but never how much they'll lose if they're wrong. Professional investors are the complete opposite: before we buy a stock, the first question is "how much can I lose if I'm wrong?" and then "how much money can I make?" Always focus on the risk first. Always protect your downside and the upside will take care of itself. As a successful investor, the first rule is not making money; the first rule is to never lose money. Rule number one, do not lose money. Rule number two, how much can I make.

No matter how great a business is, never put all your eggs in one basket, because there's no such thing as a sure thing. Things can go wrong that you can't anticipate; even a great business can be hit by news you can't anticipate and the price could go down 40, 50% in the short term. For example, Boeing. I own Boeing shares and have owned them for many, many years; it's a great business that has always been doing well. But suddenly we had the plane crash, the 737 MAX planes (transcript: "787 max") grounded for two years, with the pandemic, and all these things caused Boeing's share price to drop 50%. If you'd put your entire savings into Boeing, your portfolio would be down 50% (of course it's recovered since). We want to grow our wealth with minimum volatility. So no matter how great a company is, diversify across many great companies, so that if one or two go down temporarily, it's fine because the rest go up and buffer the short-term drawdowns, and overall your portfolio still goes up. If you buy a portfolio of great companies and buy them at less than what they are worth, collectively you can't lose money. You cannot lose money if you buy a group of great companies for less than what they are worth. So always diversify.

My suggestion is to always ensure you have a portfolio of a minimum of eight companies, at least eight, up to say 20, 30 companies, so you always risk a small percentage of your capital on any one investment. If your portfolio has 10 companies, each should have a 10% allocation of your capital. If you have 20 companies, each stock has a 5% allocation. Very, very important.

#### [28:39] Rule 10: Take responsibility and learn from mistakes
The last psychological rule is to take responsibility and learn from mistakes. In the course of investing will you make mistakes? Of course, we all do. The important thing is what you do when you make one. It's not what happens to us that determines our success or failure, but how we choose to respond to what happens. Do you learn from your mistakes, give excuses, ignore them, blame someone else? That's the critical thing.

How do you know when an investment is a mistake? You found a great company, undervalued, you bought it, and the price continues dropping 10, 20, 30, even 40% below your purchase price. Is that a mistake? No, it is not, if the underlying business is growing and is a great company that passes all the fundamental steps. Even if the price keeps going down in the short term it is not an investment mistake, because we can't control where the price goes in the short term; it's manipulation and emotions. In fact, if you still have cash and it's not a full allocation in the portfolio, you could add more shares to average down. However, if after you buy, the underlying business fundamentals have changed, meaning it's no longer a good business and has lost its competitive advantage, then it's a mistake. Or if after buying you realize it failed one of the criteria, then it's a mistake.

When you realize you've made a mistake, sell the stock immediately, regardless of whether it's above or below your purchase price. Win, lose or draw, get out, cut it, take the cash and reinvest it in a great company that can grow your money. Reinvest the proceeds in a great business instead. Don't be like some people who say "it's going to come back, wait a while". And of course, if it's a lousy company, for goodness' sake don't buy more shares.

Think of yourself as the boss of a company. If you hire a salesperson, pay him a salary, and he turns out to be a lousy employee, brings in no sales, is rude to your customers, you fire him immediately, take his salary and hire someone else for the job. Would you pay him more salary and say "stay on, I hope you'll change"? No. As an investor, when you invest in a company it's like hiring an employee and paying him a salary; the moment you find it's a lousy employee, fire him. It doesn't matter that you've already paid his salary for a few months. Get out, take the salary and hire someone better. Think logically and not emotionally. Never get emotionally attached to a stock, because the stock doesn't know or care who you are. "I've got to make the money back from this stock": no, you can make your money back from other great companies. Never stubbornly hold on to a stock of a lousy business.

Never ever blame anyone for losing money: not the market, not God, not your broker, not your mother-in-law, wife or kids. When you blame people, you are not in control of your results; it means they are controlling your results, and as long as people control your results they control your life and you can't change it. You want to control your life. Take responsibility: when you take ownership and believe "I am creating my results, I am creating my life", you give yourself the power to change your life and your results. Like the old saying, for things to change, I must change first. Don't wish for the world to get better; focus on making yourself better by constantly learning and growing your skill sets and strengthening your psychology.

#### [34:10] Review of the 10 psychological rules
These are the 10 psychological rules of successful investors. Quick review:
1. Be disciplined. Follow the rules.
2. Invest only in what you understand; stick to your circle of competence.
3. Be patient and think long term.
4. Buy slowly and sell even slower.
5. Focus on the business performance and not the stock price to judge your investment performance.
6. Think independently. Go against the crowd: be greedy when others are fearful, fearful when others are greedy (as heard: "fearful when others agree").
7. Never attempt to predict the market.
8. Pay no attention to macroeconomic and geopolitical events; focus on the business itself.
9. Risk management and capital protection: diversify and focus on protecting your capital.
10. Take responsibility and learn from your mistakes.

Remember what I said in the beginning: strategy, the how to invest, is important but it's only 30%; 70% of your success comes down to your psychology, your ability to manage your emotions, to have the focus, discipline, resilience and drive to follow through and work your investment plan. I hope you print this out, place it on your wall or desktop, and keep reading it so you integrate these things.
